- **[Real-Time Human Trajectory Prediction from RGB-D for Mobile Robots Using Bi-Mamba](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33916)**  `arXiv:2610.33916`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Basheer Al-Tawil, Magnus Jung, Ayoub Al-Hamadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting the short-term future trajectory of a person from RGB-D cameras is a key capability for mobile robots navigating in dynamic and human populated environment. The key challenge to deploying learned predictors on real robots is the train-deploy pipeline gap. Training data are often prepared with an idealized, smoothed ground truth, whereas at inference, the model is fed noisy estimates from pose detectors and depth sensors. The model is therefore evaluated on an input distribution it never encountered during training. Most existing models rely on recurrent neural networks (RNNs) or transformers, which either struggle to capture short-term temporal dependencies or suffer from computational cost. We present TrajMamba, a lightweight, bidirectional state space model for short-term human trajectory prediction using an RGB-D camera. The model uses a single shared processing path for both training and deployment, aligning human pose extraction, depth back projection, and smoothing processes. It encodes a short window of persons's hip center velocities with stacked bidirectional Mamba blocks and decodes future velocities with a GRU head. On the ETH/UCY benchmark, TrajMamba reaches an average ADE of 0.29 m FDE of 0.51 m. With 262K parameters, it runs in real time on modest hardware and is deployed on a TIAGo robot with an onboard NVIDIA Jetson Orin.
  </details>

- **[RoboJev: An Open Vision-Language Judge for Robot Success Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33718)**  `arXiv:2610.33718`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Josef Liyanjun Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many robot manipulation videos do not indicate whether the task was successfully completed. RoboJev-27B is an open-weight vision-language model that takes a task instruction, 10 frames of a manipulation attempt, and (if available) final frames from auxiliary cameras and predicts whether the robot accomplished the task. RoboJev-27B is a variant of JEV-27B-VL, trained with a rank-32 LoRA adapter on the language model only (keeping the vision encoder frozen) using 52,598 episode records. It also uses a source-specific KL penalty that applies only to research data, anchoring predictions closer to the original model on those sources. It uses a calibrated two-token readout that outputs success/failure probabilities in a single forward pass. On 268 held-out episodes of the same robot model (SO-101), RoboJev-27B attains 95.2% balanced accuracy and 0.993 AUROC. It also generalises to episodes collected by unseen labs/robots (AUROC = 0.890 over 459 episodes) as well as to clearly labelled community-collected episodes (accuracy = 93.3% over 135 episodes), where the base model attains an AUROC = 0.900 on the unseen-lab/robot subset with the same inputs. It takes 0.26 seconds per episode (median, serial) to infer on a single H100, which is about \$0.19 per 1,000 success predictions at \$2.69 per GPU-hour (not including media processing and serving costs). RoboJev-27B is released together with its inference code, along with a transparent evaluation: 20,628 predictions organized into 29 model/protocol entries are made available to facilitate browsing and to enable reproducible benchmarking of success detection. We also compare against strong API-based frontier VLMs, which take in fewer input frames (4 images from the primary camera only); note that this comparison is not strictly like-for-like because RoboJev-27B is given more frames at inference.
  </details>

- **[VPR-Space: A Benchmark for Visual Place Recognition in Space Analog Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35642)**  `arXiv:2610.35642`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Richard Fechner, Peer Neubert_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual Place Recognition (VPR) is an important means for loop closure detection in visual Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (SLAM). While state-of-the-art VPR and SLAM approaches excel in urban and feature-rich natural environments, the vast and feature-poor environments of extraterrestrial surfaces like on the Moon or Mars pose severe challenges for existing approaches. This paper presents a novel benchmark, VPR-Space, to evaluate VPR capabilities on extraterrestrial surfaces. There are three contributions: (1) We collected sixteen VPR database-query datasets based on existing data from Space Analog Domains (SAD), terrestrial environments that resemble properties of the Moon or Mars. The collection includes a range of challenges regarding environment, viewpoint change, and illumination conditions. (2) To address the ill-posed nature of VPR, we propose a novel approach to generate the ground-truth information in VPR benchmarks. Instead of defining that a query and datbase image show the same place if and only if their camera poses are within fixed position and viewing direction thresholds, we propose to make the decision dependent on whether the match would be valuable in the downstream SLAM task. To avoid the overhead and restrictions of evaluating VPR in-the-loop within SLAM systems, we provide VPR ground-truth (GT) matrices based on the performance of an ensemble of state-of-the-art Relative Pose Estimators (RPE). Under this GT we require a VPR method to identify exactly those image pairs for which a subsequently applied RPE is expected to provide a valuable pose estimate in a SLAM context. (3) We evaluated nine state-of-the-art VPR approaches. The results show considerable room for improvement and indicate directions for future research. We provide a pre-release version of the dataset on github: $\texttt{this https URL}$
  </details>

- **[Model-Aware Generation of Safety-Critical Scenes for Robust End-to-End Driving via Distilled Planner Surrogates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35448)**  `arXiv:2610.35448`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Khaled M. Seyam, Julian Wiederer, Samar Fathalah, Markus Braun, Bin Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end driving models perform well on standard benchmarks but collide frequently in safety-critical scenarios. We present a failure-mode analysis on NeuroNCAP which shows that 95.9% of DiffusionDrive's collisions are planning failures despite reliable detection, pinpointing the bottleneck in planning rather than perception. We attribute this to a fundamental data imbalance, as safety-critical interactions are rare in real driving logs, so planners are hardly ever trained on them. We close this gap with a novel model-aware framework that generates safety-critical training scenes against a specific planner. Each planner is attacked at the bird's-eye-view (BEV) level through a differentiable surrogate distilled from its own planning head. This keeps the expensive video renderer out of the attack loop. We extract 50 scenes per planner, each pairing an attack with a safe escape trajectory that serves as the fine-tuning label. A fully automated quality-diversity search generates both the attacks and the escapes, so that their variety is not specified by hand. Only these scenes are rendered to fine-tune the planner, which reduces the computational cost significantly. Fine-tuning on these scenes roughly halves the closed-loop collision rate of two planners on NeuroNCAP (SparseDrive 28.0% to 12.2%, DiffusionDrive 37.5% to 19.2%) while largely preserving normal driving. A cross-planner transfer experiment shows that the gain comes from targeting the specific planner. Each planner improves most on the scenes generated against itself, and DiffusionDrive fine-tuned on SparseDrive's scenes retains only 6.7 of its 18.3-point collision-rate reduction.
  </details>

- **[Doppler-aided Position-Velocity Tracking of Hidden Objects using mmWave Radar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34085)**  `arXiv:2610.34085`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Yerin Cheon, Kush Garg, Akshat Dave_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe indoor operation requires autonomous tracking of objects that are not directly visible to the robot. Optical non-line-of-sight (NLoS) methods depend on ambient lighting and scene geometry or on weak multi-bounce returns that need long acquisitions. Millimeter-wave (mmWave) radar offers an attractive alternative: walls act as reflectors for mmWave signals, and frequency-modulated continuous-wave (FMCW) radars enable fast, practical measurement of range, angle, and velocity. However, the low resolution of these radars leads to spatially ambiguous observations. The Doppler information, although particularly sensitive to target motion, remains underexplored in indoor NLoS localization: existing methods emphasize spatial representations and either collapse or discard the Doppler dimension before learning. To leverage the complete Range-Azimuth-Doppler (RAD) information, we acquire the first indoor NLoS radar dataset that pairs sequential RAD measurements with synchronized position and velocity ground truth under controlled variations in motion: 576 sequences totaling 720,856 frames across 12 trajectories of a mobile robot moving along varying speeds, starting positions, and material configurations. We then formulate NLoS tracking as sequential position-velocity estimation rather than frame-wise localization: a shared 3-D encoder processes the full RAD representation of consecutive frames, separate recurrent branches estimate position and velocity, and post-inference refinement combines the two into a motion-consistent trajectory. Our method achieves 8.95cm position and 1.75cm/s velocity errors on held-out trajectories and generalizes to unseen scenarios involving a moving human target. Together, our dataset and tracker establish a benchmark for tracking hidden moving targets with a low-cost radar.
  </details>

- **[Behavior Spatial Benchmark: Evaluating Spatial Understanding for Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34051)**  `arXiv:2610.34051`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Sirui Chen, Huang Huang, Qineng Wang, Wenlong Huang, Manling Li, Ruohan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot tasks require grounding spatial language in physical scenes, but task success alone cannot establish whether spatial instructions are followed. We present BEHAVIOR-Spatial, a benchmark built on BEHAVIOR-1K that measures spatial question answering, point-based target grounding, and robot action to examine how VLM spatial understanding relates to VLA behavior. It comprises 3,256 spatial questions across 49 activities, 1,240 grounding queries across 14 manipulation tasks, and action evaluations covering those 14 tasks and 10 navigation tasks. Our manipulation protocol evaluates held-out configurations and instruction following when the requested target changes within a fixed training layout. The baseline achieves 41.6% on held-out configurations but only 16.1% under changed instructions. VLM adaptation and robot-data pretraining provide limited improvements, whereas oracle visual target cues raise these rates to 60.3% and 38.0%, respectively. Perception evaluations reveal policy-dependent correlations between VLM question-answering and grounding accuracy and VLA manipulation performance. Together, these findings motivate evaluating spatial instruction following alongside task completion and examining when spatial perception performance translates into robot behavior. Project website:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[ReSight: Task-Driven Self-Improving Memory for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33487)**  `arXiv:2610.33487`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Lirong Che, Yang Chen, Yimeng Su, Daqi Liu, Zhenyu Huang, Jiong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots can use historical observations to answer spatiotemporal queries in familiar environments, but the presence of relevant evidence does not imply that existing tools support the operations a task requires. We introduce ReSight, a task-driven self-improving memory system for embodied agents that expands the capabilities for processing and accessing historical observations as task demands arise. ReSight retains keyframes and their spatiotemporal associations, separating observation evidence from extensible derived fields so that stored observations can be reprocessed for new needs. An execution agent and a coding agent share this memory through different interfaces: the former calls registered tools within a bounded online budget, while the latter uses a programmable workspace and a larger exploration budget to inspect evidence, write programs, and acquire models as needed. To realize capability expansion in ReSight, we develop Explore-Distill-Mount (EDM), which identifies capability gaps from execution and exploration records without task reference answers and converts exploration-derived solutions into reusable memory builders, query tools, and skills, extending memory representations and access capabilities as needed. Experiments on three public benchmarks demonstrate effective base memory access and capability expansion. With the execution model and online budget held fixed within each benchmark, EDM reduces mean position error on OC-NaVQA from 33.10 m to 24.72 m and improves high-level success on FindingDory from 38.3% to 63.8%. With the tools and skill frozen, success still improves by 28.3 percentage points on FindingDory episodes excluded from exploration and distillation. Experiments on real robot patrol memories further demonstrate reuse of these capabilities for subsequent queries and new memories, with fewer online tool calls on average.
  </details>

- **[CLARO: Clutter Aware Robot Orchestrator for Open-Vocabulary Grasping using VLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24016)**  `arXiv:2610.24016`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Sungsu Kim, Vineet Bhat, Prashanth Krishnamurthy, Ramesh Karri, Farshad Khorrami_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots that follow natural-language instructions unlock a wide range of autonomous applications in households, warehouses, and labs. Real-world environments are diverse and often heavily cluttered, requiring robots to plan over multiple steps, act, and replan as they work toward a goal. We present CLARO (CLutter-Aware Robot Orchestrator), a VLM agent that orchestrates a set of perception and manipulation skills: open-vocabulary object detection, segmentation, 6-DoF grasping, and non-prehensile pushing to locate and retrieve a language-specified target in clutter. CLARO maintains a memory of its past actions to support long-horizon execution, and emits manipulation targets (grasp poses/3D waypoints) allowing transfer across robot arms without task-specific training. We evaluate CLARO against a range of methods on a dynamic simulation environment and standard image-based benchmarks, demonstrating strong generalization across object classes, lighting, and clutter levels. CLARO attains an average success rate of 89.1%, a relative improvement of over 16% on the strongest competing method in high cluttered scenes. We further analyze performance along grounding and grasping axes to characterize where each method succeeds and fails. Finally, we deploy CLARO on a robot arm across diverse cluttered scenes, achieving a success rate of 88.8%, and demonstrating strong sim2real generalizability.
  </details>

- **[WorldOracle: Benchmarking Action-Conditioned World Models against Real-World Executions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18826)**  `arXiv:2610.18826`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Haoyi Duan, Liliang Chen, Nan Wang, Yang Zhang, Yuhang Lu, Xiaotong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce WorldOracle, a benchmark specifically designed to evaluate action-conditioned world models (ACWMs) against paired real-world executions. Given the same initial observation and low-level action sequence, WorldOracle asks whether a predicted rollout reproduces what happens in the real world, including physical consequences and task-state transitions. We evaluate models along four complementary dimensions: physical state correctness, interaction spatial alignment, key interaction area fidelity, and multi-view action coherence. To support this evaluation, we curate real-world training and evaluation suites that cover different interaction dynamics and embodiments. The evaluation suite contains long, multi-segment episodes with both successful and failed executions, enabling evaluation across varied action outcomes and behavior distributions. Experiments on seven representative ACWMs reveal distinct model strengths and failure modes. WorldOracle provides a systematic foundation for measuring and advancing action-conditioned world modeling for manipulation. We release the datasets, evaluation code, and leaderboard:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[WorldArena 2.0: Extending Embodied World Model Benchmarking on Modality, Functionality and Platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17912)**  `arXiv:2605.17912`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhuohang Li, Yiding Ma, Yu Shang, Lei Jin, Shengyuan Wang, Jianjie Fang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models have emerged as a central paradigm for embodied intelligence, enabling agents to predict action-conditioned future and reason about environmental dynamics. However, existing embodied world model benchmarks are still largely confined to vision-only prediction, offline embodied applications, and simulator-based evaluation, making them insufficient for assessing increasingly comprehensive world models. In this work, we introduce WorldArena 2.0, an expanded benchmark that systematically broadens embodied world model evaluation along three dimensions: modality, functionality, and platform. Along the modality dimension, WorldArena 2.0 extends evaluation from vision-only to visuotactile modalities, enabling assessment of multimodal perception and prediction. Along the functionality dimension, it extends beyond policy evaluation and planning to assess world models as interactive RL environments for policy optimization. Along the platform dimension, it moves beyond simulator-only evaluation to a diverse suite of simulated and real-world robotic settings across multiple embodiments. Under a standardized protocol, WorldArena 2.0 comprehensively evaluates perceptual quality, interactive utility, and cross-platform performance, providing a comprehensive testbed for tracking progress toward embodied world models. The project page including codes and demos is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Guardian: Detecting Robotic Planning and Execution Errors with Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.01946)**  `arXiv:2512.01946`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Paul Pacaud, Ricardo Garcia, Shizhe Chen, Cordelia Schmid_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robust robotic manipulation requires reliable failure detection and recovery. Although recent Vision-Language Models (VLMs) show promise in robot failure detection, their generalization is severely limited by the scarcity and narrow coverage of failure data. To address this bottleneck, we propose an automatic framework for generating diverse robotic planning and execution failures across both simulated and real-world environments. Our approach perturbs successful manipulation trajectories to synthesize failures that reflect realistic failure distributions, and leverages VLMs to produce structured step-by-step reasoning traces. This yields GuardianFail-36k, a large-scale failure reasoning dataset built upon the RLBench simulator and the BridgeDataV2 real-robot dataset. Using GuardianFail-36k, we train Guardian, a multi-view reasoning VLM for unified planning and execution verification. Guardian achieves state-of-the-art performance on three unseen real-world benchmarks: RoboFail, RoboVQA, and our newly introduced UR5-Fail. When integrated with a state-of-the-art LLM-based manipulation policy, it consistently boosts task success rates in both simulation and real-world deployment. These results demonstrate that scaling high-quality failure reasoning data is critical for improving generalization in robotic failure detection. Code, Data, and Models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Automotive-ENV: Benchmarking Multimodal Models in Automotive Cockpit Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21143)**  `arXiv:2509.21143`  `cs.RO` `cs.CL`  
  _Junfeng Yan, Biao Wu, Meng Fang, Ling Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal models have demonstrated strong capabilities in web, desktop, and mobile GUI environments, but their performance in automotive cockpit systems remains largely unexplored. In-vehicle GUIs introduce unique challenges beyond traditional GUI interaction, including vehicle-state reasoning, implicit intent understanding, and safety-critical decision making. To address this gap, we introduce Automotive-ENV, an interactive benchmark for evaluating multimodal models in automotive cockpit environments. Built on Android Automotive OS, Automotive-ENV combines cockpit-screen interaction with vehicle-state signals and deterministic state-based evaluation. The benchmark contains 420 carefully constructed tasks covering routine cockpit operation and safety-critical scenarios. Unlike prior GUI benchmarks that rely on action matching or LLM-based judging, Automotive-ENV evaluates model behavior through reproducible validators over UI states, vehicle signals, and safety-related conditions. We benchmark representative general-purpose multimodal models and GUI-specialized vision-language models on Automotive-ENV. Experimental results show that current models remain far from reliable in automotive environments, with substantial performance degradation in implicit and safety-critical tasks. Further analysis reveals consistent failure modes in safety-aware reasoning, vehicle-control understanding, and implicit hazard recognition. We release Automotive-ENV, together with tasks, validators, and evaluation tools, to support future research on automotive multimodal systems.
  </details>

- **[SceneAgent: An Agentic Pipeline from 3D and 4D Captures to Simulation Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34745)**  `arXiv:2610.34745`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Luke Hollis, Tianxing Fan, Heng Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual captures provide detailed scene appearance, but converting them into interactive simulation environments requires object reconstruction, physical modeling, and correction of intermediate errors. We present SceneAgent, an agentic pipeline that coordinates these steps through simulator-based visual inspection and repair. The pipeline processes 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS), photogrammetry, LiDAR captures, AI-generated 3DGS scenes, and 4D videos of dynamic scenes, and combines object segmentation, physical parameter estimation, part decomposition, articulation, and deformable simulation. To support policy learning, SceneAgent generates diverse object variants ("digital sisters") and scripted demonstrations. On a cluttered bin scene from GraspClutter6D, SceneAgent raises F1 at 1 cm for every coding agent with scorable outputs both with and without its skills, and its reconstructions are better than SimFoundry: all agent configurations exceed SimFoundry's F1 score, with a best of 60.75% versus 6.9%. On three dynamic scenes from 4DCodeBench, SceneAgent raises the mean dynamic IoU of Gemini 4 Argon from 0.86% to 99.09%. We further demonstrate diverse simulated interactions and preliminary policy transfer to a real robot.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Obstacle-Conditioned Safety in Household Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34674)**  `arXiv:2610.34674`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Hyunwoong Bae, Jisu Han, Jiyeon Choi, Dohoon Kim, Taesup Moon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe household navigation requires robots to regulate clearance and speed according to what an obstacle is and how close it is, without unnecessary conservatism. Yet existing evaluations do not test whether semantic safety distinctions are reflected in continuous motion. To that end, we introduce OSMA-Bench, an Obstacle-Conditioned Safe Motion Adaptation Benchmark built on spatially matched scenario sets that differ only in obstacle type. We also propose the \emph{Semantic Sensitivity Index} (SSI), which measures whether clearance and distance-dependent speed and smoothness follow the caution ordering of obstacles. Evaluating VLM-based and vision-language-action (VLA) policies on the benchmark, we find that neither consistently adapts its motion to obstacle safety, even with explicit safety prompts. Our analysis shows that the policies can correctly distinguish obstacles by safety relevance but fail to reflect this distinction in the motion parameters or actions they produce. For VLM-based policies, which recognize the obstacle and then generate a navigation program, adding an explicit parameter-inference step between these two stages improves adaptation. These findings expose a gap in \emph{semantic-to-motion grounding}, highlighting the need to translate semantic caution into appropriately safe motion.
  </details>

- **[FlowHOI: Flow-based Semantics-Grounded Generation of Hand-Object Interactions for Dexterous Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13444)**  `arXiv:2602.13444`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Huajian Zeng, Lingyun Chen, Jiaqi Yang, Yuantai Zhang, Fan Shi, Peidong Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent vision-language-action (VLA) models can generate plausible end-effector motions, yet they often fail in long-horizon, contact-rich tasks because the underlying hand-object interaction (HOI) structure is not explicitly represented. An embodiment-agnostic interaction representation that captures this structure would make manipulation behaviors easier to validate and transfer across robots. We propose FlowHOI, a two-stage flow-matching framework that generates semantically grounded, temporally coherent HOI sequences, comprising hand poses, object poses, and hand-object contact states, conditioned on an egocentric observation, a language instruction, and a 3D Gaussian splatting (3DGS) scene reconstruction. We decouple geometry-centric grasping from semantics-centric manipulation, conditioning the latter on compact 3D scene tokens and employing a motion-text alignment loss to semantically ground the generated interactions in both the physical scene layout and the language instruction. To address the scarcity of high-fidelity HOI supervision, we introduce a reconstruction pipeline that recovers aligned hand-object trajectories and meshes from large-scale egocentric videos, yielding an HOI prior for robust generation. Across the GRAB and HOT3D benchmarks, FlowHOI achieves the highest action recognition accuracy and a 1.7$\times$ higher physics simulation success rate than the strongest diffusion-based baseline, while delivering a 40$\times$ inference speedup. We further demonstrate real-robot execution on four dexterous manipulation tasks, illustrating the feasibility of retargeting generated HOI representations to real-robot execution pipelines.
  </details>

- **[One Scan is Enough: Demonstration-Free Adaptation for Language-Guided Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33794)**  `arXiv:2610.33794`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhuoyuan Yu, Yuxing Long, Du Wang, Junyang Wang, Hongwei Fan, Hao Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deploying a language-guided navigation policy in a new building usually means watching it fail. Public-benchmark VLAs degrade scene by scene as layout, appearance, and language drift, and the standard fix of teleoperating fresh trajectories on site does not scale. We replace human demonstration with a single brief iPhone scan. Scan2Instruction compiles that scan into a complete training environment, automatically synthesizing executable navigation episodes with grounded instructions. PRPT-KS then adapts the policy through a closed-form Pareto utility read directly off the reconstructed scene, with no learned critic and no human labels. Across five real environments, the two designs together drive our RealNav to an 85% real-robot success rate, compared with 49% for a RealNav baseline post-trained on human demonstrations, and still achieve 82% in unscanned regions of the same buildings, compared with 41% for the same baseline. Our work turns demonstration-free scene adaptation for language-guided navigation from an aspiration into a working reality.
  </details>

- **[Profiling a Frontier Agent with EBench: From Direct Control to Harness with Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35832)**  `arXiv:2610.35832`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Yifei Yao, Yude Zou, Guanbo Jiao, Xing Gao, Ning Gao, Yukai Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can control robots without robot-specific training, but how their capabilities compare and interact with post-trained vision-language-action (VLA) and world action model (WAM) policies across manipulation demands remains to be thoroughly evaluated. In this report, we evaluate GPT-6-Astra on EBench as the core of an agent that controls a robot through an agent harness under the same visual and proprioceptive observation protocol as post-trained policies. We study two agent configurations: direct control, in which the agent generates waypoint commands directly, and harness with policy, in which it can also invoke a post-trained VLA or WAM policy for action execution. Under direct control, the agent achieves competitive overall performance relative to post-trained policies. It leads on short-horizon tasks and exhibits the least performance variation across generalization dimensions, whereas it underperforms post-trained policies on long-horizon tasks, tabletop manipulation, and high-precision contact-rich tasks. In the harness with policy, invoking a post-trained VLA or WAM combines the complementary strengths of both components, raising overall task success to the level of the strongest compared post-trained policies while substantially reducing token cost. However, performance remains below the per-task upper bound of the standalone components due to suboptimal policy invocation, fixed-instruction execution without subgoals, and grasp disruption when the agent resumes control. Overall, our study characterizes the capability boundary of a frontier MLLM agent and suggests directions for improving future agent harness design. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[VLA-HWBench: Where the Time Goes When VLA Models Run on Edge Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34717)**  `arXiv:2610.34717`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Borui Xu, Hongshi Tan, Wenqi Jiang, Bingsheng He, Ming Wu, Jason Zeng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision--language--action (VLA) models increasingly run on edge devices aboard robots, where inference latency constrains closed-loop robot control. However, it remains unclear how to design VLA models, their inference software and edge hardware so that robots can run VLA inference in real time. Most VLA models report only task success, while prior studies cover limited action-generation strategies, divide inference into at most three stages, and lack comparisons of inference optimizations on the samethis http URL18 open VLA models across released and alternative backends on NVIDIA Jetson AGX Thor and AMD Ryzen AI Max+ PRO 395, with a seven-stage inference breakdown. Our study reveals three insights. First, action-generation strategy matters more than model size. It dominates 14 of 19 Jetson configurations, latency varies by $82\times$ on Jetson and $157\times$ on AMD, and parameter count explains at most 15% of latency variance. Models that generate long action chunks in a few flow-matching steps keep up with control, whereas token-by-token decoding does not. Second, kernel fusion with 4-bit weights helps most, running $\pi_{0.5}$ and GR00T N1.7 2.6--2.9$\times$ faster than PyTorch with \texttt{this http URL}, but it exists for only these two models and only on the Jetson. Optimization also moves the time to the stages it shortens least, as CPU preprocessing grows from 12\% to 39\% of the GR00T N1.7 call. Third, after optimization, memory bandwidth limits only the stages that process few tokens on the Jetson, such as the denoising steps, whereas software limits all other stages, from unfused kernels to serial preprocessing on the host CPU. Overall, low-latency edge VLAs should use few action-generation iterations, inference software should extend kernel fusion and low-bit weights to more models and platforms, and edge hardware needs high memory bandwidth and a fast host CPU.
  </details>

- **[RobotAgentBench: Towards Open-world Robot Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34649)**  `arXiv:2610.34649`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Lingjun Mao, Jinglin Cao, Lukun He, Wenpeng Xu, Yuchen Yan, Hengyu Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in vision-language-action (VLA) and world-action models (WAMs) have significantly improved robotic manipulation, yet deploying robots in the open world demands a higher level of autonomy beyond executing predefined tasks. Robot agents offer a promising paradigm for this transition, integrating task discovery, contextual reasoning, physical execution, and adaptive recovery into a closed interaction loop. However, existing benchmarks largely evaluate isolated capabilities, failing to capture the complexity of open-world robot autonomy. We introduce RobotAgentBench (RAB), a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating robot agents in realistic, open-world household environments. Through an agent-assisted, human-in-the-loop real-to-sim pipeline, RAB constructs 50 diverse household layouts with interactive objects and physically grounded tasks, encompassing 90 atomic tasks, 18 short-horizon tasks, 8 long-horizon tasks, and 4 open-ended scenarios. RAB provides a multi-level evaluation of robot agent capabilities: (1) task-level assessments systematically examine five complementary dimensions: task discovery, context reasoning, manipulation, failure recovery, and robustness; (2) scenario-level evaluation assesses how agents integrate these capabilities to autonomously fulfill high-level user needs. Furthermore, RAB enables systematic comparisons of standalone VLA/WAM models and diverse robot agent architectures under a unified planning-execution-verification framework. Extensive experiments reveal substantial gaps between manipulation and autonomy, demonstrate the advantages of agentic architectures and recursive self-improvement, and highlight the remaining challenges toward open-world robot autonomy.
  </details>

- **[POD-RoPE: Rotary Position Encoding over Physical Observation Distributions for Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33745)**  `arXiv:2610.33745`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaodi Huang, Zilin Wang, Jinpeng Liu, Pengchuan Xiao, Zhenlei Shao, Zhang Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied perception requires positional representations that capture both physical geometry and the spatial and temporal extent of observations. We introduce POD-RoPE (Physical Observation Distribution RoPE), which represents token observation footprints as distributions over camera centers, viewing directions, and sampling times. Under independent sampling, expected rotations encode relative relationships between distributions; when each distribution collapses to a single address, the construction recovers pointwise RoPE. The same rotation statistics support Q/K matching and coherence-dependent V/O transformations, allowing observation geometry to guide both information selection and content aggregation. Per-token pre-aggregation avoids increasing sequence length or processing pairs of observation nodes. We evaluate POD-RoPE across four robot policy architectures, simulation and real-world manipulation, and complementary reconstruction, depth-estimation, and novel-view-synthesis tasks. Integrating POD-RoPE into Fast-WAM improves mean success from 91.83% to 92.88% across all 50 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks, and from 32.5% to 52.5% on two real-robot tasks on Sharpa North. Controlled reconstruction ablations support the contributions of distribution encoding and V/O transformations. In H20 model-block benchmarks, POD-RoPE achieves median forward-plus-backward speedups of $1.91\times$ over P-RoPE and $5.34\times$ over RayRoPE, with a median latency overhead of 17.4% over Standard RoPE. These results support physical observation distributions as an effective and computationally efficient basis for geometry-aware attention.
  </details>

- **[WorldFailBench: Do World Models Understand Policy Failures?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33565)**  `arXiv:2610.33565`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zheng Zhang, Xiaoyang Tang, Shuaiqi Cheng, Jiakai Huang, Zhongbo Wu, Ziyi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot policy evaluation informs deployment decisions and policy improvement, but physical testing and simulator preparation require substantial effort. World models offer a complementary route through closed-loop interaction in generated environments. Recent benchmarks have demonstrated their potential as policy evaluators through outcome-based evaluation (i.e., assessing task success or failure). However, such evaluation does not establish whether world models faithfully simulate how policies fail. Misrepresenting failures can lead to incorrect diagnoses of policy weaknesses and make policies appear ready for deployment even when small scene changes cause them to fail. We therefore ask: do world models faithfully simulate how and at which stages policies fail, and under what conditions they transition from success to failure? To answer this question, we introduce WorldFailBench with three evaluation tasks. Failure reason tests whether models reproduce the observable errors policies make. Failure stage assesses whether failures concentrate at the same stages of full-task execution. Failure boundary examines whether models reproduce the conditions under which policies transition from success to failure under controlled scene changes in simulation. The benchmark covers manipulation tasks in the LIBERO and RoboTwin simulation environments and real-world manipulation tasks performed by the G1 humanoid robot. Experiments across seven world models reveal substantial gaps in their understanding of policy failures, providing guidance for developing more reliable world models for policy evaluation.
  </details>

- **[AffordSim: A Scalable Data Generator and Benchmark for Affordance-Aware Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11674)**  `arXiv:2604.11674`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Mingyang Li, Haofan Xu, Haowen Sun, Xinzhe Chen, Sihua Ren, Liqi Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many everyday robot manipulation skills are affordance-dependent, with success determined by whether the robot contacts the functional object region required by the subsequent action. Current simulation data generators obtain contacts from generic grasp estimators or per-object manual contact annotations, but generic estimators rank stable grasps without task semantics and often select contacts that are misaligned with the downstream action, while manual contact annotations must be rewritten for each new object and task. To solve these challenges, we introduce AffordSim, a scalable data generator and benchmark that integrates open-vocabulary 3D affordance prediction into simulation-based trajectory generation. Given a natural-language task description, AffordSim synthesizes a task-relevant scene, emits affordance queries, grounds them on object surfaces, samples region-conditioned grasps, and selects executable candidates with motion planning. It further randomizes object pose, texture, lighting, image noise, and cross-viewpoint backgrounds for sim-to-real transfer. We instantiate AffordSim as a 50-task benchmark across diverse manipulation skills, five robot embodiments, and 500+ rigid and articulated objects. AffordSim achieves 93% of the trajectory collection success rate of manual contact annotations on affordance-critical tasks and 89% on hard composite tasks. Vision-language-action policies trained on AffordSim data transfer zero-shot to a real Franka FR3, reaching 24% average success.
  </details>

- **[CRAFT-E: An Interpretable and Editable Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Affordance-Grounded Active Object Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04231)**  `arXiv:2512.04231`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhou Chen, Joe Lin, Carson Bulgin, Sathyanarayanan N. Aakur_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Assistive robots operating in unstructured environments must reason not only about what objects are, but how they can be used, including non-canonical and improvised uses. This requires grounding language-based action queries to objects that afford the requested function, are visually present, and can be physically retrieved. Existing approaches often rely on black-box models, fixed affordance labels, or direct object predictions, limiting transparency and controllability. We introduce CRAFT-E, a modular neuro-symbolic framework that composes an LLM-derived verb-property-object knowledge graph with CLIP-based visual alignment and energy-based grasp reasoning. The system exposes interpretable grounding paths, incorporates grasp feasibility into affordance inference, and supports targeted graph edits without retraining. We also construct a benchmark dataset with unified annotations for verb-object compatibility, segmentation, and grasp candidates, and deploy the full pipeline on a physical robot. Across static scenes, real-world trials involving 20 verbs and 39 objects, ImageNet-based functional retrieval, and COCO-Tasks transfer, CRAFT-E provides a competitive training-free prior for affordance-grounded retrieval while exposing component-level diagnostics and graph-level interventions. Under staged and full-pipeline perception, CRAFT-E matches strong flat LLM priors in several settings while adding failure attribution and editable reasoning paths. By coupling symbolic reasoning with embodied perception, CRAFT-E offers an inspectable and customizable framework for affordance-grounded object retrieval.
  </details>

- **[Preemptive Instruction Following with Resumption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35576)**  `arXiv:2610.35576`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zerun Liu, Lechao Cheng, Yimin Liu, Shengeng Tang, Yaxiong Wang, Zhun Zhong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon embodied navigation may be interrupted by a higher-priority instruction before the current task sequence is complete. The agent must finish its current atomic task, execute the inserted task, and resume the remaining sequence from the updated environment state, where its viewpoint and available navigation candidates may have changed. To evaluate this capability, we introduce PIFR benchmark, a novel task for runtime task preemption and resumption in embodied navigation. PIFR includes single, repeated, and nested interruption settings,with stage-specific metrics for inserted-task completion, resumption of the original task, and completion of the full sequence. Evaluations show that recovery performance varies across interruption conditions, while aggregate success can obscure differences in interruption handling and resumption. We further propose a lightweight candidate-ranking baseline to improve target re-grounding after interruption. Together, PIFR and the baseline provide a systematic framework for studying interruption handling and task recovery in long-horizon embodied navigation.
  </details>

- **[EmbodiedLab: Benchmarking Embodied Agents That Act to Know](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35385)**  `arXiv:2610.35385`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hongcheng Gao, Zelin Zheng, Alex Zhou, Jay Zhu, Shijia Ge, Yazhe Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied agents are expected to accomplish real-world goals by deciding for themselves how and when to interact with their surroundings. For many such goals, changing the physical state of the world is the means rather than the end: the agent changes the state of a system through interaction, observes the feedback, and explores until it either achieves the goal itself (e.g., discovering physical laws) or learns what the goal requires; it acts to know. Manipulation benchmarks stop at acting to change, with the target state given; scientific discovery benchmarks evaluate knowing but replace physical experimentation with symbolic actions or numerical queries. We introduce EmbodiedLab, a benchmark in which a robot must act to know. Quantitative science experiments make this setting controllable: the parameters of the system are resampled for every instance, so the result cannot be known in advance, yet it can be scored exactly. Across 30 experiments in five disciplines on 14 apparatus platforms, the agent receives an objective and the apparatus, chooses its own procedure, and operates a physics-based simulated laboratory, reading the instruments through its cameras. Evaluation checks what the agent came to know, grounded in readings recorded during the episode, as well as the physical state the task requires; every experiment is verified to be solvable. We evaluate six frontier models in their developers' coding agents with native observation and control, with added perception and motion planning, and with manipulation skills distilled from the strongest agents. With native control, even the strongest agent succeeds less than half the time; for every agent, most failures arise in interacting with the apparatus, and manipulation skills help weaker agents more than perception and planning. The benchmark and its evaluation code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Labference: Benchmarking the Reasoning-Action Connection in Robotic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35252)**  `arXiv:2610.35252`  `cs.RO`  
  _Shitong Liu, Jingwei Zhang, Sheng Lian, Xinyu Zhuang, Kaiwen Guo, Quan Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Realizing Embodied AI for Science requires robots to translate experimental goals into reliable physical procedures, reducing hazardous and repetitive human work while generating evidence for scientific discovery. Although language-based planning and learned manipulation provide complementary capabilities, their integration requires robots to adapt subsequent operations to actual execution outcomes. For example, an unsuccessful placement changes what must be done next, even when the experimental goal remains unchanged. Existing benchmarks assess planning, manipulation, and laboratory workflows through task completion and reasoning or execution metrics; however, aggregate performance leaves the connection between outcome-dependent choices and physical execution unresolved. To address this evaluation gap, we introduce Labference, a benchmark for evaluating how observed physical outcomes guide subsequent choices and how those choices translate into robotic laboratory actions. Its evaluation protocol is designed to test outcome-dependent behavioral adaptation through controlled evidence variations, while independently measuring operation selection, physical execution, and task completion. Matched Oracle-assisted continuations from shared checkpoints then assess which forms of support improve subsequent choices and task completion. Initial weighing evaluations reveal target-selection errors in the tested VLA policies despite successful object transfers, whereas GPT-driven control achieves correct selection but encounters execution failures and substantial computation costs. Together, these findings highlight the need to connect task-appropriate reasoning with reliable, timely action. By making this connection measurable and diagnosable, Labference supports targeted improvements toward autonomous experimentation for Embodied AI for Science.
  </details>

- **[RetroNav: Scene-Grounded Retrospection and Retry for Self-Evolving Lifelong Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35172)**  `arXiv:2610.35172`  `cs.RO`  
  _Diantao Tu, Yangdong Liu, Niannian Dai, Mengqi Rong, Shuhan Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing robot navigation methods primarily focus on isolated tasks, overlooking the need to accumulate knowledge and experience across repeated navigation episodes. Although recent lifelong navigation methods have explored cross-task knowledge accumulation, they mainly focus on scene knowledge while neglecting historical navigation experience and its connection to scene evidence, limiting the ability to reflect on past mistakes and improve future decisions. To address this limitation, this paper presents RetroNav, a training-free self-evolving navigation framework that integrates navigation, memory accumulation, and failure reflection. Specifically, we develop a unified memory system that connects geometric and semantic scene knowledge with historical navigation experience, distinguishing objective observations from task-dependent interpretations. After each navigation attempt, an independent evaluator determines whether a retry is necessary. If so, a Retrospection Agent revisits historical decisions using accumulated scene knowledge and navigation records, generating an evidence-grounded retry brief to guide the next attempt. RetroNav achieves state-of-the-art performance across five navigation benchmarks. Our method will be open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VISTA: Active Exploration for Self-Improvement in Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35051)**  `arXiv:2610.35051`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhang Zhang, Shengjun Zhang, Simin Huang, Min Chen, Chong Xia, Xin Fei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic embodied intelligence plays a foundational role in open-ended, dynamic physical environments. Rather than treating policy learning as an end-to-end mapping from instructions to actions, recent studies reveal that embodied systems depend not only on pre-trained foundation models, but also on harness. Prior approaches largely treat rollouts as static logs for post-hoc reflection, producing harness updates without autonomously testing and refining them through subsequent embodied interaction. To address this challenge, we propose VISTA, a meta-agent framework that transforms post-hoc rollout reflection into active self-exploration for embodied harness improvement. Specifically, VISTA maintains a harness graph that records the lineage of proposed harnesses, which enables the meta-agent to retrieve prior hypotheses, build on effective mechanisms, and avoid repeatedly exploring failed directions. Each iteration begins from a retained harness version and its execution evidence. Rather than directly committing an update from these historical trajectories, the meta-agent actively explores in the environment. It autonomously generates new rollouts, probes execution failures and recovery behaviors, and iteratively modifies the harness based on the resulting evidence. The refined harness is then evaluated afresh and added to the harness graph. We evaluate VISTA on the LIBERO-Pro, RoboCasa, and RoboTwin benchmarks. Experimental results demonstrate that VISTA progressively improves task success and is more rollout-efficient than static reflection, achieving higher success rates with fewer simulator rollouts.
  </details>

- **[JevVLA: Jev-Inspired Energy-Based Adaptation for Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35010)**  `arXiv:2610.35010`  `cs.RO`  
  _Wei Wang, Wenqiao Zhang, Zheqi Lv, Zheng Dong, Yingjie Niu, Jiaqi Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) policies can select among discrete candidates for pick-and-place manipulation or generate continuous action chunks for motion control. Improving both forms of decision making through a common mechanism while retaining the capabilities of a pretrained policy is an important adaptation problem. Inspired by Jev's structured, probabilistic decision interface, we propose JevVLA, a lightweight energy-based adaptation framework for VLA policies. JevVLA instantiates this inspiration by modeling the relative quality of action proposals: with the base policy frozen, it learns a differentiable scalar energy conditioned on observations, task context, and actions, using execution feedback, expert corrections, or demonstration-derived preferences. JevVLA uses this single learned action preference function in two ways: its values compare discrete candidates, while its action gradients correct continuous actions or guide their generation. Candidate selection and continuous control thus share one trainable decision core. Each benchmark uses independently trained weights and fixed input/output encodings, while retaining the same core network with approximately 80,000 trainable parameters. On the held-out test sets, JevVLA improves task success over the respective matched base-policy controls by 1.28, 2.31, 1.60, 1.52, and 1.28 percentage points on MetaWorld MT50, VIMA, LanguageTable, LIBERO Plus, and CLIPort, respectively. These results support the feasibility of a lightweight, Jev-inspired energy model for adapting both discrete action selection and continuous action guidance. The JevVLA implementation will be open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[How High Can GPT-6 Astra Climb in Contact-Rich Manipulation? PhysLadder: A Hierarchical, Matched Sim-Real Benchmark for Physical Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34527)**  `arXiv:2610.34527`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haibao Yu, Jiaqi Meng, Zijian Zhang, Bowen Jing, Lei Yang, Yue Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce PhysLadder, a hierarchical Sim-Real benchmark for evaluating physical interaction capabilities in contact-rich manipulation. Its 16 household tasks span three cumulative levels: contact selection and geometric alignment (L1), force regulation within sustained contact (L2), and coordinated contact reconfiguration and force regulation (L3). Each task is instantiated in physically and visually matched simulated and real-world environments, with shared task definitions and success criteria. We evaluate GPT-6 Astra zero-shot with three trials per task in each environment. Real-world success rates decline from 80.0% at L1 to 47.6% at L2 and 0.0% at L3, with corresponding simulation rates of 66.7%, 47.6%, and 0.0%. Ten of the 16 tasks have identical success counts across environments; the remaining six differ by one success out of three trials. Across both environments, recurring difficulties in spatial reasoning, contact selection, force regulation, and coordination often prevent local physical progress from accumulating into task completion. These findings characterize Astra's observed capability boundary across increasing interaction demands and, for this model, support matched simulation as a repeatable, scalable testbed for studying the sustained, coordinated physical interactions that limit reliable manipulation.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Dexterous Manipulation via Action Representation Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34453)**  `arXiv:2610.34453`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yanjinghao Xu, Jiexin Zhang, Huan Zhao, Jianjia Mo, Zhenghao Yin, Dingkang Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale vision, language, and video pretraining has substantially improved the perception and generalization capabilities of robotic manipulation models, but action generation for complex dexterous manipulation remains constrained by the scarcity of robot demonstrations. Limited robot demonstrations must be used to learn both action representations and task-conditioned action distributions, making it difficult to adequately learn action representations and, in turn, limiting the ability of downstream generative models to generate complex dexterous actions. To address this limitation, we propose ARPG (Action Representation Pretraining and Generation), which explicitly separates action representation learning from action generation: we first pretrain an action representation encoder on large-scale human manipulation data. We then freeze the encoder and train a compact generative backbone (130M parameters) to learn task-conditioned action distributions in the encoder's representation space. Experimental results show that ARPG achieves state-of-the-art performance on the DexJoCo benchmark and improves the average success rate on bimanual dexterous manipulation from 10.1% for $\pi_{0.5}$ to 44.7%. Scaling experiments further show that broader coverage of human manipulation behaviors effectively improves downstream performance. These results indicate that action representation pretraining provides an independent scaling dimension for improving complex dexterous manipulation capabilities.
  </details>

- **[RynnVLA-Latent: Scaling Robotic Manipulation Foundation Models with 3D-Grounded Latent Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34356)**  `arXiv:2610.34356`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haoyu Zhao, Xingyue Zhao, Zhian Su, Bohan Hou, Yinzhou Tang, Fengyi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling vision-language-action (VLA) models is constrained by scarce action-labeled demonstrations and incompatible control spaces across robot embodiments. We present RynnVLA-Latent, a framework that turns heterogeneous interaction videos into scalable supervision for robot policy pretraining. At its core, RynnLAM learns continuous, 3D-grounded latent actions from visual transitions without requiring native robot controls. By combining residual future-feature prediction, camera-compensated 3D-flow supervision, and camera-motion decoupling, RynnLAM encourages representations that capture control-relevant motion rather than appearance changes alone. We apply RynnLAM to 30.1K hours of human, robot, and simulation videos, producing 745.52M latent-action descriptors. RynnVLA-Latent then learns to predict latent-action chunks from visual observations and language through flow matching, before adapting its pretrained backbone and action expert to embodiment-specific robot actions. The resulting 4B policy achieves 98.8% success on LIBERO, 84.7% on LIBERO-Plus without benchmark-specific adaptation, 51.9% average success across five VLABench tracks, and 88.6% average success on the RoboTwin 2.0 bimanual benchmark. It also achieves the highest observed success rates among the compared methods on six real-world manipulation tasks across dexterous-hand and mobile-gripper platforms. Together with representation ablations and data-scaling results, these findings demonstrate the potential of latent-action supervision for scaling robot policy learning beyond native action-labeled data.
  </details>

- **[HuGe: Scaling Humanoid Generalist Vision-Language-Action Models with Egocentric Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34186)**  `arXiv:2610.34186`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yang Liu, Jiacheng Liu, Hengtao Li, Yuheng Huang, Zijian Yang, Ji Dai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Extending Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) to humanoid robots requires coordinated whole-body control and diverse demonstrations that are costly to collect in real-world scenarios. Although egocentric human demonstrations provide a complementary source of task experience, differences in visual embodiment and missing robot proprioception complicate transfer to humanoid policies. To address these challenges, we introduce HuGe, a family of Humanoid Generalist VLAs pre-trained on over 2,000 hours of egocentric human and robot data. Specifically, HuGe employs three existing representative VLA architectures to predict latent motion tokens for a pretrained whole-body controller, together with hand-control signals. Based on this, we introduce a three-stage training pipeline that combines egocentric pre-training, humanoid adaptation, and downstream fine-tuning. To facilitate transfer across embodiments, we construct pseudo-proprioceptive states through simulation playback during pre-training and modify arm appearance in egocentric observations through re-rendering during downstream fine-tuning. With this pipeline, HuGe achieves the state-of-the-art on simulation benchmark HumanoidArena. Furthermore, real-world experiments on the Unitree G1 indicate that augmenting robot demonstrations with visually adapted egocentric demonstrations significantly increases success on a pick-and-drop task.
  </details>

- **[Lightweight Change Detection for Adaptive Collective Perception in Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34059)**  `arXiv:2610.34059`  `cs.RO`  
  _Thiemen Siemensma, Tiago Travassos, Jiacheng Zhang, Bahar Haghighat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Swarm robotics enables scalable inspection through collective perception, where robots infer environmental properties from distributed observations. A common benchmark considers robots exploring black-white tiled environments and determining the majority tile type. While most work studies static environments, dynamic environments remain underexplored. We study collective perception in dynamic environments where the tile proportion and the spatial correlation of surface patterns may change over time. To enable implementation on resource-constrained robots, we integrate a lightweight change detection mechanism into a Bayesian collective decision-making framework that resets robot beliefs when distribution shifts are detected. The proposed detector requires constant memory and constant per-sample computation, unlike two recent competitive approaches that store the full sample stream, making it suitable for memory-constrained robots. We further introduce a method that detects spatially correlated sampling and adapts sampling intervals online to mitigate correlation effects. Our simulation results in Webots show competitive change detection delays, more consistent precision across communication settings, and improved decision accuracy under changing spatial correlations.
  </details>

- **[RoboLongR: Causally Consistent Rewards Teach Robots to Progressively Master Long-Horizon Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33863)**  `arXiv:2610.33863`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zixuan Chen, Lin Cao, Fenggang Zhang, Haotong Zhang, Yangtao Chen, Jieqi Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon manipulation requires actions whose validity depends on earlier interactions, yet visual progress can conceal missing task prerequisites. We propose RoboLongR, an offline-to-online framework that learns history-dependent rewards to guide policy adaptation to new tasks. Its central objective is causal consistency: aligning rewarded progress with prerequisites supported by execution history. Stage-shuffle supervision encourages execution-order sensitivity, recurrent memory preserves evidence of earlier interactions, and two-round training refines trajectory segments using predicted progress. The resulting rewards guide stage-conditioned policy refinement from behavior cloning to online reinforcement learning. Sparse terminal feedback connects policy experience to reward learning by triggering correction for repeated high-scoring failures. Pretrained on demonstrations from 88 RLBench tasks and Open-X data, RoboLongR achieves 31.8% average success on 18 long-horizon tasks excluded from reward pretraining, exceeding the strongest evaluated baseline by 9.2 percentage points. Across four real-robot tasks, it achieves 57.5% success, a 32.5-percentage-point improvement over the comparison method. Reward-level controls probe sensitivity to synthetic order perturbations, while ablations examine the contributions of individual components to policy improvement.
  </details>

- **[RoboWorM: Evaluating Movement Realism, Consistency and Acceleration Affects in Embodied Video World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33807)**  `arXiv:2610.33807`  `cs.RO`  
  _Doanh Le Thien, Viet-Thanh Nguyen, Pham Tri Quang, Nhi Pham, Christopher Wewer, Minh-Loi Nguyen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video world models (VWMs) are rapidly emerging as scalable neural simulators for robotic manipulation. However, most benchmarks only evaluate them as open-world video generators, using Vision-Language Models (VLMs) that lack strict 3D spatial and kinematic reasoning. To bridge this gap, we introduce RoboWorM, a comprehensive, deployment-aware benchmark that leverages known hardware priors such as robot kinematic trees and fixed camera configurations, to systematically examine structural and spatiotemporal fidelity. RoboWorM curates 1,605 episodes across single-arm, bimanual, and humanoid morphologies, spanning both Markovian and long-horizon non-Markovian tasks under single- and multi-view setups. To explicitly measure physical consistency, we propose two deterministic, VLM-free metrics: KIVA for intra-view kinematic stability, and RIGIS for multi-view geometric consistency. By exploiting intrinsic robotic invariants, KIVA improves hallucination detection alignment with human annotations by 25.8% relative to the strongest frontier VLMs, while RIGIS achieves a 130% higher Spearman correlation with human preferences. Furthermore, since real-world deployment requires low inference latency, we utilize RoboWorM to conduct the first large-scale audit of the quality-efficiency trade-off. Evaluating 9 state-of-the-art acceleration techniques across 3 leading world models (over 29,000 generated rollouts), we demonstrate how these acceleration methods can retain superficial visual quality while silently compromising deployment-critical physical constraints. Using our metrics, we achieve 2.3$\times$ maximum inference speed-up with minimal quality loss.
  </details>

- **[MimicAll: Structured Embodiment Conditioning for Cross-Humanoid Whole-Body Motion Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33508)**  `arXiv:2610.33508`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jia Li, Lingyun Xu, Yitang Li, Hao Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoid motion trackers reproduce diverse behaviors, but sharing a policy across bodies requires accounting for differences in structure, morphology and dynamics. We present MimicAll, a reinforcement-learning framework for shared whole-body motion tracking across heterogeneous humanoids. A structured embodiment descriptor is encoded into global and joint-aligned conditions, which are combined with observation history and body-matched motion references to predict joint-target offsets. Physics-consistent embodiment randomization, body-calibrated retargeting and heterogeneous simulation support training across 4,096 variants from 16 humanoid families. We introduce a benchmark of 464 bodies across five distribution strata to evaluate motion generalization, within-family embodiment shifts and unseen-family transfer. Controlled ablations show that embodiment information improves motion completion with simple concatenation, while self-attention yields the lowest global position errors. With equal training transitions, shared training achieves 99.4% 30-second success on randomized G1 bodies in 100STYLE, versus 42.9% for a single-body specialist. With Flat-MLP encoding and concatenation, continued training on a motion subset excluding obstacle categories achieves 98.3% and 93.5% 30-second success on 100STYLE for sampled training bodies and out-of-distribution variants within seen families, respectively. On an eight-motion LAFAN1 subset excluding ground and get-up sequences across 464 bodies, mismatched body conditions reduce self-attention's 30-second success from 61.7% to 47.0%, demonstrating a closed-loop benefit from embodiment information.
  </details>

- **[Tiny Recursive Policy: Recursive Latent Refinement for Visuomotor Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33288)**  `arXiv:2610.33288`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hakan Aktas, Cengiz Oztireli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Behaviour cloning has become a powerful approach for robotic manipulation, but many modern policies reconstruct their internal representation from a fixed observation window at each prediction and do not maintain persistent latent context throughout execution. We introduce Tiny Recursive Policy (TRP), a parameter-efficient behaviour-cloning policy that combines persistent temporal state with recursive latent refinement. At each control step, TRP repeatedly updates high- and low-level latent states using a shared computation module before decoding an action sequence, and propagates the resulting state to subsequent predictions. We evaluate TRP across LIBERO-100, MetaWorld ML45, and the LIBERO-Mem benchmarks. TRP performs competitively across multi-task and few-shot settings and shows substantial gains on long-horizon and context-dependent tasks, while using significantly fewer parameters than large generative baselines. Execution-time perturbations show that reliance on the persistent carry increases with temporal-context demand, while training with carry perturbations substantially improves robustness to both carry and visual corruptions. Ablations reveal a strong interaction between persistent state and recursive refinement: the benefit of carry depends strongly on the amount of within-step computation, and the resulting performance cannot be explained by parameter count alone.
  </details>

- **[Physical AI for Intralogistics: Benchmarking the NVIDIA Sim-to-Real Ecosystem for Cosmos-Enhanced COMPASS Navigation on Industrial Mobile Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28432)**  `arXiv:2610.28432`  `cs.RO`  
  _Charbel Abi Hana, Rayan Zaitouni, Kameel Amareen, Mohamad Mostafa, Hasan Sayour, Wei Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classical robot navigation stacks are reliable but often require re-engineering for each new robot type and site. Learning-based navigation shifts this effort from hand-tuned parameters to data generation and training, where a policy adapts to each new embodiment or facility by fine-tuning in simulation. Whether policies adapted this way stay reliable on real industrial robots has seen little systematic testing. We study this for NVIDIA's X-Mobility and COMPASS workflow for training learned navigation policies on Idealworks robots. In simulation, we extend the COMPASS benchmark with two embodiments, the differential-drive iw$\mkern-1.5mu.\mkern-1.5mu$hub and an omnidirectional Humanoid Mobile Platform (HMP), and with a digital twin of the Idealworks R&D center. For the real world, we fine-tune X-Mobility on data from this digital twin, with and without sim-to-real variants generated by NVIDIA Cosmos, train COMPASS on top of it from scratch in the digital twin, deploy the resulting policies on the iw$\mkern-1.5mu.\mkern-1.5mu$hub's onboard Jetson AGX Xavier, and benchmark them in the Idealworks R&D center against COMPASS trained only on NVIDIA's environments and the existing Idealworks navigation stack on task success, efficiency, safety, smoothness and onboard compute. Running every static scenario both in a digital-twin replica and in the real facility measures the sim-to-real gap and how far Cosmos-augmented data closes it. Training in the digital twin with Cosmos sim-to-real augmentation raises the real-world success rate of learned navigation from 65.3% to 91.7% across four scenarios and nearly halves its weighted travel time compared with simulation-only training, bringing it within 8.3 points of the production navigation stack, fully onboard; with an integrated global planner, it also reaches far, occluded goals in every trial.
  </details>

- **[Embodied Playground: A Long-Horizon Agentic Robotics Benchmark for Physical, Strategic, and Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27039)**  `arXiv:2610.27039`  `cs.RO`  
  _Feng Chen, Pei Zhou, Lixuan He, Yunchao Yao, Li Sun, Shenghua Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation models offer a promising basis for robotic agents, but long-horizon tasks require them to sustain coherent reasoning and reliable physical execution across interdependent stages. We introduce Embodied Playground, a benchmark for evaluating how foundation-model agents coordinate physical, strategic, and spatial reasoning throughout such tasks. Agents must track task progress, satisfy intermediate constraints, and revise their plans using execution feedback as earlier actions shape subsequent decisions. The playground comprises 410 task environments spanning basic manipulation, sequential puzzles, and multi-stage household activities, with variations in planning depth, geometric constraints, and physical execution demands. We curate EP-100, a representative selection of 100 tasks across five suites and 16 task types, together with the EP-Easy and EP-Hard difficulty splits and the compact EP-20 selection. A shared protocol specifies observation and tool access, resource budgets, repeated trials, and independent checks of goal completion, task-rule compliance, and physical validity. Embodied Playground provides a unified framework for studying whether foundation-model agents can sustain effective reasoning and action over extended task horizons.this https URL
  </details>

- **[No Eyes, No Problem: What Does Touch Contribute to Blind Baoding Ball Manipulation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23438)**  `arXiv:2610.23438`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ayush Deshmukh, Nalin Agarwal, Elle Miller, Sethu Vijayakumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans manipulate objects within their hands through touch alone with remarkable ease, but for robots, achieving sustained real-world manipulation without vision remains an open challenge. Although reinforcement learning (RL) can generate sophisticated blind manipulation policies in simulation, transferring them to a real dexterous hand remains hindered by continuously changing contacts, motion-induced tactile information drift, and the gap between simulated and physical dynamics. We study this problem through Baoding ball rotation on the Shadow Dexterous Hand Lite, using only proprioception and binary tactile feedback as observations. We achieve successful zero-shot transfer through a combination of a sensor-aligned, coupling-aware embodiment in simulation, tactile calibration, and two novel episodic domain randomisations: slew-rate action limits and STuck-AT Taxels. On the physical hand, the policy achieves sustained blind Baoding rotation, completing $\textbf{225 consecutive $\mathbf{180^\circ}$ rotations (112.5 full rotations) at $\sim0.36 \;\text{rot/s}$}$ in a single continuous trial without any vision or object-state information. Furthermore, we evaluate the contribution of tactile sensing relative to proprioception alone and identify the hand regions where tactile information is most important. This is the strongest reported hardware result for Baoding ball manipulation in both rotation count and speed, irrespective of the sensing modality used. By effectively bridging the sim-to-real gap, our framework sets a new benchmark for sustained tactile-driven dexterous manipulation on physical hardware.
  </details>

- **[H2RBench: A Real-to-Sim Benchmark for Evaluating Human-to-Robot Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24778)**  `arXiv:2609.24778`  `cs.RO`  
  _Chuyang Xiao, Haotian Zhan, Sriram Krishna, Peilin Meng, Muhammad Zubair Irshad, Sergey Zakharov, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning robot manipulation policies from human video demonstrations constitutes a promising avenue for scalable robot learning. However, comparing different human-to-robot (H2R) transfer methods remains challenging, as existing approaches are evaluated under different settings, including differing task suites, scene layouts, object instances, and amounts of robot supervision. To address this challenge, we present H2RBench, a Real2Sim benchmark for evaluating H2R transfer methods. H2RBench provides a standardized protocol built on real human video demonstrations and simulated robot demonstrations, and includes four manipulation tasks spanning diverse interaction requirements. We evaluate multiple representative H2R transfer methods, each adopting a different strategy for bridging the embodiment gap. Using H2RBench, we systematically characterize how each method scales with the amount of human demonstrations, revealing that methods differ substantially in their ability to leverage additional human data. We further show that simulation performance is broadly predictive of real-world robot performance, with an overall Pearson correlation of r = 0.89, Spearman correlation of \r{ho} = 0.85 and Mean Maximum Rank Violation (MMRV) of 0.06 across method-task configurations. These results establish H2RBench as a practical and scalable benchmark for comparative H2R evaluation prior to real-world deployment.
  </details>

- **[GeoHAT: Geometry-Adaptive Hybrid Action Transformer for Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13394)**  `arXiv:2606.13394`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xiangyu Zhu, Renjun Wu, Luzhou Ge, Jinyan Liu, Xuesong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whole-body mobile manipulation requires coordinating mobile base and manipulator under shifting viewpoints, posing challenges in geometric perception and action generation. Current policies either rely on 2D features or sparse 3D representations that lack dense spatial structure, and typically encode arm and base within one action vector that ignores their distinct control demands. Moreover, existing dense fusion strategies risk corrupting pretrained representations under noisy depth while incurring heavy computational overhead. We present GeoHAT, an end-to-end diffusion-based framework built on a simple principle: geometry should be injected only where reliable and attended to only where needed. GeoHAT employs a lightweight Fourier spatial encoder that maps dense per-pixel 3D coordinates into geometric tokens without an additional 3D vision backbone. These tokens are then selectively injected into vision foundation model features through per-token gated fusion modulated by depth validity, preserving the semantic prior while enriching spatial understanding. For action generation, a Hybrid Whole-Body Action Decoder decomposes arm and base into distinct subspaces and lets each action modality attend to its task-relevant visual context through sparse cross-attention, while causal temporal modeling captures intra-timestep coordination and inter-timestep dependencies. Experiments on the ManiSkill-HAB simulation benchmark demonstrate that GeoHAT achieves a 79.2% mean success rate, surpassing the strongest baseline by 23.6 pp. Furthermore, real-world experiments on diverse tasks also confirm consistent improvements over all baselines.
  </details>

- **[Not All Features Are Created Equal: A Mechanistic Study of Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19233)**  `arXiv:2603.19233`  `cs.RO`  
  _Bryce Grant, Xijia Zhao, Peng Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A fine-tuned Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policy can easily place the alphabet soup in the basket on command, just as it saw during fine-tuning. However, if the basket is shifted five centimeters to the left, it drops the soup off the table. We developed an interpretability pipeline to investigate what such a policy is computing. Injecting task A's activations into the action expert while the policy looks at task B's scene makes pi0.5 execute task A's motor trajectory in 99.6\% of episodes ($n{=}1{,}968$).
