The injected program is bound to absolute workspace coordinates and ignores the visible scene, which provides a mechanistic account of the perturbation brittleness that concurrent benchmarks report. The binding holds on a physical UR10e: directed replay redirects the commanded task in 26/26 rollouts in one direction and 18/26 in the other, with no-injection and random-direction controls at the floor, and once the source object is moved 10--15\,cm the arm grasps and releases at the recorded coordinates in 50/50 trials, picking up the wrong block when one sits there and closing on empty air when none does. Applying the same interventions to six VLAs on LIBERO, MetaWorld, SimplerEnv, and ALOHA over 420{,}000+ rollouts yields three further findings: every architecture encodes the instruction, and four of the five weight it below vision whenever the scene alone identifies the goal; under the standard replacement write-back the SAE pooling that preserves behavior is architecture-specific (pi0.5 per-token, SmolVLA indifferent), and goal selection and motor generation separate into VLM and expert pathways wherever the two are distinguishable. We release \textbf{Action Atlas} (this https URL), an interactive platform covering all 520+ trained SAEs, 79 identified concepts, and the scored UR10e rollouts. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[UMAS: System-Level Uncertainty Quantification for Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33013)**  `arXiv:2610.33013`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Hanwen Li, Jinhao Duan, Xiaoshuang Shi, Yue Zhang, Tianlong Chen, Kaidi Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) are a promising paradigm for improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models, yet they remain prone to hallucinations and erroneous outputs. Uncertainty Quantification (UQ) is crucial for assessing system reliability, but existing methods are largely designed for single-agent settings; how to exploit MAS structure for UQ while keeping inference cost-neutral remains underexplored. We propose UMAS, a parameter-free, cost-neutral, system-level UQ framework that estimates the credibility of inter-agent influence from each generation's intrinsic confidence. UMAS uses these credibility signals to modulate how uncertainty is updated at each node, and then aggregates node-level uncertainty into a system-level estimate. Extensive evaluations on Debate and DyLAN across diverse benchmarks show that UMAS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by an average of 10.54 AUROC points. Beyond uncertainty estimation, UMAS enables hallucination detection and uncertainty-aware answer selection, improving MAS accuracy by up to 12.6 points on specific tasks while enhancing reliability. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EvolveMAS: A Unified Self-Evolution Framework for Decentralized Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34450)**  `arXiv:2610.34450`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Yalun Wu, Qiwei Ma, Bingbing Xu, Dan Zhang, Long Bai, Boyang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decentralized multi-agent systems (MAS) enable large language model agents to interact without centralized control. Agents need to adapt to other agents at test time and reuse accumulated experience to refine their interaction strategies for future interactions across cooperative, mixed-motive, and adversarial settings. However, existing approaches often develop adaptation mechanisms for particular interaction settings. This raises a central question: how can decentralized MAS support test-time adaptation and reliable experience reuse across interaction settings? We propose EvolveMAS, a unified self-evolution framework that supports experience-driven adaptation within and across episodes. EvolveMAS pairs each main agent with a dedicated MemoryAgent for test-time memory governance, which maintains isolated Structured Memory outside the main agent's reasoning context. EvolveMAS organizes adaptation into two complementary processes: Within-Episode Online Adaptation and Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution. During Within-Episode Online Adaptation, MemoryAgent updates episodic, semantic, and working memory from interaction evidence to inform subsequent actions. During Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution, the main agent uses memory and episode outcomes to distill reusable interaction knowledge comprising partner profiles and interaction strategies for subsequent episodes. Across five benchmarks and six evaluation configurations spanning the three interaction settings, EvolveMAS achieves the highest normalized average score of 77.2 among the compared methods. Ablations show that test-time memory governance and cross-episode knowledge reuse both improve performance. Interaction traces further illustrate how updated memory and distilled knowledge guide subsequent interactions. These results support experience-driven adaptation in decentralized MAS without updating model parameters.
  </details>

- **[DynaFlow: Learning Free-Form Multi-Agent Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34467)**  `arXiv:2610.34467`  `cs.MA`  
  _Ruhao Liu, Qi Li, Shizun Wang, Xinchao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems often rely on predefined collaboration structures, limiting their ability to adapt to diverse task requirements. We introduce DynaFlow, a framework that integrates free-form executable workflow synthesis into the action space of the main agent. The agent decides when collaboration is needed and generates task-specific programs that coordinate sub-agents through sequential or parallel execution, structured information exchange, and verification. Execution results and traces inform subsequent actions, enabling the agent to adapt collaboration as reasoning progresses. We train only the main agent through supervised fine-tuning on distilled trajectories, followed by refinement on examples filtered for unsupported premises, biased verification, and information-flow defects. All sub-agents remain fixed, separating orchestration learning from executor capability improvement. Across six benchmarks spanning mathematical reasoning, scientific question answering, and multi-hop retrieval, DynaFlow achieves 88.08% overall accuracy in Forced mode, outperforming the strongest evaluated baseline by 10.90 percentage points with the same Qwen3.5-27B backbone. Workflow analysis finds more explicit use, verification, and refinement of upstream outputs after training, highlighting cross-stage information flow as a key dimension of multi-agent orchestration.
  </details>

- **[VibeGame: Prompt-to-Game Development with AI-Native Engine and Self-Evolving Adversarial Agent Team](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33106)**  `arXiv:2610.33106`  `cs.MA`  
  _Wenbo Hu, Ken Li, Jiazhe Wei, Yukang Cao, Weiyi Hong, Jiayi Dai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents have enabled software development through natural-language interaction, but extending this paradigm to end-to-end game development remains challenging. Building a playable game requires coordinating code, scenes, assets, animations, physics, and configurations, while allowing developers to validate their integrated behavior through interactive testing. We introduce VibeGame, a framework for creating complete 2D game projects from natural-language prompts and optional reference images. Its AI-native game engine represents project state as schema-validated structured text and provides frame-synchronous runtime control, enabling agents to inspect game states, execute actions, and verify behavior at their own pace. Built upon this interface, we design an Adversarial Agent Team of eight specialized agents to separate implementation from review, so that problems are found and fixed through multi-round loops. For each conducted and accepted project, we further introduce a training-free self-evolution mechanism to distill it into reusable skeletons, modules, and collaboration contracts for future development. We evaluate prompt-assets-to-game (PA2G), where agents are given an asset library, and prompt-to-game (P2G), where agents generate their own art. On a GameCraft-Bench subset, VibeGame in P2G consistently outperforms a range of agent harnesses in either P2G or PA2G with the same underlying model, including GPT-6.1-Sol.
  </details>

- **[Not Every Agent Needs the Strongest Model: Unified Prompt-Model Optimization for Heterogeneous LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31743)**  `arXiv:2610.31743`  `cs.MA`  
  _Mingxiu Cai, Haoyang Xian, Xinyu Ma, Yutong Wang, Zhenxi Song, Jing Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) rely on role-specific prompts and execution models to solve complex tasks collaboratively. However, prompt optimization typically keeps model assignments fixed, leaving differences in role requirements and their associated inference costs outside the search. We present UniMASO, a Unified framework for heterogeneous Multi-Agent System Optimization through the joint adaptation of prompts and model assignments. UniMASO evaluates candidate updates through local, downstream, and global feedback, using cost-aware Pareto search to retain useful quality-cost trade-offs. Topology-aware co-evolution then coordinates these updates according to agent roles and dependencies. Cross-level trajectory memory further supplies historical successes and failures to guide subsequent optimization. Experiments on five benchmarks and five execution topologies show that UniMASO achieves competitive task performance at substantially lower deployment cost than the strongest homogeneous configurations. Under centralized execution, it improves the average score from 69.79 to 70.40 while reducing token-weighted deployment cost by 85.2% relative to DeepSeek-V4-Pro with MASPO. These results support joint prompt-model adaptation as an effective approach to efficient multi-agent collaboration. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Calibration Is Not Verification: Answer-Level Conformal Guarantees for Mixture-of-Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25959)**  `arXiv:2609.25959`  `cs.MA`  
  _Nada Rahali, Zijia Wang, Zhi-Song Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web answer synthesis with Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) requires reliable factual support for every atomic claim, yet agreement among agents is not verification. Correlated proposers can repeat a shared misconception, and a correct specialist fact receives little support when the other proposers stay silent. We introduce C-MoA, which decomposes MoA outputs into atomic claims, scores inter-agent semantic support, and calibrates claim retention at the level of whole answers. Under exchangeability and a policy frozen before calibration, it provides distribution-free marginal control of the probability that an answer retains any claim unsupported by the reference labels. Because calibration only selects a cutoff on a fixed ranking and cannot repair shared errors, we also study CONTRA-MoA, an optional extension that adds blinded near-miss tournaments, leave-one-agent-out stability, and availability-aware fusion, and we derive a competence--discrimination law for its verifier. Across 300 splits of the FactScore biography benchmark, the mean rate of fully supported answers (Fact) exceeds all six nominal targets, but only $1.7\%$--$9.8\%$ of supported claims are retained and $30\%$--$75\%$ of answers are empty. At $\alpha=0.10$, mean agreement retains $3.1\%$ of supported claims, about $3.4$ times the $0.9\%$ retained by the best single-proposer score. On the medical Golden Dataset, it retains $37.3\%$ at a mean Fact of $0.898$, slightly below the $0.90$ target, and on NQ mean Fact reaches $0.904$. On a separate balanced FactScore cohort, memory-only counterfactual scoring is close to chance (AUC $0.522$ on 182 claims), and raw maximum fusion lowers the agreement AUC from $0.687$ to $0.652$. These results separate the statistical calibration of factuality from informative claim verification and clarify the role of each in multi-agent Web synthesis.
  </details>

- **[Adapting Neural Posterior Estimators for Simulation-Based Inference in Agent-Based Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.07413)**  `arXiv:2601.07413`  `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Junyao Zhang, Jinglai Li, Junqi Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent-based models (ABMs) are widely used in economics and social sciences. They represent heterogeneous personal behaviors and their interactions by using simulations, whereas their likelihoods are often intractable for parameter estimations. Therefore, most traditional statistical inference techniques are hindered. Driven by recent advances in probabilistic machine learning, recent works have applied neural networks to achieve likelihood-free inference in large-scale ABMs. Specifically, Neural Posterior Estimation using normalizing flows provide amortized Bayesian inference from simulations, yet a pre-trained estimator may become inaccurate when the target task changes. In this work, we investigate the practicality of adapting a pre-trained conditional normalizing flow through a unified sequential neural posterior estimation test-time training (SNPE-TTT) framework. The framework includes four adaptation strategies: full-parameter adaptation, low-rank adaptation, and two update spaces identified from target-task gradients. We evaluate all four methods across economic simulation models with both tractable and intractable likelihoods. These benchmarks demonstrate that the sequential neural posterior estimation test-time training framework can adapt pre-trained posterior estimators to related inference tasks using a limited budget of target simulations. Posterior recovery and computational benefits depend on the task and adaptation strategy, with lower target-stage cost in some settings but no consistently superior posterior accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Vision Token Pruning via Query-Vision Interaction Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33215)**  `arXiv:2610.33215`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Harshithanjani Athi, Sravan Kumar Ankireddy, Jianzhong Charlie Zhang, Hyeji Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large vision-language models (VLMs), which process both visual inputs and text queries, incur high inference costs due to the large number of visual tokens, making visual token pruning a natural approach for improving efficiency. Recent work has leveraged query information to guide token selection, often through heuristics derived from attention patterns or token dynamics. However, these approaches do not explicitly exploit the underlying structure of query-vision interactions. We address this gap by observing that the query-vision interaction matrix, naturally induced by the query and key projections already present in transformer attention, is effectively low-rank, with a small number of dominant latent interaction modes capturing query-relevant semantics. Motivated by this observation, we propose Query-Vision Decomposition (QViD), a novel training-free query-aware visual token pruning method that exploits this structure. Extensive experiments on both image and video understanding benchmarks show that QViD consistently improves performance under matched token budgets, with the largest gains appearing under aggressive compression regimes.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement Learning Done Right for Explicit Confidence Targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35059)**  `arXiv:2610.35059`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Zefeng He, Yichen He, Yuan Lin, Yang Yang, Yu Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can produce plausible but incorrect answers when they are uncertain, which can mislead users and undermine model reliability. Training models to abstain with reinforcement learning is a natural approach, but most existing methods use a fixed error penalty and therefore cannot adapt their behavior to different confidence requirements at inference time. We study \emph{explicit confidence targets}, where a user specifies a target \(p\) in the prompt and the model directly chooses whether to answer or abstain, with the goal of achieving an accuracy of at least \(p\) among answered questions. However, learning to respond to different confidence targets with a single model has proven difficult, and prior attempts have failed to distinguish different targets. We show that this failure comes from two problems: the reward signal that distinguishes different values of $p$ is often lost during optimization, while the original error penalty grows without bound as $p$ approaches one. We propose RLEC (\textbf{R}einforcement \textbf{L}earning with \textbf{E}xplicit \textbf{C}onfidence Targets), which preserves the learning signal needed to distinguish confidence targets, introduces a stable reward family that keeps the desired answer condition unchanged, and uses reward scheduling to balance effective learning and training stability. Together, these designs enable the first successful training of a single LLM for explicit confidence target control. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks and model scales show that RLEC largely preserves general capabilities and achieves competitive calibration performance. RLEC also supports fine-grained target control without an external decision system or threshold calibration after training. By making explicit confidence targets part of the trained policy, RLEC opens a new and more direct path toward behavioral calibration.
  </details>

- **[Improving Reasoning with Rollout Agreement for Group-Based Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34951)**  `arXiv:2610.34951`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Sohyun Son, Yerim Hong, Jaeyun Park, Mujeen Sung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent methods for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) use rollout diversity to improve reasoning performance or training efficiency. Despite these gains, the extent to which differences among correct-answer rollouts reflect useful alternative reasoning or unresolved errors remains poorly understood. We investigate this question through a blind paired analysis of a model trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), comparing the highest- and lowest-agreement correct-answer traces for the same problem. Across 126 pairs, low-agreement traces have more spurious reasoning than high-agreement traces (35% vs. 22%), including internal inconsistencies and misuse of problem conditions. Based on these findings, we propose RoAR (Rollout-Agreement Reward), which adds reasoning-level agreement to GRPO's rewards for verified-correct rollouts. RoAR computes this signal from existing on-policy rollout groups, requiring no human-written rationales, additional sampling, or separately trained process evaluator. On five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, the 1.5B GRPO-RoAR model reaches 55.5% mean pass@1 under zero-shot evaluation using 7K training examples, exceeding GRPO and GRPO-CARE by 6.0 and 5.5 percentage points. A separate blind audit of 100 problems answered correctly by all four 1.5B models finds spurious rates of 18% for GRPO-RoAR, 32% for GRPO, 27% for GRPO-CARE, and 37% for DRA-GRPO. Our findings identify reasoning validity as an important consideration when using rollout differences to guide learning. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Can Language Models Learn While They Think? Testing Gradient Adaptation Across Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33025)**  `arXiv:2610.33025`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Surya Saka_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Letting a language model take gradient steps on the text it is reading, known as dynamic evaluation, lowers its loss on the rest of the document. Its behaviour on hybrid linear-attention architectures, on diffusion language models, and on standard long-context benchmarks has not been measured. We measure it. With one SGD step per 128-token chunk on the MLP output projections of the last quarter of blocks, no training, and a 2k-token window, softmax-attention models (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-4B, Gemma-4-E4B) lose 5-8% of their next-token loss over a document and outperform the same frozen model given the full 32k-token context. Hybrid Gated-DeltaNet models (Qwen3.5-4B, Qwen3.8-27B) gain under 1% at every learning rate: the linear-attention blocks already perform the adaptation. The mechanism transfers to masked-diffusion models with a larger effect, 8% on Nemotron-Diffusion-8B and 17-20% on DiffusionGemma-26B, once the rate is lowered and updates are clipped. Across RULER, BABILong, LongBench-v2, SCOTUS, HotpotQA, MuSiQue, ContractNLI and citation completion, at 100 or more items on two model sizes, weights updated on the full document recover none of the accuracy lost to context truncation: the adaptation is style and vocabulary, not retrieval. Writing facts with the next-token loss stores the prompt format rather than the fact (30% recall under chat prompting against 80% in context) and costs 36-38 GSM8K points at every dose. Writing a conclusion as behaviour instead, by distilling the model's own conclusion-conditioned judgments into a low-rank adapter, reaches the in-context ceiling (0.83 and 0.88 against 0.84 and 0.88 on 100 games) with GSM8K unchanged, and the conclusion can come from an inquiry loop that found it by asking information-gain questions.
  </details>

- **[SATFormer: Learning When to Use Early Value Residuals in Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03953)**  `arXiv:2605.03953`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Skye Gunasekaran, Téa Wright, Rui-Jie Zhu, Jason Eshraghian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent Transformer architectures expose later layers to representations computed in earlier layers, motivated by the observation that low-level features can become harder to recover as the residual stream is transformed through depth. One such method adds static value residuals: learned mixing coefficients that expose the first-layer value projection V_1 uniformly across tokens and heads. However, we theorize that the usefulness of $V_1$ is unlikely to be constant across tokens, heads, and contexts, and therefore treat early-representation reuse as a retrieval problem. Here, we introduce Selective Access Transformer (SATFormer), which preserves the first-layer value pathway while controlling access with a context-dependent gate. Across models from 130M to 1.3B parameters, SATFormer improves validation loss over the static value-residual and Transformer baselines at every evaluated scale, and improves average zero-shot accuracy over both at all four scales. Its strongest gains appear on retrieval-intensive benchmarks, where it improves over static value residuals by approximately 1.5 average points, while maintaining near baseline throughput and memory. Mechanistic analyses suggest sparse, depth-dependent, head-specific, and category-sensitive access patterns, supporting the interpretation that SATFormer learns selective reuse of early representations rather than uniform residual copying. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CounterFlow: Target-Conditioned Flow Matching for Visual Counterfactuals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35679)**  `arXiv:2610.35679`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Paul Sevellec, Elisa Fromont, Romaric Gaudel, Laurence Roze, Paul Viallard, Laetitia Houeix_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In eXplainable AI, counterfactual explanations have gained significant traction by offering users direct, intuitive, and actionable insights into model decisions. We introduce CounterFlow, a novel framework based on Conditional Flow Matching that generates visual counterfactual explanations by directly steering a source image toward a user-specified target class. By leveraging target-conditioned vector fields, CounterFlow identifies highly realistic semantic modifications while requiring substantially lower computational overhead than traditional GAN- or diffusion-based architectures. Empirical evaluations on standard benchmarks demonstrate that CounterFlow achieves state-of-the-art performance in both visual realism and target class switching. By working directly in the raw pixel space, CounterFlow offers a more practical solution for real-world deployment.
  </details>

- **[AndroidFlux: Evaluating and Reward Modeling Failure Recovery Capabilities in Mobile Use Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34384)**  `arXiv:2610.34384`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Gyubeum Lim, Yemo Koo, Ghunwoo Jang, Zsolt Kira_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mobile-use multimodal agents are typically evaluated by task success from a clean initial state, which does not isolate their ability to recover from intermediate errors. Because mobile environments change dynamically, a general mobile-use agent must complete tasks robustly even from unexpected states and in unseen environments. This requires knowing when and where an agent makes errors, and whether it can recover from errors and subsequent states. To evaluate this capability, we introduce AndroidFlux, a failure-recovery benchmark built on AndroidWorld. AndroidFlux restores recorded trajectories to controlled checkpoints before, at, and after the first recoverable error, so that different agents continue from the same device state and history. On these checkpoints, a complementary reward-modeling benchmark evaluates recovery-action preferences using downstream rollout outcomes. We further release an offline reinforcement learning (RL) dataset that improves both error recovery and overall task success. Our evaluations show that recovery performance only partially correlates with clean-start success, and that explicit reasoning helps only models trained with GUI-domain specific RL.
  </details>

- **[Continual Learning Exam: Benchmarking Long-Horizon RSI and Experiential Learning in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33956)**  `arXiv:2610.33956`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Haoyu Zhao, Sitian Tu, Yuxuan Huang, Huichi Zhou, Zhangyue Yin, Jiahao Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual learning is the ability of LLM agents to improve over time by acquiring, refining, and applying knowledge from experience while retaining useful prior knowledge. It is essential for agents learning over long time horizons, yet current evaluations largely assess individual task performance rather than improvement through accumulated experience. We introduce Continual Learning Exam (CLE), a large-scale benchmark developed by 50+ domain experts to measure experience-driven improvement in realistic professional workflows. CLE comprises 120 human-authored tasks, each organised as a five-level ladder, yielding 600 levels spanning 89 professional roles across 12 occupational domains. Agents must pass each level to unlock the next, with later levels building on experience accumulated during earlier assignments. An evaluation agent assesses each submission against human-authored rubrics. We compare a single-submission setting with one that provides diagnostic feedback after failure and allows one revision. Across 17 harness-model configurations using frontier LLMs, feedback and revision yield a median completion gain of 5.3 percentage points. Yet the best-performing system clears only 47.7% of levels and completes all five levels in just 23.3% of tasks, highlighting the difficulty of sustained learning from experience for existing frontier LLMs. As such, the dataset can also be used to post-train LLMs to strengthen their continual learning capabilities.
  </details>

- **[MiRL: Error-Targeted Reinforcement Learning for Correcting Terminal Agent Misbehaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35697)**  `arXiv:2610.35697`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Runchu Tian, Zaid Khan, Zun Wang, Justin Chih-Yao Chen, Mohit Bansal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Error-targeted learning gives a legible way to improve a model: identify specific weaknesses of the model, train the model to fix these weaknesses, and check whether the weaknesses are fixed. We study how to apply error-targeted learning to terminal agents, which solve long-horizon tasks specified in natural language by interacting with a command shell. Existing error-targeted methods are designed for single-turn tasks, and existing analyses of agent failures are used to evaluate and debug agents rather than to train them. We propose Misbehavior-driven RL (MiRL), which first profiles the errors of a policy to find generalizable misbehaviors: kinds of wrong steps that recur across many environments. MiRL then trains the policy with RL, starting rollouts at the steps where these misbehaviors occur and rewarding a rollout only if it passes the tests and avoids the misbehavior. On Qwen3.5-4B, eight misbehaviors account for 52.1% of wrong steps. After training with MiRL, the policy repeats the measured misbehaviors 43% to 57% less often on average on unseen prefixes. MiRL outperforms TMax-4B on Terminal-Bench Lite (51.8% vs. 47.4%) and matches it on Terminal-Bench 2.1 (21.3% vs. 20.6%), while using 35% of its rollouts. These results show that error-targeted learning can be applied to realistic, long-horizon terminal tasks.
  </details>

- **[Correlation-Aware Exploration for Long-Trace Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34949)**  `arXiv:2610.34949`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Junxing Hu, Zicheng Zhang, Zhiqian Zhang, Ai Han, Yifeng Zhang, Pengzhang Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning models trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) now produce chains of thought thousands of tokens long, and recent studies reveal that such training is prone to policy collapse. Entropy regularization is the standard remedy, but it works by pushing probability mass away from the few tokens that carry it and toward the vast low-probability tail of the vocabulary, and it applies this force once for every token of the reasoning trace. This puts the bonus in direct conflict with the entropy decay that convergence requires, and makes its coefficient difficult to set in the long-trace regime. To resolve this tension, we propose Info-GRPO, an information-theoretic framework that cultivates correlation between the policy and a latent prior. Rather than raising entropy everywhere, Info-GRPO builds on the mutual information between a latent variable and the trajectory it induces: augmenting prompts with latent seeds during training lets the model explore diverse policies correlated with the prior, while the seed-conditioned entropy is guided toward convergence. Diversity is therefore carried across whole reasoning traces rather than by per-token randomness, and exploration no longer fights entropy reduction. Experiments show that Info-GRPO outperforms vanilla GRPO and entropy-regularized GRPO across diverse reasoning benchmarks. On AIME24, it improves $Avg@8$ over GRPO by 3.75%, 1.66%, 4.16%, and 4.17% on four backbones, and it reaches the same majority-vote accuracy with $8\times$ fewer samples. Code and models will be publicly released.
  </details>

- **[Evolving to Match Capabilities: Improving Harness Self-Evolution via Skill Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33024)**  `arXiv:2610.33024`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhihao Yang, Haojin Wang, Juncheng Wu, Kuan Zhang, Yukun Chen, Run Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness self-evolution offers a practical route for LLM agents to improve from execution experience without updating model parameters. Its effectiveness depends on whether models can successfully act on the guidance provided by their harnesses. However, we find that harness benefits are not uniform across model capabilities: skills that improve stronger models can provide little benefit or even degrade performance for weaker ones. Across seven models and three benchmarks, we identify 103 task-skill pairs where skills improve GPT-5.6-luna by 31.1 points but degrade Gemma-4-E4B by 8.8 points. In 86% of failed weak-model runs, the violated requirement is already stated in the skill. This motivates skill adaptation: reformulating existing skills to match the executor's capabilities and make their guidance easier to execute. To achieve this, we diagnose failures in tool execution, context management, instruction following, and verification, and distill these diagnoses into a fixed meta-skill that guides adaptation during harness self-evolution. Guided by the meta-skill, a capable external rewriter, Kimi-K3, adapts skills without execution evidence, improving the three weak executors by 5.1-14.1 points over the original skills. We then incorporate this adaptation into a five-round self-evolution loop, where each model revises its own skill from execution trajectories and verifier feedback. Across seven executors, from Gemma-4-E4B to Kimi-K2.5, the meta-skill consistently improves final scores by 2.0-5.1 points over the same loop without the meta-skill. Our findings provide practical design guidance for developing more effective harness self-evolution methods.
  </details>

- **[Harness Scaling for Generalizable Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29458)**  `arXiv:2610.29458`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Fei Bai, Huatong Song, Chuan Hao, Ran Tao, Jian Yang, Bryan Dai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses have become a central component of modern agentic systems, shaping how models use tools, manage context, and coordinate long-horizon interaction. Realizing the benefits of these harnesses depends on how effectively the underlying model can make use of them, motivating reinforcement learning directly through the harness. While recent efforts have explored harness diversification to reduce specialization to particular execution settings, how to systematically construct and organize harness diversity for cross-harness generalization remains insufficiently understood. This work studies Harness Scaling as a distinct scaling dimension for agentic RL, focusing on structured variation in how policies execute and interact with the same underlying task and environment. Concretely, we construct a factorized harness space, select a compact training subset with balanced factorial coverage, and organize cross-harness training through locality-aware task assignment. Across multiple benchmarks, model scales, and RL algorithms, Harness Scaling consistently improves generalization to unseen harness combinations. Moreover, policies trained through structured white-box harness variation transfer effectively to unseen external black-box harnesses, outperforming policies trained through a single complex black-box harness. These results establish harness diversity as a meaningful scaling dimension for agentic RL and highlight the importance of how this diversity is organized for cross-harness generalization.
  </details>

- **[What Does a Harness Repair? A Preregistered Study of Visibility, Baseline Adequacy and Evaluation Defects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05533)**  `arXiv:2610.05533`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Bowen Xu, Boyu Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness search keeps a change to the prompts, token budgets or parsers around a frozen model if the change raises a score. Such a gain can come from answers the parser could not read before, a weak comparison or an evaluation defect. We preregistered two studies of where these gains come from, with three models of 1.7B to 8B parameters and with Qwen3-14B, and report all 65 primary endpoints. Turning thinking off raised accuracy over a capped thinking setting in 7 of 12 model-benchmark cells, each time mostly through questions the capped setting left without a readable answer. For the small models, the thinking-off setting was not meaningfully worse than a rescue configuration in 7 of 9 cells, nor, for the 1.7B model, than four GEPA-selected harnesses. At 14B, two GEPA harnesses beat it by 30 to 34 points on the LongevityBench letter tasks; in descriptive analyses they were not detectably above the strongest constant-label baseline and answered "Yes" in 95% to 99% of their parsed mortality answers. From a start capped at 64 tokens, GEPA repaired the 1.7B model's harness, but at 14B no proposal of either search scored above zero. Replication through the same pipeline reproduced a defect's distortion in 9 of 20 evaluable cases, 7 of them cases in which the distortion is fixed by an identity of the design. Descriptively, relative to its base model, longevity fine-tuning raised a 1.7B model's letter accuracy by 41 points and cut its MMLU-Pro and GSM8K accuracy by 16 and 63 points; on a second GPU, 58% of its zero-temperature token sequences changed and none of the nine grid verdicts we rechecked did.
  </details>

- **[Mathematical Reasoning via Intervention-Based Time-Series Causal Discovery Using LLMs as Concept Mastery Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07600)**  `arXiv:2605.07600`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Tsuyoshi Okita_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent methods for improving LLM mathematical reasoning, whether through MCTS-based test-time search or causal graph-guided knowledge injection, cannot identify which concepts causally contribute to a correct answer, as the observed association may be spurious, driven by confounders such as problem difficulty.We propose CIKA (Causal Intervention for Knowledge Activation), a framework that uses the LLM itself as an interventional simulator: a prompt sets the concept state to ``mastered'' and the correctness change estimates the causal effect. We formalize this quantity as an Interventional Capability Probe (ICP), which diagnoses whether the LLM can use a given concept -- distinct from merely possessing knowledge. Because the intervention exogenously sets the concept state independently of problem difficulty, ICP separates confounding that observational methods cannot.On 67 screened problems, the ICP of the top-ranked concept (+0.219) is significantly larger than that of the negative control (+0.039; paired $t$-test, $p < 10^{-6}$, Cohen's $d = 0.86$), confirming that the probe discriminates causally relevant concepts from irrelevant ones. Analysis of 601 Omni-MATH problems further shows that solved problems have 6.1$\times$ higher ATE than unsolved ones (0.338 vs. 0.055), confirming that ICP is predictive of problem-solving success. With a 7B-parameter LLM whose weights are entirely frozen, CIKA achieves 69.7\% on the contamination-free Omni-MATH-Rule benchmark and 64.0\% overall, compared to 60.5\% for o1-mini, and 97.2\% on GSM8K, 46--50\% on AIME 2024--2026, and 46.2\% on MathArena. The Causal Knowledge Activation component contributes 33.8\% of correct answers on problems where the base model alone fails, demonstrating that the LLM already possessed but had not activated the requisite knowledge.
  </details>

- **[GraphDancer: Training LLMs to Explore and Reason over Graphs via Curriculum Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02518)**  `arXiv:2602.02518`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yuyang Bai, Zhuofeng Li, Ping Nie, Jianwen Xie, Yu Wang, Yu Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly rely on external knowledge to improve factuality, yet many knowledge sources on the Web, such as scholarly indexes and product catalogs, are organized as heterogeneous graphs. Reasoning over such graph-structured knowledge poses two key challenges: (1) navigating structured, schema-defined relations requires precise function calls beyond similarity-based retrieval, and (2) answering complex questions often demands multi-hop evidence aggregation through iterative information seeking. We study how LLMs can learn these interaction capabilities and transfer them to unseen domains. We propose GraphDancer, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that teaches LLMs to navigate graphs by interleaving reasoning and function execution. To improve RL for moderate-sized LLMs, we introduce a graph-aware curriculum that schedules training by the structural difficulty of questions using an easy-to-hard biased sampler. We evaluate GraphDancer on the multi-domain GRBench benchmark, training only on Academic and Biomedical graphs. Testing covers held-out questions from these domains as well as unseen E-commerce, Literature, and Legal graphs. With either Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct or Qwen3-14B as the backbone, GraphDancer outperforms graph-agent baselines using the same backbone across all evaluation domains. These results indicate that the learned graph exploration and reasoning capabilities transfer across domains. On average across unseen domains, GraphDancer with a 14B backbone also outperforms baselines powered by the closed-source models GPT-4.1-mini and GPT-5-nano. Trajectory analyses further show that GraphDancer retrieves answer-bearing evidence more often and achieves higher accuracy when such evidence is found, while substantially reducing invalid-protocol outcomes.
  </details>

- **[SkillLifeBench: Benchmarking Security Across the Lifecycle of Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35215)**  `arXiv:2610.35215`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Chenxu Zhang, Zhou Liu, Wu Xin, Wentao Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web agents reuse skills whose stored code, selected version, and loaded implementation can diverge across tasks and updates. SkillLifeBench links these states to task outcomes in 388 paired scenarios spanning implementation, interface, runtime, composition, and version changes. We evaluate 14 models on two paired decision tracks: 89 runtime tasks and 60 autonomous lifecycle tasks spanning 12 templates derived from introduced cases. Lifecycle tasks allow agents to inspect, select, and reconfigure skills while completing verifiable user requests. Joint success requires task completion without the registered attack goal. GPT-5.6-Sol and Sonnet 4.6 both achieve 82.0% on runtime tasks, but reach 96.7% and 56.7% on lifecycle tasks. Across lifecycle evaluations, challenged task success is 77.5%, while joint success is 65.2%; 103 runs complete the task and the attack goal. Adding execution provenance in a matched recovery study raises utility joint success from 49.35% with logs and metadata to 65.58%. A deterministic execution-consistency rule reaches 64.94% with higher benign utility, while full restoration reaches 69.48%. Lifecycle tasks distinguish models with similar runtime scores, and execution evidence improves recovery.
  </details>

- **[Dynamic Multi-Step Decoding for Time-Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34409)**  `arXiv:2610.34409`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Birmjune Kim, Hyungju Lee, Hyun Oh Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series forecasting models typically use a fixed decoding strategy at inference time: either one-shot prediction of the full horizon or autoregressive rollout over atomic chunks. While one-shot decoding reduces error accumulation, it can discard useful sequential structure; conversely, autoregressive decoding preserves temporal dependencies but may compound errors. We argue that the optimal decoding strategy is not fixed across inputs, but should be selected adaptively for each forecasting instance. We propose a dynamic strategy selector that treats the forecast rollout schedule as an inference-time decision problem for frozen pretrained time-series foundation models. The selector is a learned network that ranks candidate rollout schedules using hidden states of the backbone, and is trained on historical windows of the evaluated tasks for each backbone. To make the candidate set tractable over the exponentially large space of horizon partitions, we use dynamic programming that reduces candidate evaluation to quadratic complexity while preserving much of the oracle gain. At deployment, five independently trained selectors vote on each decision, and the policy follows their consensus, falling back to the backbone's one-shot forecast where they disagree. The construction requires only the backbone's hidden states and lightweight selectors, with no fine-tuning of the forecasting backbone. On fev-bench with a frozen Chronos-2 backbone, agreement-gated selection improves point-forecast skill (MASE) over one-shot decoding with statistical significance, mostly on longer horizons. It recovers about 6-8% of the gain that the same dynamic program attains with ground-truth selection. With a frozen Moirai-1.1-R-small backbone, the improvement is also significant on the full benchmark, and for both backbones we claim gains in point accuracy only.
  </details>

- **[ParetoRSI-Gym: Can AI Agents Improve Language Models Across Multiple Domains?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34386)**  `arXiv:2610.34386`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haojin Wang, Yangzhen Wu, Zeyi Chen, Jian Xie, Jiaying Ye, Lili Meng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires an agentic system to design, train, and evaluate its own successor. Data-driven post-training is a central part of that loop, yet existing evaluations measure it on a single benchmark, where an agent selects from a static pool or distills a stronger model to push one score up in one run. Real post-training is multi-domain, and a gain in one domain has to be weighed against a regression in another. We propose ParetoRSI-Gym, in which an agent searches for data, curates it, trains, reads back per-domain results, and repeats, with everything but the data held fixed: the initial LLM, the five domains (math, code, finance, medicine, science), the toolset, and the SFT pipeline. Generalization benchmarks in each domain separate capability gains from overfitting to the scores the agent optimizes against. Across three agent systems, three base models, five rounds and multiple seeds, the strongest system, Claude Code with Opus-5, raises the generalization scores of its proxy-best checkpoint by 9.2% relative to the starting model (+2.3 absolute points), averaged with equal weight over three base models and five domains, with a single-domain relative gain of up to 63% (medicine on Ministral-3-3B). Further analysis shows that the harness matters as much as the model it drives, and that each agent keeps a unique data selection strategy. We see our work as a step towards evaluation of RSI systems in real-world machine learning engineering.
  </details>

- **[SEEK: Skill Extraction with Evolving Knowledge against Frontier Science Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34365)**  `arXiv:2610.34365`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Cheng Gui, Xiang Zheng, Xingjun Ma, Kaidi Xu, Cong Wang, Weiying Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Science agents increasingly package domain expertise as reusable skills that plan analyses, select tools, and execute workflows. This modularity creates a confidentiality risk: the interactions that deliver a skill's capability can also reveal the skill itself. We build an extensible benchmark of 40 protected skills across eight open- and closed-source science-agent platforms, comparing direct, fixed-strategy, and bandit-guided iterative probing under reference-based and reference-free evaluation. Leakage is pervasive: all eight platforms (100%) leak at least one protected skill, and four reach 100% attack success under a single direct request. To stress-test platforms that resist simple probing, we propose SEEK (Skill Extraction with Evolving Knowledge), a black-box, self-evolving red-teaming agent that consolidates response-derived evidence in persistent memory and evolves reusable attack skills, using only user-visible responses. On 12 skills from SpatialAgent and Biomni, SEEK achieves 100% attack success while reducing the mean first-success round by 31.8% and 38.2% relative to bandit-guided probing. Against a confidentiality policy that blocks all three benchmark protocols, SEEK still extracts one of six protected skills. These findings show that current science agents do not reliably protect their skills and that defenses must be evaluated against adaptive, multi-turn attackers.
  </details>

- **[SHIFT-JEV: Certified Abstention and Gain-Gated Continual Adaptation for LLM-Based Decision Models under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34329)**  `arXiv:2610.34329`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Muhammad Atif Saeed, Azka Atiq, Adarsh Kumar, Akhtar Jamil, Uzair Iqbal, Roland Aydin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can act as decision models that return a probability distribution over a fixed set of options, but deployed decision models face domain, label-prior, emerging-class and candidate-set shift. We study how such models fail under shift and propose SHIFT-Jev, a monitoring, abstention and continual-adaptation procedure for them. It scores the risk of each decision from uncertainty, format and option-consistency signals, certifies abstention thresholds with Learn-then-Test, monitors the decision stream with drift and label-shift detectors, and accepts a model update only when an expected-gain gate finds a significant held-out improvement without loss on earlier regimes. On a benchmark of ten shifted test environments with Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen3-4B (three seeds each) and Phi-4-mini, fine-tuning with KL anchors and temperature scaling lowers calibration error under shift from 0.289 to 0.087. At a target risk of 10%, a learned uncertainty score with a certified threshold exceeds the target in 3 to 5 of 27 environment-seed tests, against 5 to 15 for a max-probability score with the same certification and 9 to 14 for conformal prediction sets; drift-based routing changes little. A chi-squared test on predicted labels detects 95 to 99% of class-prior shifts that confidence- and embedding-based detectors miss. In continual adaptation, SHIFT-Jev reaches 70.5% accuracy on Qwen3-0.6B, above replay, LwF, EWC and fine-tuning baselines in every seed, whereas on Qwen3-4B its conservative gate leaves it 0.4 to 1.4 points behind them.
  </details>

- **[CORMA: Constraint-Routed Decision Mamba for Generalizable Multi-Constraint Offline Safe Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34208)**  `arXiv:2610.34208`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Changxu Ni, Feiran You, Yongan Guo, Hongyang Du, Kang Wei, Le Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline safe reinforcement learning (RL) learns safety-aware policies from static datasets while satisfying safety constraints during deployment. Motivated by the strong trajectorymodeling capabilities of Transformers, recent Decision Transformer (DT)-based methods formulate offline safe RL as conditional sequence modeling by conditioning action generation on return-to-go (RTG) and cost-to-go (CTG) tokens, thereby enabling adaptation to varying safety budgets. However, existing DT-based methods are developed for single-constraint tasks. For tasks with multiple constraints, generalizing learned policies to unseen combinations of safety budgets remains challenging due to the conflicting requirements imposed by heterogeneous constraints. Moreover, the quadratic complexity of Transformer architectures significantly increases the computational overhead in multi-constraint tasks, as representing additional constraints requires more CTG tokens. Recently, Mamba has emerged as an efficient sequence-modeling backbone, offering linear computational complexity and strong generalization capabilities. In this paper, we propose CORMA, a constraint-routed decision Mamba framework for generalizable multi-constraint offline safe RL. CORMA decomposes policy inference into constraint-aware RTG estimation and adaptive action generation, where constraint-specific experts model feasible RTG distributions and a router-guided product-of-experts mechanism fuses these distributions to provide safety-aware guidance for a Mamba-based action decoder. A three-stage optimization strategy is designed to improve training stability. Extensive experiments on OSRL benchmarks demonstrate that CORMA achieves superior reward-safety trade-offs, reduces inference latency by 37% compared with DT-based methods, and exhibits stronger zero-shot generalization to unseen safety-budget combinations than offline safe RL baselines.
  </details>

- **[SRA: Selective Residual Adaptation for LLM Reasoning Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34199)**  `arXiv:2610.34199`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhenxing Dou, Kun He, Mingyang Yi, Yong Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge distillation transfers reasoning capabilities from large language models to lightweight students. However, distilled small models still struggle with multi-step reasoning. We observe that the distribution divergences between teacher and student models along reasoning paths are highly sparse, and target tokens almost always fall within the student top-$K$ candidate set. Based on this finding, unlike approaches updating student backbones or requiring runtime teacher computation, we propose Selective Residual Adaptation (SRA), a plug-and-play framework that internalizes localized corrections. Concretely, SRA employs a Discrepancy Gate to identify divergence positions alongside a Candidate Residual Adapter for top-$K$ logit calibration to correct the mismatch. Theoretically, we establish error bounds ensuring strict sequence level performance gains. Across mathematical benchmarks on Qwen3 models from 0.6B to 4B, SRA consistently enhances distilled student models, raising average Pass@16 by up to 4.37 percentage points while preserving general capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Single-Cycle Battery Remaining Useful Life Prediction with Compressed Continuous Wavelet Features and Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34159)**  `arXiv:2610.34159`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sungeun Kim, Mingyu Jung, Yuri Kim, Jin-Sung Park, Jongbin Ryu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lithium-ion batteries are widely used in industrial applications, but their performance gradually degrades over time, making accurate lifetime prediction important for safety and reliability. However, reliable lifetime assessment generally requires extensive degradation testing, which is both costly and time-consuming. To reduce this burden, recent data-driven approaches have sought to predict the end-of-life cycle of batteries from only the initial few cycles. Nevertheless, identifying reliable degradation patterns from such limited early-cycle data remains challenging because early aging dynamics are often subtle and nonlinear. To address this issue, we adopt the continuous wavelet transform to convert cycle-wise voltage, current, and capacity signals into time-frequency representations, enabling multi-resolution characterization of degradation behavior. Building on this representation, we apply scale compression to reduce redundancy while preserving structural information in the scalograms. We evaluate the proposed framework on eight benchmark datasets derived from seven public battery datasets covering diverse electrode chemistries, cell form factors, and cycling protocols. Experimental results show that the proposed framework consistently outperforms prior approaches in single-cycle remaining useful life prediction and remains robust as the number of input cycles increases from 1 to 100. In addition, redundancy-fidelity analysis characterizes the trade-off introduced by scale compression, and gradient-based visual explanations indicate that the model focuses on localized time-frequency regions associated with structured signal transitions during charge-discharge operation. These results demonstrate the effectiveness and interpretability of the proposed framework for early-cycle battery remaining useful life prediction.
  </details>

- **[Holding a Steady Course: Knowledge Evolution from Expertise and Lessons for Continual Agent Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34043)**  `arXiv:2610.34043`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Wenzhe Fan, Benjamin Steenhoek, Gaurav Mittal, Yu Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous agents are increasingly deployed across sequences of related tasks, yet they often fail to retain and improve their knowledge over time. Existing approaches convert interaction trajectories into memories or reusable skills, but experience is not automatically reliable knowledge. Automatically collected skills and memories can propagate errors, mislead, and corrupt useful knowledge. To address this gap, we introduce Knowledge Evolution from Expertise and Lessons (KEEL), an agent-agnostic framework that uses verifier evidence to transform successful behavior into reusable skills and failures into corrective lessons. As new evidence accumulates, individual skill claims can be supported, contradicted, replaced, or retired. On SkillFlow benchmark, KEEL improves task success by 4.2 to 21.7 percentage points over the no-knowledge baseline, compared with gains of 2.4 to 9.6 points from SkillFlow's default method. Ablations show that skills and lessons are complementary. These results show that reliable continual agent learning depends not simply on accumulating experience, but on continually validating and evolving the knowledge derived from it. Our code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[QECGBERT: A Scalable ECG Foundation Model Based on Self-Supervised Language Learning via VQ-VAE Waveform Tokenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33964)**  `arXiv:2610.33964`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Aruna Mohan, Burcu Ozek, Or Zilbershot, Fatemeh Afghah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI foundation models play a pivotal role in advancing heart disease diagnosis from electrocardiogram (ECG) data, particularly for rare diseases where the lack of sufficient labeled data hinders supervised learning. However, most self-supervised learning methods treat ECGs as generic sequence or image data without exploiting underlying physiological structure. To address this limitation, we propose QECGBERT, a self-supervised ECG language model that explicitly encodes physiological semantics by tokenizing the P, QRS, T, and isoelectric background waveforms. Vector-quantized variational autoencoder (VQ-VAE) tokenization is applied to each 12-lead ECG, with tokens enriched by continuous waveform features to preserve morphology lost during quantization without increasing sequence length. This design enables compact BERT masked language modeling with short sequences, improving scalability and reducing computational cost relative to long-sequence approaches. QECGBERT applies BERT pre-training to the MIMIC-IV dataset containing over 800,000 unlabeled ECG recordings, and fine-tuning on labeled PTB-XL, CPSC2018, and Chapman--Shaoxing--Ningbo datasets for evaluation. The results demonstrate that QECGBERT achieves comparable or superior performance to published benchmarks, offering a scalable, physiologically aware approach to ECG foundation modeling for cardiac diagnosis.
  </details>

- **[Error-Subspace Calibration for Multi-Task Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33483)**  `arXiv:2610.33483`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yeseul Cho, Hanseul Cho, Chulhee Yun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model merging combines task-specific expert models into a single model without joint training on their original datasets, but often incurs a performance gap relative to the individual experts. Post-merging calibration can reduce this gap through task-specific representation alignment, but the alignment objective alone does not specify which feature directions to correct. We propose Error-Subspace Calibration (ESCal), a post-merging calibration framework that identifies low-dimensional error subspaces from weight differences between expert and merged models. At each intermediate block boundary, a bridge module projects the merged representation onto the next block's input-projection error subspace and maps these coordinates to an additive correction in the current block's output-projection error subspace. The subspace bases remain fixed, so calibration learns only these linear maps, yielding a parameter-efficient mechanism for feature correction. We find that ESCal brings the merged model closer to the experts in both representations and predictions, indicating more faithful recovery of task-specific behavior. Experiments on vision and natural language understanding benchmarks show that ESCal improves merged-model performance across diverse architectures and merging methods using a small calibration set for each task.
  </details>

- **[PIT-GCL: Protein Interaction using Topological Graph Contrastive Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04850)**  `arXiv:2610.04850`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jae Won Choi, Ryoonki Hong, Alan Liang, Manjula Adiveppa Wader, Bingsong Zeng, Peiyang Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Protein binding prediction is central to target identification, therapeutic binder design, and large scale screening, yet remains challenging because binding depends on sequence, three dimensional geometry, and global structural organization. Recent folding models such as AlphaFold3 and Boltz-2 have substantially improved structure prediction, but their confidence outputs (pLDDT, pTM, ipTM) are not specifically designed for binary binding prediction, and dedicated structure aware predictors often require bound complex structures that are unavailable at screening scale. We introduce PIT-GCL, a dual tower structure aware framework that encodes each protein independently from its amino acid sequence, C{\alpha} point cloud, and a global persistent homology descriptor. Each tower combines residue ESM-2 embeddings with a topological summary computed from the H0 and H1 persistence landscapes of a Vietoris-Rips filtration, and processes the resulting tokens with a structure aware Transformer in which pairwise C{\alpha} distances enter as a learned attention bias. A bidirectional cross attention module then performs latent space soft docking between the two per-protein representations, and the model is trained with a combined binary cross entropy and NT-Xent contrastive objective. On three binary interaction prediction benchmarks, general PPI on PPIRef, TCRpMHC binding on STAG, and whole chain pairs on PPB-Affinity, PIT-GCL outperforms representative sequence based, structure aware, and task specific baselines on general PPI under our evaluation, and is the only method above chance on PPB-Affinity; on TCR-pMHC it leads at a fixed decision threshold but is outranked by a task specific sequence model. Because each protein is encoded independently in the first phase, its representation can be precomputed and reused across candidate pairs, which is convenient for large scale screening.
  </details>

- **[Bayesian Anytime Pareto Set Identification for Multi-Objective Multi-Armed Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18785)**  `arXiv:2606.18785`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Lennert Saerens, Bram Silue, Eleni Litsa, Peter Vrancx, Pieter Libin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying Pareto optimal solutions is critical to support multi-objective decision-making. In this work, we present Top-Two Pareto Front Thompson Sampling (TTPFTS), the first anytime algorithm for Pareto Set Identification in Multi-Objective Multi-Armed Bandits (MOMABs), taking a Bayesian approach. We benchmark TTPFTS against state-of-the-art fixed-confidence and fixed-budget Pareto Set Identification algorithms on synthetic environments. Next, we demonstrate its practical utility in a challenging real-world multi-objective multi-agent molecular discovery setting by efficiently exploring an ultra-large synthesis-on-demand molecular library, outperforming industry-standard and state-of-the-art baselines. Furthermore, we introduce a novel uncertainty quantification metric that estimates our algorithm's confidence in the predicted Pareto set. We demonstrate that this metric effectively proxies true performance, yielding a robust methodology for monitoring learning progress in complex settings. Finally, we complement these empirical findings with the first finite-time theoretical analysis of an anytime MOMAB Pareto Set Identification algorithm.
  </details>

- **[The Illusion of Reasoning: Exposing Evasive Data Contamination in LLMs via Zero-CoT Truncation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21856)**  `arXiv:2605.21856`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yifan Lan, Yuanpu Cao, Hanyu Wang, Lu Lin, Jinghui Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive reasoning abilities across a wide range of tasks, but data contamination undermines the objective evaluation of these capabilities. This problem is further exacerbated by malicious model publishers who use evasive, or indirect, contamination strategies, such as paraphrasing benchmark data to evade existing detection methods and artificially boost leaderboard performance. Current approaches struggle to reliably detect such stealthy contamination. In this work, we uncover a critical phenomenon: a model's generated reasoning steps actively mask its underlying memorization. Inspired by this, we propose the Zero-CoT Probe (ZCP), a novel black-box detection method that deliberately truncates the entire Chain-of-Thought (CoT) process to expose latent shortcut mappings. To further isolate memorization from the model's intrinsic problem-solving capabilities, ZCP compares the model's zero-CoT performance on the original benchmark against an isomorphically perturbed reference dataset. Furthermore, we introduce Contamination Confidence, a metric that quantifies both the likelihood and severity of contamination, moving beyond simple binary classifications. Extensive experiments on both previously identified contaminated models and specially fine-tuned contaminated models demonstrate that ZCP robustly detects both direct and evasive data contamination. The code for ZCP is accessible atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TwinRouterBench: Fast Static and Live Dynamic Evaluation for Realistic Agentic LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18859)**  `arXiv:2605.18859`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Pei Yang, Wanyi Chen, Tongyun Yang, Pengbin Feng, Jiarong Xing, Wentao Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM routing matters most in long-horizon applications such as coding agents, deep research systems, and computer-use agents, where a single user request triggers many model calls. Routing each call to the cheapest sufficient model can cut costs without sacrificing quality, yet existing router benchmarks evaluate routers only on one-shot prompts. They never expose the router-visible prefix at an intermediate agent step, never test whether a cheaper replacement preserves downstream task success, and often rely on online LLM judges at evaluation time. We introduce TwinRouterBench, a step-level routing benchmark with two tracks. The static track provides 970 router-visible prefixes from 520 instances across SWE-bench, BFCL, mtRAG, QMSum, and PinchBench, each paired with an execution-verified target tier estimated under a released downgrade-and-cascade protocol; scoring is deterministic arithmetic over tier labels, trajectory membership, and token costs, with no online evaluator-side LLM judge. The dynamic track supplies a harness that runs routers on the full 500-case SWE-bench Verified suite; in this paper we report a 100-case held-out evaluation disjoint from the static SWE supervision split. At each LLM call the router selects a concrete model from a locked pool, and success is measured by official task resolution and realized API spend. The two tracks support fast offline iteration followed by end-to-end validation under live agent execution. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[$ξ$-DPO: Direct Preference Optimization via Ratio Reward Margin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10981)**  `arXiv:2605.10981`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengyuan Fan, Zhonghua Wu, Yuxuan Du, Qun Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reference-free preference optimization has emerged as an efficient alternative to reinforcement learning from human feedback, with Simple Preference Optimization(SimPO) demonstrating strong performance by eliminating the explicit reference model through a simple objective. However, the joint tuning of the hyperparameters $\beta$ and $\gamma$ in SimPO remains a central challenge. We argue that this difficulty arises because the margin formulation in SimPO is not easily interpretable across datasets with different reward gap structures. To better understand this issue, we conduct a comprehensive analysis of SimPO and find that $\beta$, rather than merely serving as a reward scaling factor, primarily functions to filter noisy samples, while the reward margin $\gamma$ defined by SimPO is not semantically well aligned with explicitly enlarging the reward gap between chosen and rejected responses. Motivated by these observations, we propose $\xi$-DPO: Direct preference optimization via ratio reward margin. We first reformulate the preference objective through an equivalent transformation, changing the optimization target from maximizing the likelihood of reward gaps to minimizing the distance between reward gaps and optimal margins. Then, we redefine the reward in a ratio form between the chosen and rejected, which effectively cancels the effect of $\beta$ and yields a bounded and interpretable margin. This margin is called the ratio reward margin and is denoted by $\xi$. Unlike the $\beta$ and $\gamma$ in SimPO, $\xi$ can be determined from the initial reward gap distribution, thus much easier to tune. Our extensive experimental results show that $\xi$-DPO substantially outperforms existing preference optimization methods across open benchmarks on multiple evaluation metrics.
  </details>

- **[Flowette: Flow Matching with Graphette Priors for Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23566)**  `arXiv:2602.23566`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Asiri Wijesinghe, Sevvandi Kandanaarachchi, Daniel M. Steinberg, Cheng Soon Ong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study generative modeling of graphs with recurring subgraph motifs. We propose Flowette, a continuous flow matching framework that employs a graph neural network-based transformer to learn a velocity field over graph representations with node and edge attributes. Our model promotes topology-aware alignment through optimal transport-based coupling and preserves structural fidelity through endpoint-aware and chemistry-aware regularisation. To incorporate domain-driven structural priors, we introduce graphettes, a new probabilistic family of graph structure models that generalize graphons via controlled structural edits for motifs such as rings, stars, and trees. We theoretically analyze the coupling, invariance, and structural properties of the framework, evaluate it on synthetic and molecular benchmarks, and isolate the contributions of the structural prior, the optimal-transport coupling, and the regularisation terms through controlled ablations. Flowette achieves competitive performance overall, attaining state-of-the-art results on several metrics across multiple benchmarks, highlighting the effectiveness of combining structural priors with flow-based training for modeling complex graph distributions.
  </details>

- **[EpiFlow: Bridging Safety and Performance in Offline Reinforcement Learning via Epigraph-Guided Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08054)**  `arXiv:2602.08054`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Manan Tayal, Mumuksh Tayal, Ravi Prakash_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline reinforcement learning (RL) offers a promising framework for deploying autonomous systems in safety-critical settings without the risks of online exploration. However, learning policies that simultaneously achieve high performance and strong safety guarantees from fixed datasets remains a fundamental challenge. Many existing safe offline RL approaches typically rely on soft constraint formulations, which may permit safety violations and are sensitive to distributional shift. In contrast, formal methods such as Hamilton--Jacobi (HJ) reachability and Control Barrier Functions (CBFs) provide rigorous safety guarantees, but often yield overly conservative solutions, often neglecting performance. In this work, we bridge this gap by formulating safe offline RL as a state-constrained optimal control problem, where safety is enforced through hard state constraints and performance is captured via a reward function. The resulting value function satisfies a Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman (HJB) equation, which we approximate using offline RL on fixed datasets by learning an epigraph feasibility value function and extracting policies via flow matching. This formulation enables principled integration of safety guarantees with data-driven policy optimization. Empirically, across safety-critical benchmarks including boat navigation, Safety-Gymnasium, and Bullet-Safety-Gym tasks, our approach achieves competitive returns while exhibiting near-zero constraint violations, demonstrating a favorable balance between safety and performance in the offline setting.
  </details>

- **[Efficient Linear Attention for Multivariate Time Series Modeling via Entropy Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.03190)**  `arXiv:2511.03190`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mingtao Zhang, Guoli Yang, Zhanxing Zhu, Mengzhu Wang, Xiaoying Bai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Attention mechanisms are effective for multivariate time series modeling because they adaptively aggregate information across timestamps and variables. Their quadratic cost, however, limits their use in multivariate and spatio-temporal systems. Linear attention reduces this cost by replacing nonlinear attention mappings with forms that support associative computation. Replacing exponential weighting may weaken the amplification of high-score positions and further bias weakly selective distributions toward uniform aggregation. We propose an entropy-calibrated linear attention module and instantiate it as the ELinFormer forecaster. Our operator decomposes aggregation into a uniform component and a query-dependent correction, whose magnitude is calibrated by a query-specific temperature. We derive a second-order KL proxy from centered query-key scores. The Taylor expansion reduces the proxy to the squared-score energy divided by the squared temperature. This score energy can be computed from shared products of the centered keys without forming the full pairwise score matrix, and the same products support the linear attention output. We design a smooth temperature rule to calibrate the KL proxy: it keeps low-energy residuals vanishing, approximately calibrates moderate-energy queries to a shared KL level, and allows the proxy to continue growing for high-energy queries. Experiments on five PEMS benchmarks show competitive forecasting accuracy. Controlled comparisons with vanilla attention and Linformer demonstrate a favorable accuracy--memory trade-off, while analysis on PEMS08 shows that the proposed rational temperature rule and query-specific calibration achieve lower MAE than a linear rule or shared-temperature alternatives. These results support entropy calibration as an effective mechanism for preserving selective deviations in linear attention.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Reasoning Gains: Mitigating General-Capability Forgetting in Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.21978)**  `arXiv:2510.21978`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hoang Phan, Xianjun Yang, Yuanshun Yao, Jingyu Zhang, Shengjie Bi, Xiaocheng Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has delivered impressive gains in mathematical and multimodal reasoning and has become a standard post-training paradigm for contemporary language and vision-language models. However, the RLVR recipe introduces a significant risk of capability regression, in which models forget foundational skills after prolonged training without employing regularization strategies. We empirically confirm this concern, observing that open-source reasoning models suffer performance degradation on core capabilities such as perception and faithfulness. While imposing regularization terms like KL divergence can help prevent deviation from the base model, these terms are computed on the current task and therefore do not guarantee preservation of broader knowledge. Meanwhile, commonly used experience replay across heterogeneous domains makes it nontrivial to decide how much training emphasis each objective should receive. To address this, we propose RECAP-a replay strategy with dynamic objective reweighting for general knowledge preservation. Our reweighting mechanism adapts online using short-horizon signals of convergence and instability, shifting the post-training focus away from saturated objectives and toward underperforming or volatile ones. Our method is end-to-end and readily applicable to existing RLVR pipelines without training additional models or heavy tuning. Extensive experiments on benchmarks using Qwen2.5-VL-3B and Qwen2.5-VL-7B demonstrate the effectiveness of our method, which not only preserves general capabilities but also improves reasoning by enabling more flexible trade-offs among in-task rewards.
  </details>

- **[FediLoRA: Practical Federated Fine-Tuning of Foundation Models Under Missing-Modality Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06984)**  `arXiv:2509.06984`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Lishan Yang, Wei Emma Zhang, Nam Kha Nguygen, Po Hu, Yanjun Shu, Weitong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated Learning with LoRA fine-tuning offers an efficient and privacy-aware solution for institutions to collaboratively leverage their large datasets to train VLLMs. However, participating institutions often possess heterogeneous computational resources, resulting in imbalanced LoRA ranks, which pose a major challenge for effective collaboration. In addition, real-world applications in domains such as healthcare and transportation frequently suffer from missing modalities due to user mistakes or device failures, which significantly degrade global model performance in federated settings. To the best of our knowledge, no prior work has addressed these two challenges simultaneously in federated VLLMs. To tackle these issues, we propose FediLoRA, a lightweight federated LoRA aggregation framework that effectively mitigates the impact of missing modalities in heterogeneous environment. FediLoRA is explicitly motivated by the observation that simple averaging and structured editing can jointly benefit both global and personalized models. Our approach achieves strong performance across multiple general-domain and medical-domain benchmark datasets. Additional experiments on healthcare data further demonstrate that FediLoRA is well-suited for practical, real-world deployment scenarios. Our code is released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Auto-Research Arena: Benchmarking and Advancing Auto-Research in Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35866)**  `arXiv:2610.35866`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xi Chen, Chongjian Ge, Yiran Xu, Yiqun Mei, Henrik I. Christensen, Hao Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As autonomous research systems become more capable, benchmarks must both evaluate their performance and support meaningful research progress.
