We introduce Auto-Research Arena, a multi-target benchmark for evaluating autonomous research systems and discovering ideas in language modeling. Arena supports diverse research objectives within a shared experimental environment by separating admissibility from improvement: model quality and resource constraints determine whether a result qualifies, while the selected target measures progress among admissible results. We instantiate Arena in language model pretraining with nine targets spanning model, data, training, and inference efficiency, and evaluate seven research harnesses using two foundation models. Arena clearly differentiates existing harnesses across research objectives, with several sophisticated methods outperformed by our simple sequential search baseline, indicating substantial headroom in current auto-research systems. Beyond evaluation, Arena reveals a substantially broader frontier of model and system ideas aligned with active research directions. Reusing these discoveries in a new quality-driven search yields lower training loss under matched data and FLOPs and lower held-out BPB at the same model size. Together, Arena provides a common framework for benchmarking autonomous research and expanding its scope in language modeling. The code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[FOCUS: Active Critic Learning for Long-Horizon Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35801)**  `arXiv:2610.35801`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhengping Jiang, William Jurayj, Liaoyaqi Wang, Chunsheng Zuo, Anqi Liu, Benjamin Van Durme_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse outcome rewards make it difficult for long-horizon language-model agents to assign credit to earlier actions. Learned critics provide value estimates for intermediate states, but these estimates can be inaccurate, while estimating intermediate values with repetitive continuation samples from points within a trajectory incurs substantial costs. To mitigate these issues, we introduce FOCUS, an RL method that uses selective branching to help the critic network learn its value estimator. The rollout controller facilitates active learning for the critic by sampling continuations from states that are expected to most reduce the critic's estimation error. These refined value estimates then score the segment between consecutive states with a TD($\lambda$) advantage, while the sampled evidence supplies training targets for the critic. FOCUS supports long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning through a modular context-management interface that decouples context management from rollout allocation. Our experiments show that using compaction-induced segment boundaries for value estimation and credit assignment, FOCUS outperforms GRPO and VinePPO with matched per-step generation budgets on legal reasoning, deep search, and code localization benchmarks, improving held-out accuracy at Qwen3.5-9B scale by 7.2 points over the strongest baseline on BrowseComp-Plus (55.4 vs. 48.2) and by 4.1 points on DeonticBench (56.5 vs. 52.4).
  </details>

- **[TRACE: Memory-Efficient Time Series Class-Incremental Learning via Temporal-Spectral Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35727)**  `arXiv:2610.35727`  `cs.LG`  
  _Junhui Mao, Junru Zhang, Liming Ye, Cheng Peng, Haoran Shi, Xiaoli Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web-connected sensing applications continuously generate multivariate time series with evolving behavioral categories, making Time Series Class-Incremental Learning (TSCIL) essential for adapting to new classes without forgetting previously acquired knowledge. However, preserving diverse and evolving class distributions under strict privacy and memory constraints remains a fundamental challenge. Existing approaches often face a trade-off between retaining sufficient historical knowledge for accurate recognition and minimizing the memory required for continual adaptation. To address these limitations, we propose TRACE, a memory-efficient, replay-free framework that preserves class-specific knowledge through generative modeling rather than sample rehearsal. TRACE employs a compact, frozen temporal-spectral representation and lightweight class-specific autoencoders to capture complex intra-class variations. By independently preserving each class model, TRACE avoids recursive generation and catastrophic forgetting, while enabling direct classification through reconstruction-based scoring without retaining historical samples or large statistical matrices. Extensive experiments across five TSCIL benchmarks demonstrate that TRACE achieves competitive classification accuracy while reducing mean retained memory by approximately 98.26% compared with TS-ACL, the strongest evaluated analytical baseline, and achieving an approximately 33.43% relative improvement in accuracy on GrabMyo.
  </details>

- **[Efficient probabilistic weather forecasting at quarter-degree resolution with two-sided transport attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35545)**  `arXiv:2610.35545`  `cs.LG`  
  _Junu Choi, Youngjoon Hong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-resolution probabilistic weather forecasting remains computationally demanding. Here we present Astra, a 0.25$^\circ$ ensemble model trained on eight H200 GPUs. It combines Kantorovich Attention, a two-sided transport attention in which each region chooses where it reads from and writes to, so every region sends and receives at token resolution, with a self-featurised continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) that also scores forecasts in the model's multiscale features. In WeatherBench 2, Astra matches GenCast's CRPS averaged over five upper-air targets and days 1-10 with 9.8% of its hardware-normalised training compute. Verified against ERA5, its CRPS and ensemble-mean root-mean-square error are lower than the ECMWF ensemble's on all 600 variable, level and lead-time combinations, and its CRPS is lower than GenCast's on 73.5% of them. A 15-day member needs 39 times fewer floating-point operations than GenCast's GPU implementation, and 50 members take under 40 min on one consumer GPU, within 21 GB.
  </details>

- **[Intent-Preserving Adversarial User Simulation for Multi-Turn Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35426)**  `arXiv:2610.35426`  `cs.LG`  
  _Atri Vivek Sharma, Brian Formento, Alessio Lomuscio_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn agents are commonly evaluated with LLM-based simulated users, which can be overly cooperative and can therefore overestimate an agent's performance. We here introduce StressSim, an intent-preserving adversarial user simulator framework for stress-testing agents with challenging yet valid user interactions. StressSim optimises the simulator's high-level adversarial plan for each task instance. Candidate planner prompts are evolved with a genetic prompt-optimisation loop that uses rollout traces, granular task-completion signals, and validity feedback to favour conversations that reduce task success without changing the user's underlying goal. We evaluate the approach on the MultiWOZ dataset and the retail and airline domains from tau-bench with three target LLM agents, comparing against standard simulators and hand-designed non-collaborative user behaviours. We demonstrate the effectiveness of StressSim in exposing robustness gaps across frontier LLMs, with tau-bench pass^4 scores decreasing by up to 69% on gemini-3.8-flash and 62% on gpt-5.6-sol. StressSim maintains goal preservation performance (which decreases by at most 10%), using an optimisation budget of 20 rollouts per task. Sensitivity analysis suggests that increasing the budget further decreases task success. Overall our results demonstrate that StressSim exposes robustness gaps missed by standard and hand-designed user simulators while maintaining conversation realism. This is achieved through lightweight prompt optimisation alone, without model fine-tuning.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking uncertainty quantification for engineering regression under graded distribution shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35356)**  `arXiv:2610.35356`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ahmad Lawal, Rahim Pasbanigoloojeh, Asmau Lawal Bilbis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning models in engineering are often used beyond their training conditions, where reliable uncertainty quantification (UQ) matters most. Existing comparisons of regression UQ methods rely on random splits, which assess calibration only in distribution. We introduce a three-rung shift ladder (in distribution, soft shift and hard shift) built along physically meaningful shift variables, and evaluate 12 methods from five families on seven public engineering datasets: five extrapolation settings, an unseen turbofan fault mode and a winter air-quality sensor deployment. All tuning uses source data only. In distribution, conformal methods cover at least 0.946 of test points on every dataset. Under hard shift, mean coverage falls to 0.40-0.75 across the 11 methods that always return an interval, and no systematic difference in method ranks is detected (Friedman p = 0.63). Weighted conformalised quantile regression stays near nominal on the points it answers, but abstains on 85\% of hard-shift points on average and covers only 0.67 of the few Concrete points it answers. Source-validation gains predict improvement in distribution and under soft shift, but not under hard shift (Spearman's rho = 0.04). Engineering evaluations should split calibration data by deployment unit, report subgroup coverage and sharpness, and treat abstention as an explicit operational outcome.
  </details>

- **[OneEEG: A Unified Signal-Visual Multimodal Language Model with Task-Aware Mixture of Projectors for Multi-Task EEG Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35355)**  `arXiv:2610.35355`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiacheng Zhang, Xueyi Zhang, Xin Lu, Siqi Cai, Haizhou Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electroencephalography (EEG) is widely used in clinical assessment, brain-computer interfaces, and cognitive-state analysis, yet EEG modeling remains fragmented across task paradigms. Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) provide a promising interface for flexible multimodal reasoning, but their ability to understand EEG inputs remains limited. In this work, we propose OneEEG, a unified EEG-MLLM framework for multi-task EEG understanding. By training on our curated OneEEG-Instruct, a multi-task EEG instruction-tuning dataset, OneEEG jointly models numerical EEG representations and visual EEG representations, and introduces a task-aware Mixture of Projectors (MoP) to align heterogeneous EEG features across different task families with the LLM embedding space. We further establish OneEEG-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating current MLLMs and our method on multi-task EEG understanding. Experiments show that existing MLLMs have limited ability to interpret EEG inputs, while OneEEG achieves stronger performance by integrating numerical and visual EEG representations with task-aware EEG-to-LLM projection. Extensive ablations and analyses further reveal the effects of visual EEG renderings, MoP variants, router behavior, and feature dispersion across EEG tasks.
  </details>

- **[Conversational Generalized Linear Bandits with Hybrid Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35262)**  `arXiv:2610.35262`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yurui Zou, Qirun Zeng, Jinhang Zuo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational bandits improve recommendations by actively querying users about key-terms to elicit their item preferences. However, existing methods typically assume homogeneous feedback for items and key-terms, using either absolute rewards or pairwise comparisons. In practice, item feedback often takes the form of clicks or ratings, while comparisons are more natural for key-terms. To address this gap, we study conversational generalized linear bandits with hybrid feedback, combining absolute item rewards and relative key-term comparisons. We propose Conversational UCB with Hybrid Feedback (HyConUCB), which constructs unified confidence bounds from heterogeneous feedback and adaptively selects key-term comparisons by balancing information gain and directional coverage. Through a refined regret analysis that jointly exploits both feedback sources, we establish a regret bound that improves upon reward-only and adapted dueling-only benchmarks. Experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets validate the effectiveness of our algorithm.
  </details>

- **[LEAP: a Latent Embedding Anomaly Patch-Transformer for time-series anomaly detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35190)**  `arXiv:2610.35190`  `cs.LG`  
  _Cristhian Moya-Mota, Ignacio Aguilera-Martos, Diego García-Gil, Julián Luengo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series anomaly detection is dominated by reconstruction-based neural detectors, whose objective is structurally flawed. Since the target is also the input, an expressive model can learn to reconstruct anomalies too, leaving uninformative residuals. Recent large-scale benchmarks show that, on curated data, elaborate deep detectors rarely beat simple statistical baselines, while current leaders rely on representation learning. We propose LEAP (Latent Embedding Anomaly Patch-Transformer), which combines masked prediction with latent representation-based anomaly evidence, without relying on reconstruction or attention-based scoring. The model removes patches from a window and predicts them from the remaining temporal and cross-channel context, so prediction errors measure how well learned dynamics explain hidden segments. Deterministic complementary group masking ensures every time step is scored while hidden, with identical visible fractions during training and inference. LEAP adds three label-free evidence sources: latent-embedding novelty calibrated by local density, local temporal contrast, and per-channel novelty pooled over channels selected by six label-free criteria. Under the TSB-AD protocol, the full model achieves a VUS-PR of $0.5179 \pm 0.0022$ on $180$ held-out multivariate series. On TSB-AD-U, a univariate variant combining multi-scale masked-prediction scoring with latent novelty, without contrast or channel-pool novelty, achieves $0.6194 \pm 0.0016$ on $350$ held-out series. Both results average three random seeds. A five-stage ablation on TSB-AD-M, supported by paired Wilcoxon signed-rank tests, shows statistically significant gains from each successive component.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Memory Index Evolution for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34977)**  `arXiv:2610.34977`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuntong Hu, Tongli Su, Yuanfu Sun, Shuo Ma, Qiaoyu Tan, Liang Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon agents continually accumulate experience, yet retaining a memory does not guarantee access when it is needed. Existing systems can adapt both memory content and access; we isolate persistent access updates as a separately controlled adaptation layer. We formulate this target as Memory Index Evolution and instantiate it with Adaptive Memory Index Evolution (AMIE), a lightweight plug-in layer that evolves per-memory access state from deployment retrievals without modifying stored content or retraining the host memory system. AMIE distils a semantic teacher's keep/suppress judgments into a retrieval-conditioned policy and accumulates its selective actions into bounded state that reweights subsequent host retrievals. Surprisingly, even a zero-call policy that simply accumulates retrieval frequency can match or outperform semantic policies in several settings, revealing retrieval traffic itself as a strong signal for access adaptation, while learned selectivity improves robustness when frequently retrieved memories remain useful. Across five retrieval-based hosts and three benchmarks, trained AMIE improves downstream F1 or accuracy in all 15 comparisons, by 0.70--3.37 percentage points (up to 25% relative), and improves retrieval in all 10 comparisons. Controlled analyses show that persistent competitor suppression can expose previously unseen evidence, while suppressing repeatedly useful memories creates the complementary failure mode. Together, these results establish persistent memory access as a practical adaptation layer, distinct from the content it serves, in retrieval-based agent memory systems.
  </details>

- **[Beyond feature attribution: Recovering latent interaction structures in machine learning models via localized CBFI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34962)**  `arXiv:2610.34962`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sejong Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background: Understanding individual predictions in complex tabular models remains challenging, as existing additive frameworks like SHAP and LIME often obscure structural logic by absorbing nonlinear interactions into individual feature scores. Exposing these latent interaction structures is essential for automated auditing in high-stakes environments where understanding the logic of model behavior is as essential as predictive accuracy. Methods: To address this limitation, we introduce the localized case-based feature importance (CBFI) framework. Unlike traditional numerical attribution, our method decomposes instance-specific predictions into structurally distinct components, effectively separating independent main effects (G1) from pure interactions (G4) to capture explicit feature synergies and provide a principled approach for diagnosing feature combinations. Results: Comprehensive experiments on wine quality classification and medical insurance regression demonstrate that localized CBFI successfully disentangles complex interactions hidden by additive models. Furthermore, fidelity tests and synthetic ground-truth benchmarks confirm that the identified interactions correspond precisely to the nonlinear structural dependency patterns embedded in complex architectures, including gradient boosting and deep neural networks, thereby successfully shifting the explainable AI paradigm from simple numerical attribution to the recovery of interaction-driven contributions.
  </details>

- **[MINT: Mitigating Structural Inconsistency via Attentive Message Passing for Financial Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34950)**  `arXiv:2610.34950`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yin-Ju Wu, Bi-Cheng Yan, Berlin Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Illicit account detection is a critical task for financial risk control systems, aiming to pinpoint malicious actors within transaction networks. Existing efforts typically recast transaction networks into directed multigraphs and leverage graph neural networks (GNNs) to detect anomalies via topological structures under the homophily assumption. However, directly grafting GNNs onto transaction graphs is faced with at least two pivotal challenges, i.e., structural inconsistency and label scarcity. The former arises from deceptive connections forged by malicious actors, while the latter stems from the lack of annotated data. In light of these, we introduce MINT, a novel graph neural framework that mitigates structural inconsistency via an attentive message-passing scheme on transaction graphs. Specifically, MINT introduces a discrepancy-aware feature propagation, which tracks malicious actors by quantifying deviations between nodes across bi-directional transaction flows. A transaction salience module is proposed to mitigate structural inconsistency by adaptively regularizing the interaction strength between nodes. Furthermore, to alleviate label scarcity, MINT is optimized via Positive-Unlabeled (PU) learning, which exploits only a minority of illicit nodes coupled with a majority of unlabeled ones. Extensive experiments conducted on three financial transaction benchmarks, including two large-scale public and one in-house real-world datasets, validate the superior effectiveness of MINT over several cutting-edge baselines.
  </details>

- **[When Respondents Are Scarce: LLM-Derived Semantic Priors for Bayesian Confirmatory Factor Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34843)**  `arXiv:2610.34843`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shaohuai Feng, Mohd Wira Mohd Shafiei, Mohd Sukri Shafie, Siyang Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Across social and behavioral research, measuring latent constructs can be challenging when target populations are small or difficult to reach. Recruiting additional respondents may be costly or infeasible, leaving researchers with limited data for estimating questionnaire factor structures. Yet item wording contains structural expectations before data collection, and whether these can improve small-sample estimation without replacing empirical validation remains unclear. We introduce the Semantic-Empirical Updating Framework (SEUF), which converts large language model paraphrases and embeddings, aligned with hypothesized item-factor assignments, into prespecified priors for Bayesian confirmatory factor analysis. We evaluated SEUF using a 38-item survey of 617 industrial-park stakeholders and two public surveys, FEVS and RIASEC. Semantic structure persisted after wording controls across all three instruments. With 50 respondents, SEUF achieved loading precision comparable to conventional CFA with 70-82 respondents, equivalent to 29-39% fewer respondents at the specified precision benchmark. Results showed lower loading errors and improved credible-interval coverage relative to conventional CFA in both public surveys. Gains were greatest when respondent data were limited, highlighting the value of semantic priors under information scarcity. By harnessing structural information already embedded in questionnaires, SEUF advances a more resource-efficient approach to measurement and opens new directions for study design and empirical inference in hard-to-reach populations.
  </details>

- **[Can System-1-Style Decision Models Replace Dedicated Rerankers in RAG? A Systems Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34816)**  `arXiv:2610.34816`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qiaomu Li, Ying Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems often use a dedicated reranker to choose which retrieved candidates reach the language model. Reranking improves relevance but adds latency, so teams look for faster alternatives. System-1style decision models make direct, structured predictions instead of generating long responses. Could one provide comparable reranking quality with less inference overhead? We first screen 21 open-weight models on 11 benchmarks, then compare a promising decision model with a dedicated reranker on identical candidate lists. The default decision-model setup fails on many long candidate sets, so scoring only the requests it completes gives a misleading ranking. Limiting candidate text length largely restores reliability and makes some configurations competitive. A frozen evaluation on new retrieval data supports the reliability and ranking results, but confirms the latency requirement for only one of the two selected configurations. Across workloads, which model ranks better changes, and a tuned reranker remains faster and uses far less GPU memory. A decision model can perform competitive reranking, but replacing a reranker requires weighing quality, latency and memory together, and teams should tune the reranker they already run first.
  </details>

- **[TimeWorld: Towards Action-Conditioned Multimodal World Models for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34797)**  `arXiv:2610.34797`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hang Ni, Weijia Zhang, Hao Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series forecasting supports decision-making in web applications such as online retail and network management, where planned actions can reshape future dynamics. World models provide a natural paradigm for predicting such action-dependent changes beyond extrapolating historical patterns. However, existing time series world models (TSWMs) typically rely on fixed, domain-specific covariate schemas that limit flexible intervention specification, while multimodal forecasters treat text primarily as passive predictive context. To investigate these gaps, we introduce TSWM-Bench, a cross-domain benchmark for multimodal TSWMs spanning seven real-world datasets, with textual interventions grounded in recorded action--outcome data. Our evaluation reveals that conventional multimodal forecasters underperform history-only time series foundation models (TSFMs) despite dataset-specific training, whereas simple text-prefix adaptations improve forecasting across all evaluated TSFM backbones. These findings suggest that pretrained TSFMs encode implicit world knowledge of temporal dynamics, offering a strong foundation for action-conditioned modeling beyond conventional multimodal fusion. Building on this insight, we propose TimeWorld, a plug-in framework that adapts pretrained TSFMs through explicit action-response modeling. Specifically, the Prototype-Guided Latent Modulation (PLM) module grounds textual actions in temporal dynamics by composing representative response prototypes to modulate future representations based on the action and historical patterns. The Action Contrastive Learning (ACL) component strengthens action-specific response modeling by contrasting these updates under alternative interventions sharing the same history, distinguishing action types while preserving relative intensity relationships.
  </details>

- **[Sparse Reconstruction by Direct Measurement-to-Field Generation in Function Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34709)**  `arXiv:2610.34709`  `cs.LG`  
  _Linzheng Wang, Jason Chen, Nicolas Tricard, Zituo Chen, Xingsen Guo, Sili Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding physical systems often requires recovering their full spatial state from measurements available only at sparse locations. This reconstruction becomes challenging when physical boundaries are unknown, high-resolution training fields are unavailable, or important quantities cannot be measured directly. Sparse observations may also be consistent with multiple plausible states, leading to significant reconstruction uncertainty. Here we introduce Direct Measurement-to-Field Generation (\DMF{}), a functional rectified flow framework that learns how sparse measurements drive physical-field generation end to end. Instead of relying on grid-based rasterization or runtime guidance, \DMF{} jointly learns a sensor--global--sensor representation with the generative transport, dynamically fusing local grounding with global context to evaluate physical fields at arbitrary query coordinates. Across benchmarks involving unobserved boundaries, missing high-resolution training fields, and unmeasured physical quantities, \DMF{} outperforms alternative baselines by significantly reducing reconstruction errors and preserving the fidelity of physical properties. Furthermore, its stochastic ensemble generation reliably quantifies uncertainty while improving continuous ranked probability scores. These results extend sparse reconstruction from fixed-setting point estimation toward measurement-conditioned generation of physical fields under incomplete and changing information.
  </details>

- **[AT-AT: Automatic Tool Optimization from Agent Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34679)**  `arXiv:2610.34679`  `cs.LG`  
  _Maya Ravichandran, Pragya Tripathi, Dharamendra Kumar, Adith Swaminathan, Nathan Kallus_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efforts to improve LLM-based agents typically focus on modifying the agent itself, yet success also depends on the environment in which an agent acts. Tools mediate this interaction: a tool's description shapes how an agent calls it, and its implementation determines the results and errors the agent receives afterwards. Many tools, such as APIs and MCP servers, are shared by many agents and maintained by providers who cannot change those agents. We introduce AT-AT (Automatic Tool Optimization from Agent Traces), an agentic tool optimizer that improves a shared toolset offline from historical agent executions while holding the agents fixed. A lightweight coding agent reads agent trajectories and their evaluation results, and selectively edits tool descriptions and, when permitted, implementations. On $\tau^2$-bench-verified, AT-AT achieves the highest task completion rates in the Airline and Telecom domains at lower cost than strong prompt- and tool-optimizer baselines, and its Telecom gain is statistically significant under paired permutation tests. Optimizing the tools within a coding agent harness also improves held-out performance on TerminalBench-2 and TBLite. When applied to three production MCP servers at a large enterprise, AT-AT lowered cost or tool error rates on every server and increased held-out task passes on one. By turning accumulated execution traces into updates to shared tool environments, AT-AT provides a path toward continual improvement of agent systems through environment-side learning.
  </details>

- **[Cumulative Marginal Matching for One-Step Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34575)**  `arXiv:2610.34575`  `cs.LG`  
  _Paribesh Regmi, Rui Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The sampling procedures of diffusion and flow-matching models involve hundreds of denoising steps incurring substantial inference time, which has motivated a growing body of work on training schemes for few-step and one-step generation. Existing approaches primarily seek to align the generation trajectory of few-step and base models, yet their training objectives only involve discrepancies at a single point in the trajectory, encouraging only local \emph{point-level} alignment. While recent methods such as \emph{Shortcut-CSL} introduce cumulative objective that enforce alignment over the entire trajectory, this is suboptimal as they do not directly enforce alignment between the distributions induced by the two models throughout the generation process. We propose \emph{Cumulative Marginal Matching} (CMM), a principled objective for few-step generation: by viewing flow-matching as a sequence of intermediate marginal distributions connecting the prior and data distributions, CMM directly optimizes the cumulative discrepancy between the base and few-step marginal distributions over the entire sequence. Specifically, we formulate this discrepancy using the Wasserstein-2 distance. By exploiting the pushforward representation of flow-matching, we transform an intractable cumulative Wasserstein objective into a tractable surrogate that can be optimized using samples from the intermediate marginal, which are readily generated through the conditional interpolation process of flow-matching. Furthermore, we show that the existing \emph{Shortcut-CSL} objective arises as a first-order approximation of that of the CMM, establishing a theoretical connection between the two objectives. Experiments on standard benchmarks demonstrate improvement in generation quality over \emph{Shortcut-CSL} and other state-of-the-art one- and few-step baselines under identical training budgets.
  </details>

- **[Error Diffusion Replaces Latent Weights: Training Binary-Weight Language Models on a Few Bits of Dithered State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34566)**  `arXiv:2610.34566`  `cs.LG`  
  _Pierre-Olivier Bonin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Binary-weight language models store one bit per weight, but training them keeps a full-precision latent copy of each weight plus Adam state, about 96 extra bits per weight, so a deployed binary model cannot be adapted on the device that runs it. We replace the latent weight with a 2-8-bit residual buffer quantized by stochastic rounding, which cuts the training state per weight from 97 bits to 3-9, up to 19x less. The resulting error-diffusion (ED) optimizer moves the residual-carrying loop of image halftoning onto the weight-update path. Each buffer accumulates the unrealized part of every update and flips the weight when the accumulated value crosses a threshold, and it works only when its quantization grid has a level exactly at that threshold. Under this threshold-alignment rule, 4-bit buffers match 8-bit buffers on a binary multilayer perceptron benchmark, and the same rule lifts a different latent-free optimizer, Bop, from a 10-point deficit at 4 bits to full-precision accuracy. On character-level transformers with 97% of parameters binary, ED trains within 5-11% of the latent-weight reference at a 2,000-step budget and within 1.7% at convergence. On scaling ladders over enwik8 and wikitext-103, up to 19M parameters on enwik8 (70M with the wikitext-103 embeddings), the latent-free loss stays within -2.3% to +2.9% of the reference at all six sizes once a per-step cap on the buffer update removes a launch instability; without the cap it trails by 10% at the largest tokenized size. A deployed binary model continues training under ED at one tenth of the latent training state, matching latent-weight continuation, and a fused CPU kernel completes the adaptation within a 275 MB training-memory budget, which latent-weight training cannot. A 250-step probe sets the flip learning rate at any new scale.
  </details>

- **[Best Policy Tracking in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34501)**  `arXiv:2610.34501`  `cs.LG`  
  _Judith Echevarrieta, Etor Arza, Aritz Pérez, Josu Ceberio_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning commonly exhibits non-monotonic performance dynamics in single-task settings. During learning, a sequence of policies is generated whose true performance is unknown and may oscillate significantly across iterations. Despite this well-known phenomenon, standard experimental practice typically reports either the final visited policy or the best among periodically evaluated ones, implicitly assuming that these selection criteria reliably approximate the best-performing policy visited throughout the entire sequence.In this work, we present an empirical study on the implications of non-monotonicity for policy selection in single-task policy-based reinforcement learning. We introduce post-hoc diagnostic metrics to characterize performance degradation and assess the accuracy and efficiency of different selection criteria. These metrics allow us to evaluate commonly used selection strategies across standard continuous-control benchmarks using a well-established on-policy actor-critic method. Our results show that, in this representative experimental setting, widely adopted selection practices can deviate considerably from the best policy encountered during learning, leading to underperforming estimates and suboptimal use of computational resources. As an alternative, we recommend a validation-based policy selection strategy constrained by computational cost, which provides a more consistent and accurate approximation of the best visited policy while improving selection efficiency. Overall, our findings highlight policy selection as an underexplored factor in the evaluation protocols of reinforcement learning and motivate further study on explicit and standardized validation-based selection procedures.
  </details>

- **[Approximate Unlearning in Prototype-based-classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34495)**  `arXiv:2610.34495`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sreejita Ghosh, Mohammad Mohammadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The recent data and AI regulations and governance needs have highlighted the need for removing specific data even from models trained on or with them. While retraining the entire model is a theoretically trivial solution, it is computationally expensive, thereby motivating research in Machine Unlearning. This paper introduces a robust approximate unlearning technique for the family of prototype-based classifiers called Generalized Learning Vector Quantization, aimed at removal of effects of specified samples, either due to deletion request or due to them being identified as noisy or corrupt. We first show the impact of the different sizes of the forget set, as well as the characteristics of the data in this set, on the unlearned model. To address the sources of instabilities detected in this unlearning method we propose an adapted version of this technique, such that the unlearning is robust to the characteristics of the forget set. Thereafter using the intrinsic interpretability property of GLVQ and appropriate metrics defined in unlearning literature, we show the efficacy (approximately 0 deviation of parameters and performance of unlearned model relative to retrained model) and efficiency (6X for smallest dataset to 10^(5)X in the largest dataset) of the proposed unlearning techniques compared to naive retrained, on six benchmark datasets for tabular data recommended by literature. Although in this work we investigated only binary class problems, the proposed robust unlearning strategy can be readily applied for removing sample effects from any trained GLVQ model, i.e., it does not require any modification in the training scheme of GLVQ.
  </details>

- **[Models Verify Sufficiency, Not Validity: Usable Falsehoods Evade the Checks that Gaps Trigger](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34488)**  `arXiv:2610.34488`  `cs.LG`  
  _Naveen Madiraju_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ask a model "how many settlements did the Normans control?" and it answers that the passage does not say. Tell it "given that the Normans controlled 12 settlements of 400 inhabitants, what is the total?" and it computes 4,800. Nothing was added to the passage; the unsupported fact merely became usable. Benchmarks for unanswerable questions are built almost entirely by omission, deleting the fact a question needs and scoring refusal, so they never probe this. Holding source support fixed and varying only whether an unsupported premise is usable, flagging drops 8-30 points and commitment to an answer rises 1.5-2.3x on GSM8K across four models spanning two families and 2B-26B; all 16 paired contrasts remain significant after Holm correction. Two further manipulations reproduce it, one also on SQuAD: a fact asserted rather than asked about, and a corrupted intermediate value rather than a removed one. Models articulate the exact gap they then ignore, stating that "there is no financial information provided" for an inert premise and silently adopting a fabricated price for the same item. We read these as one behaviour: whether a model checks depends substantially on whether usable material is present, even when source support is held fixed. Because the main design never instructs models to reject premises introduced in the question, adopting such a premise can also be read as conditional reasoning, and we delimit what the design does and does not show. In the judging seat the pattern differs: asked whether the answer follows, judges pass solutions with a required step deleted 78-99% of the time at every scale tested. A natural prompt-level mitigation lowers accuracy by 22 points without improving detection.
  </details>

- **[INTRA: Interleaved Non-contiguous Token spaRse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34056)**  `arXiv:2610.34056`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxuan Xia, Wenbo Lu, Shaoyi Zheng, Shengjie Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformers achieve strong performance across modalities but are bottlenecked by the quadratic cost of attention. Sparse attention can reduce this cost, but effective sparsity must preserve useful long-range communication and map well to GPU hardware. We introduce INTRA--Interleaved Non-contiguous Token spaRse Attention--a hardware-aware static sparse attention design based on a relay-communication view under Computational Query Set (CQS) constraints. Rather than directly covering all token-to-token interactions within each sparse layer, INTRA uses complementary sparse groups to provide short mediated communication paths across layers. Scatter forms relay groups that sample broadly across local neighborhoods, while Gather reconnects these groups in subsequent layers. Under a partition model, this structure guides CQS-compatible sparse patterns with global reachability. Our CQS-aware FlashAttention-style kernel computes structured indices at runtime, loads selected non-contiguous tokens, and packs them densely for computation. On an A100, INTRA achieves a $1.53\times$ speedup in $2K$ FLUX.1-dev generation, with better measured image quality than evaluated CLEAR configurations after LoRA self-distillation. On LLaMA-3.1-8B, it improves long-context prefill efficiency with strong teacher-forced retrieval performance under matched LoRA adaptation. Attention-only benchmarks further show up to $8.4\times$ and $10.4\times$ speedups over MoBA and density-constrained NSA, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Performance of Neural Operator Baselines on 'The Well' Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33967)**  `arXiv:2610.33967`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yash Pincha, Andy Dimnaku, Andrew Chiang, Adarsh Ganeshram, Valentin Duruisseaux, Anima Anandkumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Well, a 15-terabyte dataset spanning 16 physical simulation domains, provides an extensive benchmark for developing and evaluating machine learning surrogate models for complex physical systems. The baselines released with the benchmark were presented by their authors as illustrative examples obtained at small computational cost, trained with zero to very limited hyperparameter tuning and under a highly constraining 12-hour per-run wall-clock limit. These results have subsequently been treated as fully tuned benchmarks, and in some cases effectively interpreted as unconstrained performance baselines. We perform a short non-exhaustive hyperparameter search for the Tucker-factorized Fourier Neural Operator (TFNO) under the same 12-hour per-run wall-clock limit, while using only a single input timestep rather than the history of up to four used by the published baselines. Despite this more restrictive setup, tuning alone reduces VRMSE below both published FNO and TFNO baselines on 15 of 17 tasks, sometimes by several orders of magnitude. This sensitivity to a modest tuning effort underscores that the released neural operator baselines should not be treated as architectural benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Min-Marginal Power Indices for Multi-Objective Feature Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33885)**  `arXiv:2610.33885`  `cs.LG`  
  _Wenjie Shi, Chi Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Game-theoretic feature selection scores each feature by its average marginal contribution across coalitions. An average shows whether a feature helps overall, but not whether, in some coalition, adding it turns an acceptable model into an unacceptable one. Building on minimum marginal aggregation, we define the Min-Marginal Power Index (MinPI) on a binary utility-threshold game. For a fixed quota, MinPI takes the value -1, 0, or +1 and labels each feature harmful, neutral, or decisive. A harmful label carries its own evidence, an evaluated coalition in which adding the feature drops utility below the quota, so no score cutoff is needed; finite-budget estimators keep this property but may miss harmful features. We use MinPI to propose feature removals, which are accepted only if they pass a validation gate, and to prefilter multi-objective search. On six benchmark datasets over five seeds, MinPI removed 12.5% of analyzed features with almost no loss of held-out utility. On Madelon and Abalone, where it removed the most, its removals cost far less held-out utility than random removals of the same size. Prefiltering increased the dataset-averaged hypervolume of multi-objective search in all five seeds. MinPI is therefore a conservative feature-deletion step whose gains are largest where the data contain many removable features.
  </details>

- **[DIGEST: Discovery-Guided LLM Tuning for Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33880)**  `arXiv:2610.33880`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kaifeng Jin, Ruizhong Qiu, Zihao Li, Xiao Lin, Dongqi Fu, Yinglong Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Symbolic regression (SR) seeks to discover mathematical expressions directly from data, without requiring task-specific scientific descriptions. Despite tremendous progress in both conventional search-based methods and recent LLM-based methods, two critical limitations largely remain. First (Limited Generation Flexibility), conventional search-based methods rely on either fixed search rules or specialized learned generators, severely limiting adaptive candidate generation across diverse expression structures. Second (Underutilized Search Discoveries), most LLM-based methods cannot reliably recover ground-truth expressions because they fall short in effectively leveraging the LLM itself to thoroughly digest past expressions or adaptively guide future search. To address these limitations, we propose DIGEST. To render greater flexibility in generation, DIGEST repurposes a pretrained decoder block from a lightweight LLM into a symbolic policy for Genetic Programming (GP) population seeding, thereby leveraging the broader modeling capacity of the LLM. To more thoroughly utilize search discoveries, high-quality expressions discovered by GP are fed back to update the LLM-derived policy online, thereby enabling the LLM to progressively internalize past search discoveries and translate them into improved subsequent population generation. Experiments show that on two ground-truth benchmarks, DIGEST outperforms the strongest competitor by up to 2.59% in recovery. Among LLM-based baselines, it improves recovery by at least 54.91% while using at most 17.4% of the strongest competitor's mean search time. On 122 BlackBox datasets, DIGEST achieves the highest accuracy while maintaining a moderate model complexity.
  </details>

- **[A quantum k-nearest neighbors implementation using magnitude-aware comparisons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33739)**  `arXiv:2610.33739`  `cs.LG`  
  _Christopher Rector, Jiangwen Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Much attention has recently been devoted to the k-nearest neighbors (KNN) algorithm as a case study for quantum computing algorithms. Several algorithms have devised methods to compute a similarity metric to solve Quantum KNN (QKNN). This paper presents a practical quantum encoding technique that enables Euclidean distance calculation between vectors with different L2 norms by appending a single component to each vector, then uses it in the QKNN problem. It is arguably simpler to understand and implement than comparable methods and uses fewer qubits. Further, an implementation of this algorithm is provided and is used to benchmark it against a recent Euclidean-distance QKNN method on standard labeled datasets. In addition to demonstrating proper formulation, the results show marked improvement over similar implementations during simulation with realistic noise due to the use of fewer qubits and thus more coherent circuits.
  </details>

- **[Geodesic-SVDD Latent Mixup for One-Shot Distributed Tabular Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33619)**  `arXiv:2610.33619`  `cs.LG`  
  _Andrea Moleri, Christian Internò, Markus Olhofer, Barbara Hammer, Ali Raza_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distributed Learning on structured data is a key and widely adopted approach in privacy-sensitive sectors, ranging from financial fraud prevention to federated healthcare diagnostics. While Gradient Boosted Decision Trees are widely used in centralized settings due to their efficiency, they are incompatible with standard gradient-based federated learning protocols because of their non-differentiability. Conversely, standard deep classifiers that are amenable to federated optimization often suffer from catastrophic forgetting under label distribution skew. In this work, to address these gaps we propose Geodesic-SVDD, a framework for Distributed Spherical Prototype Learning that recasts each client's task as one-class density estimation on the hypersphere, so that local models can be aggregated as probabilistic manifolds in a single round. Specifically, for each client, we employ a Residual Spherical Encoder and Geodesic Latent Mixup that enforces strict hyperspherical constraints ($\|\phi(x)\| \equiv 1$), preventing the manifold collapse observed in linear approximations. Furthermore, leveraging this insight, we propose a novel One-Shot "Product of Experts" protocol that enables privacy-preserving aggregation without iterative synchronization. Finally, we mechanistically analyze the trade-off between interpolation strategies, demonstrating that while linear mixup offers an enticing computational shortcut, it destabilizes optimization gradients by orders of magnitude ($10^9\times$). Across five tabular benchmarks and four levels of label skew, Geodesic-SVDD attains the highest mean AUROC among seven federated methods, outperforms federated tree ensembles by up to 21 AUROC points on Covertype, and its One-Shot variant matches centralized Deep SVDD under IID and mild skew with a single communication round.
  </details>

- **[H-GLow: Accelerating Graph Generation via Hazard-Guided Discrete Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33599)**  `arXiv:2610.33599`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sirine Ayadi, Stephan Günnemann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discrete flow matching (DFM) offers a promising approach to graph generation; however, generating high-quality graphs with few steps remains challenging due to the strong dependencies between nodes and edges. Although DFM decouples training from sampling, enabling flexible sampling strategies without retraining, existing graph DFM samplers often rely on task-specific heuristics, substantially expanding the hyperparameter space. Moreover, graph DFM models still require sampling budgets comparable to those of diffusion-based approaches, leaving their potential for accelerating graph generation only partially realized. In this work, we introduce H-Glow, a hazard-guided framework for accelerating graph generation built on the decomposition of Continuous-Time Markov chain (CTMC) generators into hazards and conditional jump distributions. H-Glow leverages these quantities to plan graph edits and dynamically adjust the sampling time to graph activity. H-Glow can be adapted to pretrained graph DFM models, substantially improving the generation quality with fewer steps. We further introduce a novel graph DFM model based on the conditional generator matching framework, which directly predicts hazards and conditional jumps, and show that its training objective decomposes exactly into hazard matching and hazard-weighted jump prediction. Experiments on molecular and synthetic graph benchmarks demonstrate improved generation quality with substantially fewer sampling steps.
  </details>

- **[Nowcasting aerosol particle size distributions from PM2.5 with physics-regularized flow matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33557)**  `arXiv:2610.33557`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhi-Song Liu, Jenni Köykkä, Wenqing Peng, Ammar Kheder, Petri Clusius, Michael Boy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The mass concentration of particles smaller than 2.5um (PM2.5) is cheap to measure and widely monitored, whereas the particle number size distribution (PSD) requires costly instruments and is measured at only a few research stations. Yet many aerosol effects, such as vapor uptake, cloud droplet formation and deposition in the human respiratory tract, depend on the PSD rather than on mass. We explore whether recent generative deep learning models can nowcast the PSD: from the PM2.5 record and eight meteorological parameters over a 24h window, a model generates an ensemble of plausible hourly size distributions for the same window, reflecting that one mass can arise from many size distributions. Rather than proposing a single best model, we use two contrasting Finnish stations, the boreal forest site Hyytiälä and the urban site Kumpula, as a controlled testbed. On it, we benchmark six generative models against deterministic and climatological baselines, locally and after transfer between sites, and test physical constraints for neural network training. Flow matching performs best overall; its guided variant has the lowest continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) at both sites. A constraint based on a reduced aerosol general dynamic equation (GDE) lowers the CRPS by 5.5-11.5% at the training site and reduces errors after transfer between sites. Transfer remains harder than local estimation, identifying training on large multi-station data sets as the next step towards PSD estimates at unmonitored locations. This benchmark establishes physics-informed generative models as a promising route from routine air-quality monitoring to size-resolved aerosol information.
  </details>

- **[Overlap-Aware Sub-hypergraph Pattern Frequencies for Hypergraph-Level Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33514)**  `arXiv:2610.33514`  `cs.LG`  
  _Nafiul Hasan Anik, Nazira Jesmin Lina, Md Tanvir Alam, Md. Fahim Arefin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classifying whole hypergraphs requires a fixed-length description of inputs whose numbers of vertices and hyperedges vary. Frequent sub-hypergraph patterns provide such a description, but previous work records only whether each pattern is present, which loses two kinds of information: how large the overlap between two hyperedges is, and how much of a hypergraph each pattern makes up. We extend frequent sub-hypergraph mining with overlap labels, the binned intersection size of two pattern hyperedges inherited from the target hypergraph, and represent each hypergraph by the relative frequency of each mined pattern. The vocabulary is mined from training hypergraphs only and then frozen, and validation and test hypergraphs are matched against it. A support rule that depends on pattern size keeps the vocabulary small. On the six hypergraph-classification datasets of DHG-Bench, with a single configuration chosen on validation scores, the method has a higher mean Macro-F1 than the best published mean on five datasets, by 18.67 points on Twitter-Friend and 10.02 points on Steam-Player; on RHG-3 it is 0.21 points lower. Ablations show that on Steam-Player and Twitter-Friend, replacing relative frequencies with presence lowers Macro-F1 by about 18 points, and that removing overlap labels lowers it by 4 to 9 points.
  </details>

- **[The Clustering Occupation Kernel (CLOCK) Method for Time Series Arising from Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33354)**  `arXiv:2610.33354`  `cs.LG`  
  _Michael L. Wells, Kamel Lahouel, Victor Rielly, Bruno Jedynak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce the Clustering Occupation Kernel (CLOCK) method, a novel representation-based framework for clustering time series arising from dynamical systems. CLOCK embeds observed sequences into a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) via a time-averaged occupation kernel, enabling trajectory comparison through their RKHS norm. For systems governed by differential equations, this formulation rigorously approximates the kernel mean embedding of the invariant Sinai-Ruelle-Bowen (SRB) measure, meaning our dissimilarity metric approximates the maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) between attractors. To accommodate general time series, we introduce time lags via Takens' Embedding Theorem to reconstruct the phase space. Crucially, CLOCK natively handles trajectories of unequal lengths and irregularly-spaced observations. We validate the proposed metric using spectral clustering, demonstrating superior performance against benchmark methods on both synthetic datasets and a real-world intracranial large-scale electroencephalogram (EEG) dataset. Finally, ablation studies on the Lorenz-96 system are consistent with the theoretical convergence rates of the occupation kernels and highlight the method's strong robustness to zero-mean Gaussian noise.
  </details>

- **[RadonBench: A Benchmark of Classical and Learning-Guided Search Techniques for Problems in Combinatorial Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33259)**  `arXiv:2610.33259`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoan Xu, Ethan X. Fang, Jesus A. De Loera, Junwei Lu, Luze Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classical optimization and learning-guided search offer complementary approaches to open problems in discrete mathematics, but their outputs are difficult to compare when search spaces, validity checks, and stopping rules differ. We present \emph{RadonBench}, a benchmark for constructing maximum-cardinality Radon-free lattice configurations: finite subsets of $\mathbb{Z}^d$ admitting no integral Radon partition. It remains open whether Radon-free configurations with $11$ points in $\mathbb{Z}^3$ or $21$ points in $\mathbb{Z}^4$ are possible. If they exist, they would improve the corresponding lower bounds on the integer Radon numbers. We compare exhaustive extension, greedy local search, PatternBoost, AlphaZero, and AlphaEvolve. All outputs use one success predicate: a convex target-sized configuration succeeds exactly when its number of integral Radon points is zero. PatternBoost and AlphaZero construct verified Radon-free configurations of sizes $10$ and $20$, one point short of the respective targets; AlphaZero's best convex target terminals have two integral Radon points in both dimensions. Exhaustive extension over fixed seeds and declared finite search boxes likewise reaches a minimum of two integral Radon points. Greedy local search and AlphaEvolve each produce target-sized near misses with one integral Radon point. No audited run reaches zero. These results establish reproducible near-miss baselines over explicitly scoped search domains.
  </details>

- **[Neyman Orthogonal Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33217)**  `arXiv:2610.33217`  `cs.LG`  
  _David Arbour, Erfan Jahanparast, Serina Chang, Avi Feller_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering is a popular approach for shifting LLM output toward a desired behavior, and increasingly for generating realistic synthetic data. The standard recipe estimates a steering direction by contrasting LLM activations on prompts with and without a target concept, such as truthfulness. In this paper, we separate the estimand (the steering direction these methods target) from the estimator used to recover it, two notions the literature often conflates. We frame nearly all existing methods as plug-in estimators of the average gradient of an outcome regression, with the binary contrastive label fixed as the outcome. Using this framing, we can immediately incorporate flexible modeling approaches and extend existing methods to allow for continuous outcomes such as log-probabilities or oracle ratings. Flexible ML models that fit the outcome well, however, might not estimate the gradient well, which motivates estimators that target the gradient directly. Drawing on the debiased machine learning literature, we develop a complementary estimator built on the Stein score, the Riesz representer of the gradient functional. Combining the two routes gives an orthogonalized estimator with classical double-robustness properties. We illustrate the framework on activation steering applied to a number of benchmark datasets.
  </details>

- **[Model-agnostic predictability quantification of multivariable time series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33209)**  `arXiv:2610.33209`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxuan Shu, Vasileios Lampos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multivariable time series forecasting plays an important role in various domains, where predictions depend on complex interactions among covariates. Forecasting models are commonly evaluated across numerous benchmark datasets. However, forecasting accuracy reflects both model capability and task predictability, making it difficult to characterise the difficulty of a task independently of the model used. Existing predictability measures often fail to capture nonlinear dependencies or are limited to univariate series. In this work, we propose Multivariable Predictability Score (MAPS), a model-agnostic metric for measuring the intrinsic predictability of multivariable time series forecasting tasks. MAPS uses covariate information to assess predictability across different lookback windows and forecasting horizons, and provides a quantitative assessment of the predictive information available for forecasting. We evaluate MAPS across synthetic and real-world forecasting datasets against 6 baseline predictability measures. The former confirms that our method closely aligns with the expected reference predictability (r=0.963). Empirical results further corroborate that MAPS yields more reliable projections in the majority of experimental configurations.
  </details>

- **[Training Mixed-Monotone Neural Networks for Verification Without Set Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30853)**  `arXiv:2610.30853`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jonas Fingerhut, Lukas Koller, Matthias Althoff_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most formal verification approaches for neural networks conservatively enclose the output set of the neural network. However, computing a tight output enclosure is in general computationally hard. We sidestep this challenge with a novel neural network architecture that realizes mixed-monotone functions as the difference of monotone subnetworks. By utilizing the theory on mixed-monotone systems, this reduces the computation of a tight output enclosure from complicated set propagation to two simple forward passes of opposite corners of an input interval. Moreover, because the size of the resulting enclosure is a closed-form function of the weights of the neural network, we can directly minimize it during training. We prove a lower bound on the size of any such enclosure, given by the total variation of the represented function generalized to several inputs, which makes it possible to compare the tightness of the enclosure against the optimum. Our evaluation on regression and dynamical-system benchmarks demonstrates that our training reduces the enclosure size to within 0.5% of the lower bound without sacrificing prediction accuracy. Computing our enclosure is orders of magnitude faster than CROWN and $\alpha$-CROWN and on deep networks it is also tighter than zonotope propagation and CROWN, while its size exceeds that of $\alpha$-CROWN by only 5.1%.
  </details>

- **[PerSPL: Personalized Semi-supervised Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29219)**  `arXiv:2610.29219`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoan Xu, Tingzhou Wei, Juncheng Dong, Korel Gundem, Ethan X. Fang, Zhengling Qi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized reward models require user-specific preference comparisons, but collecting such feedback is costly and provides only limited supervision for each user. At the same time, large pools of responses can be generated without user identities or preference labels, yet existing personalized reward models leave this data unused. Directly learning from these responses is difficult because pairwise objectives constrain relative scores within a prompt, whereas pseudo-labeling individual responses requires scores that are comparable across prompts for the same user. We propose \textbf{PerSPL} (Personalized Semi-supervised Preference Learning), a backbone-agnostic framework that uses pair-mean score centering to stabilize this cross-prompt score coordinate and iteratively turns unlabeled responses into user-specific training signals. Against seven representative baselines across four benchmarks---PersonalLLM, HelpSteer2, Reddit TLDR, and Chatbot Arena---PerSPL achieves the highest reported mean accuracy among the evaluated methods for both seen and unseen users. The two latter benchmarks provide real-user preference comparisons in summarization and open-domain chatbot interaction, respectively. These results indicate that unlabeled responses can provide additional supervision for personalized reward modeling when per-user preference labels are scarce.
  </details>

- **[Scalable Stress-Constrained Topology Optimization: A Simultaneous Approach Based on Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28703)**  `arXiv:2610.28703`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiangyu Sun, Shirin Hosseinmardi, Ramin Bostanabad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale topology optimization (TO) remains computationally demanding, especially for stress-constrained problems. To address this challenge, we propose the simultaneous neural topology optimization (SNTO) framework where state, adjoint, and design variables are represented with custom neural networks (NNs) whose parameters are jointly optimized within a single loop using adjoint analysis and automatic differentiation. The loss function combines the design objective, an aggregation of the local stress constraints, and the potential energy functionals. Our NNs have a unique architecture that accelerates convergence while capturing the sharp gradients and boundaries which are critical to stress-constrained TO. To scale to millions of degrees of freedom, we develop a hardware-aware training scheme in which the design domain is partitioned into subdomains distributed across multiple graphics processing units (GPUs). To control peak GPU memory, we further slice each subdomain into smaller regions whose loss gradients are evaluated sequentially and accumulated. Unlike domain decomposition, our partitioning naturally ensures field continuity across subdomains without additional loss terms. We first evaluate SNTO on two-dimensional (2D) stress-constrained benchmarks and compare the results to a conventional finite element-based approach, showing that SNTO produces designs with sharper boundaries and smaller gray areas. We then solve three-dimensional (3D) problems with up to eight million collocation points on eight GPUs. The training time decreases nearly in proportion to the number of GPUs and the peak GPU memory is reduced by increasing the number of sub-slices.Overall, SNTO offers an effective pathway to large-scale stress-constrained TO on limited computational resources.
  </details>

- **[Resample the Answer: Where to Spend Test-Time Compute in Recursive Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16427)**  `arXiv:2610.16427`  `cs.LG`  
  _Maxime Illouz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive reasoning models such as the Tiny Recursive Model (TRM) iteratively refine two latent states: an answer state y, decoded into the output, and a reasoning state z, which is never decoded. Many of their failures have been attributed to bad basins, regions of latent space that trap the recursion on wrong answers. Recent test-time approaches perturb y, z or both to recover these failures, but the impact of this choice on performance, test-time compute efficiency and scaling properties remains unclear. Through controlled experiments on TRM, we find that a failed trajectory carries its failure in its answer state. At equal compute, whether retries continue a failed trajectory (depth scaling) or draw multiple trajectories from the initial states (width scaling), resampling y recovers more failures than any perturbation of z we tested, and the performance gap widens as the test-time compute budget grows. Our approach, Gated Answer Resampling (GAR), pairs this resampling of the answer state with the model's own halting head and restarts only the puzzles that the head rejects. With at most 64 restarts per puzzle and no training or hyperparameter search, GAR takes TRM from 85.9% to 99.4% on Sudoku-Extreme and from 68.8% to 85.6% on Pencil Puzzle Bench validation. On the same checkpoints, GAR also outperforms Probabilistic TRM (PTRM), which perturbs z at each step, even when PTRM uses 42 to 197 times as much compute. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DisCoMBO: Steering Expert-in-the-Loop Black-Box Optimization via Distributional Conformance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36472)**  `arXiv:2609.36472`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jonas Seng, Bennet Wittelsbach, Kristian Kersting_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sequential Model-Based Optimization (SMBO) traditionally relies on Bayesian or ensembling surrogates for uncertainty quantification. While historically treated as fully data-driven, SMBO increasingly integrates external domain expertise to accelerate discovery. To overcome the opaque guidance and diminished integration fidelity of standard acquisition re-weighting, Probabilistic Circuits (PCs) have emerged as a generative surrogate alternative, enabling direct knowledge injection via conditional sampling. However, these generative routines lack the formal exploration-exploitation semantics required for rigorous optimization. We introduce the Distributional Conformance Score (DisCo), a novel metric that unifies the flexibility and efficiency of PCs with a formal uncertainty framework. DisCo provides a bounded, $[0, 1]$-normalized measure of model "surprise" that (1) recovers properties comparable to kernel-based uncertainty known from, e.g., Gaussian Processes, while maintaining linear-time inference, and (2) enables accurate assessment of conformance of external knowledge w.r.t. model evidence. We then present DisCoMBO, a framework leveraging these properties for robust, knowledge-aware optimization. We prove that DisCoMBO is a zero-regret algorithm and demonstrate its effectiveness across diverse benchmarks from AutoML, material optimization, and wind park optimization.
  </details>

- **[Conditional Validity for Adaptive Modality Acquisition: When the Policy Chooses Its Own Calibration Group](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15520)**  `arXiv:2608.15520`  `cs.LG`  
  _Melika Baghi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A multimodal system may begin inference while holding only some of its inputs and may acquire the rest at a cost. With adaptive acquisition, the policy determines which inputs are ultimately observed, so we state risk control conditional on that terminal input pattern. Conditional calibration typically assumes that the grouping map is fixed independently of the calibration sample, a condition that policy-induced grouping does not satisfy. We characterize when pattern-conditional risk control remains valid and give two finite-sample constructions: threshold-free routing with calibration applied at the terminal pattern, and simultaneous validation of complete policy-pattern pairs, which lets calibration data select the deployed policy. A counterexample shows that validity proved for a calibration-independent grouping map need not transfer once the policy makes the terminal group calibration-dependent. We call the resulting method RouteCert. On a clinical electrocardiogram task with a staged, cost-ordered lead protocol, the deployed policy answers 71.2% of held-out patients, with an observed 7.4% disagreement with the cardiologist's diagnosis at 48.8% of the prespecified ordinal cost of acquiring each stage, and all three acquisition stages are validated separately. On masked multimodal benchmarks, validating pointwise at each terminal pattern holds observed worst-pattern selective risk, measured against the full-information reference decision rather than the true label, at 0.034, where a pooled design reaches 0.145 against a 0.10 cap, at a comparable answered fraction (0.350 vs 0.342); under the budget-matched simultaneous comparison, the answered fraction falls to 0.305.
  </details>

- **[MARLIN: De Novo Molecular Structure Elucidation from Tandem Mass Spectra without a Ground-Truth Formula](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04774)**  `arXiv:2607.04774`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xujun Che, Xiuxia Du, Depeng Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Untargeted tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS) detects thousands of small molecules per biological sample, yet most go unidentified because they are absent from spectral libraries. These uncharacterized metabolites and natural products are precisely the compounds that matter for drug discovery, biomarker research, and exposomics. Computational de novo structure elucidation could close this gap, but almost all state-of-the-art methods assume the ground-truth molecular formula is known, an oracle that does not exist for genuinely novel compounds, whose formula must itself be predicted with substantial error. We present MARLIN, a de novo method that elucidates structures directly from a spectrum with no molecular formula at any stage. A self-supervised encoder predicts a molecular fingerprint from the raw peaks, and a block-diffusion language model generates candidate structures conditioned only on the fingerprint and the instrument-measured precursor mass. A mass-shell constraint, whose heavy-atom prune provably never excludes the true molecule, steers generation toward the measured mass without fixing the atom inventory, and candidates are accepted by exact parts-per-million mass agreement. A symmetric noise objective absorbs encoder error, and a candidate-diversity mechanism keeps the candidates from collapsing to a single structure. On the NPLIB1 benchmark, MARLIN reaches 16.9% Top-1 exact-match accuracy, higher than the formula-given baselines, and returns the closest candidate structures by structural distance and fingerprint similarity. It recovers the correct molecular formula as a byproduct about as often as a dedicated predictor without ever using one. MARLIN brings de novo structure elucidation to the discovery regime where the molecular formula is unavailable.
  </details>

- **[Gating and Learnable Temperature for Graph Attention Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29803)**  `arXiv:2605.29803`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhongtian Ma, Hao Wu, Yexin Zhang, Qiaosheng Zhang, Chao Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph attention networks learn neighbor importance through data-dependent coefficients, but standard layers lack explicit control over unreliable feature dimensions and use fixed sharpness of attention coefficient distributions. This paper proposes gated graph attention and learnable temperature for common graph attention mechanisms. Gated graph attention filters feature or message responses to reduce the influence of unreliable dimensions, while learnable temperature dynamically adjusts the sharpness of the attention coefficient distribution. We conduct experiments on five standard node classification benchmarks and five heterophilic benchmarks. On most of these benchmarks, our two modifications improve upon the corresponding graph attention backbones, and combining them can yield further gains. Controlled noise experiments further verify the behavior of the proposed mechanisms under feature perturbations. Theoretical analysis explains these results by showing that gating improves robustness when only part of the feature coordinates are reliable, while temperature is beneficial when global noise weakens the discriminability of node features.
  </details>

- **[Bastion: Budget-Aware Speculative Decoding with Tree-structured Block Diffusion Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29727)**  `arXiv:2605.29727`  `cs.LG`  
  _Soowon Oh, Nam Cao, Yujin Kim, Hojung Jung, Huzama Ahmad, Sangmin Bae, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Block-diffusion drafters have recently shown strong potential for speculative decoding by predicting multiple future-token distributions in a single forward pass. To exploit these parallel predictions, tree-based verification can check multiple plausible continuations instead of committing to a single greedy draft path, often increasing the number of accepted tokens per decoding cycle. However, existing tree-based approaches typically rely on fixed tree topologies or static verification budgets, even though the best budget depends on the local draft distribution, context length, target model, and hardware runtime. We propose a system-aware adaptive tree framework for block-diffusion speculative decoding. At each decoding cycle, our method builds a tree toward high-probability draft paths and selects the verification budget that maximizes an online speedup estimate. This estimate combines a drafter-based acceptance surrogate with an analytical verifier-latency model calibrated from observed runtimes. The framework requires no additional training of the drafter or target model and preserves the target model's decoding rule. Across target models, benchmarks, decoding temperatures, and GPU platforms, our method achieves up to a $6.93\times$ wall-clock speedup over standard autoregressive decoding. This corresponds to approximately 40% higher speedup than Dflash, the strongest existing block-diffusion drafter, and is obtained without per-setting budget tuning.
  </details>

- **[Markovian Circuit Tracing: Ground-Truth Tests of Transformer State Abstractions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20824)**  `arXiv:2605.20824`  `cs.LG`  
  _Abdullah X_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mechanistic interpretability needs tests that separate accessible information from a causally useful internal abstraction. We introduce Markovian Circuit Tracing (MCT), a controlled benchmark for testing whether transformer activations support a compact state abstraction with recoverable transition structure. We train small causal transformers on sequences from four-state hidden Markov models. The experiment fixes the latent transition law and varies emission observability. The canonical artifact contains 15 runs across five fixed seeds. Every model reaches validation loss within 0.02 nats per token of the exact Bayes predictor. Under easy observability, held-out mean rowwise Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence from the true transition matrix is 0.114. The easy shuffled control gives 0.379. Under medium observability, recovered transition KL is 0.221. The medium shuffled control gives 0.355. Recovery collapses to control performance under hard observability. Natural activation swaps and state forcing show the same decline in causal specificity. New transition-specific controls refine this result. Untrained activations yield similar transition recovery in the easy regime. In medium, trained activations improve over the untrained control by 0.049 KL on average. Probabilistic Markov-order tests show a clear first-order gain in easy. Medium shows a smaller gain. Hard shows no meaningful gain. A second latent transition law reproduces the same observability-dependent pattern. The belief-probe control fails to support learned specificity. The fixed sparse autoencoder fails to improve transition recovery. MCT therefore tests when a state-machine interpretation earns support and when the evidence rejects it.
  </details>

- **[Vib2ECG: A Co-Located Chest-Lead SCG-ECG Dataset and Benchmark for ECG Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15539)**  `arXiv:2603.15539`  `cs.LG`  
  _Guorui Lu, Xiaohui Cai, Todor Stefanov, Qinyu Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Twelve-lead electrocardiography (ECG) is essential for cardiovascular diagnosis, but its long-term acquisition in daily life is constrained by complex and costly hardware. Recent efforts have explored reconstructing ECG from low-cost cardiac vibrational signals such as seismocardiography (SCG), with prior studies primarily focusing on limb leads, while clinical diagnosis requires multi-lead ECG, including chest leads.
