In this work, we propose Vib2ECG, a dataset of synchronized, co-located ECG and chest-wall vibration recordings, which includes complete twelve-lead ECGs and vibrational signals acquired by inertial measurement units (IMUs) at the six standard precordial electrode sites (V1-V6) from 17 subjects. Based on this dataset, we also provide a benchmark. Experimental results demonstrate the feasibility of reconstructing electrical cardiac signals at variable locations from vibrational signals using a lightweight 364K-parameter U-Net. Furthermore, we observe a hallucination phenomenon in the model, where ECG waveforms are generated in regions where no corresponding electrical activity is present. We analyze the causes of this phenomenon and propose potential directions for mitigation. This study demonstrates the feasibility of mobile-device-friendly ECG monitoring through chest-lead ECG prediction from low-cost vibrational signals acquired using IMU sensors. It expands the application of cardiac vibrational signals and provides new insights into the spatial relationship between cardiac electrical and mechanical activities with spatial location variation.
  </details>

- **[Models Designed to Forget: Machine Unlearning via Key Deletion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15033)**  `arXiv:2603.15033`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sonia Laguna, Jorge da Silva Goncalves, Moritz Vandenhirtz, Alain Ryser, Irene Cannistraci, Julia E. Vogt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning for vision models is rapidly becoming a practical requirement, driven by privacy regulations, data errors, and the need to remove harmful or corrupted training images. Despite this, most existing approximate unlearning methods tackle the problem from a post-hoc perspective. They attempt to erase the influence of targeted samples through parameter updates that typically require access to the full training data. This creates a mismatch with real deployment scenarios where unlearning requests can be anticipated, revealing a fundamental limitation of post-hoc approaches. We motivate unlearning by design, a novel paradigm for approximate methods in which models are directly trained to support forgetting as an inherent architectural capability. We instantiate this idea with Machine UNlearning via KEY deletion (MUNKEY), a memory-augmented transformer that decouples instance-specific memorization from model weights. Here, unlearning corresponds to removing the instance-identifying key, enabling zero-shot forgetting without weight updates or access to the original samples or labels. Across natural image benchmarks, fine-grained visual recognition, and medical datasets, MUNKEY outperforms all post-hoc baselines. Our results establish that unlearning by design enables fast, deployment-oriented unlearning while preserving predictive performance.
  </details>

- **[Matcha: Multi-Stage Riemannian Flow Matching for Accurate and Physically Valid Molecular Docking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14586)**  `arXiv:2510.14586`  `cs.LG`  
  _Daria Frolova, Talgat Daulbaev, Egor Sevriugov, Sergei A. Nikolenko, Dmitry N. Ivankov, Ivan Oseledets, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting a protein--ligand binding pose is central to structure-based drug design, yet current docking methods still struggle to combine speed, accuracy and physical plausibility. We introduce Matcha, which refines a ligand pose by three flow-matching stages in translation, rotation and torsion space, then minimizes, filters and ranks candidates with GNINA. Across the benchmarks considered, Matcha attains the strongest physical plausibility and runs about $28\times$ faster than modern large-scale co-folding models.Code and benchmarking artifacts are available atthis https URLand archived atthis https URL. Model checkpoints are archived atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Robust Single-Parameter Point Cloud Registration using Truncated Lp Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34271)**  `arXiv:2610.34271`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Seong Hun Lee, Patrick Vandewalle, Javier Civera_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a novel method for robust point cloud registration with only one tunable parameter. Our method, dubbed TLP, uses truncated $L_p$ functions with different values of $p$ to assign priority scores to correspondences, select the hypothesis, and refine the final model. By leveraging the robustness of truncated functions, TLP effectively handles the high outlier rates commonly encountered in real-world 3D feature matching. We also exploit GPU parallelization to evaluate hypotheses efficiently, enabling registration from thousands of correspondences in under a second on average. With only one tunable parameter, our method offers greater practicality than multi-parameter methods that require dataset-specific tuning of several parameters. Evaluations on public benchmark datasets demonstrate that TLP achieves higher accuracy than state-of-the-art single-parameter methods. Furthermore, it outperforms two widely used multi-parameter methods on three of the four evaluated datasets while maintaining competitive runtime.
  </details>

- **[Gaussian Episodic Memory for Long-Term Spatiotemporal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33158)**  `arXiv:2610.33158`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Ling-Chia Chen, Abdelrhman Werby, Kaan Aytekin, Kai O. Arras_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world environments undergo continual semi-static changes, requiring a memory representation that captures both the spatial and temporal evolution of a scene. Existing spatio-temporal memories for robots typically rely either on structured representations, which are limited to the entities and attributes captured during construction, or on image-based memories, which are constrained to previously observed viewpoints. We introduce GEMS, a framework that combines a persistent spatio-temporal 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) map with an agentic reasoning pipeline. The 3DGS map is reconstructed once and subsequently updated only in regions where the scene changes, storing only these modifications while preserving visual detail across time. The agentic pipeline leverages this map to render novel views on demand, iteratively accumulating evidence to answer spatio-temporal questions and ground objects from natural-language queries. To advance future research, we introduce GEMS-Bench, a spatiotemporal question-answering benchmark focused on semi-static environments. It comprises 3 real-world and 12 synthetic environments, spanning 473 question-answer pairs. We evaluate GEMS on novel-view synthesis quality across scene updates, 3D object grounding, spatiotemporal question answering, and storage efficiency. GEMS outperforms recent baselines in 3D object grounding and spatiotemporal question answering, while achieving rendering quality on par with dedicated scene-update methods. Code and data:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Video Prediction Policy 2: Predict Better, Act Better](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10270)**  `arXiv:2610.10270`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Yanjiang Guo, Haodong Yan, Zhide Zhong, Zhongru Zhang, Qingyuan Yang, Qingzhou Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World action models (WAMs) have emerged as an important class of generalist robot policies, aiming to transfer video prediction priors to action learning. However, we find that existing WAMs frequently produce incorrect motion predictions in open-ended environment, leading to erroneous actions. We attribute this limitation to two factors: (1) base video models are not optimized for manipulation, and (2) naively incorporating action components into video models can substantially degrade their generalization capabilities. We introduce Video Prediction Policy 2 (VPP2), a WAM that enables strong zero-shot generalization in both video prediction and action generation. First, we curate a large-scale, diverse dataset of manipulation videos to continue pretraining the base video foundation model. We annotate video clips with detailed captions and perform \textit{event-level} video pretraining to promote generalization across open-ended manipulation tasks. Second, we post-train and distill the video model into a single-step visual planner with fixed prediction horizon. Finally, we introduce action module via a mixture-of-transformers (MoT) architecture to learn implicit inverse dynamics model. Experiments demonstrate three key results: (1) VPP2-14B outperforms Cosmos3-64B by 11.0\% points in video prediction instruction-following success rate on open-ended tasks; (2) VPP2 surpasses the strongest baseline by 18.5\% points in success rate on real-world zero-shot ALOHA manipulation tasks; and (3) following benchmark-specific post-training, VPP2 achieves the highest success rates among evaluated methods on the challenging LIBERO-Pro, LIBERO-OOD, and RoboDojo benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Geometry-Aware Epipolar Correspondence Graphs for Monocular Visual Odometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04554)**  `arXiv:2604.04554`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Prateeth Rao, Sachit Rao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We demonstrate Graph Representation Learning to address challenges that arise in visual odometry. In this context, the change in pose of a camera is typically estimated using matched correspondences in frames that satisfy the epipolar constraint; finding this unique set under different situations is not a simple task. Employing an existing detector-free model to find matching correspondences in a pair of images, a correspondence graph is constructed whose topology is guided by the epipolar constraint. Starting with a graph where every node is connected to $k$ closest neighbours (by distance), this graph is pruned based on an initial estimate of the Essential Matrix; this feature is in stark contrast with traditional graph formulations where graphs from local spatial neighborhoods defined by heuristics are constructed. The graph learning module is then trained to generate an aggregated representative vector that summarises the geometric context of the graph. This vector forms an input to a Multi-layer Perceptron (MLP), whose outputs are standard rotation and translation elements that define the change in pose. To investigate the effect of graph learning on this representation, four variations of Graph Neural Networks (GNNs): GCN, GAT, GIN, and Graph-based Cross Attention are evaluated under a unified learning framework. The application to monocular visual odometry is validated using outdoor benchmark datasets such as KiTTi, ETH3D, King's College, and Tartan Air. Experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements in trajectory and relative pose estimation across diverse metrics when compared with classical consensus-based estimators and learning-guided correspondence methods.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Densification in Gaussian SLAM: A Stationary Sampling Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.00705)**  `arXiv:2601.00705`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Wei-Tse Cheng, Yen-Jen Chiou, Yuan-Fu Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gaussian Splatting enables high-fidelity differentiable SLAM by representing scenes as anisotropic Gaussian primitives. Most systems rely on residual-driven densification, which couples refinement with representation growth: accuracy saturates at a moderate primitive count, yet memory grows throughout the trajectory, degrading throughput. We introduce TopoGS-SLAM, a stationary-sampling formulation that decouples refinement from capacity through coverage-triggered Gaussian initialization and bounded-topology optimization, turning mapping into a bounded-dimensional problem with stable scaling. On standard indoor benchmarks, TopoGS-SLAM matches or surpasses densification-based pipelines in localization and reconstruction accuracy while substantially improving memory scalability and throughput, showing that iterative topology mutation is not required for accurate Gaussian SLAM in bounded-area static indoor scenes with sufficient multi-view coverage. Additional details and resources are available at this URL:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Does VGGT Know What It Does Not Know?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35806)**  `arXiv:2610.35806`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Adityan Thirumani Srinivasan, David Nordström_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feed-forward 3D reconstruction models such as VGGT regress cameras and geometry in a single forward pass, but they report no uncertainty for the camera poses they predict: an incorrect relative pose looks no different from a correct one. We ask whether the frozen model nonetheless knows when its pose is wrong. On HardMatch, a benchmark of 1,000 deliberately difficult image pairs on which VGGT fails on 35% of pairs, a linear probe on VGGT's frozen features predicts pose failure on held-out categories with ROC-AUC 93.5%, compared with 79.7% for VGGT's own point-map confidence and 77.7% for a classifier trained on all of VGGT's outputs. The probe catches failures VGGT is confident about, lets a downstream system abstain on the pairs it distrusts, and transfers without retraining to rotation failures on PhotoTourism and ScanNet-1500, beyond what co-visibility explains. This knowledge is absent from the per-image encoder: it is computed partway through the layers that fuse the two views, and the same holds for a second model, $\pi^3$. In essence, we find that VGGT knows more about its own failures than it reports.
  </details>

- **[Ampe-DB: A Dataset for Human Activity and Interaction Analysis Using the Ghanaian Game Ampe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33130)**  `arXiv:2610.33130`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ruth Senior Biney, Isaac Kojo Yedonu Aboah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human activity recognition benchmarks underrepresent culturally situated forms of movement and interaction. We introduce Ampe-DB, a multimodal dataset based on Ampe, a traditional Ghanaian game involving coordinated jumping, leg movements, and two-person interaction. It contains 637 canonical clips from 15 participants, with RGB video, two-person pose representations, participant and pair metadata, four-class movement labels, and derived binary movement-relation labels.We evaluate four-class movement recognition and binary movement-relation classification using three protocols: a duplicate-safe fixed benchmark, four-fold pair-held-out evaluation, and participant-held-out diagnostics for participants with complete class coverage. Baselines comprise a frozen Kinetics-400-pretrained R(2+1)D-18 RGB encoder, a two-layer bidirectional GRU for pose sequences, and validation-selected RGB-pose late fusion.For four-class recognition, RGB macro-F1 falls from 0.955 on the fixed benchmark to 0.418 under pair-held-out evaluation and 0.384 under participant-held-out evaluation. Pose is more robust, achieving 0.957, 0.811, and 0.839 across the same protocols. Fusion reaches 0.976 on the fixed benchmark and 0.824 under pair-held-out evaluation, but does not consistently improve on pose under participant shift. Binary movement-relation recognition also becomes substantially harder under held-out evaluation, especially for different-leg configurations.These findings show that strong fixed-benchmark scores can mask failures to generalize across people and pairings. Ampe-DB supports research on fine-grained movement recognition, human-human interaction, multimodal learning, and identity-aware generalization.
  </details>

- **[OvisOCR3 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33066)**  `arXiv:2610.33066`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Yu Xia, Yinglun Li, Yuhui Chen, Jinlong Huang, Yuyang Wanyan, Shengze Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce OvisOCR3, a 0.8B model for a broad range of OCR-related tasks. OvisOCR3 unifies document parsing, general OCR, key information extraction (KIE), document figure captioning, and cross-page table reconstruction in a single model. We train the model on real and synthetic data through continued pretraining and use domain-balanced mid-training to balance supervision across tasks with different data volumes and response lengths. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation transfers expertise from five domain-specific 4B teachers into the shared model, followed by mixed-task reinforcement learning with domain-specific rewards. We compare OvisOCR3 with general-purpose vision-language models and specialized OCR models on public and in-house benchmarks covering all five task families. In these comparisons, it achieves an overall score of 96.74 on OmniDocBench v1.6 and the highest overall scores on PureDocBench, Real5-OmniDocBench, and MDPBench. On OCRBench v2, it ranks first, scoring 65.9 and 61.3 on the English and Chinese splits, respectively. It also outperforms the baselines on OCRBench and all four KIE benchmarks. On DocFigCapBench, it achieves the highest localization F1 and a caption score close to that of Qwen3.8-Max. For cross-page tables, it leads in Relation F1 on CrossTabBench and truncated-table TEDS on MPDocBench-Parse. These results demonstrate that a compact 0.8B model can achieve strong performance across diverse OCR-related tasks. OvisOCR3 is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[WAVE: Wavelet-Controlled Coarse-to-Fine Guidance for Depth Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25302)**  `arXiv:2608.25302`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Tayyab Nasir, Daochang Liu, Ajmal Mian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Guided depth super-resolution (GDSR) typically extracts RGB guidance features through convolutional hierarchies, inheriting their fine-to-coarse bias. Thus, low-level spatial cues surface in early layers, leaving the deeper layers to suppress those that do not correspond to true depth boundaries, which risks artifacts and blurred edges. The same fine-to-coarse bias persists in semantics-based methods that consume low-level tokens early and global tokens late. We present WAVE, which introduces a multi-level discrete wavelet transform (ML-DWT) as an explicit and interpretable feature-control mechanism, enabling a coarse-to-fine reconstruction by consuming sub-bands and semantic tokens in reverse of their generation order. WAVE further exploits these sub-bands to treat high- and low-frequency content separately, isolating the misleading RGB color and texture cues before any feature is learned so that their admission can be controlled explicitly, offering an intuitive alternative to the suppression learned implicitly by an opaque network. WAVE separates structure and detail reconstruction into dedicated modules that: i) model interactions within and across wavelet sub-bands, depth features, and semantic priors, ii) apply semantic gating to the high-frequency bands, and iii) fuse modalities through an invertible coupling mechanism that prevents collapse onto a single modality. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that WAVE matches or outperforms existing methods, with the largest gains at high upsampling factors, where low-resolution depth contains the least structure.
  </details>

- **[FMC-DETR: Frequency-Decoupled Multi-Domain Coordination for Aerial-View Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23056)**  `arXiv:2509.23056`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ben Liang, Hongguang Wei, Yuan Liu, Bingwen Qiu, Yihong Wang, Xiubao Sui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Remote sensing object detection is a critical technology for real-world applications such as natural resource monitoring, traffic management, and UAV-based rescue. Detecting tiny objects in high-resolution aerial imagery remains challenging due to weak visual cues and insufficient global context modeling in complex scenes. Existing methods often suffer from delayed contextual interaction and limited nonlinear reasoning, which restrict their ability to effectively refine shallow representations and ultimately lead to suboptimal performance. To address these challenges, we propose FMC-DETR, a frequency-decoupled fusion framework for aerial-view object detection. First, we propose the Wavelet Kolmogorov-Arnold Transformer (WeKat) backbone, which employs cascaded wavelet transforms to enhance global low-frequency structure perception in shallow features while preserving fine-grained details, and further leverages Kolmogorov-Arnold networks for adaptive nonlinear modeling of multi-scale dependencies. Second, we introduce the Multi-Domain Feature Coordination (MDFC) module, which refines cross-scale fused representations through partial-channel spatial, spectral, and structural coordination, thereby strengthening small-object-related feature responses in cluttered scenes. Finally, we design the Compact Partial Fusion (CPF) module, which performs compact multi-branch aggregation with progressive partial refinement to improve feature diversity and multi-scale interaction while preserving stable information flow and reducing redundant perturbation. Extensive experiments across multiple remote sensing benchmarks demonstrate that FMC-DETR achieves state-of-the-art performance and significantly outperforming the baseline detector. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Improving VLMs on Scientific Chart Analysis via Autonomous Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35077)**  `arXiv:2610.35077`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Abhinav Sudhakar Dubey, Vidushi Goel, Kiran Rana, Shikhar Mathur, Vaggos Chatziafratis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding and operating with scientific charts is a standard approach in data analysis, and while humans are good at interpreting such charts, open vision-language models (VLMs) are known to perform poorly when it comes to question answering based on such data types. Motivated by an initial observation that many of their errors are due to inexact answering, we raise the following natural question: how much accuracy can we recover without changing the model at all? In this paper, we study this question by searching over inference programs and the code around a "frozen" version of the model. Using ideas from autonomous evolution (famously used in AlphaEvolve), we first start from a baseline inference program, and in each round we show an LLM judge the code together with a few failures on training questions, and ask for an improving evolutionary move. As usual in autonomous evolution approaches, the candidate programs are kept in a small pool ranked by training accuracy, and the next edit is performed on top-scorers from this pool. We run this approach on three VLMs and four popular benchmarks on scientific charts, and we measure the fraction of descriptive questions whose answer exactly matches the ground truth. The accuracy improves in all experimental settings. On average, it rises from 16.6% with the starting program to 41.3% with the evolved one, and the largest single increase is 55.2 percentage points. As a side benefit, our evolved program is 3.8x faster than the starting program, because short answers are easier to grade and faster to generate. Our results demonstrate that a large part of the observed errors arise due to the inference pipeline, and that optimizing it can improve the "frozen" VLM without the need to retrain it.
  </details>

- **[Grounding on Request: Learning Modality Control in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33841)**  `arXiv:2610.33841`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Maxime Dassen, Alexander Martin, Andrew Yates, Reno Kriz, William Walden_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When an image and its caption disagree, can a user reliably control which one a vision-language model (VLM) follows? Accuracy alone cannot answer this question, since a model might give the correct answer from parametric knowledge without following the requested evidence. We therefore evaluate modality grounding control through conflict-preserving counterfactual interventions. Specifically, Flip measures whether an initially correct answer follows an edit to the requested modality, while Stay measures whether it remains stable when the competing modality is edited. To learn this control, we build on neologism learning by introducing new words for visual and textual grounding, training only their embeddings while keeping the pretrained model fixed. We also introduce Counterfactual Twin-Margin (CTM) supervision, which uses paired interventions to teach how answer probabilities should change when the requested evidence changes. Across three VLMs on three existing benchmarks and our multi-attribute Ground-CF benchmark, we find that learned modality words improve control over natural-language instructions. Surprisingly, a single learned embedding per modality provides strong lightweight visual control and shows little average degradation on held-out values. LoRA remains strongest overall, although its visual advantage narrows under held-out evaluation. Moreover, counterfactual supervision improves control in several settings where standard supervision is weaker. Finally, we test our method on real-world news video and find that visual control improves when the relevant evidence is made easier to access. Overall, our results show that reliable modality control requires testing not only whether an answer is correct, but whether it continues to follow edits to the modality of choice.
  </details>

- **[MIS-VQA: An Annotation-Driven Surgical Vision-Language Dataset for Complex Surgical Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34210)**  `arXiv:2610.34210`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Mohamed Hamdy, Iheb Zouari, Fatmaelzahraa Ahmed, Muraam Abdel-Ghani, Muhammed Arsalan, Ponnuthurai Suganthan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern surgical AI increasingly requires multimodal understanding rather than isolated perception, yet existing datasets remain largely limited to single-task supervision such as recognition or templated question answering. Current medical VQA resources often rely on synthetic templates, lack natural descriptive supervision, or provide sparse annotations that hinder reasoning about spatial relations and procedural context. We introduce MIS-VQA, a surgical vision-language dataset generated through an annotation-driven pipeline that converts heterogeneous surgical annotations into structured textual scene representations. Using EndoVis18 and CholecT50, we transform phase, instrument, interaction, triplet, and segmentation labels into unified contexts that guide large language models to generate clinically coherent question-answer pairs. MIS-VQA supports four response types-concise and detailed descriptions, perception-level grounding, and multi-step reasoning-enabling hierarchical evaluation from atomic visual grounding to compositional reasoning. To address annotation sparsity, we incorporate confidence-filtered segmentation and cross-annotation consistency checks, enabling spatially grounded supervision at scale. MIS-VQA establishes a unified benchmark for surgical vision-language understanding with spatial supervision and multi-tier reasoning evaluation. To demonstrate its utility, we introduce LLaVA-SurgXpert, a family of surgical vision-language models with visual encoders to analyze how visual representations influence multimodal reasoning. Experiments show consistent improvements over existing surgical VQA baselines while revealing capability gaps between perceptual grounding and higher-level reasoning hidden in prior benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Humans in Space: Can MLLMs Construct Human-Centric Spatial Understanding via Active Exocentric Exploration?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34149)**  `arXiv:2610.34149`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Tianchen Guo, Tengyue Zhang, Chen Liu, Ling Chen, Xin Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) excel at zero-shot reasoning, their spatial capabilities are primarily evaluated through static, passive observation. In complex, human-centric scenes, occlusion often hides crucial geometric evidence, and passive benchmarks force models to guess rather than recognize their own visual uncertainty. To bridge this gap, we introduce SATE-Bench, a novel interactive benchmark comprising 316 real-world scenes and 2,525 multi-turn QA pairs to evaluate visual self-awareness and active exocentric view-exploration. It combines a Passive multi-view control with an Active exploration setting, where models start from a severely occluded view and must iteratively query adjacent viewpoints until they gather enough evidence to fully ``sate'' the question, scored by a step-penalized efficiency reward. Evaluating state-of-the-art MLLMs reveals that they terminate exploration prematurely while reporting high confidence, and struggle with fine-grained 3D geometric tasks; matched-view controls further attribute the Active--Passive gap primarily to insufficient evidence acquisition. Moreover, fine-tuning with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) shows that rewarding view-seeking increases active exploration but lowers overall accuracy, as models over-explore and fail to fuse expanding multi-view contexts across turns. These findings indicate that scaling passive multi-view perception does not intrinsically yield active spatial reasoning, highlighting the need for models that know where to look, how to integrate what they see, and when to stop. Code and data are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Whispers from the Neighbors: Rethinking Caption Consensus as Inference-Time Evidence for Vision-Language Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33798)**  `arXiv:2610.33798`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Khang Nhat Hoang Vo, Belal Shoer, Abhirama Subramanyam Penamakuri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An image is rarely described only once. Retrieval benchmarks and many collections attach several captions to each picture, yet dual encoders still judge every image-caption pair in isolation. We ask what these neighboring captions contribute when a frozen encoder consults them at inference time. Fusing image similarity with similarity to an image's own captions improves text-to-image R@1 by 3.4 to 16.8 points across nine frozen encoders when the captions come from the same annotators as the queries. The gain falls to 1.9 to 7.2 points when an off-the-shelf captioner writes them, and to at most one point when they come from a different annotation source; a paraphrase-trained text encoder strengthens the text side further. A natural refinement, scoring the part of the image that the captions leave unexplained, appears to add much more under a leave-one-out evaluation. We show that this advantage arises because the correct candidate's supports are built differently from those of the negative candidates, with set size and query removal each contributing about half, and that under a fixed support index the residual reduces to a reweighting of what the captions already provide. On compositional benchmarks, the captions help by matching candidates to human descriptions of the image, without improving image-text grounding. The code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[PathKGen: Pathology Knowledge Assisted MLLM for Explainable Histopathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33691)**  `arXiv:2610.33691`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Nadun Rajapaksha, Sandun Herath, Devnith Wijesinghe, Isiri Amani Withanawasam, Hasindri Watawana, Ranga Rodrigo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whole Slide Image (WSI) analysis serves as the fundamental method for the clinical diagnosis of cancer in digital histopathology. Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have enabled state-of-the-art (SOTA) automated histopathology report generation. However, MLLMs reason purely through distributions encoded in their neural parameters, limiting the amount of knowledge and reasoning logic they can contain given the model scale. Their black-box reasoning poses another challenge to the human interpretation of intermediate reasoning. We address these issues by utilizing knowledge graphs to inject external structured knowledge for MLLMs to reason over, while making this reasoning component visible. Knowledge graphs are capable of partially externalizing the containment of medical knowledge, an area that remains underexplored in pathology report generation. We introduce PathKGen, a knowledge graph-assisted MLLM for histopathology report generation. PathKGen has two main branches: (1) a visual path that learns visual token inputs to the MLLM using pathology foundation models, and (2) a knowledge path that learns knowledge token inputs by extracting key morphological features of WSIs through querying a pathology-specific knowledge graph. A comprehensive evaluation on WSI-Bench dataset shows that PathKGen surpasses previous SOTA methods while revealing diagnostic reasoning as visually intuitive graphs. Due to its modular design, the proposed knowledge path is complementary to vision-aligned MLLMs and can be extended to other applications by curating domain-specific knowledge graphs to generate knowledge tokens.
  </details>

- **[PreView: Enhancing GUI Grounding with Preprocessed Views and Cues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33645)**  `arXiv:2610.33645`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Wenjun Hou, Kaishuai Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI grounding requires locating small controls in cluttered screenshots and identifying which control a natural-language instruction refers to. Cropping and enlargement can expose local detail, but the resulting image may still leave ambiguity about the intended target. We propose PreView, an auxiliary preprocessor that improves the inputs to an existing grounding model without further training that model. PreView prepares two complementary types of input features: task-relevant views for localization and candidate-specific cues for target understanding. A trained lightweight auxiliary model predicts and refines a center, crops the screenshot into a local view, and enlarges that view for zoomed-in grounding. It also prepares a visual cue (i.e., a red-circle marker with a location hint) and a textual cue describing the candidate's identity or function. The frozen grounding model uses the prepared inputs to predict the final click. Across five benchmarks, PreView improves the average grounding accuracy of Qwen3-VL-2B, OS-Atlas-4B, and Qwen3-VL-4B without further training these grounding models. Extensive analyses examine the contributions of views and cues, center refinement, and visual input budgets.
  </details>

- **[Of Chains and Gains: What Reinforcement Learning changes on Reasoning-based Image Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33203)**  `arXiv:2610.33203`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Yixuan Li, Yuxuan Jiang, Hanwei Zhu, Hancheng Zhu, Fan Zhang, Shiqi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has improved the performance of large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) on image quality assessment (IQA). Existing approaches often attribute these gains to improved reasoning before scoring, supported by increasingly plausible Chain-of-Thought (CoT). However, whether these gains exceed sampling effects and how they depend on explicit reasoning remain unclear. We rigorously investigate these questions through sampling-aware evaluation, controlled reasoning interventions, and shortcut experiments. We find that RL-trained models outperform base models under large-budget $\operatorname{max}_g@k$ evaluation, while improvements beyond averaging are concentrated in-distribution. RLVR produces chains that raise base-model performance when transplanted and improves scoring with the chain held fixed. Yet scoring becomes less dependent on the chain during inference, more clearly at 7B than at 32B. Steering the probed quality direction in thinking tokens also has little effect on scoring. Finally, RL can improve scoring accuracy by exploiting textual cues correlated with mean opinion scores (MOSs), which could inflate benchmark performance. Together, these findings show that RLVR raises quality scoring and produces more useful chains, but that better scoring does not imply a stronger reliance on explicit reasoning, while score-based rewards can also encourage reliance on quality-irrelevant shortcuts. We hope this preliminary study can enlighten more and more concrete reasoning schemes in IQA.
  </details>

- **[Can Multi-modal (reasoning) LLMs detect document manipulation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.11021)**  `arXiv:2508.11021`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Zisheng Liang, Kidus Zewde, Rudra Pratap Singh, Disha Patil, Zexi Chen, Jiayu Xue, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Document fraud poses a significant threat to industries reliant on secure and verifiable documentation, necessitating robust detection mechanisms. This study investigates the efficacy of state-of-the-art multi-modal large language models (LLMs)-including OpenAI O1, OpenAI 4o, Gemini Flash (thinking), Deepseek Janus, Grok, Llama 3.2 and 4, Qwen 2 and 2.5 VL, Mistral Pixtral, and Claude 3.5 and 3.7 Sonnet-in detecting fraudulent documents. We benchmark these models against each other and prior work on document fraud detection techniques using a standard dataset with real transactional documents. Through prompt optimization and detailed analysis of the models' reasoning processes, we evaluate their ability to identify subtle indicators of fraud, such as tampered text, misaligned formatting, and inconsistent transactional sums. Our results reveal that top-performing multi-modal LLMs demonstrate superior zero-shot generalization, outperforming conventional methods on out-of-distribution datasets, while several vision LLMs exhibit inconsistent or subpar performance. Notably, model size and advanced reasoning capabilities show limited correlation with detection accuracy, suggesting task-specific fine-tuning is critical. This study underscores the potential of multi-modal LLMs in enhancing document fraud detection systems and provides a foundation for future research into interpretable and scalable fraud mitigation strategies.
  </details>

- **[Imagine the Future, Internalize the Gist: Efficient VLA Reasoning via Internalized Spatiotemporal Imagination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02626)**  `arXiv:2610.02626`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Shenglan Li, Zhendong Mi, Hengyi Zhu, Jingwu Luo, Chun Kit Chan, Geng Yuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models increasingly incorporate intermediate reasoning to improve robotic manipulation, yet existing approaches primarily reason about observed states without explicitly anticipating future scene evolution. Extending such reasoning to explicit future rollouts at every inference step, however, introduces substantial computational overhead. We propose IG-VLA, a VLA reasoning framework that enables models to imagine the future and internalize the gist. Our Latent Spatiotemporal Reasoning learns to imagine task-relevant future scene evolution directly in visual representation space, guiding action prediction without costly pixel-level video generation. To further reduce inference overhead, we introduce Scene Gist Memory, which internalizes reasoning-derived scene-behavior associations into a compact Scene Gist Token, preserving the benefits of future reasoning while bypassing explicit future imagination at inference. Extensive experiments on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and VLABench demonstrate the effectiveness and efficiency of IG-VLA. On the LIBERO-Plus Language suite, both the reasoning and gist policies outperform the strongest baseline by nearly 6% in success rate. The gist policy also achieves up to 6.38x speedup over baselines, reducing inference latency from 1081ms to 169.5ms per action chunk on a single NVIDIA A6000 GPU. These results demonstrate that future spatiotemporal reasoning can be effectively internalized for efficient VLA deployment.
  </details>

- **[Recovering Black-Box Point Cloud Models from Pose Shifts via Derivative-Free Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34850)**  `arXiv:2610.34850`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Seonghwan Park, Min-Gyu Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Point cloud models are increasingly deployed through score-only black-box interfaces. Under this access model, test-time rotations outside the training distribution can substantially degrade performance. Although existing mitigations such as equivariant retraining and gradient-based adaptation are effective, they are infeasible without access to model weights or gradients. To address this limitation, we propose CanDO, a test-time canonicalization method that recovers lost performance using only forward queries. Specifically, CanDO searches for an input transformation that maximizes model confidence and uses derivative-free optimization to perform this search without gradients. We further identify three conditions for successful recovery: a training augmentation gap, an accuracy-aligned confidence surrogate, and global search over a multi-modal objective. Across multiple architectures and benchmarks, CanDO recovers 76% of the oracle gap under azimuthal misorientation and 51% under arbitrary SO(3) rotations for object part segmentation. These results show that frozen score-only point cloud models can recover from rotation-induced distribution shifts through post-deployment canonicalization alone.
  </details>

- **[ProMo3D: Probing Motion Cues in Frozen 3D Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33802)**  `arXiv:2610.33802`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoang Zhang, Mert Kiray, Yordanka Velikova, Manoj Biswanath, Benjamin Busam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feed-forward 3D foundation models have recently reshaped multi-view reconstruction, yet dynamic 4D understanding still often relies on large-scale motion supervision or hand-crafted, architecture-specific heuristics. We ask whether frozen 3D foundation model tokens already encode motion-relevant information that can be exposed by lightweight readout functions, rather than re-learning motion-specific features with high-capacity decoders. Through probing on three representative backbones, we find that static--dynamic separation is decodable from frozen token representations. Our rank-restricted and spectral analyses on $\pi^3$X reveal that the recovered motion cues are concentrated in a low-dimensional subspace. Building on this, we propose Probing-based Motion Distillation (PMD), which converts pairwise motion-state compatibility scores against a fixed anchor token into dense motion masks. Trained on only 160 YouTube-VOS videos using a single 16 GB GPU, PMD transfers zero-shot to DAVIS, SegTrackv2, and FBMS-59 without target-benchmark fine-tuning. With multi-view pretrained geometric backbones, PMD outperforms training-free heuristics and approaches substantially heavier supervised and self-supervised methods, suggesting that motion decoding from frozen 3D foundation models can be lightweight and data-efficient.
  </details>

- **[Image Clustering with Vision-Language Model Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33265)**  `arXiv:2610.33265`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _João P. Matos-Carvalho, Nuno Fachada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pretrained vision-language models provide image representations whose suitability for clustering may vary with feature extraction, image domain, and partitioning algorithm. We compare nine representation pipelines across seven image benchmarks and four clustering configurations, yielding 252 baseline combinations. Eight pipelines extract visual features, including two vision-only references; the ninth generates descriptions and embeds the resulting text. With agglomerative clustering (Ward linkage) fixed, Qwen3-VL achieves the highest adjusted Rand index (ARI) on five datasets, including 0.925 on MNIST, while BLIP-2 leads on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with ARIs of 0.925 and 0.591, respectively. A separate sensitivity analysis compares raw and L2-normalized embeddings at their original dimensionality across all 63 dataset-representation pairs. Normalization preserves the highest-ARI representation in every dataset, increases ARI in 35 pairs, decreases it in 26, and leaves two numerically unchanged. On the SVHN dataset, the highest ARI remains low, rising from 0.097 to 0.098 after normalization. The evaluated caption-mediated pipeline obtains relatively high ARI on isolated digits but does not consistently exceed direct visual features. These findings support selecting and evaluating complete representation-clustering pipelines for the intended domain, with explicit attention to extraction and normalization choices.
  </details>

- **[CountVideoBench: A Benchmark to Evaluate Prediction Biases on the Object Counting task by Video-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28356)**  `arXiv:2610.28356`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Marc Chapus, Carlos Crispim-Junior, Véronique Eglin, Atilla Baskurt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object counting in videos remains a poorly characterized capability of multimodal large language models. Counting questions are included in many existing VideoQA benchmarks, but the ground-truth count distributions in these benchmarks are heavily skewed toward small values. Across the nine benchmarks we survey, the median ground-truth count is at most five, and the three most frequent values together account for between 45% and 93% of the videos. The resulting mean accuracy is then dominated by the most frequent counts; a value-dependent bias at a rare count contributes negligibly to the average and remains invisible. We introduce CountVideoBench, a benchmark of 2,600 synthetic videos covering 26 cardinalities in the interval [5, 50] (100 videos each), grouped into six clusters of consecutive counts that span the range while enabling near-neighbor comparisons. Each video contains a single class of static objects under a controlled camera motion, removing object motion and reducing recognition as confounds. We evaluate sixteen video-language models from nine architecture families in a zero-shot setting. No model exceeds 27.6% Exact Match. The dependence of accuracy on cardinality varies across models, ranging from monotonic decline to a non-monotonic peak-and-collapse pattern, which rules out increasing difficulty with cardinality as the dominant explanation of counting failure. Every model further concentrates its predictions on its own set of preferred integers, with accuracy peaks coinciding with these values and collapsing at adjacent counts. These findings highlight the need for balanced, diagnostic counting benchmarks to reliably assess this fundamental visual capability.
  </details>

- **[OmniShowcase: Omnidirectional Subject-Consistent Video Generation from Multi-View Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35684)**  `arXiv:2610.35684`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Junyeong Ahn, Geonwoo Kim, Kinam Kim, Gihwi Kim, Hoonjin Jung, Hyojin Jang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating videos of a specific subject from a few reference images remains challenging when the camera reveals surfaces that are not visible in the input. Early subject-to-video (S2V) methods condition on a single reference image and must therefore infer the subject's unseen appearance and geometry as the viewpoint changes. To address this limitation, recent methods extend S2V to multiple reference views, providing more information about the subject from different viewpoints. However, they do not explicitly specify the 3D structure that connects these observations. In addition, existing training data do not guarantee that viewpoint changes preserve the same underlying 3D subject. We present OmniShowcase, built on two components: (i) a fully automated data synthesis pipeline that produces training videos along diverse camera trajectories, with viewpoint variation driven by underlying subject geometry, even spanning full 360-degree orbits; and (ii) a geometry-conditioning method that provides the video model with a turntable of surface-normal maps reconstructed from the reference views without requiring temporal alignment with the generated frames. We construct Showcase-4K, a corpus of 3D-asset-based videos covering twelve camera motions across 88 trajectory configurations with reconstruction-derived surface-normal conditioning, and ShowcaseBench, a held-out evaluation set with the source meshes and camera parameters used to render each video. Experiments show state-of-the-art subject identity and 3D consistency while maintaining competitive video quality and motion. Project page:this http URL
  </details>

- **[Joint Latent Reasoning and Observation for Agentic Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35229)**  `arXiv:2610.35229`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Junzhang Liu, Hao Yuan, Qi Zhang, Yiming Wu, Long Chen, Zhecan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective video agents must learn not only how to answer, but also where to look next. Transferring this capability to smaller models is challenging because each observation serves two purposes: supporting an answer and guiding the search for further evidence. Textual descriptions may capture what is currently visible while omitting cues needed for subsequent acquisition. We introduce Latent Agentic Reasoning for Video Analysis (LARVA), a framework that distills multi-round video agents through jointly learned latent reasoning and visual observations. Rather than communicating through captions, an observer passes compact continuous representations to a reasoner that selects the next acquisition action or produces the final answer. Crucially, action and answer supervision propagate through this interface, training the observer to communicate information useful for downstream decisions. Observation reconstruction and reasoning-distribution alignment provide auxiliary supervision from textual teacher trajectories. Across five video-understanding benchmarks, LARVA achieves strong performance with 4B and 7B backbones. On VideoHolmes, Video-MME, and LVBench, the 7B model outperforms a textual CoT student trained on the same teacher trajectories by 5.1, 2.5, and 3.7 percentage points, respectively. These results highlight the value of jointly learning how agents represent evidence and act on it when distilling video-understanding capabilities.
  </details>

- **[GUIFlow: Temporal Redundancy-Aware Incremental Inference for GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34959)**  `arXiv:2610.34959`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ye Li, Haoran Ding, Huanan Liu, Yuan Meng, Yutian Shen, Yuansong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI agents built on vision-language models interact with continuously evolving interfaces, yet conventional inference repeatedly recomputes representations of largely overlapping observations and histories. This mismatch makes multi-step interaction costly, while changes in context, interface content, and action arguments prevent straightforward cache reuse. We introduce GUIFlow, a framework for full-pipeline incremental inference in multi-image GUI interaction. Our central insight is that advancing a decision need not require rebuilding all supporting representations: each interaction step can instead selectively update computational states maintained across the trajectory. To support new decisions as the context evolves, GUIFlow adapts how past computation is reused: historical KV states are position-aligned and injected into the current context; change signals guide selective updates of current-observation states in the vision encoder and language model; and earlier action responses serve as speculative drafts verified by the target model. Together, these mechanisms enable cross-step reuse throughout visual encoding, prefill, and decoding without additional training, a separate draft model, or changes to the model-visible inputs and action interface. Experiments demonstrate up to 3.39x geometric-mean speedup across offline multi-image benchmarks and up to 3.49x in online, closed-loop interaction on OSWorld, while maintaining task performance broadly comparable to the dense baseline.
  </details>

- **[ToDoPro: Faster and Better Reference-Based Generation: Early Token Reduction via Downsampled Self-Probing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34782)**  `arXiv:2610.34782`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Caiyi Sun, Yujing Sun, Siu-Ming Yiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent generic reference-based image generation models natively support multiple conditions by concatenating all reference image and text tokens into a joint sequence processed by a single Diffusion Transformer (DiT). However, this versatile paradigm comes at an efficiency cost: accommodating multiple references leads to a token explosion, incurring massive $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$ computational complexity. Most acceleration methods operate inside the DiT, showing limited capability in reducing spatial redundancy and failing to alleviate peak VRAM. While some pre-model token reduction alternatives exist, they inevitably introduce external models. This raises a question: Is it possible to achieve acceleration and memory savings while preserving compatibility with in-model techniques and requiring no additional parameters? In this work, we realize this goal by proposing the early Token reduction via Downsampled self-Probing (ToDoPro). By executing a few denoising steps on spatially $2\times$ downsampled reference images, this cheap probing stage utilizes the DiT model itself to effectively and efficiently extract reference token importance. Using the probed importance, we apply a hierarchical reduction before the main denoising stage: we completely discard low-importance tokens, substitute mid-importance tokens with their downsampled counterparts to cheaply preserve global context, and keep high-importance tokens. Experiments across three base models and two benchmarks demonstrate that our early reduction accelerates inference significantly and mitigates peak GPU memory. Furthermore, we can improve generation quality by safely filtering out noisy, uninformative reference tokens, enabling the model to concentrate its limited attention capacity on genuinely useful tokens. The code will be released atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[RIDE-Splat: Reflection Removal for In-the-Wild Driving Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34632)**  `arXiv:2610.34632`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Kevin David Hayes, Alex Hanson, Allen Tu, Pranav Asthana, Matthias Zwicker, Tom Goldstein_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Driving video captured through a windshield violates the world-space consistency assumed by 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS): the road scene exhibits parallax while cabin reflections remain attached to the camera, causing standard 3DGS to embed reflected content into the reconstructed scene. We introduce RIDE-Splat, a pipeline for reflection-free 3D reconstruction from posed driving video without clean images, reflection masks, or pretrained networks. RIDE-Splat assigns world-consistent transmission through the windshield to a 3DGS scene and the dominant camera-attached reflection to one shared, signed RGB image. It builds the clean reconstruction in two passes. First, it learns the 3D geometry and the signed reflection image, representing both added radiance and transmission attenuation. Then, it uses the reflection-removed training images to fit a clean 3DGS model. At inference, the reflection image is discarded, preserving the standard 3DGS rendering path. We also introduce the RIDE-Splat Dataset, the first 3D reconstruction benchmark for reflection removal from driving video -- with real driving scenes, physical windshield reflections in training views, and separately captured non-reflective evaluation images. Across the RIDE-Splat Dataset, XLD, and Wayve, RIDE-Splat outperforms 3DGS, DC-Gaussian, and RobustSplat on all nine aggregate metrics, improving PSNR by up to 3.59 dB and reducing LPIPS by up to 0.0876. We achieve the highest metrics in 38 out of 42 per-scene comparisons and removes windshield reflections that remain visible in the baselines while preserving the transmitted scene.
  </details>

- **[GRASP: Group-Adaptive Hybrid RL-SFT Post-Training for Medical VQA without Reasoning Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34561)**  `arXiv:2610.34561`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Wanqi Dong, Wanyue Dong, Jingze Ge, Mehul Motani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adapting general-purpose vision-language models (VLMs) to medical visual question answering (VQA) is valuable for helping clinicians read images and make diagnoses, but annotated data are scarce and provide only short answers, without reasoning annotations. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) fits these labels but overfits to them and amplifies their biases, eroding out-of-domain (OOD) generalization. Reinforcement learning (RL) better preserves generalization but never uses the gold answer as a training target: GRPO-style algorithms learn from the contrast between correct and incorrect answers within a group of sampled responses, so all-correct or all-wrong groups give no correctness signal. Existing SFT-RL hybrids either apply SFT and RL in separate stages or switch between them per question, or need reasoning traces from experts or stronger models, which medical VQA data lack. We propose GRASP, which combines RL and SFT in every update using only short answers: RL lets the model explore its own reasoning, SFT teaches the gold answer after that reasoning to supply the direct supervision RL lacks, and their balance adapts to how well the model answers each question. Tuning only about 1% of the parameters with LoRA, GRASP outperforms all compared training objectives both in domain and on OOD open-ended questions across Qwen3-VL-8B, InternVL3-8B and Gemma 3-4B. On an OOD benchmark spanning eight imaging modalities, GRASP-trained InternVL3-8B and Qwen3-VL-8B beat all 10 compared general and medical VLMs in open-ended accuracy, including models trained on up to 2,600 times more data or with over 250 times more trainable parameters. We release MedTerse, a dataset of 12.9K short-answer questions, and our code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MarineLife-16K: An Attribute-Rich Video-Text Dataset for Motion-Aware Marine Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33478)**  `arXiv:2610.33478`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Quang-Trung Truong, Thanh-Nghia Nguyen, Phu-Phong Vo, Jaeyeon Kim, Cheng Chen, Jie Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Marine video understanding is crucial for a wide range of real-world applications. However, existing marine video datasets suffer from limitations, including the limited number of data samples, fine-grained text descriptions, and detailed visual attributes. To address these limitations, we introduce a large-scale, video-text dataset featuring rich, in-the-wild visual attributes tailored for complex marine environments. Our dataset comprises 2,000 video-text pairs annotated with 13 attributes covering up to four objects per scene, object visual/geometric properties, behaviors, environmental context, and camera motion, spanning diverse lighting conditions, times of day, and 7 countries. From these annotations, we generate 16,080 video-question pairs (12,080 multiple-choice, 4,000 open-ended). Furthermore, we benchmark both proprietary and open-source video-LLM models on video question answering and video captioning tasks. We find that (i) proprietary models often achieves superior performances; (ii) VLMs handle object motion well but struggle with complex camera motion; (iii) closed-source models struggle to perceive camera motion; and (iv) VLMs struggle to reason in static cameras.
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing and Mitigating Evidence Misgrounding in Multimodal Knowledge Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33075)**  `arXiv:2610.33075`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiwu Wang, Yichen Wu, Jinqian Yang, Yizhou Yang, Renzhen Wang, Qian Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal knowledge editing aims to update visually grounded facts in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) while preserving unrelated text and visual behaviors. However, standard answer-level metrics can hide evidence misgrounding: an edit may produce the target answer on the original image yet remain sensitive to incidental context rather than the intended visual evidence. To examine this issue in a controlled setting, we construct WaterBird-Edit, an analysis set that decouples bird habitat labels from background context and measures whether edited predictions remain stable when the target evidence is preserved but the background changes. Motivated by this diagnosis, we propose VICE (Visual In-Context Editing), a retrieval-based framework that stores edits in external memory and applies them through visual in-context demonstrations designed to expose task-relevant evidence. Experiments on three standard benchmarks show that VICE achieves strong lifelong editing performance, while results on WaterBird-Edit show that it substantially reduces sensitivity to the controlled background shifts. Code and dataset are available inthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MOSAIC: Towards a Universal Latent Space for Computational Pathology Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33059)**  `arXiv:2610.33059`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Dibaloke Chanda, Praveen Madiraju_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pathology foundation models (PFMs) differ substantially in architecture, pretraining objective, training data and representation dimensionality, yet it remains unclear whether they learn fundamentally different feature spaces or alternative coordinate systems of a shared underlying representation. We present MOSAIC, a systematic study of latent-space similarity, alignment and interoperability across $12$ patch-level and $6$ slide-level PFMs. We first quantify representational agreement using complementary similarity measures and analyze how architecture, pretraining objective, network depth and magnification influence latent geometry. We then evaluate multiple alignment strategies for mapping independently learned representations into a common latent space. The resulting representations are evaluated through cross-model agreement, retrieval, geometry preservation, and downstream multiple-instance learning across molecular and morphological prediction tasks. MOSAIC provides a unified framework for studying whether heterogeneous PFMs can move beyond representational similarity toward a shared and functionally transferable pathology representation space. Our code, benchmarks and additional results are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DRA-SAM: Disagreement-Driven Referential Active SAM for Low-Altitude Drone Referring Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33057)**  `arXiv:2610.33057`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jisheng Dang, Huaiyuan Peng, Wencan Zhang, Bimei Wang, Jingwen Zhao, Wenjun Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Referring segmentation in low-altitude drone imagery requires distinguishing small, visually similar instances under complex linguistic and spatial constraints. Global inference may yield multiple plausible masks or leave the intended referent insufficiently represented, motivating selective local re-inference. We propose DRA-SAM, a disagreement-driven framework that allocates an additional inference pass according to competing referential hypotheses and an unresolved state. A lightweight EvidenceScorer estimates beliefs over existing candidates and the unresolved state. A training-free Referential Disagreement LOOK policy balances belief-weighted candidate competition and unresolved-region coverage to select a crop for local re-inference. The same benchmark-adapted SAM3-I backbone processes the selected region, and the resulting evidence updates existing hypotheses and introduces new candidates. Among the compared methods, DRA-SAM achieves state-of-the-art performance on six of seven test metrics on both RIS-LAD and UAVid-RIS, including mIoU and P@0.5-P@0.9, and on all seven RIS-LAD validation metrics. On RIS-LAD, DRA-SAM improves the matched global SAM3-I baseline from 49.55 to 52.34 mIoU and from 46.80 to 58.05 P@0.5. On UAVid-RIS, the independently adapted DRA-SAM achieves 73.26 mIoU and 86.86 P@0.5. Controlled crop-policy comparisons demonstrate the effectiveness of disagreement-guided crop selection, while target-size analysis reveals particularly pronounced gains for aerial targets occupying less than 1% of an image. These results establish referential disagreement as an effective criterion for allocating local inference. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Aerial-R1: Reinforcing Aerial Reasoning and Segmentation in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33056)**  `arXiv:2610.33056`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jisheng Dang, Huaiyuan Peng, Yixuan Wang, Kun Wang, Zhang Li, Tat-Seng Chua_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have revolutionized general visual perception, existing methods suffer from severe hallucination in the aerial domain, where extreme scale variations and visual ambiguity abound. Existing Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) paradigms force models to mimic ground truth without modeling uncertainty, often resulting in blind guessing on tiny or unrecognizable objects in aerial scenes. In this paper, we present Aerial-R1, the first aerial MLLM framework to reinforce visual reasoning capabilities for aerial reasoning and segmentation. Drawing inspiration from the reasoning-incentivizing mechanisms of DeepSeek-R1, we find that trustworthy perception stems from consistency across views, establishing a visual parallel to the Chain-of-Thought in logic. To effectively adapt policy optimization for non-autoregressive mask generation, we introduce Hybrid-View Group Advantage-Weighted Regression (H-GAWR). This strategy constructs dynamic groups of high-resolution and low-resolution views, seamlessly bridging group-relative advantage estimation with advantage-weighted policy extraction. By enforcing consensus on clear inputs and strictly penalizing hallucinations on ambiguous ones, Aerial-R1 develops an explicitly incentivized refusal mechanism. Extensive experiments on VSAI-Ref, the first aerial MLLM benchmark dedicated to jointly evaluating dense reasoning and hallucination robustness, demonstrate that Aerial-R1 significantly outperforms SFT baselines in segmentation precision while substantially mitigating fatal hallucinations in low-information scenarios, paving the way for more reliable and trustworthy aerial agents. Codes are available onthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GMoE-FTI: A Universal Facial Template Inversion Method Adopting Genuine Mixture-of-Experts Mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33055)**  `arXiv:2610.33055`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zixuan Shen, Zhihua Xia, Kaikai Gan, Peipeng Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Face recognition systems have been ubiquitously deployed across daily scenarios. To protect user privacy, mainstream systems store facial templates rather than raw face images for identity verification. However, facial template inversion (FTI) technologies can reconstruct faces from templates, which endangers biometric privacy. Existing FTI methods only fit single type of templates, failing to reconstruct identity-consistent faces from unseen types. To tackle this limitation, we survey mainstream template extractors which can be classified into five categories according the template distribution: unit-sphere, angle-linear, strict unit-sphere, cone-shaped, anuniform hypersphere. Inspired by this observation, this paper proposes GMoE-FTI, a universal FTI framework powered by a genuine MoE mechanism that differs from load-balanced multi-branch alternatives. It follows a three-stage training strategy: (1) independently train five mapping experts (MEs) and a matching module (MM); (2) conduct collaborative inference by weighting each ME's output with MM's output, so as to generate unified latent codes compatible with pre-trained StyleGAN3; (3) perform end-to-end joint fine-tuning over all modules to identity consistency and boost cross-expert coordination. Extensive evaluations on the LFW benchmark validate the superiority of GMoE-FTI against SOTA methods. Evaluated on seven prevailing unseen template types including AdaFace, CosFace, CurricularFace, ElasticFace, SphereFace2, SphereFace-R, and TransFace, GMoE-FTI achieves an average 40% TAR improvement, with average FAPD and FAPC each reduced by 2%, verifying its universal generalization across heterogeneous templates.
  </details>

- **[Direct Forcing: Single-Stage Training with Representation-Space Supervision for Few-Step Autoregressive Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30861)**  `arXiv:2610.30861`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Haojun Xu, Jie Huang, Chuyang Zhao, Xin Lu, Mingchen Zhong, Zihao Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-step autoregressive video generation enables low-latency streaming, yet prevailing training pipelines separate causal adaptation from distribution matching. Recent representation-based text-to-video methods retain this separation by starting with an ODE initialization stage, even when the target distribution is defined directly by reference videos. We introduce \method, the first single-stage framework that uses reference videos both as paired examples of how a history continues and as a target population for self-generated rollouts. Specifically, Direct Forcing is trained with two complementary signals: (1) Causal Continuation Alignment learns paired spatiotemporal correspondence under reference histories; and (2) Rollout Distribution Matching employs maximum mean discrepancy to align the representation distributions of self-generated and reference videos. To coordinate these signals, we introduce Dynamic Gradient Balancing, which adapts their relative gradient contributions during training. Experiments with Wan2.1-1.3B show that Direct Forcing learns a four-step autoregressive generator in 250 updates without a separate ODE initialization stage or online diffusion-score supervision, achieving 84.63 VBench Total and 86.01 Quality.
  </details>

- **[SLVR: Structured Latent Visual Reasoning via Human-like Reasoning Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10563)**  `arXiv:2610.10563`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Albert Gao, Bing Xue, Andrea Zanette_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often answer visual reasoning questions by relying on linguistic priors rather than task-relevant visual evidence. Textual chain-of-thought reasoning can partially mitigate this issue by encouraging models to decompose visual questions into intermediate evidence-seeking steps, but generating these steps autoregressively increases inference cost. Latent reasoning avoids explicit rationale generation, but existing approaches provide limited control over what intermediate states encode, making it difficult to impose separate supervision for planning, grounding, and evidence selection. We propose Structured Latent Visual Reasoning (SLVR), a training framework that bridges explicit chain-of-thought and latent reasoning by organizing multimodal reasoning into typed latent stages for planning, grounding, evidence selection, and reasoning integration.SLVR first trains the model to rely on the image by masking answer-revealing text and contrasting the correct answer with visually plausible distractors. It then organizes reasoning into latent stages for planning, grounding, evidence selection, and integration, supervising each stage with the corresponding signal: plans, boxes, visual evidence, and final rationales. This gives latent reasoning an explicit functional structure while avoiding generated textual chains at inference time.Built on Qwen2.5-VL-7B, SLVR improves consistently across multimodal reasoning benchmarks, with absolute gains of +9.4 on MMVP and +14.2 on BLINK Relation, as well as improvements on V*, MathVista, and ChartQA. These results suggest that structured latent supervision can improve fine-grained visual reasoning without the decoding overhead of textual CoT. Project page is available \href{this https URL}{here}.
  </details>

- **[OmniReasoning: Pushing the Limits of Audio-Visual Joint Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39490)**  `arXiv:2609.39490`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Junming Lin, Yuxuan Wang, Zhenxin Lei, Yuxin Liu, Ruixun Liu, Yinsong Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances have enabled unified omni-modal models in understanding audio, vision, and language. However, existing benchmarks, training data, and learning methods largely treat the modalities independently, leaving the capability of audio-visual joint reasoning poorly evaluated and insufficiently elicited. We address this gap with a benchmark, data engine, and learning method. First, we introduce OmniReasoningBench, a benchmark where both audio and visual evidence are indispensable. It comprises 1,150 multiple-choice and open-ended questions across two tasks, reasoning over video and reasoning beyond video. Second, we develop a data engine OmniQA. It automatically constructs evidence-grounded QA pairs that explicitly necessitate audio-visual joint reasoning, together with time-stamped clue chains that guide the annotation of thinking process. Besides our benchmark, this engine produces training data OmniReasoning-SFT-112K and OmniReasoning-RL-19K. Finally, we propose an on-policy self-distillation method Modality-Factored Self-Distillation (MFSD). It evaluates each sampled response under modality-specific clue contexts, disentangling the contributions of individual clues and their cross-modal interactions for token-level credit assignment. With our training data and learning method, our model OmniReasoning-30B-A3B achieves 50.0% on OmniVideoBench and 42.5% on OmniReasoningBench, improving the base model Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B-Thinking by 12.8 and 9.3 percentage points, respectively. Moreover, it delivers substantial gains on general and long-video benchmarks, including Video-MME-v2. We hope our work offers a solid step for facilitating future research in omni-modal joint reasoning.
  </details>

- **[FOCUS: Benchmarking Retinal Model Generalization from Foundation Vision Encoders to Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33158)**  `arXiv:2609.33158`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _David Restrepo, Chenwei Wu, Luis Filipe Nakayama, Miguel L. Martins, Stergios Christodoulidis, Maria Vakalopoulou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Progress in AI-based retinal image analysis has accelerated with the adoption of foundation models, yet evaluating their real-world reliability remains challenging. Performance reported on a single dataset does not capture how models behave under dataset shift, across clinical definitions, or for different patient subgroups. This limitation is particularly critical in medical imaging, where robustness, calibration, and fairness are essential for safe deployment. We introduce FOCUS (Foundation Ophthalmic Cross-Dataset Understanding under Shift), a cross-dataset benchmark evaluating retinal fundus models across vision-only encoder models (VM), vision-language dual-encoder models (VLM), and multimodal large language models (MLLM). FOCUS harmonizes binary diabetic retinopathy, referable diabetic retinopathy, and glaucomatous optic neuropathy tasks across ten public datasets spanning diverse geographies, acquisition conditions, and label protocols. The benchmark evaluates models through a unified analysis layer that measures ranking performance, calibration, subgroup disparities, and image-quality robustness. We present a large-scale evaluation covering 532 base configurations and 228 MLLM configurations adapted via LoRA fine-tuning. Results show no model family consistently dominates across tasks and datasets: general VM encoders achieve the strongest average ranking performance, medical MLLMs are competitive but variable, and dual-encoder VLMs benefit substantially from lightweight adaptation. Fine-tuning improves in-domain performance but exhibits heterogeneous transfer to external datasets, particularly in calibration. FOCUS provides a practical framework and public benchmark to assess generalization, reliability, and robustness beyond single-dataset leaderboards.
  </details>

- **[Content-Aware Adaptive Token Pruning for Video-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03112)**  `arXiv:2608.03112`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Paribesh Regmi, Qingshuang Chen, Chi Zhang, Heba Aly, Yelin Kim, Hongda Mao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models excel at image and video understanding but suffer from high inference latency due to the need to process thousands of tokens per image, limiting their deployment on resource-constrained edge devices and in real-time surveillance applications. This challenge is further amplified in video processing, where multiple frames must be analyzed simultaneously. Existing token reduction techniques generally rely on a fixed, uniform pruning ratio applied across all inputs, which is suboptimal because the degree of redundancy can vary significantly between different videos, necessitating content-dependent pruning levels to preserve critical information. To address these limitations, we propose an adaptive token pruning strategy specifically designed for video processing. Crucially, the pruning ratio is determined adaptively based on the content of each video. This is achieved by analyzing the correlation structure of token embeddings to quantify redundancy, enabling dynamic token allocation that retains more tokens for dynamic scenes while aggressively pruning repetitive or static content. Importantly, our method is entirely post-hoc and requires no additional training or fine-tuning, while achieving strong empirical gains; notably, it improves accuracy by +7\% on a video captioning benchmark at 10\% token retention, while reducing computation TFLOPs by 95\%.
  </details>

- **[StableHand: Quality-Aware Flow Matching for World-Space Dual-Hand Motion Estimation from Egocentric Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18553)**  `arXiv:2605.18553`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Huajian Zeng, Chaohua Yao, Yuantai Zhang, Jiaqi Yang, Rolandos Alexandros Potamias, Xingxing Zuo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recovering world space 4D motion of two interacting hands from egocentric video is a fundamental capability for supervising robot policy learning, where wrist trajectories track the end-effector and finger articulations specify the grasp pose. Two major challenges arise in this setting: hands frequently leave the camera view for extended periods due to head motion, and persistent hand-object interactions cause severe occlusions of one or both hands. Existing methods uniformly condition on noisy hand motion observations without accounting for their per-frame reliability, leading to substantial performance degradation. Our key insight is that accurate world space hand motion estimation is tightly coupled with the quality of per-frame hand observations. To this end, we decompose the quality of hand motion observations extracted from an off-the-shelf hand pose estimator into four channels: wrist global translation and finger articulations for both hands.
