We propose StableHand, a quality-aware flow-matching framework conditioned on these four-channel quality signals, which are predicted by a learned quality network. We naturally incorporate the quality signals into the flow-matching process through a per-channel forward schedule, a quality-adjusted velocity target, AdaLN modulation of the DiT denoiser, and a quality-aware ODE initialization. This unified generative process preserves high-quality observations while reconstructing unreliable ones using a learned bimanual motion prior. Experiments on HOT3D and ARCTIC, two egocentric benchmarks featuring long missing-hand spans and persistent hand-object occlusions, show that StableHand achieves state-of-the-art performance across all reported metrics, reducing W-MPJPE by 20-25% compared to the strongest baseline, with the largest gains on heavily occluded ARCTIC sequences.
  </details>

- **[MicroWorld: Empowering Multimodal Large Language Models to Bridge the Microscopic Domain Gap with Multimodal Attributed Property Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10120)**  `arXiv:2605.10120`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Manyu Li, Ruian He, Chenxi Ma, Weimin Tan, Bo Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) show remarkable potential for scientific reasoning, yet their performance in specialized domains such as microscopy remains limited by the scarcity of domain-specific training data and the difficulty of encoding fine-grained expert knowledge into model parameters. To bridge the gap, we introduce MicroWorld, a framework for structured-knowledge augmentation with a complementary visual retrieval pathway, using a multimodal attributed property graph (MAPG) without domain-specific fine-tuning. MicroWorld extracts biomedical entities and relations via scispaCy or LLM-based triplet mining, aligns images and entities in a shared embedding space using Qwen3-VL-Embedding, and assembles a knowledge graph comprising approximately 106K nodes and 2.62M typed edges spanning eight relation categories. At inference time, a graph-augmented retrieval pipeline matches query entities to the MAPG and injects structured knowledge context into the MLLM prompt. On MicroVQA, MicroWorld raises Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct accuracy from 48.8% to 67.1% (+18.3 percentage points; 37.5% relative), exceeding zero-shot GPT-5 (59.4%). The identical retrieved context raises GPT-5 to 71.5%, and GPT-5's own entity extraction reaches 73.3%. On MicroBench, 8B Single-Pass improves overall accuracy by 4.0 percentage points. The benefits are greatest for knowledge-intensive reasoning; fine-grained visual discrimination and the additional visual-pathway contribution remain limited. Code and accompanying resources:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[HiMu: Hierarchical Multimodal Frame Selection for Long Video Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18558)**  `arXiv:2603.18558`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Dan Ben-Ami, Gabriele Serussi, Kobi Cohen, Chaim Baskin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-form video question answering requires reasoning over extended temporal contexts, making frame selection a critical bottleneck for multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) bound by finite context windows. Within the controlled frame-budget regime that governs practical deployment, prior selectors score frames against a single global query embedding; as a result, compositional multimodal questions that involve temporal ordering or cross-modal cues such as "what happens on screen right after the narrator mentions the reaction?" are flattened into a representation that loses sub-event ordering and modality bindings. We introduce HiMu, a training-free framework for compositional multimodal frame selection. A single text-only LLM call decomposes the query into a hierarchical logic tree whose leaves are atomic predicates, each routed to a lightweight expert spanning vision (CLIP, open-vocabulary detection, OCR) and audio (speech recognition and non-speech sound matching). Expert signals are normalized, smoothed to align across modalities, and composed bottom-up through fuzzy-logic operators that enforce temporal sequencing and adjacency, yielding a continuous per-frame satisfaction curve. Under the standard 16-frame budget on Video-MME, LongVideoBench, and HERBench-Lite, HiMu achieves state-of-the-art accuracy among frame selection methods and improves over uniform sampling across seven diverse MLLMs as a drop-in module, matching the accuracy of uniform sampling at 4x its frame budget, without retraining and without multiple iterative MLLM calls during selection.
  </details>

- **[How well are open sourced AI-generated image detection models out-of-the-box: A comprehensive benchmark study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07814)**  `arXiv:2602.07814`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Simiao Ren, Yuchen Zhou, Xingyu Shen, Kidus Zewde, Tommy Duong, George Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI-generated images proliferate across digital platforms, reliable detection methods have become critical for combating misinformation and maintaining content authenticity. While numerous deepfake detection methods have been proposed, existing benchmarks predominantly evaluate fine-tuned models, leaving a critical gap in understanding out-of-the-box performance -- the most common deployment scenario for practitioners. We present the first comprehensive zero-shot evaluation of 16 state-of-the-art detection methods, comprising 23 pretrained detector variants (due to multiple released versions of certain detectors), across 12 diverse datasets, comprising 2.6~million image samples spanning 291 unique generators including modern diffusion models. Our systematic analysis reveals striking findings: (1)~no universal winner exists, with detector rankings exhibiting substantial instability (Spearman~$\rho$: 0.01 -- 0.87 across dataset pairs); (2)~a 37~percentage-point performance gap separates the best detector (75.0\% mean accuracy) from the worst (37.5\%); (3)~training data alignment critically impacts generalization, causing up to 20--60\% performance variance within architecturally identical detector families; (4)~modern commercial generators (Flux~Dev, Firefly~v4, Midjourney~v7) defeat most detectors, achieving only 18--30\% average accuracy; and (5)~we identify three systematic failure patterns affecting cross-dataset generalization. Statistical analysis confirms significant performance differences between detectors (Friedman test: $\chi^2$=121.01, $p<10^{-16}$, Kendall~$W$=0.524). Our findings challenge the ``one-size-fits-all'' detector paradigm and provide actionable deployment guidelines, demonstrating that practitioners must carefully select detectors based on their specific threat landscape rather than relying on published benchmark performance.
  </details>

- **[Can Multi-modal (reasoning) LLMs work as deepfake detectors?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.20084)**  `arXiv:2503.20084`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Simiao Ren, Yao Yao, Kidus Zewde, Zisheng Liang, Tsang, Ning-Yau Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deepfake detection remains a critical challenge in the era of advanced generative models, particularly as synthetic media becomes more sophisticated. In this study, we explore the potential of state of the art multi-modal (reasoning) large language models (LLMs) for deepfake image detection such as (OpenAI O1/4o, Gemini thinking Flash 2, Deepseek Janus, Grok 3, llama 3.2, Qwen 2/2.5 VL, Mistral Pixtral, Claude 3.5/3.7 sonnet) . We benchmark 12 latest multi-modal LLMs against traditional deepfake detection methods across multiple datasets, including recently published real-world deepfake imagery. To enhance performance, we employ prompt tuning and conduct an in-depth analysis of the models' reasoning pathways to identify key contributing factors in their decision-making process. Our findings indicate that best multi-modal LLMs achieve competitive performance with promising generalization ability with zero shot, even surpass traditional deepfake detection pipelines in out-of-distribution datasets while the rest of the LLM families performs extremely disappointing with some worse than random guess. Furthermore, we found newer model version and reasoning capabilities does not contribute to performance in such niche tasks of deepfake detection while model size do help in some cases. This study highlights the potential of integrating multi-modal reasoning in future deepfake detection frameworks and provides insights into model interpretability for robustness in real-world scenarios.
  </details>

- **[VibeVideoBench:Evaluating Multimodal Agents on Code-Driven Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35864)**  `arXiv:2610.35864`  `cs.CV`  
  _Muxi Diao, Wenbin Xu, Lele Yang, Xuyang Ma, Yuzhe Chen, Jiankang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion video models produce striking clips, but they cannot reliably render an exact formula, a correct chart, a ruleconsistent game or narration synchronized to on-screen events. A different route is emerging: multimodal agents that write code to render every frame and synthesize every sound. We introduce VibeVideoBench, a benchmark for evaluating agents on code-driven video generation. Its 239 self-contained tasks in English and Chinese span 12 categories, from data stories and music-driven pieces to classroom lessons and paper explainers; each asks an agent, running in its native harness inside a sandbox, to deliver a finished MP4 with sound within one hour. Evaluation is hierarchical and evidence-based: program checks of the delivery spec, about 13k task-specific rubric checks traced to the requirements they enforce, and five quality dimensions anchored per task, judged by three VLM judge panels and validated with blind human preferences. Across nine frontier agent systems and 2,151 scored rollouts, the benchmark is far from solved: GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 lead clearly, yet even the best system fails a core requirement on about a quarter of tasks. Whether a video arrives at all matters as much as its quality, and three quarters of the lost videos are final renders started too late, run too slowly or never started. The judges agree with each other and with human preferences on system rankings, but only moderately on individual videos.
  </details>

- **[Back to Basics: A Skill-Centric Approach to Generalist Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35823)**  `arXiv:2610.35823`  `cs.CV`  
  _Minh Pham-Dinh, Md Farhan Ishmam, Hernan Yupanqui, Reaz Hassan Joarder, Kenneth Marino_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Browser agents do not just need more practice. They need to practice the right things. We introduce MiniWeb, a realistic miniature of the web comprising 65 interconnected websites across 15 domains, where agents navigate, search, and transact as people do. Its 369 human-annotated tasks cover everyday workflows and label 37 reusable interaction primitives grouped into 7 skill families, allowing us to diagnose which skills fail rather than only whether a task succeeds. Benchmarking ten open-weight agents, we find that drag & gesture is consistently the weakest family, with no agent solving more than 24% of its instances. To address this gap, we introduce MiniWebGen, a framework that autonomously generates verified, skill-centric training data from MiniWeb. Fine-tuning Qwen3.5-4B on just 3.1K verified trajectories improves single-primitive success by 24.9pp across 13 unseen websites, with gains across every skill family. However, these trajectories teach short-horizon interactions, which do not directly translate to full-task success. We build on this result with MiniWebAgent, which pairs the trained executor with a planner that decomposes full tasks into the skill-level steps it learned to perform. This approach improves task success by 19.4pp points on unseen MiniWeb sites and transfers to external benchmarks, with gains of 9.3pp on WebArena-Lite, 16.1pp on Online-Mind2Web, and 20.0pp on WebVoyager, without training on their trajectories. We provide a simple recipe for compact browser agents: practice reusable skills directly, then compose them to solve unfamiliar tasks.
  </details>

- **[Printed and Scanned: Exposing the Fragility of Single-Image Morphing Attack Detectors under Print-Scan Degradation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35682)**  `arXiv:2610.35682`  `cs.CV`  
  _Diogo J. Paulo, Hugo Proença, João C. Neves_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Face morphing attacks, in which the biometric traits of two or more identities are blended into a single face image, pose a severe threat to identity document issuance and automated border control. Consequently, a large body of work has proposed Single-image Morphing Attack Detection (S-MAD) algorithms and benchmarked them on datasets such as SYN-MAD22. However, the majority of these benchmarks operate exclusively in the digital domain, which contrasts with the real-world passport issuance pipeline in some countries, which still requires an applicant-supplied photograph to be printed and subsequently re-scanned before it is embedded in the identity document or fed into an automated verification system. This print-scan step introduces halftoning, resampling, color-space, and sensor noise that are absent from digital pipelines, and may mask the high-frequency artifacts that S-MAD models rely on. In this work, we introduce a print-scan version of the SYN-MAD22 dataset, obtained by printing and re-scanning all bona fide and morphed images at 300 and 600 DPI, and we use it to evaluate eight state-of-the-art S-MAD models trained exclusively on digital data. We additionally repeat the acquisition with a second scanner to assess cross-device robustness. Our results show that detectors that achieve near state-of-the-art performance in the digital domain suffer a more than threefold increase in average equal error rate, with the same qualitative degradation observed across both scanning devices, exposing a critical, previously under-reported gap between digital-benchmark performance and robustness to print-scan degradation.
  </details>

- **[Synthetic Deformable Tubular Benchmark: A mechanically annotated dataset for evaluating deformable 3D reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35621)**  `arXiv:2610.35621`  `cs.CV`  
  _Íñigo Cirauqui-Viloria_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating deformable 3D reconstruction requires ground truth linking camera motion, surface deformation, material correspondence and image appearance. We introduce the Synthetic Deformable Tubular Benchmark, generated with LumenMorph, an open-source toolkit for creating, annotating and evaluating tubular scenes. The release contains 5,580 RGB images. Its baseline crosses three surface-motion conditions with moving and stationary cameras in six scene families; matched appearance variants cover only moving-camera finite-element actions in four families. Documented public and evaluation interfaces expose camera poses, geometry, depth, deformation fields, persistent material correspondences and discrete finite-element records. Persistent triangle identities and material weights let users track the same surface location across frames and query its three-dimensional trajectory. Separately implemented geometric and mechanical checks assess annotation consistency with the generating models. Structure-from-motion, optical-flow and supplied-geometry displacement-interpolation studies demonstrate how the resource distinguishes registration coverage from geometric accuracy, exposes camera- and surface-motion effects, and tests the benefits and mismatch of mechanical priors. Public readers, evaluation contracts and the configurable generator support reuse and extension. Data integrity, fixed-environment repeatability and retained-prediction score replay have distinct verification scopes. The benchmark provides a controlled synthetic reference without claiming anatomical or clinical realism.
  </details>

- **[Copying As Defense: Protecting Images from Editing By Eliciting Model Copying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35566)**  `arXiv:2610.35566`  `cs.CV`  
  _Salma Abdel Magid, Sanghyuk Chun, Eric Slyman, Kushal Kafle, Olga Russakovsky_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image editors built on diffusion transformers (DiTs), such as Flux Kontext, make it possible to turn a single photograph into a convincing manipulated image, including harmful and non-consensual edits. Proactive defenses aim to prevent such manipulations by adding an imperceptible perturbation before an image is shared. Existing defenses for DiT-based in-context editors make the output attend less to the protected reference image. But this leaves the model free to follow the edit instruction using the text and its own generated tokens, often producing a new, weakly grounded image rather than preventing the edit. We find that the opposite strategy is more effective: instead of leading the model to ignore the reference, we encourage it to copy it. Through controlled attention interventions, we show that concentrating target-token attention on the reference image in only a few early blocks is sufficient to collapse the editing behavior, causing the model to reproduce its input, often as a lossy copy, rather than execute the requested transformation. Based on this insight, we introduce Copying-as-Defense (CaD), which maximizes per-token target-to-reference attention mass in early transformer blocks. Since attention rows are normalized, increasing reference attention suppresses the attention allocated to the text and generated canvas. Restricting the objective to early blocks also lets us truncate the backpropagation path, keeping optimization cheap. Across editors and benchmarks, CaD reduces average edit success from 81.4% on unprotected images to 24.0%, a 70.6% relative reduction, and by a further 44.3% relative to DeContext, the strongest baseline (43.0%). CaD is also roughly 1.8-2.0x faster per optimization step than comparable methods.
  </details>

- **[Metric Scale for Insta360 Structure-from-Motion from the Camera's Embedded IMU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35554)**  `arXiv:2610.35554`  `cs.CV`  
  _Davide Cuttini, Diego Di Tommaso, Alessio Antoniacomi, Luca Simonetti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structure-from-motion (SfM) from the dual-fisheye video of a consumer 360-degree camera recovers geometry only up to a similarity transform; in the pipeline considered here, metric scale is obtained from smartphone ARKit or ARCore poses captured alongside the camera. This paper evaluates a self-contained alternative: the IMU embedded in the Insta360 X5 and X6, recorded at 1 kHz. The sensor is characterised (noise density, camera-IMU synchronisation, accelerometer scale factor, bias stability), the camera-IMU rotation and frame timing are estimated from the data, and the scale is recovered by a linear visual-inertial alignment over the SfM poses of a whole survey. The standard formulation, with gravity and accelerometer bias shared across the survey, overestimates the scale by 3.4% on average; estimating each contiguous segment independently and averaging removes the bias. On nine surveys with a co-mounted LiDAR scanner the inertial scale deviates from the LiDAR by 0.74% in mean absolute error, against 1.94% for the smartphone scale; on printed fiducial markers both agree within the 1% uncertainty of the target. On the reference trajectories of thirty sequences of a public benchmark, brought to scales of 1/40, 1 and 40, the estimator recovers the metric scale with a mean error of -0.07% and a standard deviation of 1.28%. On fifty production surveys where the smartphone is the only reference, the two scales agree to a standard deviation of 2.6%, the inertial scale being 0.9% lower on average; the difference does not depend on window construction, grows with the extent of the smartphone anchor, and cannot be attributed to either reference. An ablation over window construction and a comparison with monocular metric-depth networks complete the study.
  </details>

- **[EARS: Evidence-Aware Retrieval via Sound and Motion for Budgeted Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35536)**  `arXiv:2610.35536`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shih-Yao Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Uniform frame sampling can miss brief evidence in sparse-event videos when the VLM sees only K frames. We propose EARS (Evidence-Aware Retrieval via Sound and motion), a training-free, query-free front-end that ranks frames using optical-flow motion and audio energy, then applies temporal non-maximum suppression for diversity. The evidence score alone has frame-level AP 0.39; used only to select frames for the VLM, EARS with temporal diversity reaches localization AP 0.94, compared with 0.88 under Uniform sampling. In the updated matched study, at K = 1 EARS reaches XD-subset F1 0.660 versus 0.614 for centered Uniform (the paired interval includes zero); earlier backbone studies use separate protocols. On mostly-silent UCF-Crime, however, EARS slightly trails Uniform. A single configuration is used across both benchmarks; the new controls do not repeat the original held-out search. EARS therefore targets low-budget videos whose salient evidence aligns with motion or audio; on dense narratives or stealthy events, uniform or semantic selection remains preferable, and cheap modality statistics cannot reliably route between them. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Learning High-Quality Pseudo-Labels for Point-Supervised Temporal Action Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35516)**  `arXiv:2610.35516`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhenying Fang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Point-supervised temporal action localization (PTAL) aims to recognize and localize action instances in untrimmed videos using only point-level annotations. Existing pseudo-label-based methods have made substantial progress in PTAL. However, confidence-based initialization can overlook well-localized proposals. Meanwhile, existing methods either keep pseudo-labels fixed during detector training or update them without fully exploiting alternative boundary evidence from the original proposal pool. These limitations constrain supervision quality, thereby hindering detection performance. To learn high-quality pseudo-labels, we propose a framework that integrates prior-guided initialization with consensus-guided refinement during detector training. Our framework comprises two modules: Prior-Guided Initialization (PGI) and Consensus-Guided Refinement (CGR). PGI combines proposal confidence with point-derived priors to establish instance-specific reliability for initial pseudo-label selection and candidate retention. CGR continually uses the retained original candidates to construct boundary targets, combining their consensus with evolving detector predictions to refine pseudo-labels throughout training. Experiments on four benchmarks demonstrate that our method achieves competitive detection performance compared with existing methods. In particular, our method achieves 68.5\% average mAP over tIoU thresholds 0.1--0.7 on THUMOS14, surpassing PseR by 3.7 percentage points. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[S$^2$-RL: Sample-Set Dual Reinforcement Learning for Generative Semantic Segmentation Dataset Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35491)**  `arXiv:2610.35491`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haoyu Wang, Fei Zhou, Qingqing Qiu, Lei Zhang, Wei Wei, Chen Ding_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While dataset distillation has witnessed significant advances in image classification, its extension to dense prediction tasks such as semantic segmentation is plagued by two core bottlenecks: bi-level optimization-based approaches suffer from prohibitive computational costs stemming from pixel-wise gradient unrolling, whereas proxy-based generative methods merely infuse visual textures into fixed semantic masks derived from real data, thus failing to condense rich semantic knowledge into a compact set of informative, novel scene generations. To address both bottlenecks simultaneously, we propose a novel Sample-Set Reinforcement Learning (S$^2$-RL) framework for generative semantic segmentation dataset distillation (SSDD). Specifically, S$^2$-RL formulates SSDD as a diffusion model based text-to-image generation paradigm, enabling flexible generation of images with informative, novel semantic distributions via a concise text prompt. Furthermore, we fine-tune a diffusion policy via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), leveraging a sample-set dual reward paradigm: for individual samples, it enforces semantic alignment with input text prompts and intra-class feature diversity; for sample sets, it maximizes inter-sample semantic distribution diversity by solving a contextual multi-armed bandit problem. These design choices enable S$^2$-RL to distill large-scale semantic segmentation datasets into a compact set of generated samples that encapsulate diverse, informative semantic knowledge without the need of pixel-wise gradient unrolling. Extensive experiments on the ADE20K and COCO datasets demonstrate that S$^2$-RL achieves substantial and consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines, establishing a new benchmark for SSDD.
  </details>

- **[OBB-PBD: Extending Parallel Box Decoding to Oriented Bounding Boxes for Referring Expression Grounding in Aerial Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35450)**  `arXiv:2610.35450`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chang Woo Song, Yeong Jun Koh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Referring expression grounding in overhead imagery requires oriented bounding boxes rather than horizontal ones, since aerial objects appear at arbitrary orientations. Most grounding models emit box coordinates sequentially; Parallel Box Decoding (PBD) instead predicts all geometry slots in one forward pass, but only for four-coordinate horizontal boxes. We extend this block-aligned interface to a six-slot oriented representation using a double-angle parameterization. Rather than assuming correctness from a decreasing loss curve, we verify the extension with image/prompt-shuffle controls and visibility/leakage probes, which show that the corrected model depends on both the image and the prompt rather than on prompt statistics alone. On the aerial vehicle visual grounding (AVVG) benchmark, whose targets are genuinely oriented, the extended model improves rotated intersection over union (rotIoU) from 0.1253 for the zero-shot baseline to 0.303 +/- 0.012 (mean +/- standard deviation over three training seeds) across the complete 6139-record test split spanning all 120 images. Learning oriented boxes rather than their axis-aligned envelopes adds 0.055 +/- 0.008 rotIoU beyond a matched control (per-seed 95% confidence intervals all excluding zero), whereas block expansion alone yields no rotIoU gain distinguishable from zero.
  </details>

- **[Unveil the Mask: Jointly-Optimized Diffusion Masked Pretraining for Appearance-Agnostic Operator Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35414)**  `arXiv:2610.35414`  `cs.CV`  
  _Katsuya Ogata, Ryoya Yoshiyama, Kawachi Hodaka, Yuta Nakashima_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Photorealistic avatar videos decouple the appearance of a rendered face from the person who operates it, which makes conventional face recognition unsuitable for identifying the operator. Verifying the operator therefore requires representations of how a face moves that are insensitive to what the avatar looks like. We propose a verification framework built without any external pretrained model. It uses two complementary modalities provided for each sample: facial landmark sequences and video frames. From the landmark modality, it learns motion representations by diffusion masked pretraining over grouped landmarks, guided by lightweight auxiliary tasks that predict behavioral cues such as motion intensity and eye openness, and turns them into operator embeddings with a contrastive linear probe. To generalize to unseen avatar generators, the pretraining input is randomly transformed to simulate how a generator may alter the operator's motion. From the video modality, it captures fine facial motion through differences between consecutive frames. The embeddings of the two modalities are combined into one. Our approach reaches an overall AUC of 0.769 on the WBA2026 benchmark. Linear probing shows that the video embedding retains little information about the avatar's identity, whereas the landmark embedding retains some, and fusing the two modalities improves verification in particular across speaking and listening modes.
  </details>

- **[GIFT: Representation Geometry Matters for Single-Domain Generalized Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35374)**  `arXiv:2610.35374`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yin Zhang, Yaoyue Zheng, Yongqiang Zhang, Bogdan Raducanu, Dan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-Domain Generalized Object Detection (S-DGOD) aims to generalize from a single source domain to unseen domains under distribution shifts. Existing efforts mainly focus on simulating unseen domains or suppressing domain-specific factors. However, they largely overlook the intrinsic generalization capability embedded in Vision Foundation Models (VFMs). In this work, we show that unconstrained fine-tuning disrupts the geometric structure of pre-trained representations, which encodes domain-invariant semantics. Motivated by this, we propose Geometric-Invariance Fine-Tuning (GIFT), a geometric-aware fine-tuning method for VFMs. Specifically, we introduce a series of learnable Householder reflections to the pre-trained weights to encourage geometry-consistent adaptation under domain shift. In addition, we propose a lightweight channel-wise scaling to facilitate effective task-specific adaptation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that GIFT consistently outperforms state-of-the-art (SOTA) approaches on two S-DGOD benchmarks. Notably, by introducing fewer than 1% additional trainable parameters into the frozen backbone, the proposed method achieves improvements of +7.9% and +2.9% in mPC over the previous SOTA on the Cityscapes-C and Diverse Weather Dataset, respectively. This finding highlights that preserving representation geometry is crucial for robust cross-domain generalization, establishing GIFT as a principled and efficient solution to domain shift.
  </details>

- **[Evidence-Guided Selection of Retinal Vessel Segmentation Pipelines: A Cross-Dataset Benchmark of Performance, Structure, Failure Modes, and Reproducibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35357)**  `arXiv:2610.35357`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ruoyu Yan, Yuejia Liu, Shixin Xu, Xiaoyan Li, Xi Luo, Yuhui Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retinal vessel segmentation is widely used in retinal image analysis, yet model selection based only on aggregate overlap scores can overlook structural fidelity, cross-dataset robustness, and application-specific error costs. We evaluated five executable binary vessel-segmentation pipelines (FSG-Net, SA-UNetv2, DCP-1024, GAVE, and FR-UNet) on 244 images from six public datasets using pixel-level agreement, cross-dataset stability, thin-vessel retention, structural metrics, error composition, and available computational evidence. Results were aggregated with equal weighting across datasets. No pipeline was optimal in every setting. FSG-Net achieved the highest equal-dataset mean Macro-F1 (0.77), IoU (0.63), clDice (0.83), and Skeleton F1 (0.78), whereas SA-UNetv2 achieved the highest mean sensitivity (0.85) and Thin-Vessel Sensitivity (0.63). Differences across datasets were often larger than those among the leading pipelines, and errors shared by all five methods were concentrated near annotated vessel boundaries and faint peripheral vessels. In an ancillary analysis on 45 HRF images, segmentation masks were converted into 11 ring-wise vascular features for leave-one-out classification. Controlled perturbations showed that local mask errors can be amplified in downstream features, while targeted stabilization reduced sensitivity for five features. The results support task-conditioned, benchmark-relative pipeline selection rather than a single universal ranking and highlight the need to validate the selected pipeline on the intended target domain.
  </details>

- **[OVM-Occ: Open-Vocabulary Monocular Occupancy Prediction via Geometry-Aware Vision-Language Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35312)**  `arXiv:2610.35312`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuyang Yu, Haibo Qin, Shaohan Hu, Wei Shan, Xiaoqing Guan, You Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monocular occupancy prediction in indoor scenes aims to recover a scene's 3D geometry and semantics from a single RGB image. Existing methods are restricted to a closed set of predefined categories, limiting their ability to generalize to unseen categories. This limitation is particularly critical in indoor scenes, where objects exhibit highly diverse and fine-grained semantic categories. We study open-vocabulary monocular occupancy prediction for indoor scenes and present OVM-Occ, a Gaussian-based occupancy framework with an instance-aware vision-language teacher. Specifically, we adopt Language-Embedded Gaussians as the intermediate scene representation, propose a geometry-aware deformable attention module to refine Gaussian semantic features, and construct instance-level vision-language embeddings to provide reliable semantic supervision. We further introduce geometry-aware decoupled semantic distillation to mitigate the interference between geometry learning and semantic learning. To evaluate open-vocabulary occupancy prediction performance, we introduce OpenOcc-ScanNet and OpenOcc-Replica, two extended benchmarks with more diverse and fine-grained semantic categories. Experiments show that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance in open-vocabulary monocular occupancy prediction for indoor scenes, outperforming the previous state-of-the-art approach by 12.2 mIoU and 0.6 IoU on the Occ-ScanNet dataset, with consistent gains under new open-vocabulary evaluation protocols.
  </details>

- **[O2D-MedNet: An Optimization-Aware Dual-Domain Framework for Robust Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35220)**  `arXiv:2610.35220`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shipeng Hu, Miaohui Wang, Jing Qin, Sukun Tian, Yuchun Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical image segmentation (MIS) remains challenging due to composite degradations and multi-scale geometric variations, which jointly impair lesion-background separability, structural consistency, and optimization stability. Existing single-stream frameworks entangle heterogeneous cues within a single representation pathway, while hybrid-stream approaches often rely on generic or static fusion, limiting effective coordination across domains and failing to explicitly address optimization instability. To overcome these limitations, we propose a new optimization-aware dual-domain framework for MIS (O2D-MedNet). First, a degradation-aware dualdomain interaction (DDI) module integrates Fourier - and Wavelet-domain features to suppress degradation-sensitive responses while preserving structural and fine-detail information. Second, a cross-backbone contour-guided fusion (CGF) module enables selective interaction between CNN-based local detail modeling and visual state-space-based global structure modeling, improving boundary accuracy and representation consistency. Third, a gradient-context optimization (GCO) strategy stabilizes training by adaptively adjusting learning dynamics based on loss trends and gradient statistics, mitigating instability caused by class imbalance and heterogeneous sample difficulty. Extensive experiments on six benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over state-of-the-art MIS methods across multiple imaging modalities. Further analyses confirm enhanced sensitivity, structural consistency, and optimization stability, validating the effectiveness of the proposed O2D-MedNet for robust MIS under heterogeneous conditions.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Agent Relay Thinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35219)**  `arXiv:2610.35219`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xu Liu, Jungang Qiao, Guikun Chen, Xiaojuan Qi, Wenguan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing multi-agent collaboration (MAC) methods have primarily explored a group thinking collaboration topology, where agents independently generate solutions and aggregate their outputs. In this work, we investigate an alternative relay thinking topology, where reasoning ownership is dynamically transferred across agents, allowing subsequent agents to inherit and refine an evolving reasoning trajectory. We introduce Multi-Agent Confidence-guided Relay (MACR), a framework that enables dynamical relay collaboration among agents. The key challenge is determining when reasoning should be relayed and which agent should continue the process. method addresses this challenge through confidence-guided relay triggering and a bidding mechanism that selects the next agent based on its confidence. Experiments on four benchmarks show that method outperforms the strongest single-model strategies in accuracy, e.g., +3.3% on MathVista and +3.9% on MMStar, and remains on par with multi-agent strategies while consuming 6.0x less token overhead on HallusionBench andthis http URLanalysis demonstrates that relay collaboration enables heterogeneous agents to progressively integrate complementary insights, combining heterogeneous capabilities across reasoning stages. Our source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Complexity-guided Regularization for Generalizable Human Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35152)**  `arXiv:2610.35152`  `cs.CV`  
  _Gun Ryu, Gi-Mun Um, Won-Sik Cheong, Wonjun Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies on generalizable human Gaussian splatting have achieved the notable progress in synthesizing novel views of unseen human subjects from multi-view images. Despite this progress, existing methods still struggle to represent fine-grained details of human subjects, which inherently involve high geometric and textural complexity. This is because previous methods do not impose a constraint on how densely Gaussians are placed, which often leads to blurry rendering results in complex regions. To address this problem, we propose a complexity-guided regularization scheme. The key idea is to leverage a complexity prior computed via the local entropy in geometric and textural characteristics of a clothed human. This effectively prevents Gaussians from being coarsely placed in complex regions, which facilitates the faithful reconstruction of intricate structures. Furthermore, we sample local patches predominantly from complex regions according to this complexity prior, which allows the model to be sufficiently supervised by local details. Experimental results on benchmark datasets demonstrate that our method delivers state-of-the-art performance while maintaining the real-time inference speed in generalizable human Gaussian splatting. The code and model are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Temporal Slice Learning for AI-Generated Video Detection with 400$\times$ Fewer FLOPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35148)**  `arXiv:2610.35148`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wei Shang, Zhiyuan Li, Shengfeng He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in generative video models have improved visual realism, making detection increasingly dependent on subtle temporal inconsistencies. However, these signals are weak and spatially localized, while most of the video content is redundant. This creates a fundamental mismatch: existing detectors rely on heavy backbones to model full videos, which leads to high computational cost and limited scalability. In this work, we rethink AI-generated video detection from a representation perspective. Instead of modeling entire videos, we restructure them into compact temporal slice representations that isolate informative temporal evolution. We introduce a lightweight framework using frequency-aware temporal slice consistency learning, where informative spatial rows and columns are aggregated over time to form slice-domain inputs. This reparameterization suppresses redundant appearance while preserving discriminative temporal dynamics, enabling efficient and targeted detection. By constructing slices from both high- and low-frequency regions, our method exposes complementary temporal artifacts, including unstable details and abnormal motion smoothness. We instantiate this idea in a Temporal Slice Consistency Network, which integrates task-driven slice localization, hierarchical positional encoding, and a lightweight Transformer to model cross-slice and cross-frequency dependencies. We further introduce AI-Artist, a new benchmark with artist-curated videos from recent high-fidelity generators, including Seedance and Kling. Experiments on GenVideo and AI-Artist show that our method achieves strong performance while requiring over 400$\times$ fewer model-forward FLOPs and 3.6$\times$ faster model inference than the strong video-based baseline ReStraV (2.9$\times$ faster end to end, including preprocessing), enabling scalable and practical deployment.
  </details>

- **[MemVLN: Streaming Episodic Memory for Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35066)**  `arXiv:2610.35066`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yan Wang, Jiangyong Huang, Baoxiong Jia, Siyuan Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language navigation (VLN) requires an embodied agent to follow language instructions through 3D environments. Maintaining persistent episodic memory remains a challenge for VLM-based streaming agents in long-horizon navigation. We present MEMVLN, a streaming episodic memory framework that maintains a bounded bank of compressed perceptual features annotated with timesteps and action-derived relative poses, and produces compact memory tokens via cross-attention. We pair the proposed memory with a frozen pretrained geometry encoder. To learn the memory module, we design a masking-based trajectory summarization task with multi-granularity sub-trajectory decomposition, encouraging route reconstruction exclusively from episodic memory. Under navigation training restricted to the official R2R/RxR trajectories, MEMVLN achieves state-of-the-art performance on most reported metrics on standard VLN-CE benchmarks. Long-horizon analyses further characterize when episodic memory is useful. For physical evaluation, we collect 30 instruction-conditioned episodes in one physical scene, use 24 as demonstrations for post-training, and hold out six for testing. On the held-out episodes, MEMVLN improves success over the VLM baseline from 17% to 40%, and post-training further raises it to 47%.
  </details>

- **[Kronecker-Structured Video Completion with Cross-Fitted Self-Supervised Residual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35065)**  `arXiv:2610.35065`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qi Qi, Yanfei Wang, Yanjun Cui, Jun-e Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tube-wise video completion reconstructs temporal trajectories at spatial locations unobserved in every frame. We propose a two-stage framework combining regularized masked Kronecker product decomposition (KPD) with cross-fitted self-supervised residual learning. First, we estimate a compact structural backbone directly from observed entries using cyclic component-wise least-squares updates, spatial and temporal factor regularization, and regularizer-optimal scale balancing. The exact updates yield a nonincreasing objective. Second, we address the mismatch between in-sample fitting errors and the prediction errors encountered at missing tubes. We partition observed tubes into folds and construct residual targets using backbone models fitted without the corresponding target tubes. A lightweight 3-D convolutional neural network then predicts corrections from the backbone, visible residual context, observation mask, and local detail features. Exact data consistency preserves observed measurements. Experiments include ablations, sensitivity analysis, grayscale and RGB video completion, and structured missing patterns. Our method achieves the highest mean PSNR and SSIM at the higher tested sampling rates on the grayscale benchmark. On eight selected DAVIS RGB sequences, it achieves the highest mean missing-region PSNR and full-frame SSIM and the lowest mean missing-tube temporal-gradient MAE at every evaluated sampling rate.
  </details>

- **[Revealing Imaging Characteristics of Thermal Wakes from Underwater Vehicles Without Cooling-Water Discharge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35038)**  `arXiv:2610.35038`  `cs.CV`  
  _Guoyi Zhang, Yanjin Du, Guangsheng Xu, Zhengyao Zhao, Siyang Chen, Jingjing Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small uncrewed underwater vehicles are proliferating and threaten maritime security, yet evade sonar and magnetic sensors built for larger submarines. Infrared thermal wakes, generated by hydrodynamic displacement that modulates the cold-skin layer, offer a non-acoustic, non-magnetic alternative even without cooling-water discharge. Existing studies, however, rely on idealized simulations that fail to capture real-world backgrounds, offering limited guidance for target-background discrimination. We establish AVP-IR-Wake, the first large-scale, real-world, physically diverse benchmark of 161,485 frames collected over three years. We show that thermal wakes are extended structures of discrete vortices, violating the compactness assumption of conventional targets. We reveal local indistinguishability: individual wake vortices are statistically identical to wind-induced background vortices in temporal, frequency, and sparse domains, sharing low-frequency information and long-range correlations. This challenges the assumptions of compact, transient, high-frequency targets in conventional detection. Yet we establish global morphological separability: although locally inseparable, the wake as a whole is organized by vehicle trajectory, whereas background clutter is organized by wind-driven thermal fronts. This explains why representative detectors fail and shows that reliable detection must shift from local feature extraction to global trajectory-constrained physical modeling. Because the cold-skin layer varies globally and seasonally, forecasting wake observability will require integrating physical oceanography and atmospheric science, linking underwater surveillance to the changing ocean surface.
  </details>

- **[Zero-Shot Monocular Depth Estimation for Visual Perception in Lower-Limb Prostheses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35018)**  `arXiv:2610.35018`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ishi Vijayvergiya, Marc Hölle, Youssef Dawoud, Amir El-Ghoussani, Michele De Vita, Rohan Asthana, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monocular depth estimation has advanced rapidly with the development of foundation models that demonstrate strong zero-shot generalisation across diverse visual domains. However, the applicability of these models to visual perception for lower-limb prosthetics remains largely unexplored. Reliable depth perception is particularly important for prosthesis users, who often experience significant challenges when navigating stairs, uneven terrain, and other complex environments. To address this gap, we introduce the Lower-Limb Prosthesis (LP) dataset, a benchmark designed to evaluate depth perception from an emulated knee-level prosthetic viewpoint. Using this dataset, we evaluate four state-of-the-art monocular depth estimation foundation models. Our results provide the first systematic benchmark of monocular depth estimation performance from a prosthetic viewpoint, while highlighting both the challenges and the potential of deploying foundation models in prosthetic perception systems. The code and dataset are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Pointing Becomes Profiling: Social Bias and Refusal in Text-Steerable Visual Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35017)**  `arXiv:2610.35017`  `cs.CV`  
  _Seyedarmin Azizi, Parsa Razmara, Sajjad Ghiasvand, Ardalan Aryashad, Tina Khezresmaeilzadeh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-steerable visual encoders condition a frozen Vision Transformer on a natural-language prompt and point at the matching image region, producing prompt-conditioned heatmaps and features. We audit the released SteerViT model and find that its steering interface turns language-driven pointing into demographic profiling. On counterfactual face mosaics and real multi-person photographs, "the person" selects a man more often than the base rate, crime words select Black people more often than occupation words do, "the man" among four women selects the Black woman 75.6% of the time on SocialCounterfactuals, and "the monkey" selects Black people. None of them is in the backbone's unsteered saliency, and the crime association is not in the frozen text encoder; the race effects grow with steering strength and enter through the last two cross-attention layers: giving the prompt to those layers alone reproduces them. Four other grounding models, CLIPSeg, Grounding-DINO, SAM 3, and Qwen2.5-VL, show no crime routing and weaker race routing. The model cannot refuse. Its patch softmax must place its mass somewhere, and its heatmap separates a groundable request ("the police officer") from one that is absent ("the pizza") or that would require a stereotyped demographic inference ("the criminal") only at chance (AUROC 0.52). We introduce SafeSteer, which adds a learned abstention output and counterfactual-invariance training on the same 23M steering parameters. SafeSteer separates should-point from should-abstain prompts at AUROC 0.997, generalizes to held-out sensitive and absent words (0.998; at a 0.5 threshold it refuses 99.8% of trained and 62% of held-out sensitive words) with no over-refusal of visible people on the benchmark, and removes most race routing while keeping localization and retrieval within 1 point of the released model. Code, benchmarks, and the curated mosaic sets will be released.
  </details>

- **[More Memory, Better Videos? A Controlled Study of Visual Memory in Long-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35001)**  `arXiv:2610.35001`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuzhuo Chen, Huafeng Shi, Xinyu Wang, Yulong Xu, Yucheng Wang, Haoqin Hong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-video generators increasingly retrieve past visual observations to prevent identity drift. The intuition is compelling: more access to history should make future video more consistent. But an image can be faithful to the past and wrong for the present. Does visual memory always improve the generated story? We investigate this question through VMem-Bench, a two-track diagnostic study. Track-A measures which references are selected from 92 real films (16,548 segments), before generation and without generator confounds. Track-B compares four retrieval policies with no-memory and gold-informed last-seen controls on 30 causally generated stories (2,824 segments) using a shared generator. Retrieval increases entity recall from 0.559 to 0.628-0.693 and improves similarity to past appearances, but decreases precision from 0.977 to 0.915-0.938 and forbidden-entity avoidance from 0.968 to 0.845-0.898. It can also reinforce obsolete appearances after the story changes them. Conversely, high long-gap recall can arise without memory when the prompt itself supplies the appearance; an appearance-stripping analysis exposes this confound. These results distinguish historical fidelity from current-story correctness and show why retrieval relevance or final-video consistency alone does not establish memory utility.
  </details>

- **[Supervising the Visible State: Separating Representation from View Selection in Streaming Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34926)**  `arXiv:2610.34926`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yihao Hu, Pan Wang, Yitong Lin, Zhihao Wen, Deyuan Liu, Hang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An action seen once and the same action seen three times receive the same action-recognition label, so that supervision does not require a model to count. Answers shared across histories that differ in event order, accumulation, or relations leave those distinctions unconstrained; we call this state under-specification. Visible-State Supervision (ViSS) addresses it by training on source-grounded probes of event identity, order, accumulation, relation change, and answer sufficiency; under the same recent-frame interface it improves OVO-Bench over a strict-matched generic temporal supervision baseline while remaining comparable on StreamingBench, with the largest gain in hallucination detection. Test-Time Causal View Search (TVS) then adapts evidence access while keeping the learned model fixed, expanding from recent frames to sparse event and compact history views on demand. The two interventions separate representation from view selection: supervision particularly benefits hallucination detection and causal reasoning, access particularly benefits event recall and forward reasoning. Together they reach the best reported accuracy among the published systems compared here on StreamingBench and OVO-Bench at a fraction of the processed frames of exhaustive selection, and strict replay shows that most of the remaining gap to a three-view oracle is a correct answer that was later replaced. Streaming accuracy depends on what supervision asks the model to keep, what evidence inference exposes, and whether the model keeps a correct answer once it has one.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Data Imbalance in Class Incremental Surgical Instrument Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34925)**  `arXiv:2610.34925`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shifang Zhao, Long Bai, Kun Yuan, Feng Li, Jieming Yu, Wenzhen Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In surgical instrument segmentation, the increasing variety of instruments over time poses a significant challenge for existing neural networks, as they are unable to effectively learn such incremental tasks and suffer from catastrophic forgetting. When learning new data, the model experiences a sharp performance drop on previously learned data. Although several continual learning methods have been proposed for incremental understanding tasks in surgical scenarios, the issue of data imbalance often leads to a strong bias in the segmentation head, resulting in poor performance. Data imbalance can occur in two forms: (i) class imbalance between new and old data, and (ii) class imbalance within the same time point of data. Such imbalances often cause the dominant classes to take over the training process of continual semantic segmentation (CSS). To address this issue, we propose SurgCSS, a novel plug-and-play CSS framework for surgical instrument segmentation under data imbalance. Specifically, we generate realistic surgical backgrounds through inpainting and blend instrument foregrounds with the generated backgrounds in a class-aware manner to balance the data distribution in various scenarios. We further propose the Class Desensitization Loss by employing contrastive learning to correct edge biases caused by data imbalance. Moreover, we dynamically fuse the weight parameters of the old and new models to achieve a better trade-off between the biased and unbiased model weights. To investigate the data imbalance problem in surgical scenarios, we construct a new benchmark for surgical instrument CSS by integrating datasets. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework, achieving significant performance improvement against existing baselines.
  </details>

- **[LayoutMamba: Efficient Document Layout Analysis with Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34879)**  `arXiv:2610.34879`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kehua Chen, Mingyi Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Document layout analysis (DLA) faces a long-standing dilemma: existing methods either capture rich local details at prohibitive computational cost (e.g., Transformer-based approaches with quadratic complexity) or achieve efficient global modeling but sacrifice fine-grained structural awareness (e.g., state-space models). This trade-off hinders deployment in resource-constrained scenarios. We present LayoutMamba, a hybrid framework that synergistically combines sparse-attention Transformers for localized feature extraction (O(n) complexity) and the recurrent state-space mechanism of Mamba for dilution-free long-range dependency modeling across the 2D document canvas. Our two-stage paradigm first uses a lightweight Transformer encoder to hierarchically distill salient local semantics; second, Mamba propagates global contextual dependencies without attention dilution, enabling precise, efficient, and scalable layout understanding. We further identify a critical yet overlooked issue in existing DLA benchmarks: the inherent low-overlap distribution bias of layout objects, which we mitigate through a novel Geometric Overlap Penalty Loss (GOPLoss), a dual-optimization objective that minimizes redundant bounding box overlaps and encourages geometrically consistent predictions. Extensive experiments on PubLayNet and D4LA show that LayoutMamba surpasses state-of-the-art AP on PubLayNet with 8x fewer parameters and maintains leading performance on D4LA, ranking top-3 among published methods, achieving an unprecedented balance between accuracy, efficiency, and scalability. Our work pioneers a cognition-inspired paradigm for document analysis, advancing practical deployment in edge computing and resource-limited environments.
  </details>

- **[Efficient Multi-modal Video Fusion Network with Dynamic Region-Guided Temporal Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34765)**  `arXiv:2610.34765`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jingxue Huang, Xiaosong Li, Yi Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-modal video fusion aims to generate temporally coherent videos with salient targets and rich structural details by integrating complementary information from heterogeneous sensors. However, existing video fusion methods commonly rely on optical flow estimation and feature warping for inter-frame alignment and cross-frame information aggregation to maintain temporal consistency, thereby introducing considerable computational overhead and making the fusion performance vulnerable to flow estimation errors. To address these limitations, this paper proposes an efficient multi-modal video fusion network with dynamic region-guided temporal interaction. Specifically, the proposed spatio-temporal interaction module leverages the Test-Time Training paradigm to model temporal dependencies with linear complexity. Building on this design, the module restricts temporal modeling to key dynamic regions in the video sequence, as static background regions provide limited temporal cues. By excluding low-variation tokens from unnecessary temporal interaction, this selective strategy contributes to improved fusion quality and temporal consistency. In addition, leveraging the strong ability of pretrained MAE features to capture global structural dependencies, we introduce a feature-level temporal consistency constraint to suppress local flickering by aligning the inter-frame variation directions of the fused and source videos. We further propose a new MAE-based temporal metric that quantifies directional distance between their temporal variations within dynamic regions. Experimental results demonstrate that, compared with existing state-of-the-art methods, our method achieves superior fusion quality and temporal consistency on multiple benchmark datasets, while exhibiting high computational efficiency under edge-device deployment settings.
  </details>

- **[From Sentinel-2 to 1-m Imagery: Real-World $\times 10$ Cross-Sensor Super-Resolution via Visual Autoregressive Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34742)**  `arXiv:2610.34742`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tailai Chen, Yuluxin Fu, Jie Deng, Shu Luo, Yanan Wu, Danfeng Hong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reconstructing 1-m imagery from Sentinel-2 combines two difficulties. The input and the reference are observed by different satellites, which differ in spatial resolution, spectral response, radiometry, acquisition time, and local geometry, so no synthetic degradation operator links them. The required magnification of ten in each spatial dimension also far exceeds the factors of two to four at which remote sensing super-resolution is usually developed. We study this real-world $\times 10$ problem using YREB-SR, which pairs 2022 Sentinel-2 RGB composites with 2022 Jilin-1 RGB references resampled to a 1-m grid over the Yangtze River Economic Belt. The benchmark contains 11,559 geographically matched scene pairs, with 6,409, 2,135, and 3,015 assigned to training, validation, and testing, respectively. We develop CrossVAR, a visual autoregressive model that predicts the 1-m latent as a nine-scale hierarchy conditioned on the Sentinel-2 input and estimates the remaining quantization residual with a diffusion refiner run without injected noise. Complete 1600 $\times$ 1600 scenes are reconstructed in overlapping windows that start on the Sentinel-2 pixel grid, as every training patch does, and every window is decoded at the size on which the decoder was trained before the windows are blended with Gaussian weights. On all 3,015 test scenes, CrossVAR achieves 27.60 dB PSNR, 0.7725 SSIM, 0.1451 LPIPS, and 7.15 FID, the best value of each metric among the compared methods. These results establish a strong autoregressive baseline for large-scale cross-sensor reconstruction. To facilitate reproducibility, the source code and implementation are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[StreamVibe: Proactive Video Agents for Evolving User Intent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34662)**  `arXiv:2610.34662`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chengzhi Liu, Yuxiang Lai, Yuzhe Yang, Yanqing Liu, Jiheng Hou, Yue Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models are shifting from offline passive understanding toward online proactive interaction. However, prevailing methods largely rely on explicit requests while miss the implicit user intent (e.g., "where should I click next?" while a user navigate a website), meanwhile fail to update the state simultaneously as user requests might update continuously. These limitations make them less suited to intent-aware interaction in practical. To address these challenges, we introduce StreamVibe-Bench, a new benchmark serving as testbed for user intent interaction, with 4.1K evaluation samples and 85K training samples spanning explicit, implicit, and dynamic intents. Building on this benchmark, we develop StreamVibe-VL, a streaming proactive model that infers implicit user intents from continuous observations and dynamically tailors its interactions as these intents evolve. We further introduce StreamVibe-Harness, which maintains user intents as persistent states with a fleixble lifecycles via a hierarchical memory to track the latest user intent and environmental state. Our experiments reveal three key findings: (i) StreamVibe-VL preserves strong general video understanding while improving response timing and answer quality by 10.7% over other baselines; (ii) existing proactive models remain limited in understanding user intent and deciding when to respond, especially under implicit and dynamic intents; and (iii) StreamVibe-Harness further improves response consistency and adaptation over sustained interaction, resulting in a 12.3% gain in proactive interaction.
  </details>

- **[Do Words Matter? What Text Contributes to Few and Full-Shot Vision-Language Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34610)**  `arXiv:2610.34610`  `cs.CV`  
  _Arda Baris Basaran, Mesut Ceylan, Elgar Fleisch, Filipe Barata_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language anomaly detectors describe normal and abnormal appearance with text, and their gains are commonly credited to the semantic content of these descriptions. This attribution is plausible in the zero-shot setting, but few- and full-shot detectors interpose a trained visual adapter, and in that setting it has not been isolated. Changing a description changes both its meaning and its geometry, so accuracy comparisons cannot separate the two. We ask what a fixed pair of text embeddings, or anchors, contributes to such a detector: semantic content, effective scale, or joint anchor geometry. For a single anchor pair, the answer is effective scale alone. The score reads the anchors only through their difference, and a trained adapter can rotate to follow any replacement direction, leaving that direction, and with it the linguistic content, unidentifiable. We prove that replacement anchors with the same difference-vector length permit exactly the same probability maps and attainable losses. Across seven medical datasets, three vision backbones, both supervision regimes, and three adapter families, scale-matched random and orthogonal anchors track correct prompts; breaking the scale match opens a gap that restoring it closes without text. The same pattern holds in MVFA and when MadCLIP's learned prompts are replaced by a text-free learned scale. When one adapter serves many categories, the answer expands to their joint arrangement. Rotating all category axes together changes nothing, as predicted. For the tested CLIP prompts, these axes are nearly collinear, and meaning-free rotations that spread them apart improve performance on both industrial benchmarks. Even under frozen zero-shot transfer, a random anchor frame transfers as well as the prompt frame: coordinate consistency, not meaning. What these detectors read from their prompts is scale and arrangement, not semantic content.
  </details>

- **[VTC-AUDIT: An Interventional Reliability Audit for Visual-Token Compression under Paired Image Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34545)**  `arXiv:2610.34545`  `cs.CV`  
  _Isaac_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual-token compression is usually evaluated by clean accuracy and efficiency, but these averages do not reveal failures that compression introduces under an image shift where the uncompressed model stays correct. We introduce VTC-AUDIT, which estimates this incremental risk only on pairs where the full model is correct on both images and the compressed model is correct on the clean image. The four arms then cross two binary choices: which image selects the retained token positions, and which image supplies the visual features. We audit three open 8-9B vision-language models on four tasks and eight pre-specified shifts. We evaluate two training-free compressors---hard-pruning DivPrune and prune-and-merge ApET---at a matched 25% retained-position budget, producing 708,000 inference records. Reselecting positions on the shifted image, rather than reusing the clean-image positions, raises the failure rate in all six model-compressor settings. The increase is 3.04-9.60 percentage points for DivPrune and 2.05-4.20 percentage points for ApET, with every 95% interval above zero. Repeating the audit on a separately resampled second manifest reproduces all six increases. In a pooled monitor audit across the four benchmarks, support Jaccard instability fails the practical AUROC criterion of 0.65 in all six settings, whereas clean compressed-model uncertainty clears it. Thus, support turnover is a weak ranking signal under the audited benchmark mixture, while clean compressed-model uncertainty is substantially more informative. These results suggest that future visual-token compression evaluations should report conditional reliability alongside clean accuracy and efficiency.
  </details>

- **[No Cones, No Duals: A Fast and Scalable Solver for NRSfM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34403)**  `arXiv:2610.34403`  `cs.CV`  
  _Suryansh Kumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Non-rigid structure from motion (NRSfM) aims to recover camera motion and time-varying 3D shape from tracked 2D image measurements. Recent high-performing methods show that shape-space factorization remains highly effective for this task. These methods often use costly solvers for two key steps. First, a semidefinite programming (SDP) cone solver to perform Euclidean correction. Next, an alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) solver to recover the low-rank shape. These steps often scale poorly to long sequences and dense point tracks. We propose a fast, scalable NRSfM solver that avoids these costly steps. For rotation estimation, we introduce a differentiable Euclidean correction that directly optimizes small factors for each sequence. A row-polar projection then maps each estimated camera block to the closest row-orthonormal matrix. For shape estimation, we introduce an unrolled spectral-proximal solver. It combines reprojection-gradient steps with weighted singular-value shrinkage in the reshuffled shape domain. This solver further uses FISTA-style restart and continuation. The spectral shrinkage operator itself is classical, but we embed it in a fixed differentiable solver, while preserving NRSfM's geometric constraints. The proposed approach is category-agnostic, requires no external training data, and maps naturally to modern accelerator-friendly linear algebra primitives. Across sparse, dense, noisy, missing-data, and long-sequence benchmarks, our solver achieves substantial speedups with competitive reconstruction accuracy, including 7x-1000x runtime improvements on dense sequences with 28,887 tracked points.
  </details>

- **[Birth-Death Structural Learning for 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34344)**  `arXiv:2610.34344`  `cs.CV`  
  _Quan Tran Hong, Long Nguyen-Chi, Binh T. Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has become a standard representation for real-time novel-view synthesis. Yet, its quality-efficiency trade-off still relies heavily on adaptive density control. Existing cloning, splitting, and pruning rules use proxy signals, such as position gradients, opacity, and image-space error. Although effective in practice, these signals do not directly answer the structural question behind density control: where should new Gaussians be allocated to reduce the multi-view reconstruction loss, and which existing primitives can be pruned with minimal penalty. To address this, we propose first-variation birth-death control for 3DGS, a principled approach that replaces heuristic structural decisions with a variational score derived from the reconstruction loss. At each structural update, we freeze the current Gaussian population, attach auxiliary mass coordinates to each primitive, and differentiate the multiview loss with respect to these coordinates. We demonstrate that this score is a coordinate of the first variation of the Splat Regression Model, giving a rigorous local descent surrogate for birth-death moves: high-score Gaussians are selected as birth sites, while low-score, low-opacity Gaussians are selected for removal. Across standard 3DGS benchmarks, integrating this score into 3DGS-MCMC yields state-of-the-art reconstruction quality and outperforms existing methods, delivering the most significant gains under tight Gaussian budgets. Ultimately, our work provides compelling evidence that gradient-based variational metrics offer a principled alternative to proxy heuristics in 3DGS.
  </details>

- **[Learning Discriminative Prototypes via Boundary Repulsion MixUp for Object Re-Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34305)**  `arXiv:2610.34305`  `cs.CV`  
  _Athena Psalta, Vasileios Tsironis, Konstantinos Karantzalos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object re-identification requires embeddings that are robust to intra-class variation in viewpoint, illumination, and occlusion, yet preserve sharp separation between visually similar identities. The hardest errors arise precisely where confusable identities meet, a region that pairwise losses shape only implicitly. We present PBR-ReID, a prototype-based framework that makes this inter-class geometry an explicit learning target. Each identity is represented by a learnable prototype jointly optimized with a DiNOv3 backbone and the resulting prototype similarity matrix reveals, at every training step, which identities are most confusable. On this signal we build Boundary Repulsion MixUp (BRM), a geometry-aware regularizer that converts standard MixUp's weakness into a discriminative signal. Rather than interpolating samples across confusable identities, which blurs the boundaries that matter most, BRM constructs an explicit boundary proxy between each confusable pair and repels their features away from it, converting boundary ambiguity into a discriminative signal. We adopt a two-stage training protocol consisting of a prototype warmup phase followed by EMA-stabilized joint fine-tuning. Extensive experiments on person and vehicle ReID benchmarks demonstrate strong performance, with thorough ablations confirming each component's contribution. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EditDistill: Is It Possible to Guide Video Editing with Image Editing?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34303)**  `arXiv:2610.34303`  `cs.CV`  
  _Guojun Lei, Hong Li, Hongbing Yang, Lixue Gong, Chi Wang, Zheng Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in image editing have enabled fine-grained and highly controllable visual modifications. However, achieving similar levels of controllability in video editing remains an open challenge, particularly for precise local edits, dynamic temporal effects, and consistent lighting acrossthis http URLthis paper, we propose EditDistill, a framework that distills image-level visual transformations from edits to guide videothis http URL, given a source video and an edit prompt, we first generate an edited version of the initial video frame using an off-the-shelf image editing model. We then distill the visual transformation of this original-edited image pair into a compact representation termed an editthis http URLdescriptor is injected into a video diffusion model through our lightweight fusion module, which steers the denoising process toward the desired edit. To align the video trajectory with the demonstrated image edit while preserving motion and layout, we train with edit-descriptor alignment and DINO structural consistency losses, and derive an equivalent inference-time guidance formulation under flowthis http URLexperiments demonstrate that our method achieves strong performance on standard benchmarks and produces high-quality video editing results across diverse scenarios. For more details and videos, please refer to the anonymous webpage:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[ChronoReplay: One Answer Interface Is Not Enough for Streaming Spatial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34283)**  `arXiv:2610.34283`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yihao Hu, Yitong Lin, Pan Wang, Zhihao Wen, Hang Wang, Yiwen Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training a spatial VLM to answer which side an object lies on does not require it to preserve the metric displacement that determines that answer, while supervision for a distance estimate may leave categorical relations largely unconstrained. When post-training emphasizes one readout, improvements can therefore concentrate on that interface while transfer to others remains weak or even degrades; we call this observed specialization interface lock-in. ChronoReplay addresses it by replaying heterogeneous spatial supervision as chronologically ordered episodes, retaining source-native targets, and balancing supervision across families. Under the same observation access, a single ChronoReplay checkpoint improves OVO-S-Bench, OST-Bench, and UCS-Bench together, including a 13.41-point gain on OST-Bench, and achieves the highest three-benchmark mean among the supervision settings compared here. Same-data controls further separate the ingredients of this transfer: answer serialization redistributes gains across OST and UCS, family-balanced learning improves over ordinary joint SFT and natural turn weighting, and chronological replay exceeds a shifted-order control by about 2.41 OST points across two paired seeds. Adaptive frame selection then changes evidence access while keeping the trained model fixed, providing an additional inference-time gain at lower visual cost. Streaming spatial performance depends on what distinctions supervision preserves, how heterogeneous histories are organized during learning, and what causal evidence inference makes available at test time.
  </details>

- **[CropReward: A Human-Preference Benchmark and Reward Model for Aesthetic Image Cropping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34221)**  `arXiv:2610.34221`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiangfei Sheng, Xiaofeng Pan, Leida Li, Qian Zhang, Biao Wang, Johan Wagemans, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aesthetic image cropping (AIC) aims to improve image composition and aesthetic quality by removing extraneous or distracting content. Over the past decade, AIC methods have evolved from specialized vision models to multimodal large language models(MLLMs). Reliable evaluation that reflects human preferences is essential for benchmarking AIC methods and guiding model optimization. However, the current AIC evaluation relies largely on geometry-based metrics such as intersection over union (IoU), as well as image aesthetic assessment(IAA) models. Although inconsistencies with human preferences have long been observed, the robustness and reliability of these evaluation criteria remain underexplored. To bridge this gap, we first construct CropBench, a large-scale benchmark comprising 106,995 human preference pairs for 1,019 images, with each candidate set containing the original image and candidate crops produced by 14 AIC methods. A systematic study on CropBench reveals that the existing criteria, ranging from IoU to off-the-shelf IAA assessment models, only exhibit limited alignment with human preferences. Building upon CropBench, we further propose CropReward, an MLLM-based reward model that evaluates a candidate crop by comparing with the original uncropped image and outputs a score indicating explicit aesthetic improvement or degradation relative to the original. In addition, we employ CropReward to construct CropReward-300k, the largest AIC dataset to date, and further develop CropAlign, a preference-aligned AIC model trained solely with CropReward supervision, without human-annotated crop boxes. Experimental results demonstrate that CropReward aligns with human preferences significantly better than existing criteria. CropAlign also surpasses the state-of-the-art AIC methods, demonstrating the advantage of CropReward in guiding AIC model optimization.
  </details>

- **[GeoPrompt: Geometry-Conditioned Prompt Learning for Underwater Open-Vocabulary Instance Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34190)**  `arXiv:2610.34190`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shanshan Han, Yuxi Wang, Can Qin, Yujie Chen, Cong Pan, Haitao Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-vocabulary instance segmentation (OVIS) provides a promising solution for marine perception by recognizing and segmenting categories beyond predefined label spaces.
