On our primary collection of 1,700 questions from two benchmarks, Jev's accuracy varies by less than two percentage points, yet its cumulative failure set expands by 27--29\% relative to the original order, with first failures continuing into the final presentation. This loss of initial successes is substantially larger than under fixed-order repetition, even for highly repeatable Jev reproductions. Nor does Jev show a general advantage over the LLM baselines in preserving correct choices. Aggregation offers limited protection: voting yields modest gains for Jev, while even unanimous answers are frequently wrong. These results expose a gap between repeatable decisions and decisions that remain correct across equivalent presentations, showing why stable accuracy alone cannot establish resistance to selection bias.
  </details>

- **[SEMA-Auto: Sighted and Efficient Multi-Turn Attack with Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35743)**  `arXiv:2610.35743`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingqian Feng, Xiaodong Liu, Weiwei Yang, Chenliang Xu, Jialin Song, Qiyao Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing jailbreak attackers fall into two classes: static planners fix their single- or multi-turn adversarial plans in advance, remaining simple but losing sight of the victim's replies, wasting turns and limiting transferability; interactive generators read each victim reply to generate the next-turn attack but require in-loop evaluators for success signals or hints, incurring substantial costs and inflated performance. Bridging this gap, we propose SEMA-Auto, a sighted and efficient multi-turn attacker with autonomy, interacting with the victim model and terminating the session by itself upon success. To stabilize its behaviors and boost its capabilities, SEMA-Auto is trained in two stages. Synthetic bootstrap tuning installs the notion of attack targets and conforming decisions by fine-tuning the base model with bootstrapped trajectories. Decision-supervised reinforcement learning then optimizes two objectives jointly yet separately, reinforcing the attack with higher jailbreak reward while supervising the decision at each turn against an absolute target. Our method achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) attack success rates (ASR) across multiple datasets, victims, and judges, outperforming all static and interactive baselines, within almost the fewest turns, demonstrating both effectiveness and efficiency. For example, SEMA-Auto performs a $76.0\%$ ASR@1 within an average of $3.0$ turns against DeepSeek-V4-Flash on AdvBench, while the best static baseline is $54.8\%$ with fixed $7$ turns and the best interactive baseline is $43.8\%$ in $3.7$ turns. Against safety-hardened frontier models, GPT-oss-120B, GPT-5.4, Claude-Haiku-4.5, and GPT-6-Astra, our counter-trained attacker achieves an average $59.6\%$ ASR@1 on HarmBench, $13.8\%$ over prior SOTA. Our code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Evaluating DeepSeek V4.1 Flash-1017 for loss of control and harmful manipulation risks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35671)**  `arXiv:2610.35671`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yao Huang, Zhiyuan Zeng, Yu Wang, Zhitong Zhu, Dingcheng Duan, Hao Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As frontier language models grow increasingly capable and autonomous, independent third-party evaluations are critical for characterizing their systemic safety risks. While closed-source releases are routinely accompanied by comprehensive system cards, open-weight models receive less scrutiny regarding frontier risks. To address this gap, we present an independent safety evaluation of DeepSeek V4.1 Flash-1017, the latest version of V4.1 Flash that departs from V4 with a new architecture, scaled-up training, and strong agentic capabilities. Drawing on the EU General-Purpose AI Code of Practice, we evaluate the model across 34 benchmarks, including 5 in-house suites, spanning evaluation integrity, loss of control, and harmful manipulation. Conforming to the AEF-1 standard, we compare V4.1 Flash-1017 against V4.1 Flash, earlier DeepSeek V4 models, and leading closed-source systems (GPT-6 Astra, GPT-5.6 Sol, and Gemini 3.8 Flash). Overall, V4.1 Flash-1017 shows substantial safety improvements over the V4 generation and approaches or matches leading closed-source baselines on several dimensions, while still exhibiting notable behaviors that warrant continued attention. Building on our findings, we highlight three key observations and recommendations. First, because safety can shift and even regress across minor updates, developers should integrate safety evaluation into incremental releases. Second, as final-turn outputs and end-state checks alone cannot reliably detect loss-of-control behaviors, deployments require process-level observability across both reasoning traces and action logs. Third, because public benchmarks elicit strong evaluation awareness and face data contamination risks, developing private benchmarks is essential for trustworthy frontier evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Skill Evolution for Coding Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35616)**  `arXiv:2610.35616`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jun Gao, Yun Peng, Changhai Zhou, Yuhan Zhu, Xiangyu Zeng, Shiyang Zhang Yuhua Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skills provide agents with reusable experience to guide future decisions without modifying the underlying model. However, coding trajectories are often entangled with task- and repository-specific contexts, making it challenging to distill procedural skills that generalize across diverse coding environments. To address this challenge, we present Hierarchical Skill Evolution (HSE), which progressively distills procedural knowledge from coding trajectories through hierarchical evidence aggregation. HSE first extracts task-level evidence from individual trajectories, then aggregates recurring evidence within the same repository to form repository-specific skill candidates, and finally consolidates consistent patterns across repositories into transferable skills across repositories. The evolution is driven by an external writer-evaluator loop that generates and assesses skills while keeping the underlying coding model frozen. The loop externally updates the writer and evaluator policies to improve subsequent skill generation and assessment. This hierarchy progressively filters task-specific details while preserving procedures that generalize across diverse coding environments. Successful trajectories yield reusable guidance for future tasks, while failed trajectories reveal recurring failure modes that guide avoidance and recovery. We evaluate HSE on SWE-bench Verified and DeepSWE, using GLM-5.2 across Pi and Claude Code harnesses and GPT-5.5 and Opus 4.8 with Pi. Across both benchmarks, 10 skills evolved solely from SWE-Gym improve coding performance across all evaluated settings, demonstrating transfer across benchmarks, coding models, and agent harnesses. We further evolve the skill library from target-benchmark trajectories without verifier feedback, relying solely on the learned evaluator for candidate assessment and achieving additional gains through benchmark-tailored skills.
  </details>

- **[EXCISE: Locating and Excising Distracting Sentences in Medical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35558)**  `arXiv:2610.35558`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yerim Hong, Sohyun Son, Heeseung Chung, Mujeen Sung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models are increasingly considered for clinical decision support, where their answers should be grounded in clinical evidence and remain robust to irrelevant information. Prior work shows that a single irrelevant sentence can lower the accuracy of medical models, but not where inside the model its effect can be removed, or how to do so without knowing which sentence it is. We find that blocking attention to a known distractor restores the answer, whereas head masking can suppress correct answers. We also show that the model's hidden states mark the distractor as the sentence that least fits the case even when the model follows it. To remove a distractor's effect without knowing which sentence it is, we propose EXCISE, an inference-time method that blocks the sentence most influencing the answer, but only if the model's hidden states mark it as inconsistent with the case. On MedQA-TD (MedQA with Targeted Distractors), a benchmark we construct in which each distractor targets a specific wrong option, EXCISE gains 16.7-22.8 accuracy points at a 1.5-2.1-point cost on the same questions without distractors, outperforming prompting, rewriting, and steering baselines for all three models. Its gains extend to MedDistractQA and BiasMedQA, as well as chain-of-thought generation.
  </details>

- **[MANTIS: Multi-Axis News Transformations for Instance-Wise Stress Testing and Worst-Case Training in Fake News Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35475)**  `arXiv:2610.35475`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dexin Liu, Fan Yang, Zimu Wang, Xunfei Zhu, Bolin Ren, Bingyan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated fake news detection is vital on social media, where fake news is easily rewritten to fool detectors. However, existing approaches typically rely on superficial writing cues or single-style text rewrites, resulting in poor robustness under diverse cosmetic modifications. We construct MANTIS-Bench, a multi-axis presentation-attack benchmark that pairs original news with rewritten views across writing style, emotional expression, and dissemination framing. We further propose MANTIS, an instance-adaptive adversarial framework enabling dynamic worst-case training tailored to each news article with zero inference-time overhead. This reframes fake news defense from heuristic format smoothing into targeted, fact-invariant credibility assessment. Experiments on MANTIS-Bench show that MANTIS reduces the Union Attack Success Rate (UASR) by 3.55 percentage points relative to SheepDog on GossipCop and consistently outperforms prior single-axis defenses on Macro-F1 while maintaining competitive clean accuracy, demonstrating the effectiveness of multi-axis and instance-adaptive worst-case training.
  </details>

- **[ImgMT: Wikipedia-based Dataset For Evaluating Text-in-Image Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35416)**  `arXiv:2610.35416`  `cs.CL`  
  _Šárka Uramová, Petar Kirilov Petrov, Josef Jon, Ondřej Bojar, Michal Novák_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-in-image translation aims to translate all textual content embedded in an image. In Text Image Translation (TIT), the output is translated text; In-Image Machine Translation (IIMT) additionally renders the translation back into the image. We introduce ImgMT, a massively multilingual dataset and benchmark mined from localized SVG illustrations on Wikimedia Commons, focusing on diagrams, maps, and infographics. ImgMT contains 35,612 aligned image pairs grouped into 1,855 image sets, spanning 129 languages, 20+ writing systems, and 4,886 language pairs.We propose a multi-level evaluation protocol that measures source-side text-region detection and OCR accuracy alongside target-side translation quality. We benchmark several multimodal large language models and a dedicated TIT system in English->X and X->English settings, and find that the source language and writing system substantially affect TIT quality. Finally, we show that ImgMT is not well suited for isolating the contribution of visual information in multimodal translation.
  </details>

- **[MechAlign: Evaluating Semantic Alignment at the Project Level in Mathematical Formalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35269)**  `arXiv:2610.35269`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hao Shen, Ruichen Qiu, Lihong Zhi, Xiao-Shan Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lean verifies that a formal proof establishes its stated proposition under declared assumptions, but does not check whether that proposition faithfully expresses the mathematical claim in a manuscript. Assessing this correspondence requires interpreting the main theorem through its project dependencies, which may be scattered throughout a large development. We introduce MechAlign, a framework for project-level semantic alignment review that combines formal dependency extraction with LLM-based assessment. Its extraction tool, LeanTrace, constructs a statement dependency graph from elaborated declarations, retaining definition bodies and structure constraints while excluding proof bodies and stopping at an explicit library boundary. The reviewer uses this context to investigate alignment and report discrepancies with localized evidence. We conduct a preliminary review of 201 main results from 173 OpenAI Math projects, using supplied informal descriptions and available paper context. A hyperbolic-group case illustrates how dependency inspection localizes a missing dimensional constraint relative to the manuscript, consistent with the upstream declaration of formalization scope. We also construct LeanReviewBench, comprising 501 fixed claim-statement-context triples from OpenAI Math, Fermat's Last Theorem, and Kakeya, with description provenance recorded. The resource supports controlled comparisons of more project-level semantic alignment evaluations.
  </details>

- **[The Lay of the Land in Idiom-Translation Quality Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35261)**  `arXiv:2610.35261`  `cs.CL`  
  _Malik Marmonier, Benoît Sagot, Rachel Bawden_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Idioms have long been a challenge for machine translation, and while large language models (LLMs) have proven increasingly adept at handling them, whether quality estimation (QE) systems have kept pace and can reliably score idiomatic renderings of these expressions above faulty literal ones remains unclear. We introduce the first parallel dataset pairing idiom-bearing English sentences with both literal and idiomatic translations across nine target languages, augmented into multi-sentence segments and a graded literality continuum. Using this dataset, we benchmark eleven conventional QE metrics from the COMET and MetricX families and ten LLMs from the Qwen-3, Gemma-4 and OLMo-3 families used as GEMBA-style judges. We find that most systems, especially older metrics and smaller models, display a marked literality bias, rating literal translations of idioms above idiomatic ones. Higher parameter counts and newer training sets appear to reduce this bias to an extent; nevertheless, of all the benchmarked systems, only MetricX-24-Hybrid (XL, XXL) and Gemma-4-31B consistently rate idiomatic translations of idioms above literal ones, and only Gemma-4-31B reliably ranks intermediate degrees of idiomaticity and literality. Our findings caution against deploying standard QE metrics in domains rich in figurative language, where higher scores might be rewarding undesirable literalism.
  </details>

- **[Do Language Models Understand Igbo Culture? Beyond Multiple Choice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35168)**  `arXiv:2610.35168`  `cs.CL`  
  _Blessing Ngozi Uzor, Valdini Douglace Lemofouet, Paula Chikaodinaka Anyanwu, Tassallah Abdullahi, Clemencia Siro, Idris Abdulmumin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The value of language models for any community depends on whether a model actually understands the culture of the people it intends to serve. We use the Igbo language as a case study. Existing benchmarks test translated Western ideas, lacking depth. To address this, we introduce CAB-Igbo, a benchmark developed from indigenous cultural sources and verified by native speakers across six domains. We evaluated diverse models under varying conditions, incorporating regional personas. Performance was assessed using multiple-choice accuracy and human evaluations across Epistemic Fidelity, Representational Richness, Linguistic Quality, and Pragmatic Proficiency. Results reveal a stark dissociation between language fluency and cultural knowledge. Through statistical testing, we show that for most models, the severe lack of Epistemic Fidelity and Representational Richness is already present in English and does not significantly worsen when switching to Igbo. Instead, native language prompting mainly degrades Linguistic Quality and Pragmatic Proficiency. Gemini 2.0 Flash is the only model whose cultural knowledge scores improve under Igbo prompting. We find that current models struggle to demonstrate the knowledge needed for true alignment with Igbo, and multiple-choice formats allow pattern-matching to appear as understanding. Furthermore, automated judges agree with humans on linguistic quality but fail on epistemic fidelity. Ultimately, cultural evaluation must be built from community knowledge, establishing human evaluation as the gold standard to prevent misrepresenting low-resource communities globally.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Evaluation for Principled Generalization Using a Kripkean Challenge to Meaning as Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35111)**  `arXiv:2610.35111`  `cs.CL`  
  _Niyar R Barman, Omar Naim, Nicholas Asher_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models operationalize an intuitive, view of meaning as use. We argue that it faces a rule-following challenge. Using Kripke's interpretation of Wittgenstein, we argue that finite histories of use do not determine a unique rule for future extension. This reframes the debate about OOD generalization but also about NLP evaluation; benchmarks that measure performance on fixed use cases or local robustness, we argue, do not by themselves establish semantic competence. To evaluate whether models preserve rules and concepts under principled domain extension, we propose to evaluate two capacities, central to this ability: abstraction and recursion; and we offer an experimental paradigm for testing both.
  </details>

- **[Ask Before You Answer: A Question-Driven Evidence Reasoning Architecture (QERA) for In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35083)**  `arXiv:2610.35083`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jinhu Qi, Minda Hu, Wentao Zhang, Zixing Song, Irwin King_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI assistants are increasingly asked to reason over a user's own web traces: group chats, community threads, activity logs and revision histories. On CL-bench Life, a benchmark of such real-life context learning, frontier LLMs solve fewer than one task in five. We find that the request is not the specification: over 80% of the benchmark's rubrics share less than 60% of their content words with the final request, as for a rule set two turns earlier or every record that meets the user's criterion, and LLMs answering directly pass only 67% of these implicit rubrics against 81% of the others. We propose QERA, a Question-driven Evidence Reasoning Architecture that asks before it answers. QERA first turns the request into a requirement checklist and typed information needs, filtered by code for faithfulness to the user's words; it then answers from the whole conversation, asks the same questions again with quotes verified by code, and applies a guarded local repair. It needs no training or rubrics and makes about four LLM calls per task. On all 405 tasks with four frontier LLMs, QERA attains the highest solving rate on every LLM (21.0% on average, against 18.2% for the strongest of three inference-time baselines and 14.6% for direct answering) and leads every baseline significantly when pooled across LLMs, while using fewer tokens than PSCI and 4-5% of the tokens of Ctx2Skill. Ablations on GPT-6-Luna show that the gain is settled before the answer, by a fixed answer protocol (+3.8 rubric points) and the request-specific questions (+1.6), and that it is concentrated on implicit rubrics, with checking afterwards refining mainly numeric requirements.
  </details>

- **[From Questions to Queries to Insights: A Schema-Grounded Agentic Workflow for Natural-Language Crash Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35054)**  `arXiv:2610.35054`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jingwen Zhu, Keshu Wu, Steven T. Parker, Bin Ran, David A. Noyce_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transportation agencies maintain extensive crash databases to identify safety priorities, evaluate countermeasures, and guide resource allocation. Making these data accessible to practitioners requires flexible ways to translate analytical questions into reliable results. Existing dashboards support predefined analyses, while additional requests often depend on specialists who understand database queries, coded fields, and agency conventions. This study presents CrashDB-QA, a fully local, schema-grounded agentic workflow that enables natural-language analysis of crash databases. Specialized components interpret questions, generate and validate read-only SQL, execute queries, and explain the results. A shared knowledge base grounds query construction in the agency's data dictionary and safety-analysis conventions, while recorded intermediate outputs support inspection and auditing. An optional extension, CrashDB-QA+, checks the alignment among the question, query, and execution result and supplies evidence for iterative repair. We evaluate the workflow on a frozen snapshot of 4.4 million Wisconsin crash records using a new benchmark of 300 questions across ten analytical categories and nine local open-weight models. Domain grounding substantially improves query accuracy, and a code-specialized 7B model provides the best accuracy-latency balance among the tested models. Compositional analyses remain more difficult than routine retrieval, particularly comparisons between groups or periods. Verification improves performance when the Judge uses authoritative field definitions and the Refiner receives execution evidence, whereas additional checking alone offers no consistent benefit. These findings demonstrate the potential of locally deployed, domain-grounded workflows to broaden access to crash analysis and identify the reasoning and verification capabilities needed for reliable use.
  </details>

- **[SimPOTrans: Self-Adaptation for Offline-to-Simultaneous Speech Translation via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35042)**  `arXiv:2610.35042`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haotian Tan, Hiroki Ouchi, Sakriani Sakti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simultaneous speech translation (SimulST) aims to translate ongoing speech with low latency while preserving translation quality. Recent studies have adopted large language models (LLMs) for SimulST and formulated it as a multi-turn dialogue task for efficient generation, achieving state-of-the-art performance. However, existing dialogue-style SimulST approaches typically rely on specialized training schemes to acquire simultaneous translation behavior, either using synthesized read/write trajectories, which may provide suboptimal training signals, or jointly optimizing translation quality and latency, whose competing objectives may cause optimization instability. In this paper, we introduce SimPOTrans, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that enables offline speech translation models to self-adapt to dialogue-style SimulST. Through a simple two-stage RL post-training pipeline, SimPOTrans rewards an offline model for exhibiting translation behaviors beneficial to simultaneous scenarios when given partial speech inputs, thereby enabling offline-to-simultaneous self-adaptation without synthesized read/write trajectories or explicit latency optimization. At inference time, SimPOTrans requires no external decision policy and enables flexible quality--latency control by simply adjusting the chunk size of speech input. Experiments on the ACL 60/60 and MCIF benchmarks across three language directions (En$\rightarrow$De, En$\rightarrow$Zh, and En$\rightarrow$Ja) demonstrate that SimPOTrans outperforms several strong baselines, achieving a superior quality--latency trade-off.
  </details>

- **[RubriX: A Fast, Fine-Tunable Non-Autoregressive Rubric Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34875)**  `arXiv:2610.34875`  `cs.CL`  
  _Balamurugan Paramasivam, Sudalai Rajkumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rubric-based evaluation asks which level of a rubric a response meets. It is usually done by prompting a large language model (LLM) as a judge, which needs a generative call per judgment and reads every level description, so its cost grows with the rubric. We present RubriX, a rubric judge whose architecture is inspired by contrastive language models (CLM): a LoRA-adapted encoder embeds the input and each level description, and a softmax over scaled cosine similarities gives a probability for every level without text generation. Trained on public rubric data and proprietary synthetic data, RubriX reaches 75.4% exact accuracy on held-out rubrics, against 57.1% for TypeSafe's Jev and 49.8% for a Qwen3-8B judge. On eight human-labelled benchmark sets it is within 6 percentage points of Jev's accuracy on most of them, while Jev keeps a larger lead on two and ranks responses best on average; against the results reported in the G-Eval paper, RubriX outperforms G-Eval with GPT-3.5 on every SummEval dimension and both QAGS subsets. With level embeddings cached, RubriX's latency stays at about 28 ms per judgment as level descriptions grow from 10 to 3,000 tokens, while Jev's (approximate network time subtracted) rises from 44 ms to 211 ms and the Qwen3-8B judge's from 32 ms to 516 ms, making RubriX 7.7x faster than Jev and 18.8x faster than the Qwen3-8B judge at 3,000 tokens per level.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Atoms for Scalable Text Analysis in Data Analytics in the Era of LLM-Based Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34860)**  `arXiv:2610.34860`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jinxiang Xie, Zihao Li, Rui Ding, Bartuer Zhou, Shi Han, Dongmei Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Much of the information needed for data analysis is buried in text columns: an analyst may infer customer frustration from complaint text and use it to explain resolution time. We call workloads in which processing text columns is central to subsequent data analysis TADA (\emph{Text Analysis in Data Analytics}). In an agentic setting, the user's query dynamically specifies this processing, and answering it interleaves LLM-based semantic steps with exact SQL or Python operations. This mixed execution raises two design questions: which work should be neural and which symbolic, and which semantic outputs later steps can reuse. We address them for agent-planned TADA workflows with two complementary, query-driven semantic atoms. Each atom takes a sequence of text items and a natural-language query specifying the semantic task: \textbf{Tagging} returns one string per input item, while \textbf{Categorization} returns $M$ category objects over $N$ input items. Tagging results are materialized as a row-aligned column and Categorization results as a category table; SQL or Python then performs exact computation over this exposed state. We realize this interface in TADA-Agent, whose atoms run inside generated Python programs and internally handle inputs beyond a reliable context window. We evaluate TADA-Agent on TABench, a benchmark of 40 complete analysis requests over spreadsheet tables spanning workflow composition, input--output structure, and literature-grounded analytical scenarios. Against a semantic-operator baseline and relative to a held-out TADA reference run, TADA-Agent delivers comparable row-level semantic quality, has higher complete-artifact preservation estimates, and uses over an order of magnitude fewer semantic-model requests under the evaluated native execution paths.
  </details>

- **[BehaviorMem: Building and Evaluating User Memory from Real-World E-Commerce Behavior Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34790)**  `arXiv:2610.34790`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sizhe Wang, Xinghao Fang, Rui Qiu, Zhenjia Ma, Dagui Chen, Kun Kuang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  User memory helps personalized systems build on their understanding of users over time. In e-commerce, users convey their preferences and needs through both explicit language and implicit interactions with products. We study memory built from logs of these interactions, examining how it can \emph{remember} concrete evidence and established interests, and use that evidence to \emph{infer} underlying needs and preferences that inform judgments beyond well-observed product domains. Existing memory methods primarily target language-based interactions, and evaluations offer limited real behavioral data and limited separation of these capabilities. This leaves open how to jointly support and separately evaluate remembering and inference from behavioral logs. We formulate the problem of continual behavioral user memory around these joint requirements and develop a dedicated evaluation framework and memory method to address them. \textbf{BehaviorMemBench} is a benchmark built from real e-commerce logs covering 1{,}194 users and 17{,}462 behavioral batches over more than three years, with automatic future-behavior labels and a task-independent memory interface to separately evaluate remembering and inference under a common protocol. \textbf{BehaviorMem} is a memory design that organizes explicit behavioral facts and inferred user characteristics across long-term and short-term scopes in a two-axis state, with evidence-grounded formation, continual maintenance, and query-independent readout. Experiments across two builders and two task readers show that BehaviorMem leads the evaluated agentic memories on remember and infer, and reveal different method rankings across the two capabilities and competitive statistical baselines. Validation on a public dataset further supports its usefulness for downstream tasks based on behavioral histories.
  </details>

- **[From Belief Inference to Task Action: A Multi-Level Evaluation of Theory of Mind on Verifiable Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34759)**  `arXiv:2610.34759`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhenshuai Yin, Yongxin Guo, Mingda Zhang, Zhongze Luo, Haoyue Liu, Lianmin Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective collaboration requires language models to understand what others know and believe, and to act accordingly. Models that assume others share their information may omit necessary communication and undermine coordination. Many theory-of-mind (ToM) benchmarks assess mental-state inference through story questions, but correct answers do not establish that models can use these judgments in subsequent tasks. We introduce a multi-level evaluation built on existing, verifiable tasks. We extend household collaboration and calendar scheduling with asymmetric information while retaining their original constraints and validators. Independent interfaces assess world understanding, information access, beliefs, decisions, and execution. Controls supply personal evidence or correct beliefs to distinguish state recovery from state use. Across six model configurations, these abilities diverge. On matched scheduling cases, Claude recovers all agent beliefs correctly but produces subjectively optimal plans in only 67.9% of cases. In household collaboration, supplying beliefs raises Qwen-27B success from 55.0% to 75.0%, yet Qwen-4B answers every fixed supplied-belief source question correctly while completing only 20.0% of the corresponding continuation condition. These results show why ToM evaluation must connect perspective inference to independently verified decisions and task consequences.
  </details>

- **[DecisionTraceBench: Tracking Decision-Critical Information in Multi-Party Spoken Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34746)**  `arXiv:2610.34746`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wenchao Du, Fangxin Shang, Long Wu, Runze Cui, Qing Yang, Yehui Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In high-stakes domains such as finance, critical information evolves through multi-party interaction. Recovering these changes and their sources is fundamental to reliable speech understanding. Existing speech benchmarks focus on transcription, local understanding, and turn-level state prediction, leaving joint decision evolution across speakers insufficiently evaluated. We introduce DecisionTraceBench, a speech state-tracking benchmark for Decision-Critical Information State Tracking (DCIST). It jointly evaluates decision event sequences, speaker attribution, scope, and temporal evidence, using trajectory-level F1 to measure complete event-chain recovery. Our dataset comprises Chinese financial dialogues involving two to four speakers, with 100.39 hours of synthetic audio and 8.61 hours of human-performed recordings. On a fixed cohort of human-performed recordings, five cascaded systems and two direct-audio models achieve DCIST scores of 5.73% to 21.35%. Ablations with speaker-labeled reference scripts and no temporal localization requirements yield a maximum score of only 38.72%. Audio perturbation experiments further show that overall transcription error rates do not adequately reflect complete trajectory recovery. These findings reveal limitations in jointly understanding information provenance and state evolution, providing a benchmark for evaluating decision-process understanding and decision traceability in high-stakes speech applications.
  </details>

- **[iPrefBench: A User-Centric Benchmark for Personalized Mobile Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34735)**  `arXiv:2610.34735`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuting Ning, Ruijia Cheng, Sushobhan Ghosh, Peitong Duan, HungShi Lin, Abhishek Sundararajan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An agent that truly knows and acts on behalf of its user has been a long-standing promise of AI assistants. Computer-use agents bring this promise within reach by capably executing fully specified tasks across apps. However, real-world user requests are often underspecified. To act correctly for the particular user, an agent must infer some missing information from the user's activity history and ask for other information that only the user can provide, without making unsupported assumptions or burdening the user with unnecessary questions. We present iPrefBench, a benchmark for evaluating the infer and ask capabilities of personal mobile-use agents in an executable iOS environment with an interactive simulated user. Grounded in a coherent persona with 3,051 past activity events, iPrefBench comprises 130 underspecified user tasks across 26 apps. Each omitted user-dependent requirement is, by construction, either history-inferable or ask-only. iPrefBench evaluates personalized mobile agents on both whether agents complete tasks correctly for the user (i.e., personalized task completion) and whether they seek user input when necessary (i.e., user-agent interaction). Evaluation of 7 frontier agents reveals a substantial gap between execution and personalization. Agents achieve high action completion scores (up to 91.6%) but low full task success (up to 47.7%) because personalization lags behind. They especially struggle with ask-only information, failing to request enough necessary information. Our findings highlight a challenge for personalized agents in knowing what to infer and when to ask. We hope that iPrefBench can serve as a valuable testbed for building agents that know their user and work with them.
  </details>

- **[Does the Outfit Change the Price? Context Halo in Vision-Language Model Valuation of an Identical Low-Cost Necklace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34591)**  `arXiv:2610.34591`  `cs.CL`  
  _Brianne Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A chunky gold-tone chain necklace bought online for 2.43 USD was photographed four ways: worn with formal attire against a wood-panelled wall, worn at a party, worn with casual clothing in a recycling yard, and laid flat with no person present. We asked six frontier language models to estimate its price in 1,164 independent and sequential API sessions (4,604 logged rows) and compared estimates across settings. Among the four models that answered image prompts, the same necklace was priced 1.9 to 3.3 times higher in the formal photograph than in the flat-lay (all p < 0.01). Against the yard photograph, the ratio ranged from 1.23 (Grok 4.5, not significant) to 3.57 (Kimi K3), with Claude Fable 5 at 3.32. A text-only version of the task, in which the setting is described in words, produced formal-to-yard ratios of 2.2 to 3.9 in all six models, so the effect does not require pixels. Flat-lay estimates themselves were 7.8 to 18.7 times the receipt price, so part of any headline "outfit multiplier" computed against the receipt is baseline overestimate, not a context effect. A single instruction to ignore clothing and setting significantly reduced the effect in one model (ratio of ratios 0.77, 95% CI 0.65 to 0.91) and not detectably in the others; telling the model the photo came from a company website or a personal account changed nothing significant. Asked afterward whether a different outfit would have changed their number, Claude Fable 5 said yes in 24 of 24 sessions, GPT-5.6 in 69%, and GPT-4o in 18%, while GPT-4o also refused 79% of image estimation prompts. The study isolates a mechanism with one object and one wearer at about 10 sessions per model and condition; we outline the replication needed next, with multiple objects and a human benchmark. Data, code, and raw outputs are released.
  </details>

- **[STEP: Soft Trajectory Estimation of Prediction Correctness via Per-Step Probability Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34537)**  `arXiv:2610.34537`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chenyang Yu, Zifan Zeng, Qunli Zhang, Feng Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating the correctness of large language model (LLM) predictions without access to ground truth is critical for reliable deployment in high-stakes settings. Existing approaches, such as entropy and perplexity, operate solely on final output statistics, discarding the rich information encoded in intermediate reasoning steps. To address this limitation, we propose STEP (Soft Trajectory Estimation of Prediction correctness), a training-free framework that scores each reasoning step via lightweight forward passes to obtain a soft probability distribution qi over candidate answers, from which we derive a set of soft trajectory features capturing per-step confidence and its progression toward the final answer. Extensive experiments on various benchmarks demonstrate consistent and substantial improvements, particularly in AUROC, over output-based baselines and hard-indicator trajectory methods. Notably, Temporally-Weighted Consistency, which assigns higher importance to later reasoning steps where answer distributions have stabilized, contributes the largest gains, highlighting the importance of temporal structure in reasoning trajectories. These results further validate the advantage of preserving continuous probability signals over binary discretization.
  </details>

- **[A Russian, a Brazilian, and a German Walk into a Bar, Ask the Same Question-- And Get Three Different Answers: Profiling LLM Perspective Diversity on Contested Topics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34278)**  `arXiv:2610.34278`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wiem Ben Rim, Yao Lu, Pontus Stenetorp, Emine Yilmaz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models(LLMs) have shown strong performance on tasks with verifiable answers, but many real-world queries involve subjectivity, disagreement, and conflicting evidence. Most existing benchmarks for such queries remain English-centric, overlooking the vast diversity of perspectives held by users worldwide. To systematically study how LLM perspectives vary across languages, we curate PerspectivesQA, a manually verified multilingual dataset of globally contested topics covering 18 languages, and propose ROOTS, a framework that profiles linguistic, perspective, and framing diversity. We evaluate a diverse set of eight models covering both open-source and proprietary families at varying scales, establishing that LLMs hedge their answers in English but commit to positions in lower-resource languages. Larger models maintain a more consistent cross-lingual worldview, while smaller models are more easily swayed. All models also exhibit high sensitivity to minor surface-level input variations, producing notable stance even in larger models.
  </details>

- **[The LALA Benchmark: Evaluating Literary and Linguistic Analysis in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34080)**  `arXiv:2610.34080`  `cs.CL`  
  _Michael Foster, Barbara McGillivray, Gard B. Jenset, Jakub Mácha, Jason Harding, Marcus Perlman, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce the Literature and Linguistics Analysis Benchmark (LALA), comprising 236 open-ended questions written by experts in linguistics and literary studies, together with expert-provided ideal answers. By comparing models within families and at similar training scales using human and LLM judges, LALA identifies differences in linguistic competence associated with post-training, offering a framework for evaluating future models and guiding targeted improvements. We administered questions to 17 language models and evaluated their responses using both human expert assessment and two LLM evaluators, Sol and Qwen. Base models displayed little of the linguistic competence assessed by the benchmark, whereas their post-trained versions performed markedly better, suggesting that post-training plays a central role in making linguistic knowledge accessible and usable. Parameter count, by contrast, showed no statistically significant association with partial-credit performance, and competencies remained unevenly distributed across linguistic domains. We interpret this pattern as evidence that post-training adds linguistic competencies piecemeal, without producing a general linguistic intelligence capable of extending them beyond the learned policies that support them. Models performed best on logical relations between statements and selected discourse-level tasks, while even the strongest post-trained models continued to struggle with ambiguity, literary style, reference, and misleading assumptions. Human and Sol evaluations produced nearly identical model rankings in both the original and ReliableEval runs, supporting the replicability of the comparative results.
  </details>

- **[Memory Tax: Dissecting the Net Value of Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34073)**  `arXiv:2610.34073`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xuan He, Sining Zhoubian, Ruozhen Yang, Yuhao Wen, Zhineng Wang, Yuyang Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory is increasingly used in long-horizon agents, yet the net value of building memory over direct retrieval from raw history remains largely unclear. Through a large-scale evaluation across multiple benchmarks and model backbones, we find many dedicated memory systems underperform naive RAG in many cases, indicating a negative net value. Taking RAG as a reference, we dissect the net value of memory into the gains and losses over direct retrieval, termed memory benefit and memory tax. These gains and losses arise because memory process changes how the raw information is organized and accessed. We view such changes as the double-edged deviations from the raw history. They are originally designed and introduced to benefit the task, for example by reorganizing facts or distilling preferences, but some may also remove or obscure evidence, giving rise to memory tax. Since memory benefit reflects the expected gains of its design, we then focus more on the overlooked memory tax, study how it arises from the system and evaluation side, and generalize the concept to more application scenarios of memory. We hope the notion of memory tax can provide a new lens and shift more attention from standalone performance to the net value of memory, informing more reliable memory designs and evaluations. We will open-source the code and results to the community. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Schema-Free Text-to-SQL: Learning Enterprise Database Structure from Query Logs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33946)**  `arXiv:2610.33946`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ömer Faruk Akgül, Nikhil Kapahi, Prajit Reddy Muppidi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-SQL systems achieve remarkable accuracy on academic benchmarks, yet performance drops dramatically in enterprise deployments. This gap stems from a fundamental assumption: existing approaches require complete schema documentation, but enterprise databases typically lack comprehensive documentation, featuring cryptic naming and implicit relationships. We present the first text-to-SQL framework that operates without schema documentation, instead learning database structure from historical query logs. Query logs encode implicit structural knowledge: which tables are queried together, how they join, and what columns map to business concepts. We construct a knowledge graph from parsed query patterns and introduce entity-driven GraphRAG retrieval that grounds user questions in domain terminology rather than relying solely on embedding similarity. Validated on both an industrial deployment with expert evaluation and the Beaver benchmark, our approach substantially outperforms similarity-based retrieval methods, the dominant industry approach, in both executability and answer quality. On Beaver, we achieve 95.7% execution validity compared to 60.8% for embedding-based retrieval, with expert data engineers preferring our system 70% to 10% on internal deployment. Our work demonstrates that text-to-SQL can operate effectively in schema-ambiguous enterprise environments, enabling natural language database interfaces "in the wild".
  </details>

- **[When Personas Become Harmful: Guiding LLMs with Global Citizenship Values Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33899)**  `arXiv:2610.33899`  `cs.CL`  
  _Siqi Shen, Lajanugen Logeswaran, Moontae Lee, Honglak Lee, Rada Mihalcea_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs are increasingly being used by users from diverse demographics with pluralistic values and distinct backgrounds. Maintaining safety while respecting value-laden personas is uniquely challenging because persona-conditioned requests are often not overtly malicious, unlike those in existing safety benchmarks. Alignment methods aimed at adversarial requests therefore may miss the harm that emerges from persona use, leaving models to either refuse legitimate value expression or blindly follow unsafe personas. We construct a dataset of 11k controversial topics paired with value-based personas mapping to value dimensions from the Schwartz Value Survey. Through an agentic pipeline that iteratively refines the topic and persona, we surface and collect harmful responses from well-aligned commercial LLMs with a more than 47% success rate. We use UNESCO's global citizenship principles as a minimal common ground for distinguishing valid value expressions from harmful behaviors, and introduce a controllable alignment model that lets users balance persona adherence and safety at inference time. The resulting model is safer and more persona-adherent than baselines on most samples.
  </details>

- **[IntentDelta: A Human-Constructed Benchmark for Evolving User Intent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33806)**  `arXiv:2610.33806`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jieyu Zhou, Zijian Wang, Yun Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model agents must adapt as users revise their requirements and priorities during complex tasks. We introduce IntentDelta, a human-constructed multi-turn benchmark with 638 trajectories and 3,766 turns across WebShop and TravelPlanner. Each turn pairs a user utterance with a cumulative intention state and human reference actions. The trajectories are cross-verified and cover feasibility- and preference-driven changes in constraints and priorities. We evaluate eleven agents on intention recovery and action quality. Average Intention Recovery is 33.1\% in TravelPlanner and 44.7\% in WebShop, while average Action Score is 31.6% and 41.0%, respectively. Agents struggle to preserve earlier requirements and distinguish soft preferences from must-have constraints. Correct intention recovery also does not guarantee successful execution, highlighting challenges in translating evolving user intentions into actions.
  </details>

- **[MTA-Zero: Self-Improving Multimodal Deep Search via Online QA-Solver Co-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33799)**  `arXiv:2610.33799`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiangyu Peng, Jiaxin Zhang, An Yan, Yu Li, Ran Xu, Zeyuan Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have become strong visual reasoners, yet remain limited in open-world, multi-step search that requires grounding images, selecting tools, and integrating web evidence. Existing approaches typically construct and filter fixed offline corpora whose question distributions cannot adapt as the target deep-search agent improves. Building on MTA-Agent's image-grounded QA construction, we present MTA-Zero, an online multimodal QA-Solver co-training framework for self-improving multimodal deep-search agents that requires no precomputed multi-hop corpus. Starting from image-grounded VQA seed contexts, a trainable QA Generator retrieves evidence and constructs verified multi-hop questions online. A separate deep-search Solver then selects tools, gathers evidence, and answers the generated questions. To focus generation on the Solver's learning frontier, we derive a Goldilocks reward for the QA Generator from the Solver's group-wise rollout success rate: questions solved by all rollouts or by none receive zero reward, whereas questions near the current frontier are favored. We optimize the two policies independently with GRPO, without sharing parameters or gradients. Across six multimodal deep-search benchmarks, MTA-Zero improves a Qwen3-VL-8B Solver from 36.91% to 51.58% mean accuracy and achieves performance comparable to GPT-5 under the same agent workflow. The same framework also improves a newer Qwen3.6-27B Solver by 10.82 points. These results show that verified online question generation provides a policy-specific curriculum for continually improving multimodal deep-search agents.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Prompts: A Systematic Study of LLM-Based Machine Translation Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33741)**  `arXiv:2610.33741`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dmitry Popov, Roman Bokhyan, Ekaterina Enikeeva, Artem Mekhraliev, Stepan Vysotsky, Nikolay Karpachev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based evaluators have rapidly become a standard tool for machine translation evaluation, yet the design choices that shape their behavior remain poorly understood. We present a systematic study of how instruction design, few-shot demonstrations, backbone model choice, and multi-step workflows affect span-level and ranking-based MT evaluation. We collect new expert RATE annotations on WMT24 and WMT25 English-Russian translations, with transfer validation on MQM- and ESA-annotated benchmarks across five additional language pairs. Our experiments reveal four robust findings: instruction structure shapes evaluator behavior, high-quality demonstrations matter more than quantity, frontier backbones exhibit distinct evaluator profiles (recall-, precision-, or ranking-oriented), and fusing outputs across diverse backbones outperforms single-evaluator multi-call setups. Although evaluator design exposes a large combinatorial space, several discovered design principles transfer consistently across annotation protocols and language pairs. We synthesize these findings into ORBIT, a family of LLM-based MT evaluators. Our strongest configurations achieve expert-level performance in error span detection and in Accuracy and Fluency ranking at roughly 20x lower cost than expert human annotation.
  </details>

- **[Words Are the Voice of the Mind: Depression Severity Estimation from Social Traces via Mental-Guided Evidence Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33586)**  `arXiv:2610.33586`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nada Xu, Xueqin Chen, Qiang Gao, Hongru Liang, Wenqiang Lei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Depression severity estimation is crucial for mental health assessment and intervention, yet traditional approaches depend on clinical interviews or self-report questionnaires that require users' active participation. With the widespread use of online social media, users leave rich social traces that may reflect their mental states, motivating the task of social media-based depression severity estimation. Existing efforts have progressed from textual-feature-based classification to LLM-based questionnaire completion over standardized scale items, yet they still fall short in user-specific mental priors modeling, diversified semantic retrieval, and fine-grained reasoning over mental evidence. To address these limitations, we propose UMer, a user-specific mental evidence reasoning framework for social traces-based depression severity estimation. UMer first distills a user-specific mental prior from historical social traces to capture the user's psychological tendencies, behavioral patterns, and self-expression style. It then performs scale-guided multi-query retrieval to construct evidence memories from item-relevant posts. Finally, it conducts coarse-to-fine evidence-aware reasoning to distinguish direct and indirect evidence and predict item-level scores with interpretable rationales. Extensive experiments on real-world benchmarks demonstrate that UMer not only achieves competitive performance over existing baselines, but also provides evidence-grounded interpretability and generalizes to other scale-based tasks.
  </details>

- **[Trajectory2Gym: Decision-Grounded Compositional Interaction Synthesis for Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33568)**  `arXiv:2610.33568`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yihao Hu, Zhihao Wen, Yitong Lin, Siyuan Lu, Pan Wang, Wei Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Successful tool-use trajectories are not necessarily useful supervision. Even successful interactions may underrepresent multi-step dependencies or contain actions that are unjustified by the information visible to the agent at decision time. We identify two coupled requirements for effective trajectory supervision: compositional coverage, so that important observation-action dependencies are represented, and decision grounding, so that each supervised action is supported by the learner-visible interaction history. We introduce Trajectory2Gym, which constructs executable interactions around under-supported compositional structures and derives supervision only from grounded decisions in their realized executions. Structural-support selection allocates the resulting experience, while parent-normalized supervised fine-tuning preserves the semantic interaction as the unit of training weight. This reframes trajectory generation as the deliberate construction of learnable interaction experience, rather than the collection of successful trajectories. Across five Qwen3 scales, Trajectory2Gym improves Tau1, Tau2, and BFCL at every tested scale, increasing the equal-weight three-benchmark mean by 16.9-29.8 percentage points. The gains are strongest in stateful and multi-turn interaction, where later decisions depend on earlier observations and feedback. Our results suggest that scaling tool-use agents depends not only on collecting more successful trajectories, but on constructing interactions whose dependencies are represented and whose decisions are learnable from the agent's visible history.
  </details>

- **[Eat Your Precision: Precision Objective Mitigates Reward Hacking in Rubric-based RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33512)**  `arXiv:2610.33512`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hyun Ryu, Jong-Heon Kim, Yiqing Xie, Seungone Kim, Carolyn Rose, Eunho Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rubric-based rewards enable effective reinforcement learning on non-verifiable tasks. However, because these rewards are typically defined as a weighted measure of rubric coverage, models are trained primarily to maximize a recall objective. As a result, RL-trained models produce responses that are more complete but also more verbose and contain more unnecessary or factually incorrect content, leading them to be less preferred than responses from the base model. To address this issue, we present the FORK reward, which jointly optimizes recall and a novel precision objective, which requires each unit of a model response to be both necessary and factually correct, as measured by rubric-driven necessity credits and a factual-correctness grader, respectively. Our experimental results show that FORK outperforms existing rubric-based RL methods and achieves Pareto-optimal performance that balances rubric coverage and overall response quality. Compared to the original recall-based reward, FORK substantially improves precision and reduces response length while achieving similar rubric coverage, which leads to significantly higher win rate compared to the base policy (e.g., from 10.6% to 68.0% on RubricHub-Medical and from 22.1% to 74.1% on HealthBench).
  </details>

- **[Small Edits, Shifting Decisions: Benchmarking LLM Accuracy and Reliability on French Input Variants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33493)**  `arXiv:2610.33493`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaming Yu, Xiaotian Xie, Jian Ma, Yingjie Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  French users may omit apostrophes and diacritics or make keyboard typos in everyday input. The effects of these common variations on language model performance and prediction reliability remain insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we construct a controlled French surface-variant benchmark covering seven task types, with 4,200 original-low-high triplets (12,600 input instances). Each high-intensity variant retains all low-intensity edits and adds further changes. We evaluate six models from two LLM families. The results show that accuracy decreases and Brier score and negative log-likelihood (NLL) increase with perturbation intensity. Larger models show smaller accuracy losses under high intensity, but increases in Brier score and NLL do not consistently decrease with scale. Keyboard typos cause the largest losses in accuracy and the quality of predicted probabilities at the predefined high-intensity settings. Finally, calibration temperatures fitted on original inputs transfer effectively to surface variants. Combining calibration with input restoration further improves Brier score and NLL over either method alone while preserving restored accuracy. Our benchmark provides a unified framework for jointly evaluating task accuracy and prediction reliability under common French surface variations.
  </details>

- **[Perturbation-Based Data Selection for Medical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33440)**  `arXiv:2610.33440`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuexin Wu, Dayou Yu, Vasile Rus_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selecting a compact training subset is an important step in adapting medical language models from existing question-answering banks. We investigate perturbation-based data selection using two signals derived from Gaussian perturbations of the final-block representation: prediction-change frequency and non-flipping margin contraction. After a shared entropy prefilter, Representation-Sensitivity Prioritization (RSP) combines the signals using automatic root-mean-square scaling, while RSP-Drop ranks candidates by contraction alone. Both selectors use only questions and answer options during selection. We evaluate them with MedGemma-4B on five medical QA datasets at retained-set budgets of 64, 128, and 256 examples. At the primary budget of 128, RSP and RSP-Drop obtain dataset-averaged Macro-F1 scores of 47.55 and 48.28, respectively, compared with 45.11 for entropy-pool-matched random selection. Their relative performance varies across datasets and budgets. A selected-set case study identifies contrasting clean-margin profiles among the examples exchanged by the two rules, while a full-pool ablation shows that entropy prefiltering is not uniformly beneficial. These retrospective results characterize the empirical utility and limitations of perturbation-based selection under a fixed adaptation protocol. Evaluation uses one backbone and benchmarks with documented training-family overlap; it does not establish clinical effectiveness.
  </details>

- **[Preference Optimization Improves Definition Generation: Semantic Change in EU Institutional Sustainable Finance Disclosures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33357)**  `arXiv:2610.33357`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mariia Fedorova, Andrey Kutuzov, Gustavo Cadenas Delascio_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dictionary-like definition generators are known to be useful for lexical semantic change detection (LSCD). However, they suffer from overly granular sense distribution, producing excessive number of definitions for one and the same lexical sense. In this work, we show that fine-tuning with Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) helps to overcome this problem. We test our approach both quantitatively on a curated academic LSCD benchmark and qualitatively on real-world data for a practical task of auditing diachronic changes in sustainability terminology within project documentation of EU development finance. All models, code and predictions produced in this study are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning What to Ask for Tomorrow: Active Preference Acquisition for Long-Term Personalized Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31107)**  `arXiv:2610.31107`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yiqi Wang, Zizhi Zeng, Jiaqi Zhang, Yiqun Duan, Mingkai Zheng, Taotao Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web agents increasingly serve the same user across repeated online tasks such as booking travel, shopping, and scheduling, so their long-term usefulness depends on how well they know the user's preferences. Passive memory and personalization methods cannot obtain preferences the user never reveals, and clarification methods that value a question by its benefit to the current task overlook the benefit of reusing the answer in later tasks. We study Active Preference Acquisition (APA): which missing preferences are worth the cost of asking? We define the Prospective Personalization Value (PPV) of a missing preference as its expected cumulative utility gain over future tasks minus the effort and disclosure-risk cost of asking, and select questions under a per-interaction budget. We characterize when cumulative reuse justifies acquisition despite insufficient single-task benefit, and evaluate this regime with an initial-profile diagnostic. APA computes the counterfactual gain of each candidate preference on public task prototypes with the agent's own task solver and aggregates it by the expected reuse of each task family. We evaluate it on ActiveWebPref, a controlled longitudinal benchmark with hidden but queryable user profiles and exact task utilities. At a horizon of 20 tasks, 34.4% of budget-feasible valuable missing preferences are amortization-dependent; APA acquires 80.3% of them, whereas current-task value of information acquires 5.1%. Under per-user asking-cost caps matched to APA's realized expenditure, APA achieves higher net utility than four heuristic selection baselines. In our cost setting, asking about every unknown preference yields lower net utility than not asking, while APA recovers 84.2% of its utility gain with 27.6% of its questions. The value of acquiring a user preference should account for its reuse across future tasks, not only its benefit to the task at hand.
  </details>

- **[Explore More, Validate Less: Efficient Reflective Prompt Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27367)**  `arXiv:2610.27367`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaofan Zhou, Lu Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reflective prompt optimization improves instructions using task feedback, but coupling candidate generation with full-validation-based selection can make search costly and sequential. We propose a two-stage framework that decouples reflective candidate-pool construction from validation allocation. During construction, sampled parents first answer training questions, and groups of their saved responses guide parallel reflection and revision. Each child is screened on its group's questions against the corresponding parent scores. Improving, nonduplicate prompts enter the pool and can become parents without full validation. After construction, an exploration policy selects candidates from the frozen pool under a fixed full-validation budget, and the prompt with the highest measured validation score is returned. This separation allows candidate-pool size and validation budget to be set independently. We evaluate random selection and a multilayer perceptron with similarity-based exploration (MLP + similarity) on five benchmarks using Qwen3.5-9B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. With 100 retained candidates and 20 full validations, our framework improves over the initial solver by up to 11.22 percentage points. MLP + similarity improves over the initial solver in all ten comparisons, although neither selection policy consistently outperforms the other. These results support separating reflective search from budgeted validation while leaving room for stronger candidate-selection policies.
  </details>

- **[Truncated Reasoning Under a Budget: Evaluating System-1 Models as Decision-Makers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25768)**  `arXiv:2610.25768`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhongyin Wang, Zhaoqian Xue, Junting Mu, Haozheng Liu, Ye Yuan, Haolun Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often generate more reasoning tokens than needed. Limiting the reasoning budget reduces cost, but forcing an answer from an incomplete trace can hurt accuracy. System-1 decision models select among candidate options without producing a reasoning response. We evaluate Jev as a decision reader of truncated reasoning across seven LLM reasoners and five multiple-choice benchmarks (35 pairs). Jev achieves higher mean accuracy than forced answering at tested budgets from 500 to 16,000 tokens, including the estimated costs of reasoning and answering. At a 1,500-token budget, it improves accuracy by 4.1 pp overall and 8.6 pp on truncated questions, with significant gains in 17 pairs under Holm-corrected bootstrap tests. Offline replay estimates 34.5% of the full-run cost, with a 1.13 pp accuracy loss. A policy selected on training folds saves 71.3% of the estimated full-run cost on held-out folds, with a 0.58 pp accuracy loss. We also find that Jev shows strong question-only accuracy, exceeding 500-token forced answering on average (68.0% vs. 65.5%), although this advantage varies by benchmark. These findings suggest that System-1 decision models can complement LLM reasoning and reduce the need for costly reasoning completion.
  </details>

- **[P2N: Reusing Deep Representations for Greater Effective Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25076)**  `arXiv:2610.25076`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yiqin Hao, Boyi Zeng, Zitong Wang, He Li, Shixiang Song, Feichen Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mathematical problem solving and autonomous agents are increasingly important applications of language models, demanding stronger reasoning capabilities. Computational depth is a key resource for such reasoning, but adding model layers increases both training and inference costs, while test-time scaling incurs substantial additional inference computation. We introduce P2N (Previous to Next), which reuses the previous token's deep representations, already available during generation, to increase effective computational depth with negligible decoding overhead. Specifically, P2N adds the previous token's deep hidden state to the current token's shallow representation, allowing subsequent layers to build on previously completed deep computation. This recurrence extends effective computational depth across tokens, while introducing only a single vector addition per decoding step. We train the recurrence using parallel Jacobi updates restricted to a middle-layer core. Experiments with Qwen-style models show that, at 1.5B parameters, P2N improves GSM8K accuracy by over 10 percentage points under both zero-shot and few-shot prompting, with nearly unchanged decoding latency. The benefits also extend to language modeling and average performance on general downstream benchmarks. on general downstream benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[When Does Better Acceptance Pay? A Controlled Study of Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24993)**  `arXiv:2610.24993`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yongchao Long, Chunrui Zheng, Yuxuan Zhou, Guangkun Nie, Tian-Chi Ma, Chao Pang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The throughput gains of speculative decoding depend on the trade-off between acceptance length and execution latency. We ask when additional acceptance is worth the cost of obtaining it. We establish a comparison protocol spanning training, inference, and evaluation, remeasuring released checkpoints and retraining six drafter recipes on shared target-generated data across ten benchmarks and two target models. Alignment changes the picture: gaps that appear large in published comparisons shrink substantially under shared training and inference conditions. This narrowing motivates two deeper questions: how is acceptance improved at different costs, and when do these gains improve throughput? We find that acceptance can be improved through nearly free objective and context changes or more expensive token-to-token conditioning, which pays off most clearly under sampled drafting. DCore illustrates the former route as a strong baseline with little additional measured drafting overhead. The payoff is conditional: task uncertainty and serving conditions affect acceptance returns, execution costs, and the preferred drafter and budget. These findings inform future drafter design and deployment choices.
  </details>

- **[Strong yet Controllable LLM Abstention without Learning to Abstain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16318)**  `arXiv:2610.16318`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junwei Zhang, Yifei Wang, Wanli Yang, Hexiang Tan, Rui Tang, Xin Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can reduce hallucination by abstaining from questions they would likely get wrong. Abstention can be implemented outside the model (selective answering) using a confidence threshold that controls the answer rate (coverage), or learned inside it (abstention training), which can perform better but fixes coverage once trained. To compare the two, we match their coverage and decompose risk (error rate) into answering ability and distinguishing ability, i.e., how well the model answers and how accurately it abstains, respectively. The decomposition shows that abstention training performs better mainly by answering better: with strong confidence estimators, the two have nearly equal distinguishing ability. This motivates Train for Accuracy, then Post-hoc Select (TAPS), a modular approach that keeps coverage controllable, needs only standard post-training and an off-the-shelf confidence estimator, and benefits from advances in either component. Across three LLMs and three factual QA benchmarks, TAPS with a probing-based confidence estimator attains 1.5--9.4 percentage points lower mean risk than three representative abstention training methods at matched coverage. Within TAPS, accuracy training often weakens the model's own confidence signals, yet refitting the estimator largely preserves distinguishing ability. Together, these results establish TAPS as a strong yet controllable baseline and raise the bar for future abstention training: sacrificing control over coverage should yield gains beyond what can be achieved by optimizing answering and abstaining separately.
  </details>

- **[RIVO: Lightweight Adaptive Rerouting for Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15910)**  `arXiv:2610.15910`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingchen Li, Jiatan Huang, Hong Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep Research agents rely on long-horizon reasoning and iterative tool use to solve complex information-seeking tasks. However, adaptive decision-making over these trajectories remains challenging, as agents may stop prematurely when further evidence is needed, continue unnecessarily after sufficient evidence has been gathered, or fail to redirect unproductive trajectories. Existing solutions either require costly model retraining and dense turn-level supervision, or rely on heuristic control rules that are not directly optimized for downstream research utility. This motivates a lightweight, utility-aware alternative for Deep Research control. We formulate adaptive rerouting in long-horizon Deep Research as a state-conditioned trajectory correction problem. Based on this view, we introduce RIVO, a lightweight plug-in framework for adaptive trajectory correction. The agent estimates its research state from lightweight signals and determines whether trajectory redirection is needed. Once triggered, the plug-in modifies the Host token logits to steer generation toward a more promising research direction. Theoretical analysis shows that, under a value-alignment condition and bounded local curvature, a sufficiently small logit intervention can locally improve expected downstream research utility. We further construct BrowseCore, a curated dataset with a paired local corpus, for efficient and controlled Deep Research experimentation. Building on this resource, the plug-in is trained from downstream outcomes, enabling it to learn corrections that improve subsequent research and final task performance. Extensive experiments across multiple Deep Research benchmarks and control settings demonstrate the effectiveness of our method, showing that lightweight logit intervention can improve long-horizon research without retraining the underlying Host model.
  </details>

- **[Question-Specific Knowledge Graphs for Efficient Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35942)**  `arXiv:2609.35942`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ting-Chih Chen, Emile van Krieken, Shujian Yu, Filip Ilievski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work in visual question answering has shown that vision-language models can exhibit strong reasoning capabilities by translating visual inputs into textual representations. The effectiveness of this translation depends on how well visual details are retained; models need to surface and align both explicit and implicit knowledge sufficient to support reasoning, without introducing spurious assumptions. Existing methods that leverage detailed image captions introduce visual details unrelated to the reasoning task, inflating input token counts and increasing computational cost. To address these challenges, we propose VisKG, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework in which models learn to translate visual content into question-specific knowledge graph (KG) representations. This process filters out perceptual noise while preserving the entity-relation structure needed for chain-of-thought reasoning, following the principle of minimum sufficient information. To ensure stable RL post-training, VisKG adopts Group reward-Decoupled Normalization Policy Optimization (GDPO). In addition, we strengthen the supervision stage with negative rationale samples, exposing the model to incorrect reasoning paths before RL post-training. Experimental results across science, mathematics, and general visual understanding benchmarks show that VisKG achieves performance comparable to or better than baselines, while requiring fewer tokens than caption-based representations. Moreover, training VisKG with GDPO improves accuracy by 2% over its GRPO-trained counterpart on average. These results suggest that KG representations are a promising approach for supporting multi-step reasoning and open up future work on adaptively selecting the most suitable representation for a given task.
  </details>

- **[Open-Jev Judgments on CallScreenBench: Calibrated One-Pass Scam Screening with a Small Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23959)**  `arXiv:2609.23959`  `cs.CL`  
  _Simiao Ren, Kidus Zewde, Xingyu Shen, Yuchen Zhou, Dennis Ng, Ankit Raj, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Screening a phone call for fraud needs a trustworthy probability after every caller turn, in milliseconds. Jev-style typed decisions promise exactly that: declared options go in, one calibrated probability per option comes out of a single forward pass, with no generated text. We test an open implementation of this readout, JevLite, on scam-call screening: Qwen3-4B is LoRA-tuned so that the temperature-scaled softmax over two answer-label logits is P(scam). On 41 held-out CallScreenBench scenarios (577 per-turn decisions) a three-seed ensemble reaches AUROC .974 with calibration error .052, non-inferior to an LLM judge (MiniMax-M3) at a pre-registered .02 margin, with no false alarms on legitimate calls, decisions 1.14 turns earlier under the same hang-up rule, and 64.5 ms per decision on one consumer GPU, 4.9x lower than the same backbone fine-tuned to generate its answer. The gain is in the readout and calibration, not accuracy: a fine-tuned ModernBERT encoder is not significantly worse, the recipe was selected with test-set exposure, and all callers are synthetic. We claim no architectural novelty; the contribution is the application and an evaluation reporting calibration, false alarms and decision timing alongside AUROC.
  </details>

- **[Towards Root Memory: Benchmarking and Enhancing Implicit Logical Memory Retrieval for Personalized LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23283)**  `arXiv:2606.23283`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hongxun Ding, Xiang Yu, Chengbing Wang, Jianfei Xiao, Keqin Bao, Wenjie Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory systems are essential for personalized Large Language Models (LLMs). However, retrieval within these systems primarily relies on semantic similarity, potentially missing logically critical memories with limited semantic overlap. We refer to this failure as semantic deflection, which remains insufficiently evaluated by existing benchmarks. To address this gap, we construct IMLogic, a benchmark for implicit logical memory retrieval in long dialogues. Each question is designed around the divergence between semantic and logical relevance, with diagnostic response options that probe semantic deflection, hallucination, and lack of personalization. To help systems utilize associations that are difficult to discover through semantic matching, we introduce root memory, a structured representation that distills reusable, response-guiding logic from user histories through LLM reasoning and grounds it in supporting evidence. We further develop RootMem, a plug-and-play framework that constructs and routes root memories to complement semantic retrieval. Extensive experiments reveal persistent challenges across retrieval methods and memory systems, including substantial preference-driven errors even under full-memory access. RootMem consistently improves performance, demonstrating the potential of structured personalized logic while leaving considerable challenges unresolved. Our benchmark and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Adapt or Calibrate? How Local Labels Buy Certified Coverage in Medical VQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15910)**  `arXiv:2606.15910`  `cs.CL`
