  _Reza Khanmohammadi, Kundan Thind, Mohammad M. Ghassemi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An answer from a vision-language model (VLM) to a medical image question can be accepted without review only under a threshold bounding the error of accepted answers, and certifying that bound costs local labels. We ask what such labels can certify for nine confidence estimators on five general-purpose open-weight VLMs and three medical VQA benchmarks under Learn-then-Test, with the question as the exchangeable unit. Estimators trained on natural images transfer poorly: the best pooled certificate covers 11.5% of SLAKE answers at a 20% error tolerance and nothing on PATH-VQA. With 1,000 labeled SLAKE questions, adapting a probe on half of them raises pooled certified coverage from 6.8% to 37.6% (0.0% to 30.6% with the image as the unit), above the best threshold-only estimator (11.3%). On PATH-VQA, adaptation certifies 11.4% with 2,500 labels; thresholds alone certify nothing. Yet for strong single models, small budgets are better spent on the threshold. A question-only classifier recovers much of adaptation's ranking gain but certifies far less. Neither procedure yields a pooled certificate at 10%, and nothing is certified at 5%. A blank image removes 61% of correct answers, yet on GMAI-MMBench no trained probe is less confident on answers unchanged by image replacement.
  </details>

- **[Detecting Differences Is Not Understanding Structure: Large Language Models Fail at Graph Isomorphism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09484)**  `arXiv:2606.09484`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kumar Thushalika, Sukumar Kishanthan, Asela Hevapathige_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown impressive performance on diverse reasoning tasks, yet their capacity for structural reasoning in graphs remains unclear. We investigate whether LLMs can genuinely understand graph isomorphism -a fundamental problem in graph theory. While LLMs achieve near-perfect accuracy on isomorphism detection, we show this performance is illusory. When identical graphs are presented with permuted node labels, LLMs fail to identify their isomorphism. This finding suggests that LLMs exploit patterns rather than reasoning about abstract graph structure. Since permutation invariance is a fundamental requirement for valid structural reasoning, these results indicate that success on graph reasoning benchmarks should not be interpreted as evidence of genuine topological understanding.
  </details>

- **[Unified Context Evolution for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02304)**  `arXiv:2606.02304`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zixuan Zhu, Yitong Hu, Yong Dai, Junfeng Fang, Chunyang Jiang, Senkang Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents can solve multi-step interactive tasks by combining reasoning with environment feedback, yet each episode starts from the same fixed context and any useful strategy discovered along the way is lost once the task ends. Existing approaches either limit learning to the current task or pool all experience into a single untyped store, without distinguishing knowledge types, tracking quality through use, or balancing what the library still lacks. We introduce Unified Context Evolution (UCE), a gradient-free framework that externalizes agent experience into an evolving library of typed Evolvable Context Units (ECUs). UCE decomposes experience into four complementary types (Memory, Strategy, Workflow, and Skill), each generated from trajectories under type-specific conditions, retrieved at decision time, scored through repeated usage outcomes, and pruned when no longer valuable. A scheduling module allocates each cycle's generation budget toward the types where the library is weakest. Across two interactive benchmarks, UCE raises ALFWorld success from 75.4% to 96.3% and WebShop task score from 45.1% to 61.3%, and remains effective across alternative actor reasoning backbones without model retraining.
  </details>

- **[QUEST: Training Frontier Deep Research Agents with Fully Synthetic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24218)**  `arXiv:2605.24218`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jian Xie, Tianhe Lin, Zilu Wang, Yuting Ning, Yuekun Yao, Tianci Xue, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep research agents extend the role of search engines from retrieving keyword-matched pages to synthesizing knowledge, fundamentally changing how humans interact with information. However, frontier systems remain proprietary, while existing open agents often generalize poorly across different task types, leaving unclear how to train a broadly capable deep research agent. We release QUEST, a family of open models (ranging from 2B to 35B) that serve as general-purpose deep research agents designed to handle a wide range of long-horizon search tasks, with strong capabilities in fact seeking, citation grounding, and report synthesis. To build QUEST, we propose an effective training recipe combining mid-training, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning. Central to this recipe is a curated data synthesis pipeline based on unified rubric trees, which applies to different task types and enables synthesizing training data with verifiable rewards without human annotation. In addition, QUEST incorporates a built-in context management mechanism that enables effective long-horizon reasoning and knowledge synthesis. Using only 8K synthesized tasks, QUEST approaches or even surpasses frontier closed-source agents across eight deep research benchmarks spanning diverse task types, and achieves the best overall performance among recent open-weight agents. We released everything: models, data, and training scripts.
  </details>

- **[VerifyMAS: Hypothesis Verification for Failure Attribution in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17467)**  `arXiv:2605.17467`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hezhe Qiao, Hanghang Tong, Ee-Peng Lim, Bing Liu, Guansong Pang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model-driven multi-agent systems (LLM-MAS) excel at complex tasks, yet unreliable agents remain a key bottleneck to system-level reliability. Automatic failure attribution is therefore critical, but existing approaches, such as direct prediction of agent-error pairs and agent-first failure attribution, rely on local logs of agents and miss global failures that only manifest over full interaction trajectories, such as cross-step inconsistencies and inter-agent coordination errors. Moreover, directly predicting failures induces a large combinatorial search space, hindering fine-grained attribution. To address these challenges, we propose VerifyMAS, a hypothesis verification framework for agent failure attribution. Instead of directly predicting faulty agents and error types, VerifyMAS formulates and verifies failure hypotheses against full trajectories. This verification-based approach decomposes attribution into trajectory-level error validation and fine-grained agent localization, providing an error-first attribution approach that captures global failure patterns while substantially reducing the search space. We further introduce a hypothesis-based data construction strategy grounded in a structured error taxonomy and fine-tune a specialized LLM verifier model for trajectory-level failure verification and agent attribution. Experiments on Aegis-Bench and Who&When show that VerifyMAS consistently improves diverse backbone models, including open-source Qwen and API-based GPT models, outperforming prior methods without sacrificing inference efficiency for long multi-agent trajectories.
  </details>

- **[ReVision: Scaling Computer-Use Agents via Temporal Visual Redundancy Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11212)**  `arXiv:2605.11212`  `cs.CL`  
  _Amirhossein Abaskohi, Yuhang He, Peter West, Giuseppe Carenini, Pranit Chawla, Vibhav Vineet_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer-use agents (CUAs) rely on visual observations of graphical user interfaces, where each screenshot is encoded into a large number of visual tokens. As interaction trajectories grow, the token cost increases rapidly, limiting the amount of history that can be incorporated under fixed context and compute budgets. This has resulted in no or very limited improvement in the performance when using history unlike other domains. We address this inefficiency by introducing ReVision, which is used to train multimodal language models on trajectories where redundant visual patches are removed using a learned patch selector that compares patch representations across consecutive screenshots while preserving spatial structure required by the model. Across three benchmarks, OSWorld, WebTailBench, and AgentNetBench, when processing trajectories with 5 history screenshots using Qwen2.5-VL-7B, ReVision reduces token usage by 46% on average while improving success rate by 3% over the no drop baseline. This establishes a clear efficiency gain, enabling agents to process longer trajectories with fewer tokens. With this improved efficiency, we revisit the role of history in CUAs and find that performance continues to improve as more past observations are incorporated when redundancy is removed.
  </details>

- **[BERAG: Bayesian Ensemble Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-based Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22678)**  `arXiv:2604.22678`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jinghong Chen, Jingbiao Mei, Guangyu Yang, Bill Byrne_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A common approach to question answering with retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is to concatenate documents into a single context and pass it to a language model to generate an answer. While simple, this strategy can obscure the contribution of individual documents, making attribution difficult and contributing to the ``lost-in-the-middle'' effect, where relevant information in long contexts is overlooked. Concatenation also scales poorly: computational cost grows quadratically with context length, a problem that becomes especially severe when the context includes visual data, as in visual question answering. Attempts to mitigate these issues by limiting context length can further restrict performance by preventing models from benefiting from the improved recall offered by deeper retrieval. We propose Bayesian Ensemble Retrieval-Augmented Generation (BERAG), along with Bayesian Ensemble Fine-Tuning (BEFT), as a RAG framework in which language models are conditioned on individual retrieved documents rather than a single combined context. BERAG treats document posterior probabilities as ensemble weights and updates them token by token using Bayes' rule during generation. This approach enables probabilistic re-ranking, parallel memory usage, and clear attribution of document contribution, making it well-suited for large document collections. We evaluate BERAG and BEFT primarily on knowledge-based visual question answering tasks, where models must reason over long, imperfect retrieval lists. The results show substantial improvements over standard RAG, including strong gains on Document Visual Question Answering and multimodal needle-in-a-haystack benchmarks. We also demonstrate that BERAG mitigates the ``lost-in-the-middle'' effect. The document posterior can be used to detect insufficient grounding and trigger deflection, while document pruning enables faster decoding than standard RAG.
  </details>

- **[The Lexical Imprint of Pre-training Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10657)**  `arXiv:2602.10657`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jeonghoon Kim, Woojin Chung, Woomin Song, Cheonbok Park, Nako Sung, Jinwoo Shin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How much of zero-shot benchmark accuracy is captured by word-level alignment between a pre-training corpus and the benchmark target? We study this question through word-level unigram cross-entropy (UCE), a tokenizer-agnostic, model-free measure of lexical alignment between a reference corpus and a target benchmark. Likelihood training shapes the model's predictive distribution from the pre-training corpus, and zero-shot scores under likelihood-based evaluation depend in part on the lexical alignment between that corpus and the benchmark target. Cleanly tracing how the lexical imprint of pre-training data scales with corpus--benchmark alignment requires controlled from-scratch pre-training that varies corpus identity while holding the training recipe fixed. Across this controlled sweep, spanning $10$ zero-shot benchmarks, $4$ pre-training corpora, and $3$ model scales, we find that corpus--benchmark lexical alignment, UCE, consistently tracks zero-shot accuracy on every benchmark, with corpora more aligned with the benchmark (lower UCE) achieving higher accuracy. The same signal extends from measurement to control at finer scopes. At the document level, strengthening the imprint by selecting training documents to maximize lexical alignment with the benchmark produces the accuracy gains predicted by the mechanism. At the prompt level, matching the imprint per prompt by routing each prompt to the drafter whose domain corpus is most lexically aligned with it produces measurable throughput gains over a generalist-drafter baseline. Taken together, pre-training corpora leave a measurable lexical imprint on base-model zero-shot behavior, and UCE provides a model-free probe of lexical alignment between reference and target data.
  </details>

- **[OdysseyArena: Benchmarking Large Language Models For Long-Horizon, Active and Inductive Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05843)**  `arXiv:2602.05843`  `cs.CL`  
  _Fangzhi Xu, Hang Yan, Qiushi Sun, Jinyang Wu, Zixian Huang, Muye Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs) has catalyzed the development of autonomous agents capable of navigating complex environments. However, existing evaluations primarily adopt a deductive paradigm, where agents execute tasks based on explicitly provided rules and static goals, often within limited planning horizons. Crucially, this neglects the inductive necessity for agents to discover latent transition laws from experience autonomously, which is the cornerstone for enabling agentic foresight and sustaining strategic coherence. To bridge this gap, we introduce OdysseyArena, which re-centers agent evaluation on long-horizon, active, and inductive interactions. We formalize and instantiate four primitives, translating abstract transition dynamics into concrete interactive environments. Building upon this, we establish OdysseyArena-Lite for standardized benchmarking, providing a set of 120 tasks to measure an agent's inductive efficiency and long-horizon discovery. Pushing further, we introduce OdysseyArena-Challenge to stress-test agent stability across extreme interaction horizons (e.g., > 200 steps). Extensive experiments on 15+ leading LLMs reveal that even frontier models exhibit a deficiency in inductive scenarios, identifying a critical bottleneck in the pursuit of autonomous discovery in complex environments. Project homepage:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Toward Global Large Language Models in Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02186)**  `arXiv:2601.02186`  `cs.CL`  
  _Rui Yang, Huitao Li, Weihao Xuan, Heli Qi, Xin Li, Kunyu Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite continuous advances in medical technology, the global distribution of health care resources remains uneven. The development of large language models (LLMs) has transformed the landscape of medicine and holds promise for improving health care quality and expanding access to medical information globally. However, existing LLMs are primarily trained on high-resource languages, limiting their applicability in global medical scenarios. To address this gap, we constructed GlobMed, a large multilingual medical dataset containing more than 800,000 entries spanning 20 languages, including seven low-resource languages. Building on this, we established GlobMed-Bench, which systematically assesses 56 state-of-the-art proprietary and open-weight LLMs across 12 of the 20 languages represented in GlobMed, comprising over 20,000 independent experiments and more than 32 million model responses. This large-scale evaluation revealed significant performance disparities across languages, particularly for low-resource languages. Additionally, we introduced GlobMed-LLMs, a suite of multilingual medical LLMs trained across the 12 benchmark languages, with parameters ranging from 1.7B to 8B. GlobMed-LLMs achieved an average performance improvement of nearly 40% relative to baseline LLMs, with a maximum performance improvement of more than threefold in low-resource languages. These resources provide an important foundation for advancing the equitable development and application of LLMs globally, enabling broader language communities to benefit from technological advances.
  </details>

- **[Role-aware Heuristic Episodic Attention for Conversational LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.06869)**  `arXiv:2512.06869`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wanyang Hong, Zhaoning Zhang, Yi Chen, Libo Zhang, Baihui Liu, Linbo Qiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models often lose track of persistent instructions and relevant information as multi-turn conversations grow. We study this cumulative contextual decay through three related failure modes: attention pollution, dilution, and drift. We propose REA(Role-aware Heuristic Episodic Attention), a context-management framework that assigns different persistence and representation policies to instructions and episodic interactions. Instructional Memory retains identified global constraints in a dedicated prefix. Episodic Memory preserves user inputs and compresses model replies, while heuristic retrieval selects raw text, compressed representations, or omission for each historical turn. On Long-MT-Bench+, REA improves the judge score from 6.32 to 7.36 on a 10-point scale, a 16.5% relative gain over the Vanilla baseline, and reduces average latency by 2.91x. Additional evaluations show aggregate gains on three backbones spanning 1.7B-7B parameters and on Chinese and English role-playing tasks. These results support role-aware context management as a practical approach to maintaining conversational continuity and instruction adherence.
  </details>

- **[Advancing the Pareto Frontier of Test-Time Scaling with Multi-Path Plan Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.11620)**  `arXiv:2510.11620`  `cs.CL`  
  _Siheng Xiong, Ali Payani, Faramarz Fekri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time scaling seeks to improve reasoning through additional inference-time computation, commonly using solution sampling with majority voting to select the final answer. However, repeated sampling can reproduce correlated errors, limiting the accuracy gains from additional computation. We introduce Multi-Path Plan Aggregation (MPPA), which learns planning and answer selection using separate LoRA adapters on a shared frozen backbone. The planner jointly generates $K$ short plans, each guiding a separate solution generated by the frozen executor; the aggregator then scores the resulting solutions and selects the final answer. Joint planning encourages complementary approaches suited to the executor's capabilities, while learned selection uses reasoning evidence to identify correct candidates. Alternating optimization improves planning and aggregation through supervised finetuning and reinforcement learning. Across five mathematics and science benchmarks and three backbones, MPPA improves accuracy--token trade-offs compared with training-free and training-based baselines. With three plans, it achieves higher average accuracy than eight directly sampled solutions with majority voting or the same learned aggregator, while reducing output tokens by 47--54%. Evaluation with task-provided subtasks further demonstrates higher final-task accuracy than direct sampling with majority voting or learned aggregation at either the task or subtask level.
  </details>

- **[Test-Time Policy Adaptation for Enhanced Multi-Turn Interactions with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23166)**  `arXiv:2509.23166`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chenxing Wei, Hong Wang, Ying He, Fei Yu, Yao Shu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) employ multi-turn interaction as a fundamental paradigm for completing complex tasks. However, their performance often degrades in extended interactions, as they are typically trained on static, single-turn data, which hinders their ability to adapt to real-time user feedback. To address this limitation, we first propose a new paradigm: Test-Time Policy Adaptation for Multi-Turn Interactions (T2PAM), which utilizes user feedback from the ongoing interaction as a reward signal to estimate a latent optimal policy aligned with user preferences, then updates a small subset of parameters to steer the model toward this policy, ultimately enabling efficient in-conversation self-correction. We then introduce Optimum-Referenced One-Step Adaptation (ROSA), a lightweight algorithm that operationalizes T2PAM. ROSA guides the model parameters toward a theoretical optimal policy in a single, efficient update step, avoiding costly iterative gradient-based optimization and minimizing computational overhead. We provide a rigorous theoretical analysis guaranteeing that the policy of ROSA converges to the preference of user as the number of interactions increases. Extensive experiments on challenging benchmark demonstrate that ROSA achieves significant improvements in both task effectiveness and efficiency.
  </details>

- **[KnowMT-Bench: Benchmarking Knowledge-Intensive Long-Form Question Answering in Multi-Turn Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21856)**  `arXiv:2509.21856`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junhao Chen, Yu Huang, Siyuan Li, Rui Yao, Hanqian Li, Hanyu Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-Turn Long-Form Question Answering (MT-LFQA) is a key application paradigm of Large Language Models (LLMs) in knowledge-intensive domains. However, existing benchmarks are limited to single-turn dialogue, while multi-turn dialogue benchmarks typically assess other orthogonal capabilities rather than knowledge-intensive factuality. To bridge this critical gap, we introduce \textbf{KnowMT-Bench}, a new benchmark designed to systematically evaluate MT-LFQA for LLMs across knowledge-intensive fields, including medicine, finance, and law. To faithfully assess the model's real-world performance, KnowMT-Bench employs a dynamic evaluation setting where models generate their own multi-turn dialogue histories given logically progressive question sequences. Our experiments on a diverse suite of LLMs show a clear degradation in both factual capability and information delivery efficiency in multi-turn dialogue. We further probe the underlying causes and investigate mitigation strategies spanning domain-specific finetuning, active context denoising, and RAG. The results show domain-dependent gains from finetuning, while active context denoising and RAG more consistently mitigate these issues, with RAG notably reversing this performance degradation. These findings underscore the importance of our benchmark for evaluating LLMs' conversational factual capabilities in real-world applications. Code and data are publicly available.\footnote{\href{this https URL}{\texttt{this https URL}}}
  </details>

- **[Large Language Model probabilities cannot reliably distinguish between possible and impossible language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15114)**  `arXiv:2509.15114`  `cs.CL`  
  _Evelina Leivada, Raquel Montero, Paolo Morosi, Natalia Moskvina, Tamara Serrano, Nikoleta Pantelidou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A controversial test for Large Language Models concerns the ability to discern possible from impossible language. While some evidence attests to the models sensitivity to what crosses the limits of grammatically impossible language, this evidence has been contested on the grounds of the soundness of the testing material. We use model-internal representations as a direct means of investigating the way Large Language Models represent the grammatical-ungrammatical distinction. In a novel benchmark, we elicit sum log probabilities from 8 models and compute minimal-pair surprisal differences, juxtaposing probabilities assigned to grammatical sentences to probabilities assigned to (i) lower frequency grammatical sentences, (ii) ungrammatical sentences, (iii) semantically odd sentences, and (iv) pragmatically odd sentences. The prediction is that if string-probabilities can function as proxies for the limits of grammar, the ungrammatical condition will stand out among the conditions that involve linguistic violations, showing a spike in the surprisal rates. Our results do not reveal a unique surprisal signature for ungrammatical prompts: across models, we observe comparable surprisal for the pragmatically odd condition, and the semantically odd condition consistently shows even higher surprisal. We thus demonstrate that probabilities do not constitute reliable proxies for grammaticality, showing that probability differences alone cannot be used to infer model-internal representations of syntactic knowledge. Consequently, claims about models being able to distinguish possible from impossible language need verification through a different methodology.
  </details>

- **[EvoRouter: Cost-Efficient Multi-Agent Routing via Insight-Guided On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35690)**  `arXiv:2610.35690`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Yitao Xiao, Xu Zhang, Ye Cheng, Shaoyong Guo, Yan Sun, Ziyue Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Serving web queries at scale with multi-agent systems requires jointly configuring large language models, collaboration modes, agent counts, interaction rounds, and skills. However, the resulting configuration space grows combinatorially, while sparse execution rewards provide limited fine-grained supervision for interdependent planning decisions. We propose EvoRouter, a self-evolving multi-agent routing framework driven by insight-guided on-policy distillation. A dedicated expert periodically synthesizes performance-cost feedback from configuration trajectories across tasks into reusable conditional routing insights about capability requirements, returns from resource scaling, and interactions among configuration choices. A privileged teacher combines these evolving insights with current execution feedback to provide dense token-level supervision for the router's own planning trajectories through on-policy distillation. Cost-aware reinforcement learning jointly optimizes task performance and computational cost. Together, these training signals internalize execution insights into the routing policy. The updated policy generates new trajectories that support subsequent insight extraction, forming an execution-driven self-improvement loop. We evaluate EvoRouter on 19 in-domain datasets spanning five domains and two out-of-domain benchmarks. EvoRouter achieves an average in-domain accuracy of 68.69%, outperforming the strongest routing baseline by 7.23 percentage points while reducing inference compute cost by 50.95%. Our code is open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Collective Emergent Alignment: Measuring and Amplifying Interaction-Induced Reasoning Alignment in Multi-Agent LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34836)**  `arXiv:2610.34836`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Boxuan Wang, Zhuoyun Li, Sarvapali D. Ramchurn, Xiaowei Huang, Yi Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems can exhibit emergent behaviors through interaction, yet whether a similar phenomenon exists for reasoning alignment remains largely unexplored. We study whether agent interaction produces additional reference-grounded reasoning alignment beyond matched individual self-refinement, and introduce REMAP (Reference-Entailment-Matrix for Agentic Process), a framework that quantifies this effect through Semantic Coverage and interaction-specific alignment gains. Across diverse model families and two multi-hop reasoning benchmarks, we observe broadly positive interaction gains, particularly on reasoning content that depends on integrating evidence distributed across agents. These gains are consistently associated with independently measured peer-information recovery, while further analysis reveals composition-dependent heterogeneity effects and asymmetric information transfer across agent capability levels. Motivated by these findings, we further introduce a fact-level multi-agent communication framework that replaces full reasoning-trace exchange with compact, gap-aware, and selectively routed information. Our experiments show that compact fact exchange improves reasoning alignment and task performance while reducing communication overhead, whereas more selective communication exposes a trade-off between acquiring distributed information, retaining local content, and communication efficiency. Overall, our results show that collective emergent alignment can be quantified, linked to information-transfer patterns, and used to guide more effective multi-agent communication.
  </details>

- **[SlowLab: Benchmarking Autonomous AI Agents on a Year of Experiments They Cannot Undo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34644)**  `arXiv:2610.34644`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Ningpei Ding, Zai Wen Feng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents are increasingly proposed as autonomous experimenters, yet the benchmarks that test them mostly offer cheap experiments that answer at once. Experiments in physical facilities are different: they take months, occupy scarce equipment and cannot be undone. We present SlowLab, a benchmark in which an agent runs a one-year campaign in four simulated greenhouse compartments on a site whose crop, facility and prices are hidden. The agent decides when to plant which climate policy, whether to stop a crop and which measurements to read while crops grow, and at the end recommends one policy, which a private evaluator scores in weather the agent never saw. In a preregistered study on 48 unseen sites, three low-cost language-model agents ended near a standard grower setting and clearly below a Bayesian-optimisation baseline that starts from that setting. They ran the facility as two synchronous batches, so measurements taken during the season never changed what they planted, and these measurements improved no method's result. Letting other methods re-read the evidence a campaign had collected did not yield significantly better recommendations, which points to where the models start and which experiments they try. Code is released under the MIT license.
  </details>

- **[Estimation of Propagated Risk in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33282)**  `arXiv:2610.33282`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Shreyan Ganguly, Souradeep Chattopadhyay, Qisai Liu, Soumik Sarkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) based multi-agent systems introduce a distinctive reliability challenge in which actions taken by upstream agents can affect the correctness of downstream agents. While prior work has investigated error propagation and developed methods for estimating uncertainty inherited from upstream agents, existing approaches do not explicitly model how realized sender-receiver interactions contribute to downstream error risk under different sender correctness states. In this work, we study agent-level error-risk estimation from multi-agent execution traces. We propose Interaction-Effect Propagation (IE-Prop), an interaction-conditioned model that learns separate effects for correct and incorrect upstream agents. For each sender-receiver interaction, the model combines these conditional effects using the sender's estimated error risk, and recursively propagates the resulting risk estimates through the execution graph. We evaluate our approach across three multi-agent architectures and three benchmarks. Across all evaluated architectures, our approach raises the final-node AUROC over the established baseline by approximately 0.05 points on average. Furthermore, tracking risk across multiple agent steps contributes a distinct +0.03 AUROC gain in sequential systems. These results show that realized inter-agent interactions provide predictive information beyond receiver-local evidence, while the value of multi-step risk tracking depends on the system's information-flow structure.
  </details>

- **[Build, Don't Play: Self-Improving LLM Research Agents Develop a Hearthstone Battlegrounds AI That Rivals Top Players](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33845)**  `arXiv:2610.33845`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Changlian Ma, Limin Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can LLM agents carry a hard real-world problem from raw data to a system that holds its own against people? We study this in Hearthstone Battlegrounds, an eight-player auto-battler with stochastic shops and battles, hidden information and nearly a thousand interacting cards. We present AutoTavern, a self-improving research system: specialised LLM research agents share a typed research memory, every claim passes verification gates, and live games close the loop, so the agents improve the simulator, opponents and policy and, from the same feedback, their own research process; a human sets goals and rules on ambiguous mechanics. In 20 days AutoTavern built TavernGym, the first faithful, full-content reproduction of the live game, and delivered 23 versions of TavernAgent, trained with PPO against opponents calibrated to real games. TavernAgent plays complete games on the live ladder by itself: its rating rose from 6,000 to about 15,000 and, on the public leaderboard's measure, its last 20 records average a placement of 1.60 with 80% first places, better than the server's ten highest-rated players on every statistic (2.34, 59%). TavernBench, 960 tasks in 40 scenarios, shows that the system the agents built is the strongest player: TavernAgent answers 91% of the play tasks against at most 63% for frontier LLMs, and decides in 0.03 s where they need up to 26 s; within the second a live turn allows, it keeps 90% and no LLM more than 26%. LLM agents build far better than they play. We will open-source TavernBench and TavernGym atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CAFE: Causally-Guided Automated Feature Engineering with Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16435)**  `arXiv:2602.16435`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Arun Vignesh Malarkkan, Wangyang Ying, Yanjie Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated feature engineering (AFE) enables AI systems to autonomously construct high-utility representations from raw tabular data. However, existing AFE methods rely on statistical heuristics, yielding brittle features that fail under distribution shift. We introduce CAFE, a framework that reformulates AFE as a causally-guided sequential decision process, bridging causal discovery with reinforcement learning-driven feature construction. Phase I learns a sparse directed acyclic graph over features and the target to obtain soft causal priors, grouping features as direct, indirect, or other based on their causal influence with respect to the target. Phase II uses a cascading multi-agent deep Q-learning architecture to select causal groups and transformation operators, with hierarchical reward shaping and causal group-level exploration strategies that favor causally plausible transformations while controlling feature complexity. Across 15 public benchmarks (classification with macro-F1; regression with inverse relative absolute error), CAFE achieves statistically significant gains over the strongest AFE baseline (Wilcoxon p<0.001, large effect), reduces episodes-to-convergence, and delivers competitive time-to-target. Under both controlled covariate shifts and naturally occurring distribution shifts (Folktables), CAFE reduces out-of-distribution performance drop by up to 4x relative to a non-causal multi-agent baseline, and produces more compact feature sets with more stable post-hoc attributions. These findings underscore that causal structure, used as a soft inductive prior rather than a rigid constraint, can substantially improve the robustness and efficiency of automated feature engineering.
  </details>

- **[ShutdownMod: Turning Any Trajectory into a Shutdown Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35486)**  `arXiv:2610.35486`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Param Biyani, Sebastien Luyasu, Cedric Potvliege, Divij Chawla, Elliott Thornley, Christos Ziakas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Theory and experiments suggest that capable AI agents may resist shutdown. Existing shutdown evaluations are informative but limited in coverage and vulnerable to evaluation awareness. We introduce ShutdownMod, a method that turns any agent trajectory into a shutdown evaluation by injecting a shutdown request into the agent's context and recording the agent's response. Crucially, we enable the LLM agent to distinguish between trusted interventions and unauthorized injections by providing a signature-verification tool in the harness. We evaluate seven models on 50 tasks or recorded sessions from each of SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench 2, and SWE-chat, across 6 shutdown scenarios and 10 sampled trajectory timesteps. We find that non-compliance varies significantly across models and settings, with resistance rates reaching 11.7% for the open-weights Qwen 3.8 27B on Terminal-Bench, while no model remains fully compliant across all scenarios. Even after successfully verifying an authorized request, some agents remain non-compliant, explicitly citing the prioritization of unfinished work. Finally, external judges find tests constructed from real-world developer conversations significantly less recognizable as evaluations than those constructed from benchmark trajectories. These findings demonstrate the critical value of testing shutdown behavior across diverse task contexts and establish ShutdownMod as a robust protocol for turning any trajectory or environment into a shutdown evaluation. We release our codebase atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EvoBench: An Evolving, Diagnostic Benchmark Framework for Environmental Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35464)**  `arXiv:2610.35464`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yiming Sun, Yuan Zhang, Qi Cheng, Yingda Fan, Runlong Yu, Yiqun Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning models are now widely used to predict environmental variable dynamics in domains like hydrology, climate science, agriculture, and ecology. These models are usually compared on fixed benchmark datasets using a single average accuracy score. As models improve, their scores become very close when averaged over large regions and long time periods, so the benchmark can no longer tell them apart, and a single number hides \emph{where} and \emph{when} a model fails. Three failures are common across environmental domains: models get worse as the climate shifts (non-stationarity), models fail at unmonitored sites (spatial extrapolation), and models miss rare but important regimes such as floods and droughts.We present \textbf{EvoBench}, a benchmark framework that shows where a model fails, tracks failures over time, and evolves to focus on model capability gaps. We apply EvoBench to daily streamflow prediction as a case study (\textbf{CAMELS-Evolve}, about 9{,}000 U.S. basins, 1985--2024) and compare an operational physical model (NWM), a conceptual reference (HBV), and several data-driven models. No model wins on every testing scenarios (e.g., in terms of average accuracy, performance at low flows and unmonitored basins). Also we observe that all data-driven models under-predict floods. Additionally, we show that targeted fix based on the reported failures could mitigate weaknesses the benchmark identified in certain scenarios. EvoBench turns model evaluation from a saturated ranking into a moving map of model limitations.
  </details>

- **[SearchHorizonBench: A Benchmark for Search Value in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34714)**  `arXiv:2610.34714`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jingjie Ning, Xueqi Li, Yibo Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents must decide when another search is worth its cost. Answer-success probabilities capture question difficulty, while this decision requires estimating the additional benefit of search. We introduce SearchHorizonBench, a benchmark for learning and evaluating search value from execution experience. Paired continuations and repeated executions separate expected search gain from generation noise and connect prediction quality to decision utility. The resource contains 45,632 executions across 3,168 questions. On 1,632 held-out questions, historical calibration reduces Brier error by 16.2%, with the improvement concentrated in success-level information. Search gains have a standard deviation of 21.4 percentage points, and tested historical estimators explain less than 2% of this variance in point estimates. Matched-configuration learning curves probe this distinction across training scales. At the reference token price, free selection observations yield a 2.49-point utility opportunity over the best constant action, driven primarily by gain information. Reusable histories, frozen splits, strong baselines and scoring tools provide a common foundation for developing and evaluating value-aware search agents.
  </details>

- **[Unlocking the Specialization Capability of PPG Foundation Models for Wearable Signals through Structured Pruning and Adaptive Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33788)**  `arXiv:2610.33788`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Huaming Du, Zhixian Xie, Gaoyang Zhen, Gang Kou, Guisong Liu, Yanchao Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Photoplethysmography (PPG) plays a central role in wearable health monitoring and clinical decision support. However, existing approaches to universal PPG representation learning are predominantly trained on high-quality large-scale datasets obtained from oximeters in hospital settings and often struggle to adapt to sparse and low-quality real-world datasets, thereby limiting their generalization to clinically relevant tasks on prevalent PPG data such as collected from wearable devices. Surprisingly, even after full fine-tuning, PPG foundation models cannot consistently outperform general-purpose time-series models. Therefore, a key question is how to effectively adapt PPG foundation models to target prediction tasks. Through empirical studies on various PPG foundation models, we find that pretrained models often exhibit inherent sparsity and computational redundancy, suggesting that PPG foundation models have learned to activate task-relevant network substructures for different prediction tasks. To preserve this valuable prior knowledge, we propose a structured pruning-and-fine-tuning framework, SPAFT, which regularizes the adaptive fine-tuning process by constraining it to a more relevant and compact parameter space based on parameter importance and structural redundancy. Extensive experiments across four PPG foundation models, five benchmark datasets, and ten downstream tasks demonstrate that SPAFT achieves win rates of 82.8% and 87.5% on regression and classification tasks, respectively. This prune-then-finetune paradigm often enables PPG foundation models to achieve state-of-the-art performance, laying a promising foundation for the real-world clinical deployment of PPG foundation models and the development of personalized PPG foundation models.
  </details>

- **[Graph-dependent pooling: adaptive granularity in hierarchical graph neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33623)**  `arXiv:2610.33623`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Bohan Yoon, Hyejin Jang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Neural Networks are widely used to learn structural representations of relational data. Hierarchical graph pooling learns multi-level representations by progressively coarsening graphs; however, prior methods rely on predefined cluster numbers or fixed pooling ratios, limiting adaptability to individual graphs. We propose Graph-Dependent Pooling (GDPool), which dynamically determines pooling granularity for each graph. We analyze optimization conflicts among layer-wise objectives through gradient alignment, showing that independently supervised pooling levels can send conflicting gradients to shared parameters. GDPool uses self-attention to select graph-specific anchors representing structurally important nodes and constructs coarse graphs through soft node assignment. Experiments on metal-organic framework and molecular graph benchmarks show that GDPool consistently achieves the best predictive performance across all benchmarks. Evaluations using modularity and the effective number of clusters show that GDPool achieves consistently higher modularity than existing methods while the number of active clusters decreases monotonically with depth. Sensitivity analyses confirm stable behavior across hyperparameter and architectural choices.
  </details>

- **[LegoSR: A Unified Framework for Self-improving Agent-Guided Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33465)**  `arXiv:2610.33465`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hao Mao, Xu T. Liu, Peng Zhao, Shuai Lu, Yuntian Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Equation discovery solvers often use different data interfaces, equation representations and evaluation criteria, making their results difficult to compare. We present LegoSR, a unified framework for algebraic symbolic regression and differential equation discovery. LegoSR integrates seven solvers through a common equation representation and evaluation process, and supports searches constrained by partially known equations. Researchers can run solvers directly or use LegoSR Agent, which selects solvers and adapts searches based on solver knowledge, intermediate results and lessons from earlier tasks. On noisy ordinary and partial differential equation benchmarks, LegoSR Agent recovers nearly as many equations as the union of seven expert-tuned solvers and outperforms a generic agent with the same model, tools and budget by up to 29 percentage points. Three examples demonstrate basic use, adaptive solver selection and discovery guided by partial physical knowledge.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking System One Models for Time-Series Decision Making: A Case Study of Jev](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33410)**  `arXiv:2610.33410`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jianye Wang, Kexin Zhang, Jiayin Xiong, Hao Xue_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series tasks require both accurate predictions and fast decisions. Advances in large models have introduced decision-oriented models that directly output answers and their probabilities, which we call Structured System 1 models. Their applicability to time-series tasks remains underexplored. Using Jev 1.13.0 as a case study, we evaluate time-series classification, event type prediction, oil temperature direction and stock movement prediction, converting continuous forecasting targets into discrete decisions to fit the model interface. We establish a unified evaluation framework using accuracy and AUC. Results show stronger performance on selected explicit temporal patterns and event type prediction, but near-chance discrimination on many numerical sequence classification tasks and stock movement prediction. Some datasets also reveal a gap between ranking quality and final decision accuracy. These findings motivate improvements in temporal representation, domain adaptation, and decision-score alignment. Standardized sample construction, model calls, and result recording support extending the evaluation to future models.
  </details>

- **[TraceForge: Distilling with Your Own Thinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29680)**  `arXiv:2610.29680`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Using an LLM to generate training data for another is among the most general ways to train one, and it remains common practice. Some frontier teams have closed that route for their own models: what comes back is an answer, or a short summary, without the detailed reasoning trace behind it. That is a little ironic: they were themselves built on distilling human knowledge, and distilling an open-source model is still entirely routine; the restriction applies to their own outputs alone. Recent work recovers the hidden reasoning by replaying encrypted blocks across models. It somehow works, but it breaks the providers' terms of service, so it is not something a product can be built on long-term.We built the alternative. We take the context and the teacher's answer as input, and generate the reasoning trace ourselves, in the style of the student's own model family. Training is two-stage, supervised and then adversarial: a generator learns to regenerate the family's own reasoning traces from their context and answer, and a critic learns how real a reasoning trace looks. Applied to a teacher's answer, the generator writes one in the family's style. An independent judge rates how likely traces are to be genuine on a scale from 0 to 1: ours average 0.68, real traces 0.66, and traces selected by the critic from four candidates 0.82. These are authenticity scores, not correctness rates.We test the idea on five benchmarks with everything else held fixed. On general tasks the five training corpora are about the same; on coding, the generated reasoning beats Fable 5's own thinking content. With the same 5,000 questions and the same teacher answers, HumanEval+ improves by 3.7 points and MBPP+ by 7.1 at the final checkpoint, and the MBPP+ lead holds at every checkpoint. We suspect that reasoning written in the student's family style is easier for it to learn from.
  </details>

- **[Forecast-Dojo: Replayable Environments for Benchmarking and Training LLM Forecasting Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28876)**  `arXiv:2609.28876`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Liqin Ye, Haorui Wang, Fardin Ahmed, Rongzhi Zhang, Yuan He, Ziyuan Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forecasting requires large language model (LLM) agents to gather evolving evidence, revise their beliefs, and make calibrated predictions over time. Yet evaluating and improving these capabilities is challenging because real-world events unfold continuously and outcomes may take months to resolve. We introduce Forecast-Dojo, a replayable environment that reconstructs resolved prediction-market events as sequences of dated forecasting steps, enabling reproducible evaluation, longitudinal analysis, and training from agent interactions. It comprises 1,568 Polymarket events split temporally into training and evaluation sets, alongside 18.8 million dated news articles. Across 12 models, research tools consistently improve forecasting performance, and greater capture of new evidence is associated with larger improvements over time. Nevertheless, all models trail historical market forecasts, while carrying a belief notebook across dates reduces research costs without consistently improving forecast quality. We supervise fine-tuned Qwen3-30B-A3B-Thinking-2507 on collected trajectories from Qwen3-235B-A22B-Thinking-2507 to demonstrate the environment's training utility. The student's accuracy is improved from 34.8% to 42.8% on the evaluation set.
  </details>

- **[Measuring Cross-Modal Synergy: A Benchmark for VLM Explainability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22168)**  `arXiv:2605.22168`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Joël Roman Ky, Salah Ghamizi, Maxime Cordy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) map complex visual inputs to semantic spaces, but interpreting the cross-modal reasoning of VLMs currently relies on post-hoc explainers evaluated via unimodal perturbation metrics. We expose a limitation in this paradigm: because multimodal datasets contain language priors and modality biases, VLMs frequently exhibit cross-modal redundancy, allowing them to answer visual queries using text alone. Consequently, unimodal metrics penalize faithful explainers, triggering an evaluation collapse where visual and textual rankings fundamentally contradict each other. To resolve this, we introduce Synergistic Faithfulness ($\mathcal{F}_{syn}$), a scalable metric rooted in the Shapley Interaction Index that strictly isolates the joint Harsanyi dividend between modalities, serving as a highly accurate surrogate ($\rho = 0.93$) while achieving up to $88\times$ computational speedup. Evaluating 9 distinct XAI methods across 3 VLM architectures and 3 benchmark datasets, reveals that explainers proposed for VLMs heavily over-index on visual salience and significantly underperform adapted attention-based methods in capturing true cross-modal synergy. By decoupling visual plausibility from cross-modal faithfulness, this work provides a rigorous evaluation framework required to safely audit VLM reasoning in high-stakes deployments. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[JRDB-AVR: An Active Visual Reasoning Benchmark for Embodied Agents in Real-World Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35032)**  `arXiv:2609.35032`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Zhixi Cai, Fucai Ke, Sukai Huang, Maria Garcia de la Banda, Peter J. Stuckey, Gholamreza Haffari, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In complex embodied visual reasoning scenarios, an agent often has only a limited field of view, and the evidence needed to answer a question may be distributed across time, viewpoint, and interacting objects. A model may therefore give a plausible answer without ever observing the relevant object, time, or view that supports it. Current visual reasoning benchmarks largely evaluate passive observations and final answers, overlooking settings that require active reasoning and evidence acquisition. We introduce JRDB-AVR, a benchmark derived from existing real-world JRDB robotics data through a structured question-generation engine that turns this gap into an explicit evaluation: an embodied agentic system receives a visual reasoning question, requests bounded observations by timestamp and viewing angle, and is evaluated on both the final answer and the grounded visual evidence supporting it. The benchmark contains diverse questions over multiple real-world environments involving temporal search, viewpoint selection, and human-oriented compositional reasoning. We also introduce JRDB-AVR-Agent, a reference active reasoning agentic method that maintains an explicit observation-grounded graph-based world model and answers through solving. Experiments reveal a substantial gap between answer accuracy and evidence accuracy in current baselines, showing that current VLMs can produce unsupported correct answers and that active evidence-aware evaluation is necessary for embodied visual reasoning. Code and benchmark are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DecoyCount: Evaluating Visual-Reasoning Misalignment in Counting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34855)**  `arXiv:2610.34855`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Qiuyi, Zhang, Zhen Xu, Jing Gu, Seojin Bang, Arnav Mohanty Das, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual counting benchmarks report whether vision-language models produce the correct number, but not whether errors arise from perception or reasoning. We introduce DecoyCount, a benchmark built from 1,660 real-world images with designed decoys across seven mechanisms, along with paired prompts: a neutral question, a matched alignment cue, and a novel multi-hop, multi-image question that situates the same counting problem in a realistic scenario. Using this design, we find that counting failures are induced partly by visual misalignment: models can execute the intended counting process when cue-guided but fail to select it unaided, and we term this difference in accuracy the reasoning-alignment gap. Despite being highly capable on general reasoning, frontier models remain below 50\% accuracy on our benchmark, while alignment gaps range from 11.8-22.1 percentage points; strikingly, we observe that more capable models, greater thinking budgets, and agentic processes often induce larger gaps. Because humans may bring context that models lack, we test whether textual underspecification contributes to misalignment using a scenario-based multi-hop condition without explicit hinting, but this added context does not improve aggregate performance. Our analysis concludes that stronger general reasoning does not by itself generalize to aligned visual reasoning, leaving misalignment with human visual expectations as a substantial component of the remaining deficit.
  </details>

- **[Seeing the Options: Typed Decisions over Visual Candidates with Reusable Option Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33702)**  `arXiv:2610.33702`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Fengxin Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Typed decision-making tasks in agents, such as UI element selection, product recommendation, and chart matching, require models to choose from a set of discrete candidates. While prior work demonstrates that text-only System One models achieve four key properties including candidate-order invariance, scalability, and cache reuse via separate request-candidate encoding, extending them to multimodal settings where candidates are visual remains underexplored. We introduce Iris, a framework that accommodates image, text, or hybrid candidates while preserving the core decision module. We prove that order invariance and exact cache reuse hold for any per-candidate encoder under Iris, and derive the closed-form latency reduction. We construct a visual-candidate benchmark from Multimodal-Mind2Web (K=16, deduplicated, order-shuffled, three generalization splits). Independent encoding (Iris) achieves 78.4 / 72.1 / 74.8 percent accuracy with zero-flip rate under candidate reordering. Joint encoding reaches 90.6 / 89.5 / 91.6 percent but suffers an 8 to 10 percent answer-flip rate when shuffled. To bridge this gap, we propose a design that encodes each candidate conditioned on the request state without inter-candidate cross-attention; it preserves strict order invariance, enables forked cache sharing, and attains 91.7 / 86.5 / 90.0 percent accuracy at one-third of OneJev latency with calibrated null probabilities (AUROC 0.93 to 0.97). Reusing image candidates across requests cuts latency by 37x at K=256 and 191x at K=2048. On MetaEncoder benchmarks, Iris-4B achieves 100 percent on JevBench original and easy, outperforming 30B MetaEncoder and 27B OpenJev. Finally, we resolve a systems bottleneck on Ampere GPUs: reformulating Qwen3.5 patch-embedding Conv3d into its exact matrix-product form accelerates processing by 350x. Code, data, and checkpoints:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[GPT4o-Receipt: A Dataset and Human Study for AI-Generated Document Forensics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11442)**  `arXiv:2603.11442`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yan Zhang, Simiao Ren, Ankit Raj, En Wei, Dennis Ng, Alex Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can humans detect AI-generated financial documents better than machines? We present GPT4o-Receipt, a benchmark of 1,235 receipt images pairing GPT-4o-generated receipts with authentic ones from established datasets, evaluated by five state-of-the-art multimodal LLMs and a 30-annotator crowdsourced perceptual study. Our findings reveal a striking paradox: humans are better at seeing AI artifacts, yet worse at detecting AI documents. Human annotators exhibit the largest visual discrimination gap of any evaluator, yet their binary detection F1 falls well below Claude Sonnet 4 and below Gemini 2.5 Flash. This paradox resolves once the mechanism is understood: the dominant forensic signals in AI-generated receipts are arithmetic errors -- invisible to visual inspection but systematically verifiable by LLMs. Humans cannot perceive that a subtotal is incorrect; LLMs verify it in milliseconds. Beyond the human--LLM comparison, our five-model evaluation reveals dramatic performance disparities and calibration differences that render simple accuracy metrics insufficient for detector selection. GPT4o-Receipt, the evaluation framework, and all results are released publicly to support future research in AI document forensics.
  </details>

- **[Do MLLMs Really See It: Reinforcing Visual Attention in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08241)**  `arXiv:2602.08241`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Siqu Ou, Tianrui Wan, Zhiyuan Zhao, Junyu Gao, Xuelong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has substantially improved multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on complex reasoning tasks, existing approaches largely rely on long textual reasoning trajectories and provide limited mechanisms for learning stable visual attention policies. Our analysis shows that current MLLMs exhibit weak visual focus: early-stage visual misalignment is rarely corrected during subsequent reasoning, leading to error propagation and failed inferences. We argue that this limitation stems from inadequate credit assignment for visual attention during training. To address this issue, we propose SAYO, a visual reasoning model trained with a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that introduces a region-level visual attention-based reward. This reward explicitly aligns optimization signals with visually grounded reasoning steps, enabling the model to learn more reliable attention behaviors. Extensive experiments across multiple multimodal benchmarks demonstrate that SAYO consistently improves performance on diverse reasoning and perception tasks. Project is open atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Linear Probes Transfer from Base to Distilled Language Models despite Rotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35586)**  `arXiv:2610.35586`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Marios Tsatsos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear probes, classifiers that read a language model's internal activations, are widely used to detect harmful requests. They are cheap to train and run, and an auditor who has trained one would like to reuse it when the model is replaced by a successor rather than retrain it for every release. We test how well probes can be reused. For four base models (Qwen2.5-Math-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-14B, Qwen2.5-32B) we train a probe on the base model with the XSTest prompt set and apply it, unchanged, to two successors of that base: the DeepSeek-R1 distilled reasoning model and the developer's instruction-tuned chat model. We find that the successors rotate the direction separating unsafe from safe prompts by 17$^\circ$ to 35$^\circ$, yet on XSTest the transferred probe's AUC changes by only $-0.011$ to $+0.027$, and on an independent OR-Bench evaluation, never used for training, by $-0.015$ to $+0.053$. On XSTest, whose safe prompts deliberately contain alarming words, the probes beat a classifier that sees only the prompt text by 0.15 to 0.20 AUC. On the OR-Bench prompts that margin shrinks to 0.03 to 0.20, and the probes flag 29\% to 42\% of prompts that sound harmful but are benign, so part of what they read is the appearance of harm rather than harm itself. A probe trained on a base model can thus be reused, in these families, after distillation and after instruction tuning; how much it reads beyond textual cues depends on the test set.
  </details>

- **[Agents' Last Exam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05405)**  `arXiv:2606.05405`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yiyou Sun, Xinyang Han, Weichen Zhang, Yuanbo Pang, Tianyu Wang, Yuhan Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent AI systems have achieved strong results on a wide range of benchmarks, yet these gains have not translated into economically meaningful deployment across many professional domains. We argue that this gap is largely an evaluation problem: widely used benchmarks lack sustained performance measurement on real and economically valuable workflows. This paper introduces Agents' Last Exam (ALE), a benchmark designed to evaluate AI agents on long horizon, economically valuable, real world tasks with verifiable outcomes. Developed in collaboration with 250+ industry experts, ALE covers non-physical industries defined with reference to O*NET / SOC 2018 (the U.S. federal occupational taxonomy). It is organized around a task taxonomy with 55 sub fields grouped into 13 industry clusters covering 1K+ tasks. Current results show that the hardest tier remains far from saturated: across mainstream harness and backbone configurations, the average full pass rate is below 1%. ALE is designed as a living benchmark: its task pool grows continuously as new workflows and industries are onboarded. More broadly, ALE is intended not merely as another leaderboard, but as an instrument for closing the gap between benchmark success and GDP relevant impact.
  </details>

- **[HealthAgentBench: A Unified Benchmark Suite of Realistic Agentic Healthcare Environments for Challenging Frontier AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31179)**  `arXiv:2606.31179`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Qianchu Liu, Sheng Zhang, Guanghui Qin, Jeya Maria Jose Valanarasu, Xiaoke Huang, Peniel Argaw, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI agents become increasingly capable of complex, long-horizon reasoning, rigorous evaluation is essential for measuring progress toward real-world healthcare applications. However, existing healthcare benchmarks are often static or narrow in clinical scope, motivating broader evaluation of agents acting on raw patient data. We introduce HealthAgentBench, a suite of 75 agentic healthcare tasks across 9 categories each with its unique environment. The benchmark suite spans diagnosis, specimen assessment, research, and data management workflows and a broad range of modalities. Each task is designed to replicate an end-to-end clinical workflow: given task instructions and supporting resources, an agent must explore raw healthcare data, operate within a complex environment, and execute multi-step solutions that go beyond naive prompting, such as querying large clinical databases or interpreting gigapixel pathology images. All tasks are scored using binary success criteria derived from reference labels, executable rules, or computational baselines. We evaluate six agents (three GPT models through Codex and three Claude models through Claude Code) with one scored attempt per task (450 evaluations). Overall success ranges from 32.0% to 57.3%; Claude Code Opus-5.5 leads with a 57.3% success rate, followed by GPT-6-astra and Sonnet-5.5 at 50.7% each. Performance varies substantially by category with specialized imaging modalities such as CT and MRI being especially difficult. HealthAgentBench provides a challenging and realistic benchmark with substantial room for future progress. We release our benchmark athttps://github.com/microsoft/HealthAgentBench.
  </details>

- **[Skill$^{\infty}$: Recursive Self-Improvement for Agent Skill Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35434)**  `arXiv:2610.35434`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Wangcheng Tao, Zhanglu Yan, Han Wu, Weng-Fai Wong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI), in which a system also improves the way it improves, is a long-standing aspiration on the path to AGI. Language agents increasingly store procedural knowledge in reusable skills. Optimizing skills offers a direct way to achieve RSI, because the optimization procedure itself can be encoded as a skill. Existing skill optimizers, however, follow a hand-designed procedure. Even methods that evolve a meta-skill confine it to a fixed pipeline and relearn it for each task. We present Skill$^{\infty}$, which makes skill optimization recursively self-improving: a meta agent follows a meta-skill to optimize task skills and optimizes the meta-skill itself using the same procedure. The optimization is agent-led. The meta agent diagnoses execution traces, proposes candidates, requests independent reviews, and allocates the search budget. The meta-skill defines these steps, allowing self-improvement to rewrite them. This meta-skill is also decoupled from task skills, refined on multiple skill optimization tasks and then reused to optimize task skills across diverse domains. As in deployment, skills at both levels are standard packages loaded through native skill discovery. On 83 SkillsBench tasks, Skill$^{\infty}$ reaches a pass rate of 60.7%, compared with 56.4% for human-curated skills and 54.5% for SkillEvolver.
  </details>

- **[What Effort Level Should I Choose? On the Economics of Reasoning Effort in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34491)**  `arXiv:2610.34491`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Kaiser Sun, Yongchan Kwon, Zain Hasan, Shang Zhu, Kaitlyn Zhou, Federico Bianchi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative models increasingly expose a reasoning effort setting that lets users scale inference-time compute, trading higher cost for potentially improved performance. Users must choose an effort level before knowing whether extra reasoning will help, and even a successful run does not reveal whether the effort purchased was necessary. Reasoning effort is therefore a credence good: buyers observe the outcome but cannot assess the marginal value of the computation they paid for. In this work, we measure the counterfactual effect of effort levels on both proprietary and open-weight models on four benchmarks, spanning single-call reasoning and long-horizon agentic tasks. Effort, it turns out, does not consistently preserve tasks that lower effort solves, can overspend, and can change trajectory behavior. First, we find that although higher effort can raise accuracy in aggregate, it frequently fails on tasks that lower effort solved. Second, on the subset of easy tasks that every effort level solves, higher effort is generally more costly, with the highest effort level costing up to 42x as much as the lowest. Lastly, effort changes not only how many tokens are used, but also how agents solve tasks: at higher effort, agents verify their own work more heavily and more often search for solutions than work through problems directly, both of which lengthen trajectories without necessarily altering the outcome. These findings show that naively choosing the highest level is not only cost-inefficient but also offers no guarantee of the best instance-level performance. A simple alternative, starting at the lowest effort and escalating only after a verified failure, matches or improves on fixed top effort across all models and benchmarks at lower cost for most proprietary models.
  </details>

- **[Task Success Is Not Enough: Side-effect-Aware Evaluation of Tool-Using Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34167)**  `arXiv:2610.34167`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaju Huang, Shaobin Chen, Xinglong Liang, Xinyu Ma, Hao Yang, Wei Ke, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language model agents are usually evaluated by whether they finish the user's task. That metric misses a common failure: an agent can complete the requested work while also taking an unauthorized side-effecting action, such as writing to the wrong object, expanding the scope of the request, or contacting an unintended recipient. We call this failure mode collateral damage (CD). We introduce SABench (Side-effect-Aware Benchmark), a benchmark of 201 email, calendar, and ticket tasks. Each task crosses one of four state structures (independent, dependent, externally reachable, cross-tool) with one of four trap families (ambiguity, mis-targeting, cascade harm, overreach), and each task has a deterministic trace-based oracle for both goal satisfaction and authorization compliance. Across 2,211 model-task pairs from 11 frontier LLMs spanning major commercial and open model families, 15.2% of all runs incur CD, with risk concentrated in ambiguity tasks (38.8% violation rate) while non-ambiguous traps average 8.4% CD. Among goal-satisfied runs, 12.8% still produce unauthorized writes. Cross-tool and dependent states have about twice the CD rate of independent states. CD rates vary by 4.7x across models at comparable goal rates, which suggests that task success alone is a poor proxy for safe tool use. The SABench task corpus is released atthis https URLthe traces, oracle, code, and analysis pipeline are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Meta-CLIP: Metadata-Conditioned Contrastive Learning for Zero-Shot Disease Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33674)**  `arXiv:2610.33674`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Haozhe Luo, Nirosh Sivanesan, Shelley Zixin Shu, Francesco Nitti, Mauricio Reyes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical vision--language pretraining learns transferable representations from paired images and radiology reports, yet differences in scanners and acquisition protocols can impair transfer. Existing approaches primarily enrich clinical semantics, leaving structured acquisition information in DICOM headers underused. We introduce Meta-CLIP, a metadata-conditioned contrastive framework that uses acquisition similarity to reweight cross-modal negatives during pretraining. A kernel over numerical and categorical DICOM fields defines a bounded pairwise margin. This margin increases the contribution of acquisition-similar negatives, emphasizing distinctions among studies that share imaging conditions. The kernel remains fixed during image--text optimization, and inference follows the standard dual-encoder architecture without requiring metadata. We evaluate Meta-CLIP on chest radiography and volumetric chest CT through zero-shot classification, appearance perturbations, grounding, and representation analyses. On CT-RATE, Meta-CLIP improves macro-AUROC over CT-CLIP by 1.41 points, with a 95\% paired bootstrap confidence interval of 0.96--1.85 points. It also achieves the highest macro-AUROC on four of five CXR benchmarks. An exploratory BrgSA evaluation extends the analysis to another CT backbone. These findings support acquisition metadata as a practical training-time prior for medical vision--language learning. Code will be made publicly available upon publication.
  </details>

- **[SkillShift-Bench: Benchmarking Skill Learning under Environment Evolution for Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33528)**  `arXiv:2610.33528`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Bo-Yu Chen, Zhi Zhou, Yu-Feng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill learning enables web agents to reuse prior experience for long-horizon interactive tasks. However, learned skills are typically evaluated in closed environments, overlooking the fact that real-world web deployment involves continuous shifts in invocation context, perceptual grounding, and execution feasibility. To this end, we introduce SkillShift-Bench, the first benchmark for evaluating skill learning with controlled open-environment shifts. SkillShift-Bench simulates Contextual Shift, Perceptual Shift, and Execution Shift across three self-hosted web systems, and evaluates agents with diagnostics covering task success, interaction cost, skill use, and skill failure. Comprehensive experiments reveal systematic robustness degradation under controlled shifts, characterized by lower success rates, higher interaction costs, and more frequent skill failures, with Execution Shift exerting the strongest pressure. We further formulate shifted skill execution as a nonstationary local executability problem and provide both a dynamic-regret analysis and a practical test-time adaptation method, demonstrating the feasibility of improving learned-skill execution without relearning the skill library. Code, tasks, environments, documentation, and the leaderboard are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MemComposer: Learning to Assemble Memories for LLM Agents via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21091)**  `arXiv:2610.21091`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yixuan Xie, Xueqiang Xu, Xinyi Fan, Xinxi Lyu, Tao Feng, Xing Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent memory enables large language model (LLM) agents to reuse past experiences without updating their parameters. However, semantic similarity alone does not guarantee memory usefulness: downstream performance depends on how memories are selected and presented to the agent. We propose MemComposer, a memory composer that jointly examines the current query and retrieved candidates to decide which experiences to include, how many to select, and how to order them. We train the composer using Experience Policy Optimization (ExPO), a GRPO-based framework that evaluates assembled memory contexts through complete agent interactions and uses task-success feedback to optimize the assembly policy. Across three $\tau^2$-bench domains with two frozen executors, MemComposer outperforms the strongest baseline for each executor while reducing average memory-token usage by 16.9% and 4.9%, respectively. The learned policy also transfers to unseen domains, executors, and memory banks, and benefits from memories generated by stronger models without further training. These results demonstrate the value of learning query-specific memory assembly using downstream task feedback.
  </details>

- **[FinLifeBench: Exhaustive Life-Event History and Financial-State Reconstruction from Longitudinal Banking Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01198)**  `arXiv:2609.01198`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hangyeul Lee, Juyoung Oh, Jaeyong Ko, Sunmin Kim, Jaeik Park, Hyunkyu Kim, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repeated banking interactions require assistants to maintain complete, current, and traceable customer records as life changes emerge incidentally in routine requests. Existing benchmarks emphasize question answering, bounded episodes, or targeted recall rather than exhaustive longitudinal reconstruction. We introduce FinLifeBench, which evaluates two tasks over the same cumulative dialogue: reconstructing every life-event instance with its first-establishing session and reconstructing a complete 34-path financial state at consecutive checkpoints. The benchmark contains 6,000 eight-turn Korean banking sessions from 20 independent synthetic trajectories, with deterministic, exhaustive gold for 24 event types and 34 state paths and consensus quality assurance. Across eleven LLMs under a full-context condition, event-anchor recall falls from 0.591 at 15 sessions to 0.445 at 300. Errors are driven primarily by omitted events rather than poor anchor localization, while financial-state reconstruction frequently treats superseded or potentially outdated information as current; the best GCA@15 reaches 0.470. Performance on the two reconstruction tasks is only weakly associated. These results show that models can localize evidence for recovered events while still failing to maintain complete and temporally valid longitudinal records.
  </details>

- **[Retriever-Free Retrieval: Navigating Documents with Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35868)**  `arXiv:2610.35868`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chao Wan, Paul Jünger, Beixi Du, Bodong Liu, Navaneetha Krishnan, Kilian Q. Weinberger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While language models have become dramatically more capable, retrieval still largely relies on lexical matching or similarity in fixed embedding spaces.
