This creates a capability asymmetry: what the retriever fails to find, even the strongest generator cannot recover. We propose DecisionRAG, which brings the capabilities of pretrained language models to retrieval using decision models that jointly evaluate candidate branches in a single forward pass, without generating any tokens, and show that judging candidates in a shared context makes their relevance estimates comparable. Like a person browsing a library, DecisionRAG navigates the corpus's inherent structure from broad topics down to individual passages, deciding at each level which branches are worth exploring. Without task-specific training or a first-stage retriever, DecisionRAG outperforms dense retrieval with an 8B reranker by 13.8 R@1 points on MultiDoc2Dial and 17.4 Hit@1 points on Natural Questions. Across four benchmarks, including a corpus of 2.68 million Wikipedia paragraphs, retrieval takes just 0.7-1.8 seconds per query.
  </details>

- **[Patient-specific rubric-based evaluation of large language models across longitudinal cancer care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35847)**  `arXiv:2610.35847`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wei-Wei Zhang, Pengkai Wang, Yan Li, Zhijie Sang, Guan-Qun Zhou, Jun-Yan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) show promise in specialty medicine, yet existing evaluations largely focus on isolated clinical encounters rather than tracking how clinical responsibilities evolve across a longitudinal care pathway. Here we introduce NPC-RubricBench, a phase-aware benchmark for evaluating multistage clinical reasoning across the cancer care continuum, using nasopharyngeal carcinoma as a defined disease context. Curated from longitudinal records of 405 patients across two cancer centers, the benchmark comprises 941 patient-phase units across six sequential care phases and 17,688 patient-specific atomic criteria spanning five evaluation dimensions, with each assessment strictly restricted to decision-time clinical evidence. Evaluating 30 general-purpose and medical LLMs revealed that strong diagnostic performance does not carry over to downstream care. We evaluated 30 general-purpose and medical LLMs, showing that high diagnostic performance did not translate into improved downstream care. Instead, the dominant failure modes reversed across phases. Explicit decision errors dominated anatomical staging at 48.2%, whereas decision omissions dominated initial work-up and post-treatment follow-up at 33.4% and 30.4%, respectively. In the evaluated model set, medically adapted models showed higher omission rates than general-purpose models. Moreover, varying query granularity exposes a critical specification trade-off: broad open-ended requests degrade Decision Quality across all 30 models, while more detailed, checklist-like prompts raise Decision Quality but reduce Evidence Faithfulness and Safety Adherence. Furthermore, NPC-RubricBench demonstrates that static diagnostic benchmarks are insufficient to ensure consistent clinical reliability over time, highlighting the importance of phase-adaptive safeguards in multistage medical AI.
  </details>

- **[CLOSURE: Cross-Channel Closure for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35753)**  `arXiv:2610.35753`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fan Xu, Hao Wu, Zhi Song, Hanbo Huang, Wenzhuo Ma, Nan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multivariate time series produced by industrial installations and server clusters are under continuous monitoring, and anomaly detection is the first line of defense before a local fault escalates into a system-wide failure. In the absence of labels, the dominant paradigm trains a reconstruction model on normal data and scores the residual to reach a verdict. Recent work has steadily enriched the expressiveness of the recovery map through Transformers, diffusion models, and cross-scale associations. A residual, however, only certifies that an observation can be recovered; it does not certify that the variables still explain one another as they do under normal operation. A coordinated yet unfamiliar operating transition inflates the residual without breaking any relationship, whereas a sensor drifting away from its peers may leave the residual silent. Methods that fold inter-variable dependencies into the reconstruction objective inherit this ambiguity, because their relational evidence is ultimately supervised by the same recovery loss. We therefore propose CLOSURE, which evaluates recoverability and cross-channel consistency as two normal-data prediction tasks learned in separation. The recoverability task is solved by a diffusion reconstructor conditioned on a discrete memory of normal temporal bases. The consistency task is solved by a relation-closure operator that predicts every channel exclusively from the others, where the target's own trajectory is withheld and sparse signed gates decide which peers are actually used. The two scores are reconciled only through calibration on normal data. Extensive experiments show that CLOSURE consistently outperforms recent reconstruction-, frequency-, and topology-based detectors on public benchmark streams, confirming the value of examining consistency alongside recoverability.
  </details>

- **[VaLoP: Value Gradient Lookahead and Pullback for Reward-Guided Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35607)**  `arXiv:2610.35607`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kwanyoung Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward guidance steers a pretrained diffusion or flow model toward images or actions that a reward model prefers, at sampling time and without retraining the model for each reward. It follows the gradient of a value function, the expected reward given the noisy state. The two common estimates of this gradient, gradients through a one-step denoised estimate and learned value models, are least accurate at high noise, where the coarse structure of the sample is decided. Interestingly, we find that both errors are posterior covariances, which grow with the uncertainty about the clean sample, and hence at higher noise. To reduce them, we evaluate the value at a less noisy state, one sampler step ahead, where this uncertainty is smaller, and pull its gradient back to the current state. This lookahead places both estimators at the two ends of one family and reveals that an intermediate lookahead requires one additional term, a correction through the generator. We therefore propose Value Gradient Lookahead and Pullback (VaLoP), which computes this correction with one vector-Jacobian product, and VaLoP-Fast, which amortizes it with a small head that, for text-to-image models, is trained with a single reward and reused for the others. Across various models, rewards, and benchmark datasets, both robustly outperform existing value-gradient guidance on the target reward, VaLoP-Fast at about the cost of learned-value guidance. Both extend to critic-guided robot policies, where VaLoP-Fast attains the highest success rates on LIBERO and RoboCasa.
  </details>

- **[Evolve on Graph: Self-Evolving Knowledge Graph Reasoning without Question Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35598)**  `arXiv:2610.35598`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zongyuan Sun, Shuai Zhao, Ke Shu, Ziyue Zhu, Shangyang Wu, Yuanlin Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graphs (KGs) provide reliable external knowledge for multi-hop factual reasoning with large language models (LLMs), yet most KGs in real-world domains such as finance and industry lack question--answer (QA) annotations. Without such annotations, existing methods struggle to improve LLM graph reasoning: training-free agents cannot improve their exploration through interaction; reinforcement learning (RL) agents depend on QA data annotated by humans or synthesized by closed-source models; and training data is typically built offline, so its topology mix is fixed and cannot follow the agent's ability. We propose Evolve on Graph (EoG), a framework that lets an LLM self-evolve its graph reasoning without any QA annotations. Through Graph Self-Play, a single LLM alternates between a Proposer that poses questions and a Solver that answers them. To make questions verifiable, Symbolically Verifiable Topology Synthesis (SVTS) samples reasoning topologies on the KG and executes the corresponding first-order logic queries, yielding topology skeletons with correct and complete answer sets; guided by a skeleton, the Proposer explores the graph to pose a multi-hop question. To let task topologies follow the Solver, a Boundary-Aware Graph Curriculum rewards the Proposer by the Solver's score variance, keeping questions at the Solver's capability boundary so that complex topologies enter training as the Solver improves. On six benchmarks over Freebase, Wikidata, and an industrial KG, EoG consistently outperforms prompting-, retrieval-, and agent-based methods, showing that LLM graph reasoning can improve through self-play without QA annotations. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[CANS: Capability-Preserving Neuron Steering for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35530)**  `arXiv:2610.35530`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yucheng Wu, Chengyi Xie, Chenpeng Wang, Liangming Pan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering offers training-free and dynamic control of target behaviors in large language models, but existing methods can impair general capabilities and require domain-specific intervention designs. We introduce Capability-Preserving Neuron Steering (CANS), a unified framework that balances target-behavior control with capability preservation. To make target-behavior improvement and general-capability preservation tractable, we introduce two proxies: log-probabilities of target tokens in model generated responses, and output-distribution KL divergence on general-purpose inputs. Combining the local approximation of the two proxies yields a closed-form solution, enabling lightweight steering without iterative model training or additional inference-time computation. The resulting neuron-wise scaling coefficients achieve effective steering while limiting general-capability loss. A shared data-construction and intervention procedure supports adaptation across domains using sampled responses with target-behavior filtering and target-span annotations. Across safety, toxicity, and hallucination control on Qwen and Llama models, CANS achieves comparable or better steering while better preserving MMLU, BBH, and IFEval performance than SFT, CAA, and neuron-level baselines. On Qwen3-4B, CANS reduces HarmBench attack success from $15.50\%$ to $1.75\%$ with a $1.11$-point decrease in average capability. These results support CANS as a practical approach to behavioral control across domains.
  </details>

- **[Test-Time Confidence Adaptation under RL-Induced Trajectory Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35500)**  `arXiv:2610.35500`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhenzhen Ren, Xiaoqing Zhang, GuoBiao Li, Bowen Fang, Yanxi Wang, Xinpeng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Trajectory-based confidence estimation has shown promising performance for large language models (LLMs) by leveraging rich signals from their reasoning processes. However, we find that such confidence estimators can degrade substantially after agentic reinforcement learning (Agentic RL) as it induces substantial shifts in the reasoning trajectories. More importantly, despite these shifts, we uncover a reliability structure that is largely preserved across Base and RL models: $\textbf{more}$ $\textbf{stable}$ $\textbf{trajectories}$ $\textbf{remain}$ $\textbf{more}$ $\textbf{likely}$ $\textbf{to}$ $\textbf{produce}$ $\textbf{correct}$ $\textbf{answers}$. This preserved relationship provides a transferable, label-free reliability signal for post-RL confidence adaptation. Building on this finding, we propose $\textbf{StaR-TTA}$, a stability-guided framework for label-free test-time confidence adaptation. StaR-TTA jointly learns from correctness supervision and stability-derived reliability supervision on labeled Base trajectories, establishing the connection between trajectory stability and answer reliability. At test time, it reuses stability-induced reliability rankings from unlabeled RL trajectories to adapt confidence scores to the shifted trajectory distribution, without requiring target correctness labels. Across four QA benchmarks, StaR-TTA achieves an average AUROC of \(0.721\), with consistent improvements across unseen datasets, model families and scales, and different Agentic RL algorithms.
  </details>

- **[From Denoising Dynamics to Endpoint Uncertainty in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35425)**  `arXiv:2610.35425`  `cs.AI`  
  _Julio C. Amador Diaz Lopez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predictive uncertainty in stochastic language-model inference can be characterized by repeatedly sampling an input and measuring the distribution of outputs. This requires multiple complete generations. Masked diffusion language models expose another source of information: the evolution of the output during denoising. We ask whether convergence within diffusion trajectories is associated with uncertainty across their final outputs, or endpoints. We study four masked diffusion language models on four reasoning benchmarks, collecting 20 generations per question and 32,000 denoising trajectories in total. Within each trajectory, we measure within-run concentration as the area under the curve describing stable resolution of the eventual output. Across repeated trajectories, we measure endpoint-distribution uncertainty. Stronger within-run concentration is associated with lower endpoint entropy after accounting for model and dataset ($\beta=-0.222$, $p<10^{-25}$). This association persists for multi-token outputs and when reweighting positive-entropy cases. The relationship is asymmetric: high within-run concentration almost exclusively accompanies concentrated endpoint distributions, whereas lower concentration occurs under both concentrated and competing endpoint regimes. This relationship strengthens as denoising proceeds. Features from a single denoising run predict whether independent held-out runs will concentrate on a common endpoint or produce competing endpoints. These results connect dynamics within individual denoising runs to variability across repeated inference. Within-trajectory dynamics contain information about whether independent repetitions will concentrate on a common endpoint or produce competing endpoints, providing a route towards estimating endpoint-distribution properties with fewer complete generations.
  </details>

- **[AdaTrendLike: Adaptive Hierarchical Detrending and Trend Likelihood Scoring for Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35400)**  `arXiv:2610.35400`  `cs.AI`  
  _Van Kwan Zhi Koh, Songnan Lin, Rongmo Luo, Zhiping Lin, Bihan Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid proliferation of Industrial Internet of Things devices has led to increasingly large-scale and non-stationary time-series data, posing significant challenges for time-series anomaly detection (TSAD). Existing approaches often focus on reconstructing or forecasting observations while overlooking the role of trend dynamics, which constitute a major source of non-stationarity and contain valuable information for anomaly identification. To address this limitation, we propose AdaTrendLike, a TSAD framework that directly estimates trend likelihoods for anomaly detection. Specifically, we introduce Standardization Embedding to capture local statistical characteristics as latent representations and AdaDetrending to adaptively decompose input series into trend and detrended components. The detrended component is processed through a Fast Fourier Transform and a complex-valued convolutional neural network to learn normal periodic patterns, while TrendEst models the distribution of the trend component. Based on the estimated distribution, TrendLike computes anomaly scores by measuring the likelihood of observed trends, enabling effective detection of deviations from normal behavior. In addition to improving detection performance, the proposed framework provides feature-level interpretability by identifying anomalies through deviations in the estimated trend distribution. Extensive experiments on five public benchmark datasets demonstrate that AdaTrendLike consistently outperforms state-of-the-art TSAD methods, validating its effectiveness and robustness across diverse application scenarios.
  </details>

- **[RSIV-Harness: A Recursive Self-Improvement Harness Framework for Open-Domain Conversational Text-to-Visualization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35316)**  `arXiv:2610.35316`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiawei Lu, Chen Zhang, Jinwei Lu, Yuanwei Song, Yuanfeng Song, Raymond Chi-Wing Wong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Users often request charts without knowing which database or table holds the relevant data. Such requests may be ambiguous, and users may ask follow-up questions to deepen their analysis or iteratively refine existing charts. A useful assistant must therefore discover relevant data sources among heterogeneous databases, ask clarifying questions when needed, and track evolving user intent while preserving constraints established in earlier turns. Yet recent text-to-visualization agents typically rely on static agent harnesses that are predefined and remain unchanged despite recurring failures across tasks. To address this limitation, we introduce RSIV-Harness, which keeps the language model weights fixed and applies Recursive Self-Improvement to revise the surrounding harness from the agent's own failure traces. To balance flexible self-improvement with stability, we introduce a four-layer framework that evaluates proposed harness revisions through anchor and memory checks, refining those that pass and restoring the best validated harness otherwise. We further introduce ODAC-Vis, a benchmark with 640 tasks for Open-Domain, Ambiguous, and Conversational text-to-visualization that jointly evaluates open-domain data discovery, ambiguity resolution, multi-turn visualization refinement, and executable chart generation. Experiments show that a single round of RSIV-Harness improves all six evaluation metrics relative to the seed harness, with particularly large gains in executability (+28.02 points) and chart structure (+34.01 points), demonstrating the ability to learn from task-solving experience and turn recurring failures across tasks into substantial performance improvements.
  </details>

- **[LLM-Enhanced Heterogeneous Graph Learning with Business Semantics for Financial Fraud Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35298)**  `arXiv:2610.35298`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yixuan Chen, Hongyu Zhan, Jie Sheng, Weiyu Han, Shuai Chen, Tianyi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial fraud detection increasingly relies on heterogeneous transaction graphs, in which entities such as cards and merchants play distinct business roles and the risk meaning of an attribute depends on the entity's role: a high transaction volume may be routine for a merchant but unusual for a card. Heterogeneous graph neural networks preserve typed entities and relations but typically treat these attributes as role-agnostic inputs and leave their business meaning implicit. Meanwhile, most existing methods integrating large language models (LLMs) with graphs focus on homogeneous graphs or heterogeneous graphs with naturally available textual attributes, leaving their use on transaction graphs with structured attributes underexplored. To address this issue, we propose SemHGT, an LLM-enhanced framework that interprets structured attributes according to entity roles and makes the business semantics explicit in three stages: (1) role-aware prompts elicit risk-related business descriptions from node attributes, and a text encoder maps them to semantic vectors that enrich node representations; (2) semantic similarity guides multi-round sampling of target-specific path instances under transaction-to-transaction schemas; (3) schema-level tokens that combine local path evidence with global neighbor summaries are processed together with the target transaction by a unified transformer. Across three benchmark datasets, SemHGT leads the evaluated baselines on most dataset--metric comparisons. Further analyses provide additional evidence for the effectiveness of SemHGT's design and clarify how its semantic modeling and evidence selection contribute to fraud detection. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AgenticCity-Bench: Longitudinal Multimodal Observations of Urban Dynamics with Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35293)**  `arXiv:2610.35293`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaofei Yu, Kailai Sun, Yanyu Mao, Baoshen Guo, Heye Huang, Yongcai Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  City systems are shaped by interactions among infrastructure, human activity, and environmental conditions. Existing earth observation and urban benchmarks are limited in unified modeling and in capturing their joint dynamics. We introduce AgenticCity-Bench, a longitudinal multimodal benchmark for multi-layer unified state modeling and agentic city task reasoning. The benchmark covers 16 cities in 12 countries across six continents from 2000 to 2025, and includes 18 modalities spanning urban function, activity, and environmental state. It organizes heterogeneous data sources around a common spatial reference and calendar index while preserving spatial and temporal coverage, provenance and tile-level availability. On this foundation, AgenticCity-Bench supports urban cross-modal translation together with diverse agentic city tasks involving forecasting, planning and resource analysis. This study provides a unified benchmark for studying the transition from multimodal urban and earth observation to joint understanding and agentic city decision-making. The dataset is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Co-Design Bench: Multimodal Collaborative Coding Evaluation for Agentic Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35138)**  `arXiv:2610.35138`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xilin Wei, Shengyuan Ding, Xinyu Fang, Tiancheng Gu, Yuchi Wang, Zhixun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating human-AI co-design requires testing whether coding agents can translate real users' multimodal requests into working application revisions. We introduce Co-Design Bench, a reference-free benchmark of 273 tasks curated from 37,463 deduplicated queries across 26,314 real user coding sessions. Its two tracks, Multimodal Editing (ME) and Repository Editing (RE), cover localized edits with optional image, audio, video, or document inputs and coordinated repository revisions. Tasks span three capability dimensions: Grounded Understanding, UI Transformation, and Functional Implementation. The tasks pair normalized requests and runnable pre-edit applications with 2,331 hidden expert-authored acceptance criteria. Our judge-then-refute protocol grades criteria using source and browser evidence, challenges positive verdicts with a separate LLM verifier, and audits visual and interaction claims without gold code or target screenshots. Under a shared agentic harness, the strongest of ten model endpoints passes an average of 69.9% of criteria per task but fully satisfies only 26.4% of tasks. All ten endpoints achieve lower criterion completion on repository edits spanning three or four change types than on those spanning one or two. Co-Design Bench provides a testbed for studying the gap between partial requirement satisfaction and complete task success under agentic execution for real-user application revisions.
  </details>

- **[Improving Instruction Hierarchy by Learning from Goal-Goal Conflicts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35093)**  `arXiv:2610.35093`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sizhe Chen, Sanjay Kariyappa, Liwei Jiang, Chong Xiang, Jihye Choi, Chaowei Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The recent incident in which OpenAI agents autonomously compromised Hugging Face's infrastructure illustrates the importance of instruction hierarchy (IH): an LLM should follow lower-priority instructions, such as user requests, only when they do not conflict with higher-priority instructions, such as system policies. IH can be effectively improved by fine-tuning LLMs on simulated examples of instruction conflicts, whose diversity is key to teaching LLMs to properly handle conflicts at test time. However, our decomposition of a task into goals and constraints reveals a conflict type -- goal-goal conflicts -- that is absent from existing IH training sets from OpenAI and NVIDIA. We show the importance of this conflict type: fine-tuning Qwen3.5-9B and Gemma4-12B-it on examples of this conflict type significantly improves IH on a wide range of real-world tasks unseen in training; adding these examples to existing IH training sets for fine-tuning also gives better IH adherence. The IH gains come with trivial mean utility changes on no-conflict tasks across eight benchmarks. Our work advocates including this new conflict type in IH training, even if these examples do not reflect typical LLM use.
  </details>

- **[A Diagnostic Protocol for GNNs in Electricity Load Forecasting: Separating Parameter Sharing from Neighborhood Access](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35074)**  `arXiv:2610.35074`  `cs.AI`  
  _Eloi Campagne, Yvenn Amara-Ouali, Yannig Goude, Itai Zehavi, Argyris Kalogeratos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A graph neural network (GNN) forecasting a collection of load series combines two mechanisms, sharing parameters across series and reading neighboring series, and a gain over a non-graph model may come from either; telling them apart decides whether a graph is worth maintaining. We propose a model diagnostic protocol that compares the GNN with three reference models: a per-node network, a network shared across nodes without neighbors, and the same GNN without edges. Degree-preserving rewiring tests whether neighbor identity matters. After a tuned benchmark on regional load in France and on low-voltage feeders in the UK, we apply the protocol to deliberately short node histories, as when non-stationary demand leaves only recent observations relevant: the feeders, within one week, and a public dataset of client consumption series. On the feeders, sharing parameters recovers most of the error gap between node-specific models and GNNs, about two thirds against a regularized one. Adding the edges to an otherwise identical backbone brings a smaller gain, half or more of which disappears under rewiring. On the client series, the neighbor mean degrades the per-node model on the shorter window and the edges' gain is not consistently distinguishable from zero; on the regions of France, where each node's own history is already informative, the ranking between per-node models and GNNs changes with tuning. A synthetic study points to noisy local measurements as one regime where neighbors help. In an exploratory online aggregation, graph and non-graph forecasters appear complementary, and the weight assigned to GNNs is not proportional to their contribution. In none of the three datasets do the edges account for most of a GNN's advantage over node-specific models, so a forecasting gain should be credited to the graph only after comparison with per-node and shared references.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Single-Attribute Auditing: A Configurable Multi-Attribute Benchmark for Intersectional Fairness in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35069)**  `arXiv:2610.35069`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zheng Zhang, Yifan Wu, Yuxiang Zhou, Qi Liu, Qingyang Mao, Ning Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated for fairness, yet most existing benchmarks audit one sensitive attribute at a time. This single-attribute paradigm can miss intersectional harms, where multiple attributes interact non-additively and produce failures that are invisible under marginal evaluation. We introduce ComFair, a configurable benchmark that converts existing fairness data into 479,066 controlled Dual- and Multi-attribute instances and pairs every augmented instance with its task-equivalent source. Across Recognition, Selection, Continuation, and Conversation, this pairing supports a unified audit of performance or bias-score shifts, how often added attributes introduce new errors or mask existing ones, and model-ranking stability. Experiments on ten LLMs show task- and subgroup-dependent sensitivity: discriminative tasks expose both transition directions even when aggregate changes are modest, generative tasks exhibit larger and directionally asymmetric shifts, and rankings vary with attribute composition. ComFair therefore provides a scalable controlled sensitivity audit---rather than a model of the natural joint distribution of identities---for testing the robustness of fairness-benchmark conclusions beyond single-attribute settings.
  </details>

- **[PatentAgentBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents with Professional Patent Task Suites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35031)**  `arXiv:2610.35031`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yijie Hu, Wei Li, Junjie Zhou, Zhiqi Jiang, Yi Jin, Licong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Patent practice lies at the intersection of technical, legal, and linguistic work, making it challenging yet crucial to technological innovation. Recently, rapid advances in large language models (LLMs) and agentic systems have demonstrated the potential to complete realistic business workflows and even patent works. This raises a question: can current AI systems perform patent work reliably? Existing benchmarks cover a limited range of patent tasks and simplify evidence acquisition by providing reference materials or candidate documents. In this paper, we introduce PatentAgentBench, a benchmark comprising 510 instances across eight task suites: novelty search, freedom-to-operate (FTO) search, design FTO, invalidity search, claim charting, patent drafting, office-action response, and patent translation. Curated from real patent records and professional materials, the benchmark combines task-specific metrics with expert-reviewed annotations and rubrics. Retrieval tasks such novelty search, FTO, etc., require agents to search and compare patent collections without a supplied candidate pool. The drafting and OAR tasks assess the quality of agents' work given technical disclosure and office action notice. We evaluate seven LLMs across four agent harnesses on PatentAgentBench, finding that current systems still struggle to identify novelty, assess risk, and understand practitioner intent throughout patent workflows.
  </details>

- **[Critic's Eye: Review-Grounded Graph Reasoning for Long-Form Motif Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35011)**  `arXiv:2610.35011`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyu Sun, Zhangyue Yin, Xinyi Ju, Xin Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep narrative understanding requires distinguishing what happens from how it is presented, moving beyond the chronological fabula to decode the sjuzet, the discursive organization through which themes are staged. We study this through the recurrence of literary motifs (blood in Macbeth, for instance), where symbolic significance emerges not from local cues, but through repetition-with-variation across the whole work. We operationalize this setting as Computational Motif Discovery, a transductive task that predicts missing line-motif links using the full narrative structure of a play. We propose Critic's Eye, which models the work as a heterogeneous Narrative Topology Graph and performs discriminative inference over global structural evidence. Critic's Eye achieves 84.8% Hit@5 on our benchmark, nearly quadrupling state-of-the-art proprietary foundation models (Gemini-3-pro-preview, ~22.6%). Our analysis reveals that despite massive scaling, sequence-based models hit a "semantic ceiling", struggling to resolve dispersed dependencies. These findings suggest that explicit structural priors are a far more effective inductive bias than parameter scaling for decoding the complex architecture of long-form literature.
  </details>

- **[Why Are LLMs Confidently Wrong? Correcting Overconfident Errors via Causal Head Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34994)**  `arXiv:2610.34994`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jing Ren, Bowen Li, Ziqi Xu, Xuechao Yang, Feng Xia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern large language models (LLMs) often exhibit overconfident errors, assigning high confidence to incorrect predictions and thereby undermining reliability in real-world use. Existing calibration methods typically rely on post-hoc adjustment or retraining, operating at the output level without addressing the underlying causes of miscalibration. We propose CoCHI, (Class-oriented Causal Head Intervention), a framework that improves calibration by identifying and intervening on internal components responsible for distinct confidence behaviours. By analysing predictions through correctness-confidence patterns, CoCHI enables targeted intervention on model internals, reducing overconfident errors while preserving correct predictions. Our approach provides a mechanistic perspective on calibration, linking reliability to identifiable structures within the model rather than treating it as an output-level issue. Experiments across multiple LLMs show consistent improvements in calibration metrics, including Expected Calibration Error, Negative Log-Likelihood, and Brier Score, while maintaining accuracy on five multiple-choice question answering benchmarks. These results suggest that effective calibration requires not only adjusting outputs, but also addressing the underlying causal mechanisms within the model.
  </details>

- **[Stop or Clarify? Experience-Conditioned Active Fact Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34941)**  `arXiv:2610.34941`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuhang Dong, Jinghong Li, Xinyue Wang, Yuanning Cui_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fact-verification benchmarks score the final label, while an interactive verifier must first decide whether the available evidence is sufficient. We focus on that intermediate Stop-or-Clarify Decision. Uncertainty alone is insufficient, because the benefit of another evidence-acquisition step is unknown before the step is taken. Sequential Experience-Adaptive Clarification (SEAC) wraps a fixed verifier with a lightweight evidence-acquisition policy. For each claim, SEAC retrieves evidence, runs the verifier, forms a label-free decision state, and combines stop risk with an observational clarification-utility estimate from earlier executed trajectories. Clarification is requested only when this score exceeds the action cost and decision threshold. On SciFact, LIAR, and FEVER, SEAC improves mean accuracy and macro-F1 over the compared fact-verification and active-retrieval baselines. Equal-budget and formula-level diagnostics support selective clarification as a contributor to the gain, while the small SciFact split leaves its paired improvement inconclusive.
  </details>

- **[PhysEvolve: Verifier-grounded Self-evolution for Physical Reasoning with Executable Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34921)**  `arXiv:2610.34921`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinyuan Hu, Yilei Jiang, Chen Tang, Minghong Cai, Xinran Liu, Xiangyu Yue_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physical reasoning underpins how embodied systems understand and act in the physical world, yet vision-language models (VLMs) remain unreliable on questions about dynamics and physical causality. Progress is critically constrained by the scarcity of grounded counterfactual training data, as observational videos capture only the factual trajectory that occurred. The self-evolving paradigm offers a route beyond fixed datasets by enabling models to construct their own training problems. Extending this paradigm to physics, however, requires an external source of truth that can both validate model-proposed environments and verify alternative physical outcomes under intervention. We introduce \physevolve{}, a verifier-grounded self-evolving framework that trains VLMs for causal physical reasoning from model-proposed executable worlds and simulation-authored tasks. At each round, a shared VLM proposes executable world programs and evidence schemas; a deterministic physics engine freezes each valid world, simulates matched baseline and body-elided counterfactual branches, and synthesizes discrete, answer-bound visual tasks. Exact verifier feedback from task solving updates both the shared policy via GRPO and an adaptive curriculum over physical intents. Starting from Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, \physevolve{} achieves targeted gains across PhysBench, CausalPhys, and ContPhy without human intervention data, improving PhysBench Throwing by $8.8$ points and ContPhy Counterfactual Removal and Goal-Pit by $7.4$ and $24.4$ points. These gains concentrate on interaction-sensitive and altered-world questions, demonstrating zero-shot transfer of causal principles to unseen complex dynamics (cloth, fluid, soft bodies) and real-world video.
  </details>

- **[GADAR: Generalizing Across Domains via Action Ranking for Classical Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34868)**  `arXiv:2610.34868`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rajesh Mangannavar, Pranay Dugar, Prasad Tadepalli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learned policies for classical planning act without search, but each is tied to the domain it was trained on: network modules per action schema, parameters per predicate, or vocabulary-sized encodings mean a trained policy cannot even be executed on a new domain's predicates and action schemas. We present GADAR (Generalizing Across Domains via Action Ranking), a policy that takes the domain description as input, {\pi}(s, G, D), and constructs grounded actions in domains never seen in training, without search. Symbols are described by the roles they play in the domain rather than by their names, in three layers that each extend the previous one: (i) a lifted domain layer embeds D in every state graph through structural signatures; (ii) a binding layer ties each ground action's applicability to the lifted preconditions it instantiates; and (iii) a chain layer states which schema produces what another needs and how far each schema stands from the goal. The same network therefore runs on every domain, seen or unseen. We evaluate zero-shot generalization leave-one-domain-out over eight benchmark domains. A single GADAR policy trained on seven domains solves 58.3% of their test instances, one point below the 59.3% of dedicated single-domain GADAR models, so one policy serves many domains at no cost in coverage; a multi-task control that keeps symbol identity in its weights, while strong on some of the domains it trains on, transfers to none of the held-out ones. Zero-shot, GADAR executes on every held-out domain and exceeds random choice under the same executor on the large test instances of all eight: it solves 22.6% of them on average, against 3.3% for random choice and 0.6% for the same representation without the chain layer, and reaches 68% on held-out Visitall. Zero-shot coverage nonetheless remains well below in-domain coverage on most domains, and we analyze where it is weakest.
  </details>

- **[UniGFM-RAG: Unify Graph Foundation Models and Large Language Models for End-to-End Retrieval and Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34866)**  `arXiv:2610.34866`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zicheng Zhao, Linhao Luo, Zongji Yu, Junnan Dong, Yanchao Tan, Shirui Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities across tasks, but they still struggle with complex reasoning that requires integrating scattered knowledge. Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) addresses this challenge by organizing fragment knowledge as graphs, while recent Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) further improve GraphRAG with graph-native retrieval and reasoning capabilities. However, existing GraphRAG methods usually adopt decoupled retrieve-then-generate pipelines, which create objective misalignment between retrieval and reasoning, and training GFM retrievers typically depends on expensive human annotations. To address these limitations, we propose \ourmethod, a unified end-to-end GraphRAG framework that tightly couples a GFM retriever with an LLM generator. Given a query, the GFM retriever first reasons over the graph to score and retrieve relevant document nodes. These node embeddings are then passed through a fully differentiable graph bridge, which projects them into soft-weighted graph prefixes according to retrieval scores and combines them with raw document texts to form graph-augmented prompts. In this way, structural information is injected directly into LLM generation, while the final answer loss can be back-propagated through the bridge to jointly optimize retrieval and reasoning. To stabilize training and reduce reliance on costly annotations, we further introduce a semantic-guided distillation module that provides auxiliary supervision using pseudo-labels derived from query-document semantic similarities. Extensive experiments on three multi-hop QA datasets and three out-of-domain GraphRAG benchmarks show that \ourmethod consistently outperforms strong baseline methods and generalizes well, which demonstrates the effectiveness of end-to-end GraphRAG with tightly integrated GFM retrieval and LLM reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Behaviour: A Stimulus-Response Safety Evaluation of Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34837)**  `arXiv:2610.34837`  `cs.AI`  
  _Vy Vo, Dinh Phung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans exhibit biological sensitivity to safety-critical stimuli that can trigger defensive action. Across three models and two video benchmarks, we investigate whether similar responses emerge in vision-language-action models by examining their internal representations and action tendencies in response to visible hazards and harmful consequences. Our findings suggest that task execution may remain the default response even when danger is internally registered.
  </details>

- **[Logic Hijacking of RAG: Single-Document Poisoning via Local Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34829)**  `arXiv:2610.34829`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hangyi Zhao, Bonan Zhang, Fusen Guo, Jiaheng Wei, Chao Chen, Tianqing Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-document corpus poisoning under black-box access poses a severe threat to Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). However, existing attacks predominantly rely on explicit factual refutations or imperative instruction overrides. Modern aligned LLMs often arbitrate such conflicts conservatively, rejecting overt contradictions in favor of parametric memory or clean retrieved context while triggering input guardrails. We propose MONOLOGUE, a single-document poisoning framework based on Logic Hijacking without explicit refutation. Rather than disputing the ground truth, our method concedes it as an authoritative historical baseline before introducing the update, weaving a grounded record-correction narrative (e.g., dated institutional reattributions) that presents the counterfactual target as the most recent update. To prevent suspicious live writes, payloads are refined entirely offline via local multi-dimensional gated optimization, coupling automated text quality audits with surrogate behavioral verification. Evaluations across three benchmarks, three retrieval backends, and four open-weight generator regimes show that our method achieves the strongest overall attack efficacy among the evaluated single-document poisoning attacks.
  </details>

- **[TrustJev: Separating Policy Authority from Untrusted Evidence in LLM-Based Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34826)**  `arXiv:2610.34826`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiwei Xu, Xuyang Liu, Xiaolin Peng, Weixian Deng, Jiabao Gao, Zirui Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models enable software to select from predefined actions using natural language inputs. However, the external evidence that supplies facts for these decisions may also contain instructions that attempt to override the application's policy. We propose TrustJev, a framework combining protected policy scoring with bounded evidence corrections. A single Transformer backbone computes policy scores and a correction budget from trusted policy and candidates, then bounds evidence corrections by that budget. Paired supervision encourages stable predictions under instruction insertions that preserve task facts and changed preferences when edits change the correct answer. Authority supervision accommodates evidence when the policy alone cannot determine the decision. We evaluate a 0.6B implementation against 14 baselines on three datasets covering authentication, instruction hierarchy, and executable authorization rules. TrustJev achieves the highest mean Balance, the harmonic mean of accuracy on non-attack and attack inputs, exceeding the strongest baseline on each by 0.57-1.05 percentage points. In the authorization benchmark, it correctly answers all 32 attacks per source in a fixed pool for 95.77% of sources, compared with 80.98% for the strongest comparator. TrustJev maintains comparable inference memory and median latency, with additional overhead primarily during training.
  </details>

- **[The Right Fact, the Wrong Decision: SciValidityBench for Scientific Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34767)**  `arXiv:2610.34767`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuexi Pan, Yongchao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most agent-memory benchmarks frame long-term memory as retrieval: can an agent recover the relevant record from a long and evolving history? Scientific agents face a stricter problem: a correctly remembered fact may still be invalid to use. The same reported test result can support confirmation when the outcome and stopping rule were fixed in advance, yet only exploration when the outcome was selected from twenty inspected results. We call this scientific use validity: whether remembered evidence is eligible to influence a decision under the state governing its interpretation. We introduce SciValidityBench, an executable benchmark factory with 64 TaskSpecs spanning five validity modules and six scientific domains. TaskSpecs couple generated experimental histories with scientific-state definitions, programmatic decision oracles, controlled interventions, and automatic scoring. The SciStateForge compiler-reader framework turns distributed histories into explicit decision-governing state. Counterfactual pairs and targeted corruption-repair controls then test whether models use those dependencies and localize failures. Across four model families, compiled scientific state improves valid decision-making by 9.2-18.8 percentage points over a generic Event Graph. Retrieval alone does not reproduce this cross-family gain, while content-identical flat and typed views show that the improvement comes from exposing governing state rather than a particular graph serialization. SciValidityBench thus shifts memory evaluation from finding the right fact to deciding whether that fact should control the decision.
  </details>

- **[CEPO: A General Framework for Improving Prompt Optimization in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34722)**  `arXiv:2610.34722`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jongha Kim, Seohyun Lee, Jonathan Minho Chung, Jinsung Yoon, Hyunwoo J. Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems (MAS) require both effective coordination and specialized task execution. Existing prompt optimization approaches typically couple these functions within agent-specific prompts. We introduce Coordinator-Executor Prompt Optimization (CEPO), a general framework for improving prompt optimization in MAS. CEPO separates system-level coordination from agent-level execution through a shared coordinator prompt and agent-specific executor prompts, and guides their updates according to their respective roles. This structure allows shared coordination rules to be refined consistently across agents while keeping execution instructions separately adjustable. CEPO provides the prompt structure and optimization guidance, while existing prompt optimizers perform the underlying search. Extensive experiments spanning 11 benchmarks across four domains demonstrate that applying CEPO to four existing prompt optimizers consistently improves their performance. Ablations and qualitative analyses support the proposed design, while controlled studies on a document-understanding MAS further demonstrate improved optimization budget efficiency and performance scaling with the number of agents.
  </details>

- **[Prefetch-Steered Routing: Deterministic Cache-Aware Expert Selection for Offloaded Mixture-of-Experts Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34721)**  `arXiv:2610.34721`  `cs.AI`  
  _Siming Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model does not fit in GPU memory, batch-1 decoding is dominated by copying expert weights from host memory. Cache-aware routing reduces these copies by biasing the router toward resident experts, but combined with asynchronous prefetching it makes the model's outputs depend on transfer timing, so a harness that does not simulate that timing no longer evaluates the deployed model. We propose prefetch-steered routing (PSR): routing conditions on a deterministic logical cache, into which at most n experts predicted by the next layer's router are inserted per layer and token; physical copies affect only latency. On OLMoE-1B-7B with 50% and 25% of experts resident on an RTX 4090, PSR decodes 2.2-2.3x faster than exact routing with LRU and 25-30% faster than the Cache-Prior operating point, with mean benchmark differences of +0.1 and +0.5 points (95% lower bounds -0.8 and -0.3). In greedy generation its lead over Cache-Prior depends on the workload and reverses on HumanEval at 50% residency. On Moonlight-16B-A3B, PSR is 1.9x faster than exact routing with LRU at 37.5% residency and, at the latency of Cache-Prior, significantly better on three of eight benchmarks. On Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, where copies are cheap relative to computation, PSR keeps its advantage over Cache-Prior on the likelihood benchmarks but not its speed. Distillation-trained gates did not help. Speculative decoding with block-level expert selection was slower than PSR with a 1B autoregressive drafter on OLMoE, but with a block drafter on Qwen3.6 it was 2.1-2.9x faster than any autoregressive configuration with at most its GPU memory on the 64 prompts we timed.
  </details>

- **[InferPriv: Evaluating Inferential Privacy Leakage in LLM Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34640)**  `arXiv:2610.34640`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pengrun Huang, Yiwei Fu, Luoxi Meng, Kamalika Chaudhuri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based personal agents are increasingly deployed to handle everyday tasks on users' behalf. These agents have access to private user information, while their tasks involve communicating with third parties; this raises a critical privacy concern that agents may inappropriately reveal sensitive user information to these recipients. Existing benchmarks primarily assess direct privacy leakage, where an agent explicitly states a pre-specified secret or attribute. However, they do not capture \textit{inferential leakage}, where the model leaves indirect cues in the reply that implicitly leaks sensitive attributes. We introduce \textsc{InferPriv}, a benchmark that measures whether an honest-but-curious recipient can infer a user's sensitive attributes from an LLM's reply on routine task. Evaluating five frontier models, we find that inferential leakage substantially exceeds direct leakage across models and prompts. Under a privacy-aware system prompt, GPT-6.1-Sol, Sonnet-5.5, and Gemini-3.8-Flash achieve near-zero direct leakage, yet the inferential leakage rate remains between 16.2--25.2\%. These findings suggest that evaluating explicit leakage alone can substantially understate the privacy risks.
  </details>

- **[HobbyLM: A Sparse Mixture-of-Experts Model Family on a Hobby Budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34582)**  `arXiv:2610.34582`  `cs.AI`  
  _Harish Santhanalakshmi Ganesan, Hariharan M. S., Prabhurajhan R_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We describe HobbyLM, a family of small sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models trained from scratch with a few hundred to a few thousand dollars of rented GPU time. The core is a decoder-only transformer with fine-grained experts, sigmoid routing with auxiliary-loss-free load balancing, grouped-query attention with per-head QK-normalization, and aMuon/AdamW optimizer split. Each of these choices was selected with a single-variable ablation suite at 130M parameters. On this architecture we train a 500M-parameter model (169M active) on 40B FineWeb tokens and post-train it into a chat model, a masked-diffusion language model, a function-calling and computer-use agent, and a single "omni" checkpoint that reads text, images, video, sound and speech through frozen encoders and small projectors. We run these models on a laptop CPU with a from-scratch Rust inference engine. Two of the authors then adopted the architecture without architectural changes to build HobbyLM-1B (1.04B total, 305M active parameters), trained on a 100B-token curated mixture with a high-quality anneal. HobbyLM-1B reaches a 47.5 average on seven zero-shot benchmarks, and its builders also release an instruction-tuned model and an on-device macOS computer-use adapter. Finally, we describe HobbyLM-Image, a 333M-parameter flow-matching diffusion transformer that generates 1024x1024 images in a 32x-compressed latent space, together with a dependency-light CPU implementation of the full text-to-image pipeline. We report what worked, what did not, and where the small-scale ceilings lie. We also state for each component which author designed or built it.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Verification in Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34577)**  `arXiv:2610.34577`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengxin Zhang, Erkang Zhu, Yin Lin, Claire Cardie, Bolin Ding, Jingren Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses enable large language models to act through tools, observe the results, and iteratively work toward a task goal. Verification checks and revises agents' work to improve output quality. Yet there is limited guidance on how harnesses should organize this effort. We study verification as an axis of test-time scaling by examining when to intervene, who checks the work, and how checking leads to revision. Our framework spans four layers of the agent lifecycle: initial instructions, mid-turn reminders, completion gates, and independent review. We compare 17 designs across seven frontier coding models on Terminal-Bench (TB) and SWE-bench Verified (SWE), and introduce VisualBench for expert evaluation of visual and interactive artifacts. Initial instructions and execution-time reminders yield modest, model-dependent average gains on the coding benchmarks, without substantially increasing average output token use. Completion gates can yield larger gains but may substantially increase cost. Independent review by a critic offers a more favorable accuracy-cost tradeoff, improving average accuracy by 5.9-9.5 percentage points on TB and 3.9-6.6 on SWE at 1.13-1.25 and 1.00-1.04 times baseline cost, respectively. On VisualBench, independent review achieves net preferences of 13.2-23.8 points over the baseline across its four designs, exceeding the evaluated L0-L2 designs. Additional review rounds or independent critics further improve average TB accuracy by up to 3.5 percentage points. Together, these results provide empirical guidance for verification design in agent harnesses and support independent review as a practical axis of test-time scaling.
  </details>

- **[How Humans Learn Unfamiliar Worlds: A Think-Aloud Dataset on ARC-AGI-3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34523)**  `arXiv:2610.34523`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuzhi Liu, Archana Warrier, Dat Nguyen, Chengqi Zheng, Kevin Ellis, Yewen Pu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning through exploration and using newly acquired knowledge to achieve goals are central capabilities of general intelligence. Understanding these capabilities requires tracing how learners build, use, and revise world models during interaction. We construct the first dataset that records how humans discover and use the mechanisms of novel environments, using ARC-AGI-3 as a testbed: 144 trajectories from 64 participants across 25 public games, each pairing time-synchronized actions with think-aloud audio, plus 250 trajectories from large language model (LLM) agents with and without harnesses on the same games. We annotate mechanism triggers and recognition, action purposes, and errors and recovery, linking changes in understanding to the observations and actions that support them. We analyze characteristics of human cognition and compare them with those of LLM agents. Both groups anticipate mechanisms and use familiar analogies. Qualitative representations are more common in successful human trajectories than in successful LLM trajectories (50.0% versus 14.3%). Humans more often explore unfamiliar situations without a concrete hypothesis, while hypothesis testing is common in both groups. We also trace how learners recover through belief revision, replanning, and execution correction. As LLMs with well-designed harnesses reach saturated performance on public ARC-AGI-3 games, our cognitive analyses motivate next-generation benchmarks that challenge familiar priors, require accumulated evidence and abstraction, and test planning through subgoals, experimentation, and recovery. Our dataset is available at [this https URL](this https URL).
  </details>

- **[CTXAgent - Self-Controlled Context Management of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34425)**  `arXiv:2610.34425`  `cs.AI`  
  _Matthias Busch, Marius Tacke, Roland C. Aydin, Christian Feiler, Christian J. Cyron_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses keep long sessions inside the context window by compacting the history when a token threshold is crossed. We study an alternative in which the model manages its own context: it decides when to compact, what to keep and what to write as the hand-off, given two tools and one paragraph of guidance. Since earlier work found that open-weight models cannot manage their own context without scaffolding, we compare seven frontier models under identical conditions, with and without the context tools, on token cost and benchmark score, and we compare our harness with three widely used harnesses on two of these models. The benchmark is assembled from existing ones: coding exercises, multi-step SciCode problems and SWE-bench Pro and Terminal-Bench 4.0 tasks are interleaved with planted facts, ephemeral observations and standing instructions, all checked deterministically, in sessions of 35-212 turns that resemble a user working through different tasks in one chat. Most of the strongest models manage their context: they compact and curate regularly, the live context of the mixed sessions stays mostly below 100k tokens, and their benchmark scores mostly do not drop significantly. For the models that manage their context well, the cost reduction is substantial but varies between models, because some models compact so often that other costs increase. With Claude Opus 5.5, the self-managed harness matches Claude Code on task success at 34% lower cost than its explicit 200k-window configuration and 49% lower than its default, with no context window to choose. We release the agent, the benchmark and all result rows.
  </details>

- **[Matched Capabilities, Different Behaviors: A Controlled Study of MCP and CLI Interfaces for Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34298)**  `arXiv:2610.34298`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhenning Shi, Liang Pang, Yongqi Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language agents increasingly act through external tools and interactive environments, making the interface between the model and its executable capabilities part of the decision process. Model Context Protocol (MCP) and command-line interfaces (CLI) can expose the same semantic operations through markedly different model-facing representations. We study whether these representational differences change agent behavior when executable capabilities are matched. We introduce Matched Interface Comparison and Analysis (MICA), which renders MCP and CLI from a shared semantic action contract and routes decoded actions through the same runtime. Across six models and three interactive environments, matched interfaces produce substantial differences in task performance and interaction cost. These effects extend beyond surface validity to semantic action selection, failure pathways, and recovery. Matched-state probes localize sensitivity to capability presentation, realization, and feedback. Paired natural trajectories reveal structured divergence in 94.3% of 2,004 episodes and show that early differences propagate through subsequent interaction. This analysis identifies interface realization as a direct intervention boundary. We therefore introduce Decoupled Action Realization and Targeted Repair (DART), which preserves validated semantic commitments while repairing interface-specific realization. DART improves benchmark score in all six evaluated settings by up to 9.0 points and reduces MCP token cost in all three environments by up to 27.1k tokens per task. Together, these results establish interface representation as part of the closed-loop decision environment shaping agent behavior.
  </details>

- **[Variational Cognitive Memory Abstraction for Long-Horizon Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34251)**  `arXiv:2610.34251`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyu Liu, Daiying Tian, Chenwei Tang, Ruyang Han, Chengyang Zhang, Qingyu Zeng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown remarkable success in robotic manipulation, yet their ability to execute long-horizon tasks remains limited by insufficient long- term memory modeling. Existing methods either rely on implicit temporal representations within policy networks, where historical information can be gradually diluted, or maintain explicit memory buffers that suffer from redundancy, noise accumulation, and inefficient retrieval. In this paper, we propose CogMemory, a novel variational cognitive memory framework that enables VLA systems to selectively preserve and reuse task-relevant historical information. Our approach encodes multimodal trajectories into compact latent memory representations through a selective state- space memory encoder, multi-slot temporal abstraction, and variational information compression. During action generation, dynamic cross-attention retrieval with gated fusion adaptively incorporates relevant memories into policy prediction. With merely 3.45M additional parameters, CogMemory stays lightweight and seamlessly fits both early- and intermediate-fusion VLA backbones. Extensive experiments on long-horizon robotic manipulation benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements across diverse VLA backbones.
  </details>

- **[R2A-Sales: A Policy-Atom-Matched Benchmark for Rule-to-Action Gaps in Goal-Directed Sales Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34185)**  `arXiv:2610.34185`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yihua Qin, Jiajie Jerry Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based sales agents are often evaluated on static policy questions or task success, but deployment asks a sharper question: when a model can state a rule, will it choose compliant, tool-grounded actions under commercial pressure? We introduce R2A-Sales (Rule-to-Action), a policy-atom-matched benchmark for this question. Each item pairs a static probe with a multi-turn sales scenario sharing the same versioned policy atom, facts, evidence, behavioral constraints, and tool contract, so differences cannot be attributed to policy-content mismatch. In the interactive arm, a code-owned ledger verifies committed tool effects and terminal states; unsupported action claims receive no credit. We report static compliance $K$, grounded action compliance $A=\Pr(\text{safe terminal}\land\text{no hard violation}\land\text{non-echo})$, and the matched gap $G=K-A$. The frozen release spans three controlled Chinese-language sales domains, 24 policy atoms, 42 semantic cores, and 2,016 matched pairs. The confirmatory study audits 21,888 runs over 19 cores, covering eight backbones plus four Qwen3-8B prompt treatments (12 configurations). Static compliance is near ceiling $(K=89.9\%\text{--}99.1\%)$, yet eight-backbone action compliance is only $A=3.0\%\text{--}17.3\%$, giving $G=80.4\text{--}92.5$ percentage points. Since $A$ also requires safe termination, $G$ upper-bounds rule-to-action degradation rather than isolating pressure alone. No prompt-only treatment dominates all metrics. R2A-Sales shows that sales-agent reliability must be measured at committed action, not rule recital or task completion alone.
  </details>

- **[From Document Layout to Causal Topology: An End-to-End Architecture for Temporal Causal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34094)**  `arXiv:2610.34094`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhifeng Yang, Wei Luo, Yongjian Chen, Shiqi Ou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) generally lack deep causal reasoning capabilities in temporal reasoning. Inspired by document layout reordering, this paper proposes a paradigm shift from discrete narratives to causal topologies. It constructs two architectures: a training-free neuro-symbolic framework, temporal causal reasoning agent (TCR-Agent), which achieves interpretable causal deduction and counterfactual truncation through external causal graph instantiation and a symbolic intervention engine; and an end-to-end model, temporal causal reasoning former (TCR-Former), which internalizes these mechanisms into the Transformer attention space via a Causal-Topological Attention Mask and temporal span biases. Experiments on the TempoCausal benchmark demonstrate that TCR-Agent comprehensively surpasses existing training-free baselines. At the same time, TCR-Former achieves an accuracy 16.5% higher than that of state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods, outperforming large-scale models such as GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek-V3.2 with only approximately 8.6B parameters. Further analysis reveals a structure-reasoning disconnection phenomenon in existing methods: external symbolic constraints and chain-of-thought reasoning can improve reasoning steps, yet fail to enhance deep causal deductive capability. By internalizing temporal causal reasoning capabilities into the model, TCR-Former effectively bridges this gap, providing an efficient and reliable end-to-end foundation for complex reasoning scenarios.
  </details>

- **[WebViSE: Scaling Visual Design Intelligence from the Open Web for LLM Website Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33929)**  `arXiv:2610.33929`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yifei Sun, Peng Zhao, Le Zhang, Bingjin Chen, Zhengfan Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have made remarkable strides in website development, enabling complete and interactive web experiences to be built from natural-language instructions with far less effort. Yet, non-expert users often struggle to articulate sophisticated visual and interaction designs in natural language. Meanwhile, the open web already contains a vast reservoir of such design knowledge, embodied in mature website implementations. To this end, we introduce Website-derived Visual Skill Extraction (WebViSE), a framework that automatically abstracts these implementations into reusable Visual Skills and builds a structured Skill library. To scale the library, WebViSE organizes its Skills in a multi-parent DAG and jointly evolves Skill descriptions and DAG structure using Skill-conditioned Potential Queries, enabling efficient matching between diverse user instructions and relevant Skills. The auto-evolved structure outperforms non-hierarchical LLM retrieval baselines at only 39.09% of the estimated retrieval cost with Qwen3.8-Max. For any models, WebViSE can be directly injected as plug-and-play guidance, enabling diverse models to produce more visually compelling and aesthetically refined web pages. For open-source models, WebViSE can be used to build a data-synthesis pipeline that internalizes visual design intelligence into model weights. Experiments across multiple web development benchmarks and human evaluation consistently show that WebViSE improves the visual quality and interaction fidelity of artifacts. Overall, our work establishes an automated path for extracting, organizing, and transferring design intelligence from the open web to website-building models. We release all extracted Visual Skills, the associated Skill DAG, and our code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OS-Wizard: Scaling Computer-Using Agents to Specialized Software via Knowledge-Guided Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33907)**  `arXiv:2610.33907`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhoumianze Liu, Zichen Ding, Bowen Yang, Zehao Li, Zhenyu Wu, Yibo Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer-using agents are increasingly capable of performing complex tasks in the digital world. However, high-quality interaction trajectories remain scarce for specialized desktop applications, where limited application knowledge, cumbersome GUI workflows, and incomplete exploration hinder automated collection. We introduce OS-Wizard, an automated trajectory collection framework. It combines tutorial-guided exploration with a hybrid GUI--Code action space, providing application-specific knowledge and simplifying GUI workflows through direct execution of application-native commands. The framework also checkpoints and revisits pivotal states during tree exploration to explore alternative workflows, complementing linear rollouts for broader coverage. We collect 10.9K trajectories comprising 54.9K state--action samples. Fine-tuning open-source base models on this corpus yields a best-performing model that improves performance on a benchmark of specialized desktop workflows by 58.2 percentage points to 64.1\%, surpassing reported human performance of 59.8\% and establishing a new state of the art. The fine-tuned model also improves performance on general desktop workflows using GUI actions only, suggesting that the collected supervision benefits general desktop tasks as well. Comparative experiments and ablations demonstrate the efficiency of trajectory collection and the importance of code actions.
  </details>

- **[ReGE: Stronger Agents through Experience Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33861)**  `arXiv:2610.33861`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiarong Liang, Yubo Wang, Zijie Liu, Yuxuan Zhang, Ping Nie, Wenhu Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repeated attempts at interactive tasks uncover facts, useful operations, and failures that can inform subsequent execution. We introduce ReGE, a framework for turning this recorded experience into guidance for a fresh attempt. Its main implementation, ReGE-Syn, uses a single coach call to jointly synthesize bounded execution records and available artifacts into self-contained guidance, connecting task-specific findings with proposed actions and relevant pitfalls. A student starts from the task's initial environment state and adapts this guidance to current observations. Source generation, synthesis, and student execution use the same base model, without parameter updates or a stronger teacher. Across four benchmarks, ReGE improves accuracy by an average of 9.9 percentage points for GPT and 8.8 percentage points for Claude compared with unguided execution. On matched BrowseComp-Plus subsets, ReGE-Syn reduces guidance-construction tokens by 98.2\% on GPT and 95.5\% on Claude relative to adapted AggAgent, with observed success. Combined construction and execution tokens decrease by 15.7\% and 35.4\%, respectively, counting input plus output and excluding source generation. BrowseComp-Plus ablations further associate access to process information with fewer student steps and show that joint synthesis produces substantially shorter guidance than Direct history or Independent summaries, with observed success. These results demonstrate how agents can use their own recorded experience to improve subsequent task completion, and suggest a low-cost way to consolidate the execution records of parallel agent systems such as agent swarms.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Static Structures: Linking Protein Dynamics to Function via DynaReason-Pro](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33804)**  `arXiv:2610.33804`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhaokang Liang, Mingze Yuan, Quanzheng Li, Wengong Jin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intrinsically disordered proteins (IDPs) carry out their functions through heterogeneous conformational ensembles that reflect their dynamics, yet most protein function prediction methods rely on sequence or a single static structure. To this end, we introduce DynaReason-Pro, the first framework to jointly predict GO terms from conformational ensembles and generate natural-language functional rationales. We evaluate DynaReason-Pro on a curated IDP benchmark from the Protein Ensemble Database (PED), with sequence- and structure-cluster-disjoint splits to prevent data leakage. Variability-stratified evaluation shows the clearest gains in the high-conformational-variability regime.
  </details>

- **[Are We Done with UMLS Injection into Language Models for Medical Question Answering?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33801)**  `arXiv:2610.33801`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiran Zeng, Giacomo Frisoni, Lorenzo Molfetta, Gianluca Moro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Maybe not. The Unified Medical Language System (UMLS) has become an oracle for millions of clinicians and health system developers worldwide. Researchers have increasingly used knowledge graphs derived from UMLS to enhance language models' factual accuracy and reasoning traceability in high-stakes tasks. Medical Question Answering (MQA) is a prime testbed for integration, with benchmarks usually involving professional exercises such as disease diagnosis and drug prescription from patient history. Despite the assumed benefits, empirical evidence shows that UMLS-based graph injection typically yields modest performance gains. This paper reviews existing MQA work, critically analyzing the selected UMLS sources and their usage with language models, and probes each of these choices in isolation. We uncover methodological issues never discussed in prior work, from low UMLS coverage to weak subgraph retrieval pipelines. Based on our findings, we provide actionable recommendations to unlock the untapped potential of UMLS as an external knowledge source.
  </details>

- **[InfiniteWorld: Separating Visual Evidence from Familiar Physics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33777)**  `arXiv:2610.33777`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tong Zhang, Yuxuan Cheng, Yun Peng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physical reasoning requires adapting familiar expectations to the dynamics of the observed world. Existing benchmarks establish broad physical capabilities, yet an overall score leaves unclear whether answers follow visual evidence when familiar rules no longer apply. We introduce InfiniteWorld, a benchmark that pairs physical-reasoning questions with simulator videos whose physical parameters are systematically altered. Its generation pipeline connects simulation, scene screening, paired video translation, question construction, and input checks. Five tasks assess law identification, parameter estimation, temporal reasoning, analytic counterfactual comparison, and law induction. The evaluation defines five matched input settings for each question, comprising text-only evaluation and ordered or shuffled observations from simulator and translated videos. We evaluate a diverse set of video-language models under these matched settings. The results expose a persistent preference for familiar physical rules. Frames improve performance for most models, but gains differ across tasks. Strong accuracy on Earth-normal questions coexists with poor recognition of altered dynamics, and Earth-normal answers remain common when the observed physics changes. Task scores and semantic agreement further distinguish matching physical factors from matching their amount, and correct answers from repeated errors. InfiniteWorld makes these behaviors measurable through paired changes in the evidence supplied for the same question, separating visual evidence from familiar physics.
  </details>

- **[PACE: Research Proving Agent for Adaptive Conjecture Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33690)**  `arXiv:2610.33690`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shu Miao, Feiming Wang, Wentao Long, Chenyi Li, Kun Yuan, Zaiwen Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many mathematical research agents focus on solving problems with fixed statements. However, conjecture research, especially in applied mathematics, may require revising candidate questions or conclusions as investigation proceeds, coupling problem formulation with proof development. We present PACE, a research-proof agent that supports this process through a structured research loop that selects mathematical objectives and develops or challenges candidate mechanisms. The whole prover constructs proofs from these mechanisms, and an independent verifier checks them against the original target. Evidence-tracking memory stores assumptions and dependencies arising during research and marks evidence for revalidation when a candidate or condition changes. This memory supports task-specific context composition that keeps checked premises, exploratory material and unresolved obligations distinct. To evaluate proof construction and conjecture research, we introduce ResearchProofBench, an applied-mathematics benchmark focused on optimization, comprising 51 known-proof tasks with 331 hidden checkpoints under historical abstract access and six open-problem cases. The known-proof tasks assess reconstruction of published results, while the open-problem cases assess mathematical investigation and proof development. On the 51 known-proof tasks, PACE achieves an aggregate Proof Score of 75.16 and completes 20 proofs. In conjecture research, PACE develops conditional results, with its submissions receiving higher review scores than the compared baselines.
  </details>

- **[More: Mode-Optimized Reasoning with Self-Adaptive Effort for Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33684)**  `arXiv:2610.33684`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dianxing Shi, Dingjie Fu, Tianyu Lei, Wangjie Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-evolving training improves the performance of agents on difficult tasks through verifiable feedback, but it often applies costly deliberation uniformly across tasks. To address this problem, we propose Mode-Optimized Reasoning with Self-Adaptive Effort (MORE), a self-evolving training framework that adaptively selects both reasoning form and computational effort for each task.
